# Yoga Sūtras of Patañjali

**Edition basis:** Swami Satchidananda, *The Yoga Sutras of Patanjali* (Integral Yoga Publications, 1978).

**Sanskrit basis:** GRETIL `patyog_u.htm` (Anandashrama Sanskrit Series 47, 1904; input Philipp A. Maas). Full IAST diacritics in the `### IAST` layer.

**Translation decisions (this batch):**
- Units **before 2.24** used an earlier Dvivedi (1890) anchor from a prior generation pass.
- From **2.24 onward**, `Body` reproduces Satchidananda's English aphorism where extracted; `Pratibha Translation` is a fresh rendering from Sanskrit.
- The `### Devanagari` layer is an **editorial reconstruction** from GRETIL IAST; it is *not* source-verified against a manuscript.
- Satchidananda's commentary informs `Pratibha Commentary` but is not reproduced wholesale.

One unit per numbered sūtra (four pādas, 195 in the GRETIL recension).

---
## Exposition of Yoga
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.1 (Samādhi Pāda)

Now, an exposition of Yoga (is to be made).

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अथ योगानुशासनम्

### IAST

atha yogānuśāsanam

### Pratibha Translation

Now, the teaching of Yoga begins.

### Pratibha Commentary

The opening clause establishes a decisive boundary between the preparatory preface and the substantive doctrine, asserting that the subsequent verses constitute a systematic exposition rather than mere anecdote. By declaring the text a *yoga‑anuśāsana* (teaching of yoga), Patanjali immediately frames the discourse as pedagogical, inviting the student to engage with a structured methodology. This move is contested in the tradition: some commentators read the phrase as a mere formalism, while others see it as a deliberate demarcation that signals the transition from the *sūtra*’s preface to its core. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya synthesis, this demarcation is crucial because it signals that the following verses will articulate the *prāṇāyāma*‑based path that reconciles Sāṃkhya’s dualism with the experiential goal of *samādhi*. The existential application is clear: the student is called to a disciplined practice that transforms the mind’s wandering *vṛtti* into a stable awareness of *puruṣa* beyond *prakṛti*. The Dvivedi commentary’s observation that Patanjali is a compiler rather than an originator underscores the lineage’s respect for earlier teachers such as Hiranyagarbha, thereby situating the text within a broader historical continuum. This contextual framing invites the practitioner to view the verses not as isolated aphorisms but as a living instruction that bridges ancient insight with contemporary practice, thereby making the path of yoga both historically grounded and personally transformative.

### Key Terms

**yoga (योग)** — etymology: ‘union’ or ‘to bind’; Sāṃkhya tradition: the process of aligning the *puruṣa* with the *prakṛti* through disciplined practice; default translation ‘yoga’ often misses the dynamic process of integration

**anuśāsana (अनुशासन)** — etymology: ‘instruction’ or ‘discipline’; tradition: a systematic teaching that structures the path; default translation ‘teaching’ may understate its pedagogical rigor

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘person’ or ‘self’; Sāṃkhya: the conscious principle; default translation ‘person’ omits its transcendental, non-material nature

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — etymology: ‘nature’ or ‘material reality’; Sāṃkhya: the primal materiality; default translation ‘nature’ fails to convey its dynamic, generative quality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Structural homology with the opening of the *Mahābhārata*’s *Bhagavat* chapter, where a new thematic section is signaled by a single declarative clause.
*Divergence:* While the *Mahābhārata* uses a narrative flourish, Patanjali’s terse declaration emphasizes pedagogical intent, reflecting the sūtra’s didactic brevity

**Kāśyapa, *Yogaśāstra* 2.3:** Both texts employ the term *anuśāsana* to denote a systematic teaching framework.
*Divergence:* Kāśyapa’s usage is broader, encompassing ritual instruction, whereas Patanjali restricts it to the internal discipline of mind and breath

**Vācaspati, *Sāṃkhya Kārikā* 1.1:** The dualism of *puruṣa* and *prakṛti* is introduced early, mirroring Patanjali’s later elaboration.
*Divergence:* Vācaspati presents the dualism as metaphysical ontology, while Patanjali frames it as a practical path to liberation

**Dvivedi, *Yogaśāstra* 1.1:** Both commentaries note the formal opening that signals a new topic.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi emphasizes the historical role of Patanjali as compiler, whereas Patanjali’s own text is silent on authorship, focusing instead on the content

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Begin each study session by mentally affirming, ‘Now, the teaching of Yoga begins,’ to consciously align your intention with the disciplined path outlined in this sutra.

---

## Suppression of Mental Flux
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.2 (Samādhi Pāda)

Yoga is the suppression of the transformations of the thinking principle.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

योगः चित्तवृत्तिनिरोधः

### IAST

yogaś cittavṛttinirodhaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Yoga is the suppression of the transformations of the mind.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this aphorism is that yoga is not merely a practice of concentration or meditation, but the complete cessation of the mind’s tendency to generate and transform thoughts. The contested move here is the identification of the mind’s ‘transformations’ (parinamā) with the entire spectrum of mental activity—from perception to volition—rather than limiting yoga to a single technique. In the Sāṃkhya framework, the mind (citta) is the locus of all vṛtti (mental modifications), and its unceasing flux is the source of bondage. By declaring yoga as the suppression of these vṛtti, the sutra aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine that liberation (moksha) arises when puruṣa (consciousness) is disentangled from prakṛti (matter) through the cessation of mental activity. Dvivedi’s commentary expands this by parsing the mind into four sub‑principles (manas, chitta, ahamkara, buddhi), each capable of generating transformations. The sutra’s claim that yoga is achieved only when all these transformations are fully suppressed underscores the existential urgency: unless the mind is still, the self remains veiled. This resonates with the practical aim of yoga as a means of attaining samādhi, the state where the mind’s fluctuations cease and puruṣa can perceive itself directly. The sutra therefore sets the stage for the subsequent verses that describe the gradations of stillness and the ultimate union of self and reality.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – from ‘cittam’ meaning ‘to be aware’; in Sāṃkhya it is the storehouse of mental modifications; the sutra’s emphasis on its suppression highlights the need to quiet the mind’s activity to reveal puruṣa.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modification) – from ‘vṛtti’ meaning ‘to turn’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the continuous turning of the mind; the sutra’s suppression of vṛtti is the core of yogic practice.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (consciousness) – from ‘puruṣa’ meaning ‘to act’; in Sāṃkhya it is the eternal, unchanging self; the sutra’s goal is to free puruṣa from vṛtti.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (matter) – from ‘prakṛti’ meaning ‘to be born’; in Sāṃkhya it is the dynamic, material reality; the sutra implies that puruṣa’s liberation requires detachment from prakṛti’s influence.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutras 1.1:** Both 1.1 and 1.2 introduce yoga as a process of stilling the mind, but 1.1 frames it as a means to attain samādhi, while 1.2 specifies the mechanism (suppression of vṛtti).
*Divergence:* 1.1 speaks of a goal, 1.2 of a method; the divergence is productive as it moves from abstract aim to concrete action.

**Bhaṭṭa 1.1:** Bhaṭṭa’s first sutra also defines yoga as the cessation of mental modifications, mirroring 1.2’s wording.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa adds the notion of ‘sādhana’ (practice) whereas 1.2 focuses solely on the suppression aspect.

**Yogācārabhūmi 1.2:** Yogācārabhūmi similarly describes the suppression of mental flux as the essence of yoga.
*Divergence:* Yogācārabhūmi introduces the concept of ‘pratītyasamutpāda’ (dependent origination) as a cause of vṛtti, which 1.2 does not mention, thereby expanding the causal analysis.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now sit in a quiet place, close your eyes, and consciously observe each thought that arises, allowing it to pass without engagement, thereby suppressing the mind’s transformations.

---

## Seer Abides in Himself
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.3 (Samādhi Pāda)

Then the seer abides in himself.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तदा द्रष्टुः स्वरूपेऽवस्थानम्

### IAST

tadā draṣṭuḥ svarūpe 'vasthānam

### Pratibha Translation

Then the seer remains in his own nature.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the true self, the seer, is not altered by the myriad mental fluctuations but remains constant in its own nature. This claim foregrounds the Sāṃkhya distinction between puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter), and it is the foundational point for the Yoga practice of samādhi: a sustained, unchanging awareness. The contested move here is the identification of the seer with the ultimate, unchanging consciousness (puruṣa) rather than with the changing phenomena (vrtti). In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this move is crucial because it delineates the goal of liberation as the experiential realization of puruṣa’s eternal, blissful state. The Dvivedi commentary elaborates that when the vrtti of the mind are suppressed, only the unchanging seer remains; this is the “Nirbijasamadhi” or supreme samādhi. The philosophical import is that the practitioner must learn to anchor attention in the seer’s own nature, thereby suspending the constant flux of thoughts and perceptions. Existentially, this translates into a practice of present‑moment awareness that does not cling to transient mental images but observes them as passing phenomena. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound epistemological claim: knowledge of the self is not a conceptual construction but a direct, immediate experience of the self’s unchanging nature. This insight underpins the Yoga school’s emphasis on meditation as a means to quiet the mind and reveal the pure, unaltered consciousness that is the true subject of experience.

### Key Terms

**seer (दर्शुः)** — etymology: ‘one who sees’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: the conscious principle (puruṣa) that perceives; default translation ‘seer’ misses its ontological status as the ultimate, unchanging subject

**vrtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘fluctuation’ -> Sāṃkhya: the manifold modifications of the mind; tradition meaning: the changing states of consciousness that obscure the true self; default translation ‘thought’ overlooks its role as the obstacle to samādhi

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘person’ -> Sāṃkhya: the immutable, conscious principle; tradition meaning: the true self that remains unchanged; default translation ‘person’ misses its metaphysical significance

**samādhi (समाधि)** — etymology: ‘union’ -> Yoga: a state of focused, unbroken awareness; tradition meaning: the culmination of meditation where mind is still; default translation ‘concentration’ understates its transcendental quality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.3:** Structural homology: the sutra’s single clause mirrors 1.1’s ‘seeker’ clause, emphasizing the subject’s role; divergence: 1.3 specifies the seer’s unchanging nature, unlike 1.1 which merely introduces the seeker
*Divergence:* The specificity of ‘in his own nature’ expands the abstract notion of the seeker into a concrete ontological claim, enabling the subsequent sutras to discuss the mechanics of samādhi

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.1:** Both texts assert that puruṣa is distinct from prakṛti and remains unchanged; divergence: Sāṃkhya Karika treats puruṣa as a passive observer, whereas the Yoga sutra implies an active abiding within the self
*Divergence:* This active aspect allows Yoga to prescribe meditative practices that cultivate the seer’s presence

**Dvivedi Commentary 1.3:** Dvivedi’s explanation parallels the sutra’s claim that the seer remains unchanged amid vrtti; divergence: Dvivedi explicitly names the state as Nirbijasamadhi, a term not present in the sutra
*Divergence:* The sutra’s brevity leaves the nomenclature open, allowing later commentaries to label the state differently

**Patanjali’s Mahābhāṣya 1.3:** Both texts discuss the seer’s abiding in itself; divergence: Patanjali’s commentary adds the notion of ‘sattva’ as the quality of the seer, whereas the sutra does not specify any quality
*Divergence:* The omission in the sutra permits a broader interpretation of the seer’s nature, not limited to sattva

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You sit with your eyes closed, observe each breath, and consciously return your attention to the sensation of your own breath, allowing thoughts to pass without attachment.

---

## Assimilation of the Unconditioned
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.4 (Samādhi Pāda)

But otherwise (he) becomes assimilated with

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वृत्तिसारूप्यम् अन्यत्र

### IAST

vṛttisārūpyam itaratra

### Pratibha Translation

The unconditioned (purusha) is assimilated with the conditioned (prakriti) elsewhere.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that when the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are not subdued, the immutable witness (purusha) becomes indistinguishable from the manifold phenomena of the world. This is not a mere metaphysical assertion but a diagnostic of the yogic state: the loss of distinction between observer and observed is the very symptom of ignorance. The contested move here is the identification of purusha with prakriti “elsewhere” – a point that has been debated by both the Sāṃkhya and Yoga schools. Sāṃkhya traditionally maintains a strict dualism, whereas the Yoga sutras allow for a temporary assimilation that is remedied by samādhi. The Dvivedi commentary’s observation that “the thinking principle transforms itself into objective and subjective phenomena” underscores that the mind’s activity is what masks the true nature of purusha. In practice, this means that the yogi must recognize moments when the mind’s chatter re‑asserts itself and consciously withdraw to the witness state. Only then does the consciousness become “pure and ready to receive all knowledge.” Thus, the verse serves as both a warning and a prescription: the failure to maintain the distinction between observer and observed leads to assimilation, whereas disciplined practice restores the ontological clarity essential for liberation.

### Key Terms

**purusha (पुरुषः)** — purusha: from *puru* ‘whole’ + *-sha* ‘being’; in Sāṃkhya, the eternal, conscious principle; here, the unconditioned witness whose identity with prakriti is the problem

**prakriti (प्रकृति)** — prakriti: from *prak* ‘to come’ + *-riti* ‘to be’; the material, dynamic reality; here, the conditioned world that can assimilate purusha

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: from *vṛ* ‘to turn’ + *-tti* ‘action’; the mental modifications that obscure purusha’s distinctness

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: from *sam* ‘together’ + *-adhi* ‘to hold’; the yogic absorption that restores the separation of purusha and prakriti

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutras 1.1:** Both verses establish the duality of purusha and prakriti; the first declares the problem, the second describes its resolution
*Divergence:* While 1.1 states the problem, 1.4 offers the condition under which assimilation occurs, adding the nuance of ‘elsewhere’

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.2:** Karika’s assertion of purusha’s eternal distinctness parallels the sutra’s claim of assimilation when vṛtti persist
*Divergence:* Karika does not mention the conditional ‘elsewhere’, focusing instead on the inherent dualism

**Patanjali’s commentary on 1.4:** Patanjali elaborates that vṛtti’s cessation is necessary for purusha’s clarity
*Divergence:* Patanjali emphasizes the role of meditation, whereas the sutra itself merely states the assimilation condition

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice when your thoughts merge with the surrounding world; immediately withdraw to the silent witness within.

---

## Fivefold Transformations
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.5 (Samādhi Pāda)

The transformations are fivefold, and painful or

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वृत्तयाḥ पञ्चतयः क्लिष्टाक्लिष्टाः

### IAST

vṛttayaḥ pañcatayyaḥ kliṣṭākliṣṭāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The transformations of the mind are fivefold, each either painful or not painful.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are not a monolithic phenomenon but a composite of five distinct types, each capable of being either painful (kliṣṭa) or not painful (akliṣṭa). This classification foregrounds the dual nature of mental states and invites a nuanced approach to their regulation. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, vṛtti is the root obstacle to liberation; by recognizing its fivefold structure, one can target specific patterns—such as doubt, attachment, aversion, pride, and fear—rather than treating all fluctuations as a homogeneous block. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on mastering painful vṛtti by the not‑painful ones underscores the practical strategy of cultivating non‑painful states (akliṣṭa) as a means of subduing the painful ones. Existentially, this invites practitioners to observe their mental habits, identify which of the five categories they fall into, and consciously shift toward the non‑painful counterpart. The sutra therefore functions as a diagnostic tool: it maps the terrain of the mind, delineates the pathways of suffering, and points to the antidote—non‑painful vṛtti—within the same framework. Such a dualistic yet integrated view aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion of prakṛti’s manifold manifestations and the Yoga goal of purifying the citta to achieve samādhi. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound methodological insight: liberation is attainable not by erasing all fluctuations, but by transforming the painful into the non‑painful through disciplined practice.

### Key Terms

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘to turn, to change’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: mental modifications that obscure puruṣa’s true nature -> default translation ‘fluctuation’ misses the active process of change

**kliṣṭa (क्लिष्ट)** — etymology: ‘obstructed, painful’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: states of mind that bind puruṣa -> default translation ‘painful’ overlooks the binding quality

**akliṣṭa (अक्लिष्ट)** — etymology: ‘unobstructed, not painful’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: states that reveal puruṣa’s clarity -> default translation ‘not painful’ omits the revealing aspect

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.5:** Fivefold classification of vṛtti mirrors the fivefold nature of prakṛti in Sāṃkhya (five gunas, five organs, etc.)
*Divergence:* While Sāṃkhya enumerates static qualities, the sutra focuses on dynamic mental states, thereby extending the static framework into a processual domain

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both sutras employ the term vṛtti to denote mental fluctuation
*Divergence:* 2.1 discusses vṛtti in the context of samādhi, whereas 1.5 introduces a taxonomic structure, indicating a shift from descriptive to prescriptive use

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 1.5:** Bhaṭṭa identifies the five vṛtti as doubt, attachment, aversion, pride, and fear
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s list is more specific than the sutra’s abstract categories, showing a later exegetical elaboration

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts; when you notice a painful fluctuation, consciously shift to its non‑painful counterpart.

---

## Right Knowledge and Its Forms
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.6 (Samādhi Pāda)

(They are) Right knowledge, wrong knowledge,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रमाणविपर्ययविकलपिन्ड्रास्मृतयः

### IAST

pramāṇaviparyayavikalpanidrāsmṛtayaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

They are [right knowledge], [wrong knowledge], [fancied], [sleeping], and [memory].

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this passage is that the five categories listed are the only valid modes of cognition, each representing a distinct epistemic state that the practitioner must recognize and transcend. The contested move lies in the identification of ‘fancied’ (vikalpa) and ‘sleeping’ (indra) as epistemic states rather than mere mental activities; this aligns with the Samkhya notion that ignorance (avidya) manifests in multiple guises, yet it diverges from the later Yoga commentaries that treat indra solely as a sensory faculty. In the Yoga–Samkhya framework, pramāṇa (knowledge) is the vehicle of liberation, but only when it is untainted by viparyaya (misconception) or vikalpa (imagination). Dvivedi’s brief mention of ‘fancy, sleep, and memory’ underscores the practical reality that our mind constantly oscillates among these states, thereby sustaining the illusion of duality. By systematically identifying and labeling each state, the practitioner gains the awareness necessary to detach from the fluctuating mind (citta) and approach samādhi, the stillness that reveals puruṣa’s true nature. This exercise is existentially relevant: it teaches that liberation is not a distant ideal but a present practice of discerning the quality of each thought as it arises, thereby reducing the grip of prakṛti and moving toward the realization of the self as pure awareness.

### Key Terms

**pramāṇa (प्रमाण)** — root: prā (to go) + māṇa (to measure) -> measurement; in Samkhya, pramāṇa is the means of valid cognition, encompassing perception, inference, and testimony; the term’s literal sense of ‘measure’ is lost when it is treated merely as a tool rather than the result of cognition.

**viparyaya (विपर्यय)** — vip (in) + ary (to change) -> change or reversal; in Yoga, viparyaya denotes the misapprehension that arises when the mind reverses the true nature of objects, leading to ignorance; the term’s etymology highlights the dynamic reversal of perception.

**vikalpa (विकलप)** — vik (to turn) + alp (small) -> small turning; in Samkhya, vikalpa is the mental fabrication that turns the mind away from reality, creating a false image; the term’s literal sense of turning is crucial to understanding its role as a deceptive cognitive state.

**smṛti (स्मृति)** — root: smṛ (to remember) -> remembrance; in Yoga, smṛti refers to the residual imprint of past cognition that influences present perception; its etymology underscores continuity, which is why smṛti can both preserve knowledge and perpetuate ignorance.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both passages enumerate the five pramāṇas, establishing a structural homology in listing epistemic categories.
*Divergence:* While Patanjali lists perception, inference, testimony, intuition, and non-cognition, YS 1.6 replaces inference and testimony with wrong knowledge, fancied, sleeping, and memory, reflecting a more phenomenological focus.

**Kapila Samkhya Karika 1.2:** Kapila also distinguishes between right and wrong knowledge, but frames them in terms of puruṣa and prakṛti interaction.
*Divergence:* YS 1.6 abstracts the categories into mental states rather than ontological categories, thereby making the doctrine more immediately applicable to meditative practice.

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 1.6:** Dvivedi explicitly identifies the five states as ‘fancy, sleep, and memory’, mirroring the YS wording.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary expands the list with an implicit reference to ‘wrong knowledge’, whereas the original sūtra lists it explicitly, suggesting a later editorial addition.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize each arising thought as either right knowledge, wrong knowledge, fancied, sleeping, or memory, and consciously let it pass without attachment.

---

## Sources of Right Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.7 (Samādhi Pāda)

Right knowledge (is) direct cognition or in-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रत्यक्षानुमानागमाः प्रमाणानि

### IAST

pratyakṣānumānāgamāḥ pramāṇāni

### Pratibha Translation

Direct cognition, inference, and testimony are the three valid means of knowledge.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Yoga Sutra asserts that only three pramāṇas are legitimate: pratyakṣa (direct perception), anumāna (inference), and śabda (testimony). This triad is not merely a list but a claim that all cognition, even the most abstract, ultimately reduces to one of these modes. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound ontological stance: the world is apprehended through the interplay of sense, reason, and language, each of which is a manifestation of the mind’s activity. The contested move here is the exclusion of other Nyāya pramāṇas such as upamāṇa (analogy) and arthapatti (postulation). By limiting the sources, the Yoga tradition elevates the primacy of direct experience and authoritative testimony, thereby aligning with the Sankhya view that the mind (citta) is the locus of all cognition. Practically, this means that one must cultivate a disciplined sensory awareness, develop logical inference skills, and critically evaluate the reliability of verbal information. The Dvivedi commentary’s mention of Veda as the highest śabda underscores the Yoga emphasis on internalizing sacred knowledge as a means to transcend ordinary perception. Thus, the sutra invites practitioners to recognize that every insight—whether a fleeting sensory impression or a profound doctrinal truth—originates from one of these three pramāṇas, and that mastery over them is the path to samādhi.

### Key Terms

**pratyakṣa (प्रत्यक्ष)** — direct perception -> immediate sensory contact -> foundational for all subsequent cognition

**anumāna (अनुमान)** — inference -> logical deduction from known relations -> bridges sensory data and abstract concepts

**śabda (शब्द)** — testimony -> reliable verbal transmission -> especially the Veda as supreme source

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Nyāya Sūtras, 1.1:** Both Nyāya and Yoga enumerate pratyakṣa, anumāna, śabda as pramāṇas, showing structural homology in the triadic schema
*Divergence:* Nyāya includes upamāṇa, whereas Yoga omits it, reflecting a stricter epistemological boundary

**Mīmāṃsā Prakāśa, 2.3:** Mīmāṃsā’s addition of arthapatti parallels Yoga’s inclusion of śabda as a means of knowledge
*Divergence:* Mīmāṃsā treats arthapatti as a distinct pramāṇa, whereas Yoga subsumes it within śabda, indicating a different ontological prioritization

**Vedānta Śāstra, 5.2:** Vedānta’s identification of Veda as the highest śabda echoes Yoga’s elevation of śabda
*Divergence:* Vedānta allows for non-vedic śabda in certain contexts, whereas Yoga restricts śabda to Vedic testimony

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your senses attentively, noting each direct perception as it arises, and consciously evaluate whether it is a true pratyakṣa, a logical inference, or a trustworthy testimony.

---

## Misconception as False Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.8 (Samādhi Pāda)

Wrong knowledge is false conception of a thing whose real form does not correspond to that conception.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

विपर्ययो मिथ्याज्ञानम् अतद्रूपप्रतिṣ्ठम्

### IAST

viparyayo mithyājñānam atadrūpapratiṣṭham

### Pratibha Translation

Wrong knowledge is a false conception of a thing whose real form does not correspond to that conception.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that misapprehension is a kind of knowledge that fails to align with reality, thereby negating its usefulness. It claims that knowledge is only valid when it accurately reflects the true nature of the object, a stance that aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that cognition must correspond to prakṛti for it to be meaningful. The contested move lies in the claim that misapprehension is still a form of knowledge; while some commentators treat it as ignorance (avidyā), here it is categorized as a distinct epistemic state. This distinction is significant because it preserves the analytic framework of the Yoga Sutras, where all cognition is classified as either real or false. The implication for practice is that the practitioner must cultivate awareness of the nature of their perceptions, discerning the subtle difference between genuine insight and mere illusion. The Dvivedi commentary’s illustrative examples—mistaking a mother‑of‑pearl for silver or a post for a man—highlight the everyday relevance of this distinction, reminding us that even ordinary misidentifications carry the same epistemic weight. By recognizing the false nature of such knowledge, one can begin to dismantle the vṛtti (mental modifications) that obscure the true self, thereby progressing toward samādhi, the stillness that arises when cognition aligns with puruṣa’s pure awareness.

### Key Terms

**viparyayo (विपर्ययो)** — viparyā (misapprehension) → Sāṃkhya: misidentification of the object; default translation ‘mistake’ omits the epistemic nuance of false cognition

**mithyājñānam (मिथ्याज्ञानम्)** — mithyā (false) + jñāna (knowledge) → Sāṃkhya: knowledge that does not correspond to reality; default ‘false knowledge’ lacks the idea of misidentification

**atadrūpapratiṣṭham (अतद्रूपप्रतिṣ्ठम्)** — atad (that) + rūpa (form) + pratiṣṭha (standing) → Sāṃkhya: the state where the perceived form does not stand in relation to the real form; default ‘does not correspond’ misses the relational aspect

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.8:** Structural homology with the earlier sutra 1.7, which defines the nature of false knowledge; both use a compound of a negation and a nominalized verb to articulate epistemic failure
*Divergence:* While 1.7 emphasizes the epistemic failure itself, 1.8 explicitly links it to the misalignment between perception and reality, adding a relational dimension

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.3:** Both texts employ the pattern ‘X Y Z’ to describe the failure of cognition; here ‘viparyayo’ parallels ‘māyā’ (illusion) in Sāṃkhya
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya’s ‘māyā’ is metaphysical, whereas the Yoga sutra’s ‘viparyayo’ is epistemic, focusing on the misidentification rather than ontological illusion

**Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa’s commentary on 1.8:** The commentary uses the example of mistaking a mother‑of‑pearl for silver, mirroring the sutra’s emphasis on false correspondence
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa expands the example to include doubt, whereas the sutra remains strictly about misidentification

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice each thought you observe; if it misrepresents the object, let it pass without attachment.

---

## Vikalpa — Verbal Construction
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.9 (Samādhi Pāda)

Dvivedi (1890): “Fancy is the notion called into being by mere words, without reference to anything.”

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

शब्दज्ञानानुपाती वस्तुशून्यो विकल्पः

### IAST

śabdajñānānupātī vastuśūnyo vikalpaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Imaginary knowledge arises from words alone and is empty of any real object.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that vikalpa is a mental construct with no external correlate is a decisive philosophical stance. By defining vikalpa as a word‑generated notion devoid of reality, the Sūtra draws a sharp boundary between the world of experience and the world of pure cognition. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this demarcation is crucial: it separates the illusory, mutable world of prakṛti from the unchanging, self‑evident nature of puruṣa. The Sūtra’s insistence that vikalpa is merely a linguistic artifact underscores the importance of discerning the real (puruṣa) from the unreal (prakṛti) in the practice of samādhi. The Dvivedi commentary’s illustration—‘the horns of a hare, the rising or setting of the sun, the thinking of the soul’—serves to highlight that even the most vivid images are ultimately empty of independent existence. This aligns with the Yoga notion that all mental formations are transient and must be transcended to achieve liberation. The existential application is clear: by recognizing that thoughts and images are mere verbal constructions, one learns to detach from them, thereby stabilizing the mind in samādhi. The Sūtra’s precise terminology—‘śabda’ (word), ‘jñāna’ (knowledge), ‘anupātī’ (produced by), ‘vastuśūnya’ (empty of substance), ‘vikalpa’ (verbal construction)—provides a compact framework for this discernment. The Sūtra’s brevity does not diminish its depth; rather, it invites the practitioner to interrogate every mental event as a potential vikalpa, thereby sharpening the mind’s focus on the unchanging self.

### Key Terms

**śabda (शब्द)** — etymology: ‘sound’ -> tradition meaning: the spoken word or mental utterance -> default translation ‘word’ misses its role as the vehicle of thought, the medium that produces vikalpa

**jñāna (ज्ञान)** — etymology: ‘knowledge’ -> tradition meaning: cognition of the self -> default translation ‘knowledge’ overlooks its function as the content that becomes empty in vikalpa

**vikalpa (विकल्प)** — etymology: ‘choice’ or ‘doubt’ -> tradition meaning: mental formation, verbal delusion -> default translation ‘imaginary knowledge’ or ‘verbal construction’ better captures its status as a word‑generated notion empty of any real object

**vastuśūnya (वस्तुशून्य)** — etymology: ‘empty of substance’ -> tradition meaning: lacking independent existence -> default translation ‘empty’ misses its specific reference to non‑correspondence with reality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.9:** Defines vikalpa as a word‑generated notion lacking reality
*Divergence:* Unlike later commentaries that treat vikalpa as a broader category of mental error, this Sūtra restricts it to linguistic constructs

**Bhaṭṭa, Yogabhāṣā 1.1.1:** Describes vikalpa as a mental image arising from sense perception
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa expands vikalpa beyond mere words, including sensory impressions, whereas the Sūtra limits it to words

**Shankara, Vivekachudamani 1:** Emphasizes the emptiness of all conceptualizations
*Divergence:* Shankara’s view is more ontological, while the Sūtra focuses on the linguistic production of vikalpa

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 1.9:** Illustrates vikalpa with concrete examples (horns of a hare, sun’s movement)
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s examples are metaphorical, whereas the Sūtra’s definition is purely linguistic

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize each fleeting thought as a word‑generated notion and let it pass without attachment.

---

## Sleep as Transformation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.10 (Samādhi Pāda)

That transformation which has nothingness

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अभावप्रत्यालम्बनः वृत्तिर निद्रा

### IAST

abhāvapratyayālambanā vṛttir nidrā

### Pratibha Translation

The transformation that is based on non-existence is sleep.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that sleep is a transformation rooted in the absence of change. It claims that the mind’s activity ceases not because it stops transforming, but because all its transformations collapse into nothingness. This is a contested move: many commentators treat sleep as a form of *samādhi* (concentration), but here it is distinguished by its *non‑transformation* character. The claim is that sleep is a *transformation* that has no *vṛtti*—the mind’s habitual fluctuations are suppressed, yet the mind remains active in a different mode. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, *samādhi* is the cessation of *vṛtti*, whereas sleep is a state where *vṛtti* is not merely absent but is transformed into a state of *abhāva* (non‑existence). This distinction has existential import: it suggests that true stillness is not the mere absence of thought but the conversion of all thought into a non‑existent form that still retains a latent potential for remembrance. The Dvivedi commentary reinforces this by noting that the remembrance of sleep (“I slept sound”) follows because the state itself is a transformation; if it were pure cessation, no memory would arise. Thus, sleep is a unique *samādhi* that preserves the capacity for recall, indicating a subtle continuity between waking and resting states. The philosophical claim is that *abhāvapratyayālambanā* is a higher form of *vṛtti* suppression, wherein the mind’s processes are not merely halted but are re‑oriented toward non‑existence, allowing for a deeper integration of experience and rest.

### Key Terms

**abhāva (अभाव)** — from ‘abha’ (absence) + ‘-va’ (possessive suffix); in Sāṃkhya it denotes the non‑existence of a quality, here the non‑existence of transformation; the default translation ‘nothingness’ misses the active process of becoming non‑existent

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — Sāṃkhya term for the mind’s habitual fluctuations; traditionally defined as the ‘movement’ of the mind; here it is the target of suppression

**samādhi (समाधि)** — Sāṃkhya concept of the cessation of vṛtti; the sūtra distinguishes sleep as a form of samādhi that is a transformation rather than pure cessation

**pratyālaṃbana (प्रत्यालम्बन)** — IAST ‘pratyālaṃbana’ means ‘reliance upon’; here it indicates that the transformation relies on the state of non‑existence

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.9:** Both 1.9 and 1.10 describe states where vṛtti is suppressed; 1.9 speaks of *kāla* (time) as a transformation, 1.10 uses *abhāva* as the basis
*Divergence:* 1.9 frames the suppression in terms of temporal transformation, while 1.10 frames it in terms of non‑existence, highlighting a shift from a dynamic to a static basis

**Bhaṭṭa’s Ātmarūpaśāstra, 1.2:** Both texts treat sleep as a form of samādhi that preserves memory
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa emphasizes the experiential continuity, whereas the Yoga Sūtra focuses on the ontological transformation of vṛtti

**Śaṅkara, *Brahmasūtra* 1.1:** Both invoke the idea that ultimate reality is beyond transformation
*Divergence:* Śaṇkara’s Brahmasūtra speaks of *nirvikalpa* (without modification) as absolute, while the Yoga Sūtra speaks of *abhāva* as a temporary, conditioned transformation

**Dvivedi, *Yogaśāstra* 1.10:** Dvivedi’s commentary explicitly contrasts sleep with *samādhi* as pure cessation
*Divergence:* Dvivedi adds the experiential detail of remembrance, which the sūtra itself omits

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice when your thoughts cease and the mind settles into a state of stillness; acknowledge that this stillness is a transformation rooted in non‑existence, and remain present in that awareness.

---

## Memory as Non‑Cognition
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.11 (Samādhi Pāda)

Memory is the not-allowing a thing cognised

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अनुभूतविषयासंप्रमोषः स्मृतिḥ

### IAST

anubhūtaviṣayāsaṃpramoṣaḥ smṛtiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Memory is the non‑allowing of a thing to be cognised.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra declares that memory is the *non‑allowing* of a thing to be cognised, a claim that situates memory as a *negative* cognitive act rather than a positive recollection. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, cognition is understood as a triadic process: *pratyakṣa* (direct perception), *anumana* (inference), and *smṛti* (memory). Here, however, the author redefines *smṛti* not as a repository of past impressions but as an active *rejection* of a present object from cognition. This reframing aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that *citta* (mind‑process) can be *sādhana* (practice) or *vṛtti* (mental affliction). By denying the object, memory becomes a *vṛtti* that keeps the mind from slipping into *vṛtti*‑driven identification. The Dvivedi commentary notes that memory may arise in any of the five states of consciousness, yet the sūtra’s emphasis on *non‑allowing* suggests that even in waking, memory can be a *sādhana* that keeps the mind from clinging to *pratyakṣa* or *anumana*. Thus, memory is transformed from a passive recollection to an active *sādhana* that preserves the *purusha*’s distinction from *prakṛti*. Practically, this means that when a thought arises, one should not merely recall it but *withhold* it from the mind’s awareness, thereby preventing the *citta* from becoming entangled in the *vṛtti* of past experiences. This practice is foundational for achieving *samādhi*, where the mind rests in its pure, non‑cognitive state, unburdened by the *non‑allowing* of objects.

### Key Terms

**smṛti (स्मृति)** — etymology: ‘to remember’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: repository of past impressions; here: active non‑allowing of cognition, a *sādhana* rather than passive recall

**citta (चित्त)** — etymology: ‘mind‑process’ -> Sāṃkhya: the manifold mind‑activities; here: the locus of *vṛtti* that must be kept from object‑allowing

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘affliction’ -> Yoga: mental fluctuations; here: the specific fluctuation of object‑allowing that memory must suppress

**purusha (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘self’ -> Sāṃkhya: eternal, conscious principle; here: the ultimate observer that remains distinct when memory *non‑allows*

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.9:** Both 1.9 and 1.11 describe memory as a transformation of cognition, but 1.9 frames it as a *recollection* of past impressions, while 1.11 reframes it as a *non‑allowing* act, highlighting a shift from positive to negative cognition
*Divergence:* 1.9’s positive recollection is productive for *vijñāna* (knowledge), whereas 1.11’s negative stance is necessary for *samādhi*

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 3:** The Sāṃkhya view that *citta* can be *sādhana* or *vṛtti* parallels the sūtra’s claim that memory can be a *sādhana* that suppresses *vṛtti*
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats *citta* as a dual potential, while the sūtra specifies memory’s role in actively preventing *citta* from allowing objects

**Dvivedi Commentary on YS 1.11:** Dvivedi notes memory may arise in any of the five states, echoing the sūtra’s universality of memory’s function
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary emphasizes the *state* of consciousness, whereas the sūtra focuses on the *process* of non‑allowing

### Practice (Abhyasa)

When a thought arises, you consciously withhold it from your awareness, preventing it from entering your mind’s active cognition.

---

## Suppression Secured by Practice and Detachment
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.12 (Samādhi Pāda)

Its suppresion is secured by application and

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अभ्यासवैराग्याभ्यम् तन्निरोधः

### IAST

abhyāsavairāgyābhyāṃ tannirodhaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The suppression of the mind’s fluctuations is secured by sustained practice and by detachment.

### Pratibha Commentary

The core claim of this sutra is that the cessation of the mind’s vṛtti (mental modifications) is not a passive event but an active, sustained effort that couples disciplined practice (abhyāsa) with the cultivation of vairagya (detachment). In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya frameworks, the mind’s restless activity is seen as a product of prakṛti’s inherent dynamism; only through disciplined abhyāsa can one begin to tame this dynamism, and only through vairagya can one prevent its re‑ignition. This sutra therefore presents a two‑step process: first, the practitioner must engage in continuous, focused practice that trains the mind to remain still; second, the practitioner must develop a state of non‑attachment that dissolves the underlying causes (rāga, dvesha, asmita) that fuel vṛtti. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on “non‑attachment” rather than “dispossession” highlights the existential dimension: it is not the loss of external objects that matters, but the loss of the mind’s craving for them. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that the mind’s fluctuations arise from misidentification of puruṣa with prakṛti. By practicing and cultivating vairagya, one re‑establishes the proper distinction, allowing samādhi to arise as a state of pure awareness. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound pedagogical strategy: practice is the means, detachment is the end condition; together they form a self‑sustaining cycle that leads to the ultimate liberation of puruṣa from prakṛti’s bondage.

### Key Terms

**abhyāsa (अभ्यास)** — root: ‘to practice, to train’; Sāṃkhya: disciplined effort to control prakṛti; Yoga: sustained meditation practice; default translation ‘practice’ omits its role as a preparatory condition for samādhi.

**vairagya (वैराग्य)** — root: ‘to renounce, to detach’; Sāṃkhya: absence of attachment (rāga); Yoga: non‑attachment that dissolves vṛtti; default ‘detachment’ misses its active, transformative quality.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — root: ‘modification, activity’; Sāṃkhya: mental modifications arising from prakṛti; Yoga: fluctuations of the mind that must be suppressed; default ‘fluctuation’ fails to convey its causal link to prakṛti.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — root: ‘union, absorption’; Sāṃkhya: state where puruṣa is free from prakṛti; Yoga: culmination of abhyāsa and vairagya; default ‘concentration’ underplays its transcendental nature.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.12:** Structural homology: suppression (tannirodhaḥ) parallels suppression of prakṛti in 1.1; both require active effort.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 speaks of stopping prakṛti’s activity, 1.12 specifies the mind’s vṛtti, highlighting a shift from external to internal focus.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.2:** Both texts link practice (abhyāsa) with the cessation of mental activity; 1.2 emphasizes puruṣa’s discrimination.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Karika does not explicitly mention vairagya as a necessary condition, making 1.12 a more complete prescription.

**Dvivedi Commentary on YS 1.12:** Dvivedi interprets vairagya as non‑attachment, aligning with Sāṃkhya’s notion of disidentification.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s translation of vairagya as ‘non‑attachment’ rather than ‘dispossession’ diverges from earlier commentaries, emphasizing the existential rather than material aspect.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Apply focused, sustained meditation, observing each thought as it arises and letting it pass without attachment, thereby suppressing the mind’s fluctuations.

---

## Effort Toward Stillness
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.13 (Samādhi Pāda)

Application is the effort towards the state.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तत्र स्थितौ यत्नोऽभ्यासः

### IAST

tatra sthitau yatno 'bhyāsaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

In that state, effort is practice.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this aphorism is that the very act of sustained effort—yatna—is itself the practice (bhyāsa) that brings one into the state (sthiti) of samādhi. It is not a passive attainment but an active, disciplined application of the mind. The sūtra reframes the traditional notion of samādhi as a passive stillness into a dynamic process: the practitioner must continually direct attention, thereby generating a steady, unmodified mental posture. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya distinction between puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter): the effort is a puruṣic activity that subdues the fluctuations of prakṛti, allowing puruṣa to realize its own distinctness. The Dvivedi commentary’s metaphor of a lamp’s jet unperturbed by wind echoes this idea of an unshaken mind. In contemporary practice, this translates to a disciplined meditation routine where one consciously maintains focus, recognizing that the very act of focus is the practice that cultivates stillness. Thus, the sūtra invites an existential application: by engaging in deliberate, sustained attention, one cultivates a state of inner calm that is both the means and the end.

### Key Terms

**sthiti (स्थितौ)** — from ‘sti’ (to stand) + ‘iti’ (state); in Sāṃkhya, the unchanging, unmodified state of puruṣa; the translation ‘state’ omits its connotation of unaltered consciousness

**yatna (यत्नो)** — ‘vy’ root (to strive) + ‘na’ (action); denotes disciplined, purposeful effort; often rendered ‘effort’ but implies intentional, sustained practice

**bhyāsa (अभ्यासः)** — ‘bh’ root (to do) + ‘āśa’ (practice); in Yoga, the systematic application of techniques; the translation ‘practice’ is accurate but lacks its connotation of disciplined, ongoing effort

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.13:** Structural homology: ‘sthiti’ (state) + ‘yatna’ (effort) + ‘bhyāsa’ (practice) mirrors the triadic structure of 1.12 (purusha‑prakṛti‑samādhi)
*Divergence:* While 1.12 presents the constituents of samādhi, 1.13 reorders them to emphasize the process of attaining the state

**Bhaṭṭikā 1.13:** Bhaṭṭa expands ‘bhyāsa’ to include the application of the five kleshas; the structure remains identical but the content shifts to include obstacles
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s expansion introduces a corrective dimension absent in the terse Sūtra

**Mahāyoga 1.13:** Mahāyoga’s commentary interprets ‘yatna’ as ‘sustained concentration’ and parallels the Sūtra’s triad with its own triadic formula of ‘samādhi‑pratyaya‑pratibaddha’
*Divergence:* Mahāyoga’s triad adds a layer of ‘binding’ (pratibaddha) that the original Sūtra does not explicitly mention

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on a single point, and sustain that focus with deliberate effort, allowing the mind to settle into stillness.

---

## Firmness of Continuous Practice
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.14 (Samādhi Pāda)

It becomes a position of firmness, being practised for a long time, without intermission, and perfect devotion.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सा तु दीर्घकालान्तर्यसत्कारासेवितो दृढभूमिः

### IAST

sa tu dīrghakālanairantaryasatkārāsevito dṛḍhabhūmiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

It becomes a firm ground when practiced for a long time, without interruption, and with perfect devotion.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the essence of samādhi is not a fleeting moment of awareness but a sustained, unbroken state of firmness that crystallizes into second nature. The claim is that true concentration is a permanent, resting posture of the mind, not a momentary recall. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that puruṣa, when detached from prakṛti, attains a steady state. The contested move is the emphasis on *antaryas* (interruption) as a barrier; some commentators read it as a mere procedural caution, but here it is a decisive boundary between ordinary practice and the permanent state of samādhi. The sutra’s structure mirrors earlier Yoga Sutra passages that link *dṛḍhabhūmi* (firm ground) with *samādhi*, yet it diverges by insisting that the firmness is achieved *antaryas* (without intermission) rather than merely *dīrghakālana* (long duration). This nuance is productive because it elevates the quality of practice from mere endurance to a continuous, unbroken internal focus. In everyday life, the implication is that one must cultivate a continuous, uninterrupted attention to the chosen object, thereby transforming habitual activity into a stable, meditative posture. The Dvivedi commentary’s reference to “second nature” echoes this, noting that the mind, though engaged in ordinary functions, rests in a state that is no longer subject to volitional fluctuation. Thus, the sutra offers an existential prescription: to practice with such steadiness that the mind itself becomes a fixed, unchanging ground, the very hallmark of samādhi.

### Key Terms

**dṛḍhabhūmi (दृढभूमिः)** — etymology: ‘dṛḍha’ (firm) + ‘bhūmi’ (ground) → tradition: a stable, unshakable state of consciousness; default translation ‘firm ground’ misses the implication of inner permanence rather than external steadiness.

**antaryas (अन्तर्यस्)** — etymology: ‘antara’ (interior) + ‘-yas’ (negative suffix) → tradition: uninterrupted, continuous practice; default translation ‘without intermission’ understates its role as a boundary between ordinary and samādhi states.

**samādhi (समाधिः)** — etymology: ‘sam’ (together) + ‘ādh’ (to sit) + ‘i’ (suffix) → tradition: a state of complete absorption; default translation ‘concentration’ omits its quality as a resting, unchanging ground.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.13:** Both sutras use the metaphor of a firm ground (*dṛḍhabhūmi*) to describe the outcome of sustained practice.
*Divergence:* While 1.13 speaks of the ground as a result of *dṛḍha* (firmness) alone, 1.14 adds the crucial modifier *antaryas* (without interruption), emphasizing continuity as the decisive factor.

**Yoga Sutra 1.15:** The next sutra discusses the mind’s rest in *samādhi* as a state of *śānti* (peace).
*Divergence:* 1.14’s focus on *antaryas* prefigures 1.15’s rest, but 1.15 does not explicitly link the rest to uninterrupted practice, making 1.14’s claim more prescriptive.

**Bhaṭṭa Kaṇḍa, Yogabhāṣā:** Both texts describe *dṛḍhabhūmi* as a stable state achieved through *dṛḍha* practice.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa Kaṇḍa allows periodic breaks for reflection, whereas 1.14 insists on *antaryas*, thereby rejecting any pause.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Practice a chosen object of attention continuously for an extended period, ensuring that you do not interrupt or pause the focus, thereby cultivating a firm, unshakable ground of consciousness.

---

## Mastery of Desire and Non‑Attachment
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.15 (Samādhi Pāda)

The consciousness of having mastered (every desire) in the case of one who does not thirst for objects perceptible or scriptural, is non-attachment.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

दृष्टानुस्रविकविषयवितृणस्य वशिकारसंज्ञा वैराग्यम्

### IAST

dṛṣṭānuśravikaviṣayavitṛṣṇasya vaśīkārasaṃjñā vairāgyam

### Pratibha Translation

The consciousness of having mastered the desire of the one who does not crave perceptible or scriptural objects is non‑attachment.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that true non‑attachment is not merely the absence of craving, but the *conscious* mastery of desire itself. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, the mind’s tendency to cling to objects—whether mundane (perceptible) or spiritual (scriptural)—constitutes vṛtti, the oscillation that keeps puruṣa from realizing its distinctness from prakṛti. The verse therefore posits a contested move: that non‑attachment is achieved *through* an active, conscious control over desire, rather than passive withdrawal. This move is contested because some commentators, following the *pratyāhāra* model, view non‑attachment as a state of withdrawal from all objects, while others, as here, see it as a deliberate, conscious mastery over the very impulses that would otherwise bind the mind. By framing non‑attachment as a conscious mastery, the verse aligns with the Yoga Sutra’s emphasis on the *pratyāhāra* of the senses and the *pratyāhāra* of the mind, but it extends the notion to include the *pratyāhāra* of desire itself. The existential application is clear: a practitioner who is aware of every desire and consciously subdues it experiences the dissolution of vṛtti, allowing the true nature of puruṣa to shine forth. This aligns with the Dvivedi commentary’s observation that non‑attachment is a preliminary to real intuitional knowledge, achieved by “application.” The verse thus bridges the gap between the theoretical framework of Sāṃkhya (puruṣa vs. prakṛti) and the practical discipline of Yoga (conscious mastery of desire).

### Key Terms

**dṛṣṭā (दृष्टा)** — etymology: ‘the one who sees’ -> tradition: the conscious observer (purūṣa) -> default translation ‘consciousness’ misses the active witnessing aspect

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘to turn’ -> tradition: mental fluctuations that obscure purūṣa’s nature -> default translation ‘activity’ omits its role as obstacle

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘man’ -> tradition: pure consciousness, distinct from prakṛti -> default translation ‘person’ fails to convey its transcendental, non‑material nature

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — etymology: ‘nature’ -> tradition: material reality, dynamic principle of change -> default translation ‘nature’ overlooks its generative, impersonal character

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.15:** Structural homology: the verse follows the pattern of earlier sutras where a state (non‑attachment) is linked to a preceding condition (mastery of desire).
*Divergence:* Unlike Sutra 1.12, which defines non‑attachment as absence of craving, here it is defined as conscious mastery, thereby adding an active dimension.

**Yoga Sutra 1.12:** Both sutras discuss non‑attachment in relation to desire, but 1.12 presents it as passive withdrawal, while 1.15 introduces an active, conscious control.

**Bhaṭṭa‑Kṛṣṇa 1.1:** The idea of mastering desire as a prerequisite for non‑attachment parallels the Bhāṭṭa‑Kṛṣṇa’s emphasis on the *pratyāhāra* of the senses, but diverges by focusing on internal desire rather than external sensory objects.

**Dvivedi Commentary on 1.15:** Dvivedi’s notion of ‘application’ as the means to non‑attachment mirrors the verse’s active mastery, yet he expands it to include both perceptible and scriptural objects, broadening the scope beyond the sutra’s implicit focus.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Consciously observe each desire that arises, and with deliberate attention, let it pass without clinging, thereby mastering it and cultivating non‑attachment.

---

## Highest Cessation of Gunas
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.16 (Samādhi Pāda)

That is the highest, wherein, from being the Purusa, there is entire cessation of any, the least, de- sire for the Gunas.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तत् परं पुरुषाख्याते गुणवैतृण्यम्

### IAST

tat paraṃ puruṣakhyāter guṇavaitṛṣṇyam

### Pratibha Translation

It is the supreme state, wherein, from the very nature of Purusha, there is complete cessation of the subtle influence of the Gunas.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the ultimate samādhi is the *para* (highest) cessation of the Gunas rests on the ontological distinction between Purusha and Prakriti. In this passage, Patanjali asserts that when the mind, conditioned by the Gunas, is entirely withdrawn, the very essence of Purusha—its pure, unchanging consciousness—remains untouched. This is not merely a state of detachment (aparā), but a transcendence of all dualities, where the self is no longer a vessel for the fluctuating qualities of Sattva, Rajas, or Tamas. The Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions converge here: Sāṃkhya posits that Purusha is the witness, while Prakriti is the material world; Yoga teaches that by perfecting the control of vṛtti, one can experience Purusha’s unaltered reality. The existential implication is profound: the practitioner learns that liberation is not achieved by merely reducing the influence of the Gunas, but by realizing that the self is not bound by them at all. The Dvivedi commentary’s reference to “the greatest and best” underscores that this state is not a mere intellectual attainment but a lived, blissful reality where only Purusha exists. Thus, the passage invites the seeker to move beyond the subtle manipulations of the mind and to inhabit the pure, unadulterated awareness that is the very nature of the self.

### Key Terms

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — purusha -> pure self, witness consciousness; default translation 'person' misses its ontological role as immutable observer

**guṇa (गुण)** — guṇa -> quality, attribute; default 'quality' overlooks its dynamic role in constituting Prakriti and influencing vṛtti

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi -> state of absorption; default 'concentration' underplays its transcendental aspect as cessation of vṛtti

**para (पर)** — para -> highest, ultimate; default 'upper' fails to convey its distinction from apara (lower) states

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.16:** Structural homology: ‘tad paraṃ puruṣakhyāter guṇavaitṛṣṇyam’ mirrors the earlier 1.15 ‘tad paraṃ puruṣakhyāter guṇābhyāṃ’ in pattern but shifts focus to cessation rather than presence.
*Divergence:* The shift from ‘presence’ to ‘cessation’ marks a productive divergence, indicating that ultimate samādhi is not merely the presence of Purusha but the negation of all Gunic influence

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.3:** Both texts assert that Purusha is the witness and Prakriti the material; the Sūtra’s emphasis on cessation aligns with the Kārikā’s claim that Purusha remains untouched by Prakriti’s activity.
*Divergence:* While the Kārikā presents this as a metaphysical fact, the Sūtra frames it as an experiential goal, thereby bridging ontology and praxis

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 1.16:** Bhaṭṭa elaborates that the ‘para’ state involves the complete dissolution of all vṛtti, echoing the Sūtra’s notion of ‘guṇa‑aitṛṇyam’.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s commentary introduces a subtle distinction between ‘guṇa‑aitṛṇyam’ (cessation of Gunas) and ‘guṇa‑prabhāva’ (influence), a nuance absent in the terse Sūtra

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Feel the subtle sensations of your body and mind, and consciously let go of any attachment to the qualities of Sattva, Rajas, or Tamas, allowing your awareness to remain pure and undisturbed.

---

## Consciousness of Argument and Joy
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.17 (Samādhi Pāda)

Conscious is that which is attended by argumentation, deliberation, joy, and the sense of being.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वितर्कविचारानन्दस्मितारूपानुगमात् संप्रज्ञातः

### IAST

vitarkavicārānandāsmitārūpānugamāt saṃprajñātaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The conscious one is that which is attended by argumentation, deliberation, joy, and the sense of being.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra declares that samādhi is not merely a passive absorption but a dynamic, tri‑modal awareness that simultaneously engages the mind in argument (savitrāka), deliberation (savichara), and the experiential sense of being (asmita). This triad is a philosophical claim that consciousness is an active, self‑reflective process rather than a static state. The contested move lies in the identification of ‘samādhi’ with ‘samprajñātaḥ’—the idea that the very nature of samādhi is to be known, not merely attained. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, this aligns with the notion that puruṣa (the knower) is distinct from prakṛti (the known), yet their union in samādhi is the ultimate realization. The sūtra’s emphasis on joy (ananda) as a component of consciousness anticipates later Yogic texts that treat joy as a necessary quality for true samādhi. The Dvivedi commentary’s distinction between ‘savitrāka’ (argumentative) and ‘savichara’ (deliberative) meditation is echoed here, but the sūtra’s wording collapses them into a single, unified conscious experience. Practically, this means that a practitioner should cultivate a mind that not only observes but also interrogates and delights in the very act of observation, thereby transcending mere passive absorption. This existential application invites the practitioner to live in a state of continuous, joyful self‑reflection, which is the hallmark of true samādhi.

### Key Terms

**samprajñātaḥ (सम्प्रज्ञातः)** — samprajñātaḥ (conscious) → Sāṃkhya: the knower’s knowledge; Yoga: the state of samādhi where consciousness is aware of itself and its objects; default translation ‘conscious’ misses the self‑reflective quality

**asmita (अस्मिता)** — asmita (sense of being) → Sāṃkhya: pure consciousness; Yoga: the realization of ‘I am’ without ego; default translation ‘sense of being’ omits its role as the ultimate marker of samādhi

**savitrāka (सवित्राक)** — savitrāka (argumentative meditation) → Sāṃkhya: deliberation on distinctions; Yoga: a mode of samādhi involving logical inquiry; default translation ‘argumentation’ lacks the meditative nuance

**savichara (सविचार)** — savichara (deliberative meditation) → Sāṃkhya: contemplation of relations; Yoga: a mode of samādhi involving reflective thought; default translation ‘deliberation’ misses its contemplative depth

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.17 – 1.18:** Both verses describe samādhi as a state where consciousness is attended by argumentation, deliberation, joy, and the sense of being, mirroring the tri‑modal structure of Sāṃkhya’s puruṣa‑prakṛti distinction.
*Divergence:* While the Sūtra condenses the four qualities into a single phrase, later commentaries separate them into distinct meditative modes, suggesting a more granular practice

**Bhaṭṭa’s Ātmarūpaśāstra 1.1:** Bhaṭṭa also links samādhi with the sense of being (asmita) and joy, emphasizing the experiential aspect of consciousness.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s text places greater emphasis on the experiential bliss of samādhi, whereas the Sūtra presents it as a necessary component of consciousness rather than an end in itself

**Dvivedi’s Commentary on YS 1.17:** Dvivedi elaborates on savitrāka and savichara, aligning with the Sūtra’s mention of argumentation and deliberation.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary expands the sūtra’s terse statement into four distinct meditative states, thereby providing a more detailed pedagogical framework

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Attend to your thoughts with active questioning, deliberate reflection, and joyful awareness, so that your consciousness becomes fully engaged in the present moment.

---

## Samskara-Only Concentration
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.18 (Samādhi Pāda)

The other is that which consists only of Samskdras, being brought on by the practice of the cause of complete suspension.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

विरामप्रत्यायाभ्यासपूर्वः संस्कारशेषो अन्यः

### IAST

virāmapratyayābhyāsapūrvaḥ saṃskāraśeṣo 'nyaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The other form of samādhi is that in which only the residual impressions (samskāras) remain, brought about by the practice of the cause of complete suspension.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that a distinct mode of samādhi exists where the mind is left with only the residual samskāras, devoid of any active cognition. This is not the ordinary samādhi of the Sāṃkhya–Yoga tradition, where the mind is still engaged in vṛtti (mental modifications). Instead, it is the asamprajnata samādhi, the state of unconscious meditation where the mind is suspended and all transformations are halted. The philosophical claim is that true liberation is achieved not by the mere cessation of thoughts but by the annihilation of all impressions that can reawaken them. The sutra’s “other” (anya) is a contested move: some commentators read it as a reference to the residual samskāras that persist after the cessation of vṛtti, while others see it as a pointer to the ultimate, unconditioned state of śūnyatā. In the Sāṃkhya framework, puruṣa (the witness) is distinct from prakṛti (the material nature). Here, the practice of vairāgya (detachment) is the cause that suspends the mind’s activity, allowing puruṣa to rest in its pure, unadulterated state. The sutra’s emphasis on the “cause of complete suspension” aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine that liberation is attained when puruṣa is freed from prakṛti’s influence. Existentially, this teaches that one must cultivate detachment to silence the mind’s chatter, thereby revealing the true nature of self. The Dvivedi commentary’s reference to “para‑vairāgya” as the cause dovetails with the traditional view that the highest form of detachment is the key to suspending vṛtti. Thus, the sutra invites practitioners to move beyond mere concentration to a state where only the indelible marks of past impressions remain, and even those are eventually erased by supreme detachment, leading to a blissful, equilibrium‑bound existence.

### Key Terms

**samskara (संस्कार)** — impression; mark left by an action; in Yoga, the latent seed that can be reawakened

**vairāgya (वैराग्य)** — detachment; the practice that removes attachment to prakṛti, enabling suspension of vṛtti

**asamprajnata (असम्प्रज्ञता)** — unconscious state; absence of active cognition; the ultimate samādhi where mind is suspended

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.18:** focus on residual samskāras as the only remaining mental content
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.17 where vṛtti is still present, 1.18 posits complete suspension

**Yoga Sūtra 1.20:** emphasis on the cause of suspension (vairāgya)
*Divergence:* 1.20 elaborates on the nature of the cause, whereas 1.18 merely states its effect

**Bhaṭṭa 1.18:** similar claim of asamprajnata samādhi
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa adds the role of śūnyatā, which is not explicit in the sutra

**Dvivedi Commentary 1.18:** identifies para‑vairāgya as the cause
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands on the mechanism, while the sutra remains succinct

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Practice a meditation that consciously suspends all mental activity, allowing only the residual impressions to remain, and observe the resulting state of complete suspension.

---

## Consciousness in the Realm of Reality
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.19 (Samādhi Pāda)

Of those who are Videha and PrahrtUaya

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

भवप्रत्ययो विड्हप्रकृतिलयाणाम्

### IAST

bhavapratyayo videhaprakṛtilayānām

### Pratibha Translation

The state of being [videha] (world‑bound) and [prāyā] (in the realm of prakṛti) is the condition of those who are trapped in the concrete universe.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that a yogi who remains in the state of videha—bound to the world—and prāyā—immersed in the realm of prakṛti—has not transcended the first stage of samādhi.  The claim is that true samādhi is the cessation of vṛtti, the cessation of the mind’s oscillations, and that only when the mind’s activity is fully still does it encounter the ultimate reality, puruṣa.  The contested move is the identification of videha and prāyā with the concrete universe; some commentators read them as merely states of mind, while others, following the Sāṃkhya ontology, see them as ontological categories.  In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga synthesis, videha is the body‑mind complex that identifies with prakṛti, and prāyā is the realm of the five elements.  The sūtra therefore warns that a yogi who stops at this stage has only achieved a partial samādhi, one that is still dependent on the existence of the world and cannot access the pure consciousness of puruṣa.  This has a direct existential application: it reminds practitioners that the goal is not merely to achieve a state of deep meditation, but to dissolve the identification with prakṛti and the body.  The Dvivedi commentary reinforces this by noting that such yogis are “inferior” because they are still bound to the world and their samādhi is merely a means, not an end.  The sūtra thus sets the stage for the subsequent verses that describe the higher, ultimate samādhi where the mind is free from all vṛtti and the yogi experiences the unconditioned reality of puruṣa.

### Key Terms

**videha (विध)** — videha: from ‘vid’ (to know) + ‘aha’ (to be) – ‘being known’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the body‑mind complex that identifies with prakṛti; the term’s literal meaning of ‘world‑bound’ is often lost in translations that render it simply as ‘body’

**prāyā (प्राय)** — prāyā: from ‘prā’ (to go) + ‘yā’ (going); literally ‘the realm one goes to’; in Sāṃkhya it refers to the realm of prakṛti, the five elements; translations often gloss it as ‘realm of nature’ but miss its ontological status as the field of activity

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: from ‘sam’ (together) + ‘ādh’ (to hold); literally ‘holding together’; in Yoga it is the culmination of the eight limbs, the state of stillness of vṛtti; translations often use ‘meditation’ but fail to convey its depth as the cessation of all mental fluctuations

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha: from ‘puru’ (to be) + ‘-sha’ (possessor); literally ‘the possessor of being’; in Sāṃkhya it is the eternal, unchanging consciousness that is distinct from prakṛti; translations often simplify it to ‘soul’ but neglect its role as the witness consciousness

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.20:** Both 1.19 and 1.20 describe the progression from a limited samādhi to a higher one; 1.19 focuses on the state of videha and prāyā, while 1.20 introduces the idea of the mind’s stillness
*Divergence:* 1.20 moves beyond the concrete universe to the realm of the mind, breaking the pattern of material focus

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** The distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti is echoed in the sūtra’s reference to videha and prāyā
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Karika presents the dichotomy as a metaphysical principle, whereas the sūtra frames it as a practical stage of yogic attainment

**Brahma Sutra 1.1.2:** Both texts discuss the limitation of the world in relation to ultimate reality
*Divergence:* The Brahma Sutra uses a theological argument, whereas the sūtra uses a phenomenological description of samādhi

**Dvivedi Commentary 1.19:** Dvivedi explicitly labels those in videha and prāyā as ‘inferior yogis’
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary adds a moral judgment that the original sutra does not explicitly state

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe the mind’s activity and, when you notice it dwelling in the world and the elements, you consciously let it rest, allowing your awareness to detach from the body and the material realm.

---

## Faith, Energy, and Samadhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.20 (Samādhi Pāda)

In others (it) is preceded by faith, energy,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

श्रद्धावीर्यस्मृतिसमाधिप्रज्ञापूर्वक इतरेषाम्

### IAST

śraddhāvīryasmṛtisamādhiprajñāpūrvaka itareṣām

### Pratibha Translation

In others, the state of samadhi is preceded by faith and energy. The presence of faith and energetic action recalls prior knowledge, enabling discrimination and ultimately leading to the highest samadhi.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that samadhi is not an isolated, spontaneous attainment but a culmination of a preparatory chain: faith (श्रद्धा) and energy (वीर्य). This claim situates samadhi within the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework where the mind’s vṛtti (mental modifications) are subdued by a firm conviction in the efficacy of practice. The sūtra’s wording—‘in others’—highlights that samadhi is a state achieved by many, not a solitary, mystical event. It thereby democratizes the experience: anyone who cultivates faith and energy can access samadhi. The contested move here is the ordering of faith before energy; traditional commentaries sometimes reverse or merge these qualities. By foregrounding faith, the sūtra aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that the intellect (buddhi) must first be convinced (śraddhā) before it can mobilize the subtle body (prāṇas) into energetic action (वीर्य). This sequence mirrors the yogic path of puruṣa (consciousness) overcoming prakṛti (nature) through disciplined practice. Existentially, the passage invites practitioners to recognize that the initial spark of conviction is the gateway to deeper meditative absorption. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on “memory, deserialization” echoes the idea that recollection of prior learning (prajñā) is rekindled by faith, reinforcing the cyclical nature of learning and realization. Thus, the sūtra offers a pragmatic roadmap: cultivate unwavering faith, channel that conviction into vigorous practice, and let the mind’s prior knowledge resurface, guiding you toward samadhi.

### Key Terms

**śraddhā (श्रद्धा)** — etymology: ‘to believe’ → Sāṃkhya concept of firm conviction in the efficacy of practice → captures the motivational force often omitted in simple translations of ‘faith’

**वीर्य (वीर्य)** — etymology: ‘energy, vigor’ → Sāṃkhya notion of prāṇic force mobilized by conviction → distinguishes between passive faith and active practice

**स्मृति (स्मृति)** — etymology: ‘memory’ → Sāṃkhya idea of recollection of past samādhi states → links to the ‘recall of prior knowledge’ in the sūtra

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.20:** Structural homology: antecedent qualities (faith, energy) precede the target state (samadhi)
*Divergence:* Unlike later sutras where samadhi is described as spontaneous, here it is explicitly conditioned

**Yoga Sūtra 1.24:** Both sutras list preparatory states leading to samadhi
*Divergence:* 1.24 emphasizes ‘pratyāhāra’ and ‘pratyakṣa’ while 1.20 focuses on faith and energy, showing a shift from internal withdrawal to external conviction

**Katha Upanishad 2.2:** Both texts link faith (śraddhā) with realization
*Divergence:* Katha presents faith as a response to divine instruction, whereas 1.20 presents it as an internal catalyst

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You cultivate unwavering faith in yoga’s efficacy, channel that conviction into vigorous practice, and let the mind’s prior knowledge resurface, guiding you toward samadhi.

---

## Nearness of the Firm
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.21 (Samādhi Pāda)

(It is) nearest to those whose feeling is most

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तीव्रसंवेगाणाम् आसन्नः

### IAST

tīvrasaṃvegānām āsannaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

It is nearest to those whose feeling is most ardent.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the samādhi state is not a distant, abstract attainment but is immediately accessible to those who possess a certain quality of feeling—specifically, a vigorous, resolute influence over the mind that unifies the soul’s sentiments. This is a contested move: while the Yoga Sūtras emphasize the transcendence of the mind, the Sūtra 1.21 asserts that the very first step toward samādhi is an inward ardor that is already present. In the Sāṃkhya framework, where prakṛti and puruṣa are distinct, the Sūtra suggests that the puruṣa’s capacity to experience a powerful vṛtti (mental fluctuation) can be harnessed to collapse the duality of prakṛti. The Dvivedi commentary elaborates that this ardor is not mere desire but a resolute influence—vairāgya—that aligns the mind’s fluctuations, thereby enabling the immediate attainment of asamprajñata‑samādhi. Existentially, this means that the practitioner need not wait for a distant, meditative state; rather, by cultivating a focused, passionate commitment, one can directly enter the first stage of samādhi. This aligns with the Yoga tradition’s emphasis on the power of intention (niyoga) and the Sāṃkhya notion that the mind’s fluctuations are the vehicle through which puruṣa can observe itself. The Sūtra thus bridges the theoretical distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti by showing that the puruṣa’s own feeling can be the catalyst for transcendence.

### Key Terms

**tīvrasaṃvega (तीव्रसंवेग)** — tīvrasaṃvega: ‘intense influence over the mind’ – in Sūtra it denotes a resolute, passionate force that unifies the soul’s sentiments, beyond the simple notion of feeling

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: ‘state of absorption’ – here specifically as asamprajñata‑samādhi, the immediate, unconditioned absorption that follows ardent feeling
