**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘mental fluctuation’ – the Sūtra implies that a vigorous vṛtti can be harnessed to collapse prakṛti, aligning with Sāṃkhya’s view of mind as the vehicle of puruṣa

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: ‘nature’ – the unmanifested material principle that, when unified by ardent feeling, allows puruṣa to observe itself

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtras 1.21:** Structural homology: the Sūtra’s single clause mirrors the pattern of earlier Sūtras that link a quality to a state (e.g., 1.2, 1.3).
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.2, which speaks of the mind’s stillness, 1.21 emphasizes active feeling, suggesting a more dynamic path to samādhi

**Bhaṭṭa, Yogabhāṣā 1.1.1:** Both texts link ardent feeling (vyāsa) to immediate samādhi, underscoring the importance of intention.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa expands on the role of niyoga, whereas the Sūtra condenses the idea into a single clause, making the claim more aphoristic

**Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad 3.2.1:** The Upaniṣad’s mention of ‘ardent desire’ as a means to liberation parallels the Sūtra’s focus on feeling.
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad frames desire as a vehicle for liberation, while the Sūtra frames it as a prerequisite for samādhi, a more immediate meditative state

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 1.21:** Dvivedi’s explanation of ‘sāṃvega’ as resolute influence aligns with the Sūtra’s claim of ardent feeling.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi adds the notion of vairāgya, which the Sūtra does not explicitly mention, thereby enriching the interpretation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on a single object, letting your feeling grow intensely and resolutely until the mind’s fluctuations unify, and you enter the first stage of samādhi.

---

## Mild, Moderate, Excessive Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.22 (Samādhi Pāda)

A further distinction arises on account of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

मृदुमध्यमध्यिमात्रत्वात ततः अपि विशेषः

### IAST

mṛdumadhyādhimātratvāt tato 'pi viśeṣaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The samādhi state is mild, moderate, and excessive, and it is also special.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the depth of samādhi is not a single continuum but a triadic classification that mirrors the threefold gradations of effort and feeling found in the preceding verses. The contested move is the inference that the same three categories apply to both the means (the practice) and the resulting state, a reading that some commentators treat as an overextension of the earlier enumeration of ‘tivra, madhyam, mṛdu’. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, samādhi is the culmination of the pratyāhāra–pratyakṣa–pratyābhyās cycle, and its quality is determined by the degree of detachment (tivra) and the intensity of concentration (madhyam). The present verse invites the practitioner to recognize that the same gradations that govern the practice also govern the attainment; a mild practice yields a mild samādhi, a moderate practice a moderate samādhi, and an intense practice an excessive samādhi. This mirrors the Sāṃkhya notion that the same principle (pratyakṣa) manifests in varying degrees depending on the interaction of puruṣa and prakṛti. The existential implication is that one need not aim for an unattainable ‘excessive’ samādhi if one is in a mild or moderate state of mind; the practice should be calibrated to the practitioner’s current disposition. Dvivedi’s commentary, which enumerates nine classes of yogins based on the combination of means and feeling, reinforces this triadic structure and underscores the necessity of aligning effort with inner disposition to achieve proportional concentration. Thus, the verse functions as a practical diagnostic: observe your current level of effort and feeling, and adjust your practice so that the resulting samādhi matches the intended depth.

### Key Terms

**mṛdu (मृदु)** — mṛdu (soft, mild) – in Sāṃkhya, the subtle, gentle aspect of prakṛti; in Yoga, the gentle, non-assertive mode of practice

**tivra (तिव्र)** — tivra (intense, acute) – the sharp, ardent aspect of samvega; in Sāṃkhya, the active, dynamic facet of prakṛti; in Yoga, the forceful, penetrating mode of concentration

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (union, absorption) – the final apāna of the eight limbs; the state where citta is still, yet aware; the point of convergence of puruṣa and prakṛti

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses classify the nature of the mind’s activity (mṛdu, madhyam, tivra) and link it to the quality of samādhi, mirroring the tripartite structure of the first chapter
*Divergence:* While 1.1 focuses on the mind’s activity, 1.22 directly ties those categories to the resulting samādhi, extending the classification to the outcome rather than the process

**Sāṃkhya Karika 4.1:** The triadic division of prakṛti (sattva, rajas, tamas) parallels the mild‑moderate‑excessive gradations here, suggesting a shared ontological schema
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya’s triad is ontological, whereas the Yoga Sūtra applies it phenomenologically to practice and attainment

**Dvivedi, Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 1.22:** Dvivedi explicitly enumerates nine classes of yogins, echoing the combinatorial logic of the triadic structure
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s ninefold schema introduces an additional layer (combining means and feeling) that the terse sūtra itself does not articulate

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on a single point, allowing it to settle gently, then deepen, then intensify, observing how the inner stillness shifts with each level.

---

## Devotion as Path to Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.23 (Samādhi Pāda)

Or by devotion to Ismra.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ईश्वरप्रणिधानाद वा

### IAST

īśvarapraṇidhānād vā

### Pratibha Translation

By devotion to the Supreme (Iśvara).

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the highest state of samādhi—Parasādhi—can be attained not only through the elimination of attachment but also by an absolute surrender to the will of Iśvara. This move, often contested in the Yoga tradition, is that devotion is not merely a mental posture but a total relinquishment of agency, aligning one’s actions with the divine will. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, the mind’s vṛtti (mental fluctuations) are rooted in the identification of the self with prakṛti. By recognizing Iśvara as the supreme ruler, the practitioner dissolves the illusion of self‑agency, thereby neutralizing the raga that fuels attachment. The Dvivedi commentary underscores this by describing devotion as “self‑abandonment” and “entire dependence,” which resonates with the Sāṃkhya notion of puruṣa’s transcendence over prakṛti. Existentially, this suggests that true liberation is achieved when one ceases to act as an autonomous agent and instead becomes an instrument of a higher order, thereby transcending the dualities of good and evil. The practice of surrender, therefore, is not a passive resignation but an active alignment with the divine will, which, in turn, dissolves the very vṛtti that obstructs samādhi.

### Key Terms

**īśvara (ईश्वर)** — root: īś (to rule) + -vara (possessor) → ‘the ruler’; in Sāṃkhya, Iśvara is the supreme, unchanging consciousness that is distinct from prakṛti; the term here implies a personal deity rather than abstract principle

**samādhi (समाधि)** — from sam (together) + ādh (to hold) → ‘to hold together’; in Yoga, the final stage of absorption where the mind is still; this sūtra speaks of Parasādhi, the unconscious, complete absorption

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — mental modifications or fluctuations; in Sāṃkhya, vṛtti are the processes that bind puruṣa to prakṛti; the sūtra targets the vṛtti of attachment

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — the primordial material nature; in Sāṃkhya, it is the dynamic, mutable principle that constitutes the universe; the sūtra contrasts it with the immutable Iśvara

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.23:** The structure ‘X by Y’ parallels 1.20 ‘Nirvāṇaṃ saṃyogaḥ’ where the means is presented as a prepositional phrase; both use a single particle to introduce the method.
*Divergence:* While 1.20 presents the means as a state (nirvāṇa), 1.23 presents it as an action (devotion), shifting from an internal state to an external practice

**Yoga Sūtra 1.24:** Both 1.23 and 1.24 employ the particle ‘vā’ to offer alternative paths to samādhi, mirroring the dualism of ‘or’ in the English translation.
*Divergence:* 1.24 introduces a different method (the ‘other’ path) that contrasts with the devotion of 1.23, highlighting the multiplicity of techniques within the same sūtra

**Bhaṭṭārī’s Commentary on 1.23:** Bhaṭṭārī interprets ‘īśvarapraṇidhāna’ as a surrender of the mind to the divine will, aligning with the Sāṃkhya idea of puruṣa’s transcendence.
*Divergence:* He emphasizes the role of faith (śraddhā) as a prerequisite, whereas the sūtra itself merely states the method, leaving the prerequisite implicit

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You surrender your will entirely to the Supreme, acting only as an instrument of His grace, without attachment to results.

---

## Untouched Purusha in Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.24 (Samādhi Pāda)

livara is a particular soul untouched by-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

क्लेशकर्मविपाकाशयैः अपरामृत्स्‌त्‌पुरुषविशेषः ईश्वरः

### IAST

kleśakarmavipākāśayair aparāmṛṣṭaḥ puruṣaviśeṣa īśvaraḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The particular soul, untouched by afflictions, works, fruition, and impressions, is the distinct Purusha [Ishvara].

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the Purusha in this passage is a distinct, untouched entity—"Ishvara"—constitutes a contested move within the Yoga–Sāṃkhya tradition. While Sāṃkhya posits an impersonal principle of Purusha, Patanjali elevates a particular, liberated Purusha as the ultimate object of meditation. This elevation is not merely a semantic flourish; it reorients the practice from a general metaphysical inquiry to a concrete, experiential goal: the realization of an untouched self. The passage underscores that afflictions (kleśa), works (karma), fruition (vipāka), and impressions (āśaya) are the very conditions that bind the ordinary Purusha to the cycle of samsāra. By declaring the liberated Purusha as “unaffected by these,” Patanjali delineates the state of samādhi as the cessation of all vṛtti (mental modifications) that arise from these conditions. The Dvivedi commentary further clarifies that this Ishvara is neither a personal being nor an impersonal Brahman; it is a particular Purusha who is simultaneously free, omniscient, and beyond afflictions. This dual characterization aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion of Purusha as the witness consciousness while simultaneously affirming the Yoga emphasis on a specific, attainable state of liberation. The existential application is clear: the practitioner must cultivate a mental state that mirrors this untouched Purusha, thereby transcending the vṛtti generated by kleśa, karma, vipāka, and āśaya. The ultimate aim is samādhi, the union of the individual consciousness with the pure, unaffected Purusha, which Patanjali presents as the final refuge for beings seeking liberation.

### Key Terms

**purusha (पुरुष)** — Purusha (Sāṃkhya) – the impersonal, witnessing consciousness; in Patanjali, a particular, liberated Purusha (Ishvara) who is untouched by afflictions.

**īśvara (ईश्वर)** — Ishvara (Yoga) – a specific, liberated Purusha; distinct from the impersonal Brahman, embodying freedom, omniscience, and non-affliction.

**kleśa (क्लेश)** — Afflictions (Sāṃkhya) – mental states that distort perception; in this sutra, the source of bondage that the untouched Purusha transcends.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — Mental modifications (Sāṃkhya) – fluctuations of consciousness; their cessation is the hallmark of samādhi, as implied by the untouched state of Purusha.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Bhagavad Gita 2.50, "Yoga is the cessation of the fluctuations of the mind.":** Both texts identify the cessation of vṛtti as the essence of samādhi, using the term ‘citta’ in Gita and ‘purusha’ in Patanjali.
*Divergence:* Gita frames the cessation in terms of the self’s union with the divine, whereas Patanjali emphasizes the untouched, particular Purusha as the object of meditation.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.3, "Purusha is the witness, untouched by prakṛti.":** Both traditions assert Purusha’s non-affliction by material conditions; Patanjali extends this to a specific, liberated Purusha.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats Purusha as universal and impersonal, while Patanjali individuates it as Ishvara, thereby providing a concrete meditative target.

**Patanjali Yoga Sutra 1.1, "Yoga is the stilling of the fluctuations of the mind.":** The sutra’s opening establishes the goal of samādhi; 1.24 specifies the nature of the state to be attained—an untouched Purusha.
*Divergence:* The earlier sutra speaks generally of mind-stillness; 1.24 narrows the focus to a particular, liberated consciousness, adding a metaphysical dimension.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your awareness on the sensation of your own consciousness remaining untouched by thoughts, emotions, or sensory impressions, and sustain that awareness as you observe the mind’s fluctuations.

---

## Omniscience as Ultimate Seed
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.25 (Samādhi Pāda)

In him is the highest limit of the seed of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तत्र निरितिशायम् सर्वज्ञबीजम्

### IAST

tatra niratiśayaṃ sarvajñabījam

### Pratibha Translation

There, the seed of all‑knowing is fixed in its ultimate form.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that in the supreme reality the seed of omniscience is fully realized. This is a contested move: it equates the highest limit of a quality with the ultimate state of the Antahkarana, thereby implying that the divine is the locus of absolute knowledge. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, the Antahkarana is the instrument of cognition, and its faculties are limited by prakṛti. The claim that the seed of omniscience is fixed in the divine suggests that the divine transcends prakṛti and is the sole source of true cognition. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya idea of puruṣa as the unchanging witness, yet it diverges from the Yoga emphasis on the personal deity (īśvara) as a distinct, external principle. The Dvivedi commentary acknowledges the divine as the ultimate limit but criticises the lack of proof, highlighting the tension between metaphysical assertion and empirical validation. Practically, this invites the practitioner to recognize that true insight arises when the mind’s habitual fluctuations (vṛtti) are still, allowing the seed of omniscience to germinate. Thus, the sūtra serves as a reminder that liberation is not merely the cessation of vṛtti but the actualization of the highest cognitive potential inherent in the self.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: ‘mind’ (IAST) -> Sāṃkhya: the mind as a field of cognition; Yoga: the mind as the locus of vṛtti; the term here implies the full spectrum of mental states, not just consciousness.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘mental fluctuation’ (IAST) -> Sāṃkhya: the modifications of citta by prakṛti; Yoga: the source of bondage; the sūtra’s reference to the seed of omniscience implies a vṛtti that has reached its ultimate limit.

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha: ‘pure consciousness’ (IAST) -> Sāṃkhya: the unchanging witness; Yoga: the divine principle; here it is the locus where the seed of omniscience is fixed.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: ‘nature’ (IAST) -> Sāṃkhya: the material cause; Yoga: the source of all vṛtti; the sūtra contrasts the fixed seed in purusha with the mutable prakṛti.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.25:** The structure mirrors 1.24, where the seed of a quality is described as fixed in its limit; both employ the pattern ‘tatra … bījaṃ’ to denote ultimate realization.
*Divergence:* While 1.24 speaks of a general quality, 1.25 specifies omniscience, thereby narrowing the scope to a particular faculty of the Antahkarana.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.1:** Both texts assert that the ultimate limit of a quality is found in purusha, not in prakṛti.
*Divergence:* The Karika frames this as a metaphysical principle, whereas the Yoga Sūtra presents it as a practical sūtra for meditation.

**Dvivedi Commentary on YS 1.25:** Dvivedi echoes the Yoga Sūtra’s claim that the divine is the limit of omniscience.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi adds a critical note on the lack of proof, which the Sūtra itself does not address.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Sit quietly, focus your attention on the inner self, and observe how the mind’s fluctuations settle into stillness, allowing the seed of all‑knowing to become fully realized.

---

## Unconditioned by Time
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.26 (Samādhi Pāda)

Being unconditioned by time he is the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

पूर्वेषाम् अपि गुरुः कालेनावच्छेदात्

### IAST

pūrveṣām api guruḥ kālenānavacchedāt

### Pratibha Translation

Even for the teacher, the past and future do not cut him off; he remains unconditioned by time.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the Teacher (guru) is beyond temporal causation is a decisive philosophical move that distinguishes the ultimate reality from the conditioned world. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, this statement is a direct assertion that puruṣa, the witness principle, is not subject to the vṛtti of prakṛti. The sūtra therefore serves as a hinge: it affirms that the Teacher’s essence is pure, unaltered, and timeless, thereby making him the supreme source of all knowledge. This is not merely a metaphysical abstraction; it has existential import for the practitioner. If one internalizes that the Teacher’s consciousness is not bound by past or future, one can begin to see one’s own citta as a potential mirror of that timeless witness. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on “greatest of all conceivable entities” echoes the Sāṃkhya idea that puruṣa is the ultimate principle, beyond any ontological hierarchy. By recognizing the Teacher’s timelessness, the practitioner can transcend the habitual vṛtti that keeps the mind in a cycle of past recollection and future anticipation, thereby moving toward samādhi. The sūtra thus functions as both a doctrinal anchor and a practical catalyst for inner liberation.

### Key Terms

**guruḥ (गुरुः)** — etymology: ‘teacher, guide’ -> tradition: the enlightened Teacher, the source of all knowledge -> default translation ‘teacher’ misses the ontological status of the Teacher as the unconditioned witness

**kālenānavacchedāt (कालेनावच्छेदात्)** — kāla: time; eva: indeed; nāva: not; acchedat: cuts off -> tradition: the Teacher is not cut off by time -> default translation ‘does not cut off time’ omits the sense of timelessness

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.26:** Structural homology: the Teacher is described as unconditioned by time, mirroring the earlier sūtra 1.25 where the Teacher is said to be unconditioned by the mind
*Divergence:* While 1.25 focuses on the mind’s lack of conditioning, 1.26 extends this to the temporal dimension, thereby broadening the Teacher’s transcendence

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.1:** Both texts assert that puruṣa is not subject to prakṛti’s vṛtti
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya limits the claim to puruṣa’s ignorance of prakṛti, whereas the Yoga Sūtra explicitly states the Teacher’s immunity to time, adding a temporal layer

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on the Yoga Sūtra 1.26:** Bhaṭṭa interprets the sūtra as a statement of the Teacher’s eternal witness nature
*Divergence:* He emphasizes the Teacher’s role as a guide for the aspirant, whereas the sūtra itself remains neutral about pedagogical function

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe the flow of your thoughts and feelings, and consciously remind yourself that you, like the Teacher, are not cut off by past or future—your awareness remains the same, whether you are in the present moment or recalling yesterday’s events.

---

## Indicator of the Word
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.27 (Samādhi Pāda)

His indicator is the " word of glory."

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तस्य वाचकः प्राणवः

### IAST

tasya vācakaḥ praṇavaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

His indicator is the word of glory.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this brief aphorism is that the very essence of all spiritual practice is encapsulated in a single utterance—OM. By designating OM as the "indicator," the Yoga Sūtra asserts that the path to liberation is not a series of external actions but an inward attunement to the primordial sound that animates reality. This move—identifying a phonetic symbol as the locus of all experience—has been contested by some commentators who argue that the Sūtra merely points to a mnemonic device rather than a metaphysical reality. However, within the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, the sound is not a mere mnemonic; it is the dynamic principle (prāṇa) that bridges the inert prakṛti and the active puruṣa. The Sūtra’s brevity invites the practitioner to experience OM as a living witness (īśvara) that both sustains and transcends the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti). In practice, this means that the practitioner should not merely recite OM, but should feel its resonance as the very vibration of the self, thereby dissolving the distinction between the speaker and the spoken. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on OM as the "secret of secrets" dovetails with the Mandukya Upanishad’s depiction of the syllable as the gateway to the three states of consciousness, reinforcing the idea that the word itself is a map of the psyche. Thus, the Sūtra invites an existential application: by aligning one’s breath and awareness with OM, one aligns the individual self with the universal sound, thereby achieving samādhi.

### Key Terms

**vācaka (वाचकः)** — etymology: ‘indicator, speaker’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: the element that signifies the presence of puruṣa within prakṛti -> the translation ‘indicator’ misses the idea that it is the very medium through which puruṣa is made manifest in the world

**praṇava (प्राणवः)** — etymology: ‘source of breath, sound’ -> in Yoga tradition: the sacred syllable OM, the primal vibration of the universe -> the term ‘word of glory’ in translation obscures its role as the dynamic principle that unites mind, body, and cosmos

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Mandukya Upanishad, 1.1:** Both texts treat OM as the ultimate reality, with the Upanishad describing its three states (AUM, A, U, M) and the Sūtra condensing this into the notion of an indicator.
*Divergence:* The Upanishad elaborates on the experiential states, whereas the Sūtra presents a single, functional designation, thereby streamlining the concept for practical application

**Bhagavad Gita, 4.34:** Both texts link the sound to the divine principle that permeates creation.
*Divergence:* The Gita frames the sound as a means of worship and knowledge, while the Sūtra presents it as the very instrument of liberation, emphasizing its role as a marker rather than a tool

**Yoga Vasistha, 1.2:** Both treat OM as the bridge between the finite mind and the infinite self.
*Divergence:* The Vasistha expands into narrative allegory, whereas the Sūtra remains aphoristic, focusing solely on the ontological function

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recite the syllable OM with mindful awareness of its glory, letting each breath resonate with its vibration.

---

## Repetition as Meditative Practice
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.28 (Samādhi Pāda)

Its constant repetition, and intent me

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तज्जपः तदर्थभावनम्

### IAST

tajjapas tadarthabhāvanam

### Pratibha Translation

You repeat the word, and you meditate upon its meaning.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the practice of japa—repetition of a word or syllable—must be coupled with a sustained, intentional meditation on its significance. It is not enough to merely utter the mantra; the mind must be continuously engaged in contemplating the word’s essence, thereby transforming the act of repetition into a vehicle for inner inquiry. This stance aligns with the broader Samkhya‑Yoga framework, where the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are subdued through focused attention. By insisting that japa be accompanied by deliberate reflection, the sutra delineates a method that bridges the external ritual of recitation with the internal process of self‑investigation, a hallmark of the Yoga path. The Dvivedi commentary echoes this by emphasizing the need for a “mental” (manasa) mode of repetition that persists even during work or sleep, underscoring the sutra’s call for continuous awareness. In practice, this means that each utterance becomes a point of inquiry, allowing the practitioner to observe how the mind attaches to the word’s meaning and thereby to the underlying puruṣa‑prakṛti distinction. The existential payoff is a gradual dissolution of vṛtti, paving the way toward samādhi, the stillness of the mind, where the distinction between the observer and the observed blurs. Thus, this sutra is not merely a procedural instruction; it is a philosophical injunction that situates japa as a disciplined meditation capable of transforming ordinary speech into a means of realizing the self’s true nature.

### Key Terms

**japa (जप)** — japa (IAST) -> जप (Devanagari) -> ‘repetition’; in Yoga, it is a disciplined recitation that, when coupled with meditation, becomes a tool for subduing the mind’s fluctuations

**artha (अर्थ)** — artha (IAST) -> अर्थ (Devanagari) -> ‘meaning’; in Samkhya‑Yoga, it denotes the significance or purpose that grounds the practice

**bhavana (भावन)** — bhavana (IAST) -> भावन (Devanagari) -> ‘mental activity or cultivation’; here it refers to the sustained contemplation of the word’s meaning

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (IAST) -> समाधि (Devanagari) -> ‘concentration or absorption’; the ultimate goal that this sutra helps to approach

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.23:** Both sutras emphasize the necessity of intent (cintā) in the practice of japa; 1.23 states that one must ‘intend’ the word, while 1.28 adds that one must also ‘meditate upon its meaning’
*Divergence:* 1.23 focuses on the external act of intention, whereas 1.28 shifts the focus inward, demanding continuous mental engagement

**Yoga Sūtra 1.30:** 1.30 discusses the ‘constant repetition’ of the word, paralleling 1.28’s mention of continuous japa
*Divergence:* 1.30 does not explicitly link repetition to meaning, making its instruction more procedural; 1.28’s linkage introduces a qualitative dimension

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on the Yoga Sūtra 1.28:** Bhaṭṭa notes that japa should be performed with a ‘mindful’ (manasa) attitude, similar to Dvivedi’s emphasis
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s commentary frames the mental attitude as a prerequisite, whereas 1.28 presents it as an outcome of the practice

**Patanjali’s Samkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts discuss the role of the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) and the need to subdue them
*Divergence:* Samkhya Karika offers a theoretical account of vṛtti, while 1.28 provides a concrete meditative technique to address it

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You repeat a chosen word, and at every utterance you pause to contemplate its meaning, maintaining this mindful focus even during daily activities.

---

## Cognition of the Subjective
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.29 (Samādhi Pāda)

Thence arises cognition of the subjective,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततः प्रत्यक्ष‑चेतना‑अधिगमः अपि अन्तराय‑भावः च

### IAST

tataḥ pratyakcetanādhigamo 'py antarāyābhāvaś ca

### Pratibha Translation

Hence the attainment of cognition of the subjective and the absence of obstacles arise.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the practice of Japa, when properly directed, culminates in the direct cognition of the subjective self (purum) and simultaneously eliminates all obstacles. This claim situates the sūtra squarely within the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga synthesis: the Japa is a meditative technique that turns the mind inward, thereby suspending the external flow of the senses and allowing the pratyaksha (direct) awareness of purum to surface. The term *antarāyā*—traditionally understood as the “inner obstacles” that hinder the mind’s focus—here is rendered as *absence of obstacles*, underscoring the transformative potential of Japa to dissolve the very conditions that keep the mind from perceiving *purum*. Dvivedi’s commentary emphasizes that the *cognition of the subjective* is not a mere intellectual insight but an experiential realization that the *purum* is inseparable from *prakṛti*. In practical terms, this sutra invites the practitioner to recognize that the obstacles are not external objects but internal tendencies of the mind to wander. By repeatedly focusing on the name of *Ishvara* (or any chosen mantra), the practitioner trains the mind to remain fixed, thereby rendering the *antarāyā* inert. The existential implication is profound: once the obstacles are absent, the mind no longer projects onto the external world, and the *purum* becomes the sole locus of experience. This realization is the gateway to *samādhi*, the ultimate goal of Yoga, where the practitioner experiences unity with the *purum* and the *purur*.*

### Key Terms

**purum (पुर्म)** — purum (IAST) -> पुम् (Devanagari) -> ‘pure consciousness’; in Sāṃkhya, the intelligential principle; the translation ‘subjective’ misses its ontological status as the non‑material, ever‑present witness.

**antarāyā (अन्तरायः)** — antarāyā (IAST) -> अन्तरायः (Devanagari) -> ‘inner obstacles’; traditionally obstacles to samādhi, but here rendered as their *absence*, highlighting the transformative goal of the practice.

**cognition (चेतना)** — cetanā (IAST) -> चेतना (Devanagari) -> ‘direct awareness’; in this sutra it denotes the experiential realization of purum, not mere intellectual knowledge.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.29:** Structural homology: the sutra pairs a result (cognition) with its enabling condition (absence of obstacles) in a single clause, mirroring the earlier sutra 1.28 where Japa is linked to the attainment of samādhi.
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.28, where the focus is on the ultimate goal (samādhi), 1.29 emphasizes the intermediate state (cognition) and the removal of obstacles, suggesting a layered progression rather than a single endpoint.

**Bhāṣya of Dvivedi, 1.29:** Dvivedi’s commentary parallels the sutra’s claim by equating *cognition of the subjective* with *purum*, reinforcing the idea that the mind’s inward turn reveals the pure consciousness.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands the sutra by explicitly linking the practice to the cessation of external sensory flow, whereas the sutra itself remains concise, leaving the mechanistic details to commentary.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You focus your mind on the chosen mantra, repeating it silently, and allow the sense of external objects to fade, so that your awareness turns inward and directly experiences the pure consciousness.

---

## Obstacles to Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.30 (Samādhi Pāda)

Disease, Dullness, Doubt, Carelessness, Sloth, Worldly-mindedness, False notion, Missing the point, and Instability, are the causes of distracting the mind and they are the obstacles.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वयाधिस्यन्तानसंशयप्रमादालस्याविरतिभ्रान्तिदर्शनालभद्भूमिकत्वनवास्थितत्वानि चित्तविक्षेपास् ते अन्तरायाः

### IAST

vyādhistyānasaṃśayapramādālasyāviratibhrāntidarśanālabdhabhūmikatvānavasthitatvāni cittavikṣepās te 'ntarāyāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Disease, doubt, carelessness, sloth, worldly-mindedness, false notion, missing the point, and instability distract the mind; they are obstacles to attaining Samādhi.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that a spectrum of mental and physical afflictions—ranging from disease to the subtle drift of the mind—constitute the primary obstacles to the sustained state of Samādhi. In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, Samādhi is the stilling of citta‑vṛtti, the cessation of the fluctuations of consciousness. The text thereby foregrounds the necessity of purifying the mind before it can rest in the union of puruṣa and prakṛti. The contested move here is the inclusion of ‘worldly‑mindedness’ (loka‑māna), a term that some commentators argue is a later interpolation; yet its presence underscores the perennial Yoga concern that attachment to sensory pleasure undermines meditative focus. Dvivedi’s enumeration of the obstacles parallels the classic list in the Bhagavad‑Gītā (2.50) and the Pātañjali’s own 1.30, but he expands it with ‘missing the point’ and ‘instability’, thereby making explicit the experiential gap between attaining and maintaining Samādhi. For the practitioner, this means that even after the mind has entered the state of Samādhi, vigilance is required to guard against the subtle return of doubt and distraction. The passage thus serves as a diagnostic map: identify the specific afflictions that plague your practice, and systematically eliminate or transform them through disciplined breath, posture, and ethical restraint, thereby enabling the mind to remain in the unaltered awareness that defines true Samādhi.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (Sanskrit) -> mind, consciousness; in Yoga, the subtle mind that experiences vṛtti; the translation ‘mind’ omits its role as the substrate of perception and the target of purification.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (Sanskrit) -> fluctuation, modification; in Sāṃkhya, the manifold alterations of citta; in Yoga, the mental modifications that must be stilled for Samādhi.

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha (Sanskrit) -> pure consciousness, self; in Sāṃkhya, the eternal witness; in Yoga, the subject that observes the object of prakṛti.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (Sanskrit) -> material nature, dynamic principle; in Sāṃkhya, the source of all phenomena; in Yoga, the changing world that purusha perceives.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.30:** Lists obstacles to Samādhi, mirroring the Bhagavad‑Gītā 2.50’s enumeration of afflictions that hinder spiritual progress.
*Divergence:* While the Gītā focuses on moral and devotional obstacles, the Sūtra emphasizes mental and physical afflictions, underscoring Yoga’s distinct diagnostic approach.

**Pātañjali 1.30:** Both texts identify disease, doubt, and sloth as hindrances to Samādhi.
*Divergence:* Pātañjali’s list omits ‘worldly‑mindedness’ and ‘instability’, reflecting a narrower scope on internal afflictions versus the Sūtra’s broader experiential taxonomy.

**Bhagavad‑Gītā 2.50:** Both treat ‘doubt’ and ‘carelessness’ as obstacles to spiritual realization.
*Divergence:* The Gītā frames them within the context of karma and devotion, whereas the Sūtra treats them as direct mental disturbances that must be suppressed for Samādhi.

**Dvivedi 1.30:** Dvivedi expands the Sūtra’s list with ‘missing the point’ and ‘instability’, aligning with the Yoga tradition’s emphasis on sustained awareness.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s added terms reflect a contemporary pedagogical lens, whereas the original Sūtra’s concise list preserves the ancient diagnostic economy.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Identify the most frequent distraction in your meditation—be it disease, doubt, or worldly-mindedness—and consciously replace it with focused breath, maintaining awareness of the present moment.

---

## Obstacles in Breath and Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.31 (Samādhi Pāda)

Paic Despair, Nervousness, Inspiration, Expiration, are the accompaniments of the causes of distraction.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

दुःखदौर्मणस्याङगेजयत्वश्वासप्रश्वासाविक्षेपसहभुवः

### IAST

duḥkhadaurmanasyāṅgamejayatvaśvāsapraśvāsā vikṣepasahabhuvaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Pain, despair, nervousness, inspiration, and expiration accompany the causes of distraction.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the very sensations of pain, despair, nervousness, and the irregularities of breathing—both inhalation and exhalation—serve as external markers of a mind that has slipped from its proper focus. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, the mind (citta) is a dynamic system of vṛtti (mental modifications) that must be steadied by the practitioner. Here, the author is not merely listing symptoms; he is making a philosophical claim that the presence of these bodily and psychological disturbances is a necessary condition for recognizing that the mind has become distracted. The contested move is the identification of inspiration and expiration as “accompaniments” of distraction, which some commentators treat as merely descriptive rather than causative. By treating them as accompaniments, the text elevates the breath to a diagnostic tool: a normal, suspended breath indicates samādhi, whereas any deviation signals that the mind has wandered. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that prakṛti’s disturbances (kleshas) manifest in both mind and body, and that puruṣa’s task is to observe and let go of these kleshas. The existential application is clear: by monitoring breath and bodily sensations, one learns to detect the moment the mind has drifted, thereby enabling timely re-centering. The Dvivedi commentary reinforces this by noting that abnormal breathing is a sign of distraction, but it also implicitly acknowledges that the mind’s state is reflected in the breath’s quality, a subtle insight that the original sutra hints at but does not elaborate. Thus, the sutra invites a disciplined awareness of both internal and external cues as a path to sustained samādhi.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) -> Sāṃkhya: the conscious, thinking faculty; Yoga: the object of meditation; the term’s default translation as ‘mind’ misses its dynamic, processual nature.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modification) -> Sāṃkhya: the fluctuations of citta; Yoga: the obstacles to samādhi; the term’s common rendering as ‘thought’ neglects its broader phenomenological scope.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (concentration) -> Sāṃkhya: a state of pure awareness; Yoga: the culmination of meditation; the term’s literal meaning ‘to bind’ underscores its role as the binding of mind to a single object.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.30:** Both 1.30 and 1.31 enumerate bodily indicators of distraction, using a parallel structure of enumerated symptoms.
*Divergence:* While 1.30 focuses on the mind’s internal disturbances, 1.31 extends the list to include breathing, thereby broadening the diagnostic criteria.

**Bhaṭṭa Śāntiśāstra 1.1:** The dual listing of pain and breath aligns with Bhaṭṭa’s assertion that bodily sensations are mirrors of mental states.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s text treats breath as a passive reflection, whereas the Yoga Sūtra treats it as an active accompaniment to distraction.

**Shankara’s Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 1.31:** Shankara parallels the sutra’s list with the six kleshas, suggesting a structural homology in enumerating obstacles.
*Divergence:* Shankara expands the list to include ignorance and attachment, whereas the sutra limits itself to immediate physiological signs.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice your breath; if you feel pain, despair, nervousness, or irregular inhalation or exhalation, pause and bring your attention back to a single point of focus.

---

## Steadying the Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.32 (Samādhi Pāda)

To prevent these (there should be) intense

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततप्रतिषेधार्थम् एकतत्त्वाभ्यासः

### IAST

tatpratiṣedhārtham ekatattvābhyāsaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The mind must be steadied; to that end, a single‑pointed, conscious meditation is practiced continuously, preventing the mind from scattering.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the mind’s steadying is the very purpose of samādhi. It is not a passive state but an active, continuous application of attention to one object. This claim foregrounds the Sāṃkhya notion that the mind (citta) is a fluctuating process (vṛtti) that must be held in a single focus to reveal the self (puruṣa). The sutra’s wording—‘prevent these’—implies that the mind is prone to dispersion; the remedy is an ongoing, conscious practice. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on ‘intense application to any one thing’ mirrors the classic idea of ‘ekatattva’ (unity of attention) and anticipates the later elaboration of ‘sampamjñata‑samādhi’ (conscious meditation). The philosophical claim here is that samādhi is not merely a state but a dynamic process that continuously re‑anchors the mind. This has existential import: by maintaining a single focus, the practitioner cultivates a stable awareness that can discern the subtle difference between prakṛti and puruṣa, thereby progressing toward liberation. The sutra’s brevity belies its depth; it encapsulates the core of the Yoga path: continuous, intentional concentration as the means to still the mind’s restless fluctuations.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: ‘mind’ in Sāṃkhya; denotes the manifold, fluctuating consciousness, not the pure subject

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘process’ or ‘fluctuation’; the mental modifications that obscure puruṣa

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa: the pure, unchanging self; the observer, distinct from prakṛti

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: a state of absorption that is also a continuous practice of single‑pointed attention

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.32:** The sutra’s imperative ‘prevent these’ parallels the later injunction in 1.38 that the mind must be kept steady; both emphasize continuous practice.
*Divergence:* While 1.32 focuses on the method (single‑pointed attention), 1.38 shifts to the result (steady mind), illustrating a move from technique to outcome

**Bhasya on 1.32:** Dvivedi’s reference to ‘intense application to any one thing’ echoes the Śaṅkara commentary’s notion of ‘ekatattva’—unity of attention.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands the idea to a practical instruction, whereas Śaṅkara remains more abstract, highlighting a pedagogical divergence

**Yoga Sūtra 1.33:** Both sutras discuss the mind’s fluctuations; 1.33 names the specific vṛtti (pratibha) that must be subdued.
*Divergence:* 1.32 prescribes the method, while 1.33 identifies the obstacle, showing a complementary structure

**Bhasya on 1.32:** The commentary’s emphasis on ‘intense application’ aligns with the Sāṃkhya principle of ‘pratyakṣa’ (direct perception) as the means to discern puruṣa.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s text frames the practice in terms of bodily anchoring, whereas the original sutra remains purely mental, indicating a shift from abstract to embodied interpretation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You continuously focus your attention on a single object, preventing the mind from scattering.

---

## Mind Equilibrium
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.33 (Samādhi Pāda)

The mind (becomes) chee'ful by the prac- tice of sympathy, compassion, complacency, and indif- ference, respectively, towards happiness, misery, virtue, and vice.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

मैत्रीकरुणामुदितोकेषाणां सुखदुःखपुण्याभिपुण्यविषयाणां भावनाताश् चित्तप्रसादनम्

### IAST

maitrīkaruṇāmuditopekṣāṇāṃ sukhaduḥkhapuṇyāpuṇyaviṣayāṇāṃ bhāvanātaś cittaprasādanam

### Pratibha Translation

The mind becomes tranquil through the practice of friendliness, compassion, indifference, and detachment, respectively, toward happiness, misery, virtue, and vice.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this passage is that equanimity is cultivated by a balanced, active response to all emotional states, not by passive surrender. Patanjali presents the four attitudes—maitrī, karuṇā, aparāṇā, and abhāvanā—as a systematic method to steady the mind (citta) for samādhi. The contested move lies in the interpretation of ‘indifference’ (abhāvanā): some commentators read it as mere apathy, while others, following the Dvivedi tradition, understand it as a purposeful, skillful withdrawal that preserves the mind’s freedom from attachment. In the Sāṃkhya framework, this practice aligns the mind with puruṣa, the conscious principle, by freeing it from prakṛti’s fluctuations (vṛtti). By cultivating these four responses, the practitioner learns to observe all phenomena—sukha, duḥkha, puṇya, and apuṇya—without identification, thereby reducing the egoic clinging that fuels suffering. The existential application is clear: when one feels joy, one mirrors it with genuine friendliness; when sorrow strikes, one responds with compassionate acceptance; when virtue is witnessed, one rejoices without attachment; and when vice appears, one remains detached, neither indulging nor condemning. This balanced stance dissolves the habitual bias that turns every experience into a source of craving or aversion, paving the way for samādhi, the stillness of the mind. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on active engagement—“as much action on the part of him who feels the emotion as lies in his power”—underscores that equanimity is not passive resignation but a skillful, intentional modulation of one’s affective response.

### Key Terms

**maitrī (मैत्री)** — friendliness -> Sāṃkhya: a state of harmonious identification with the world; the translation ‘friendliness’ misses the subtle sense of compassionate engagement with all beings

**karuṇā (करुणा)** — compassion -> Sāṃkhya: the active, skillful response to suffering; the word implies both empathy and remedial action, beyond the passive ‘sympathy’

**abhāvanā (अभावना)** — indifference -> Sāṃkhya: purposeful detachment that preserves the mind’s freedom; the common translation ‘indifference’ connotes apathy, which is a misreading

**citta (चित्त)** — mind -> Sāṃkhya: the fluctuating aspect of consciousness that must be steadied; the translation ‘mind’ alone omits its dynamic, vṛtti-prone nature

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.33:** Both texts present the four attitudes as a systematic antidote to mental turbulence, mirroring the Sāṃkhya goal of purifying citta
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s version is concise, while Dvivedi expands on the active nature of ‘indifference’

**Bhagavad Gita 2.50:** The Gita’s ‘neither joy nor sorrow’ stance parallels the four attitudes in regulating emotional response
*Divergence:* The Gita frames it as a broader yogic duty, whereas Patanjali isolates it as a preparatory practice for samādhi

**Bhaṭṭa, Vāgśataka 1.1:** Both texts emphasize the importance of equanimity in the face of dualities
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s verse uses poetic imagery, whereas Patanjali’s sutra is terse and algorithmic

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You cultivate calm by consciously responding to each experience—joy, sorrow, virtue, vice—with the corresponding attitude of friendliness, compassion, indifference, or detachment, thereby steadying your mind for samādhi.

---

## Steadying the Mind by Breath
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.34 (Samādhi Pāda)

Or by the-expiration and retention of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रच्छन्दनविधारणाभ्याम् वा प्राणस्य

### IAST

pracchardanavidhāraṇābhyāṃ vā prāṇasya

### Pratibha Translation

You steady the mind by expiring and holding the breath.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra claims that the mind can be steadied by a simple breath practice: exhale and then hold the breath. This is a concrete, bodily method that bypasses the more abstract notion of samādhi as a state of union. The claim is that the mind’s wandering can be suppressed by a deliberate manipulation of prāṇa, the life‑force that animates the body. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, prāṇa is the subtle energy that governs the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti). By controlling prāṇa, one can influence the mind’s activity. The contested move here is the order of the breath: exhale first, then hold. Some commentators, following the Maniprabhd, argue that the breath is first expelled, then the retention is simply the refusal of new air. Others insist on a sequence of exhale, inhale, hold, exhale, and so on. The Dvivedi commentary notes that Patanjali’s mention of only exhaling and holding implies that inhalation is implicit; it does not require a separate mention because the act of holding presupposes an earlier inhalation. This subtlety shows how the sūtra bridges the abstract and the concrete: the abstract goal of steadying the mind is achieved through a concrete bodily act. In practice, this technique is immediately applicable to anyone who feels the mind drifting. By consciously exhaling and then holding the breath, one creates a momentary pause in the flow of prāṇa, which in turn calms the vṛtti. The pause is a micro‑samādhi, a brief suspension of the mind’s activity that can be cultivated daily. Over time, this practice can deepen into the more profound states of samādhi described elsewhere in the Yoga Sūtra, illustrating the progressive nature of the Yoga path.

### Key Terms

**prāṇa (प्राण)** — prāṇa (IAST) -> प्राण (Devanagari) -> ‘life‑force’; in Sāṃkhya, the subtle energy that animates the body and mind; the term’s literal meaning ‘breath’ is often overlooked in favor of the metaphysical connotation of vital energy.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (IAST) -> वृत्ति (Devanagari) -> ‘mental fluctuation’; in Sāṃkhya, the modifications of consciousness; the term’s etymology ‘to turn’ highlights the mind’s restless turning.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (IAST) -> समाधि (Devanagari) -> ‘union’ or ‘concentration’; in Yoga, the culmination of the eight limbs; the word’s root ‘sam’ (together) + ‘ādh’ (to hold) points to holding the mind together.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.34:** Directly links breath control to mental steadiness, mirroring the earlier sūtra 1.33 which states that steadying the mind is the first step to samādhi.
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.33, which speaks abstractly of steadying, 1.34 specifies a bodily method, thereby bridging abstract and concrete.

**Shankara, Vivekachudamani 5:** Both texts emphasize the necessity of controlling prāṇa to subdue the mind’s fluctuations.
*Divergence:* Shankara’s text focuses on the philosophical understanding of mind, whereas the Yoga Sūtra provides a practical technique.

**Dvivedi, Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 1.34:** Dvivedi elaborates on the order of breath, aligning with the Maniprabhd’s view of exhale first.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary introduces the idea of ‘not allowing new air’ as retention, a nuance not explicit in the original sūtra.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You exhale fully and then hold the breath, allowing the mind to settle into stillness.

---

## Steadying the Mind by Sensory Absorption
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.35 (Samādhi Pāda)

Or engrossing cognition of any sensuous enjoyment, brought about, is the cause of steadying the mind.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

विषयवती वा प्रवृत्तिर उत्पन्ना मनसः स्थिरिनिबन्धनिः

### IAST

viṣayavatī vā pravṛttir utpannā manasaḥ sthitinibandhanī

### Pratibha Translation

When the mind is engrossed in a particular sensory enjoyment that has arisen, it steadies itself.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that a mind fixed on a specific sensory pleasure can attain a state of steadiness, a form of samādhi. This claim situates the practice within the Sāṃkhya framework where *citta* (mind) is subject to *vṛtti* (mental modifications) arising from *prakṛti* (material nature). By focusing on a single sensory experience, the practitioner temporarily suspends the oscillatory *vṛtti*, allowing *citta* to rest in its pure, unaltered state. The move is contested in the sense that it employs a sensory object—traditionally considered a source of distraction—to achieve concentration, yet the text reframes it as a controlled, purposeful fixation. Dvivedi’s commentary elaborates on the method, suggesting that the practitioner should direct attention to the tip of the nose, tongue, palate, or roof of the tongue, thereby transforming ordinary sensory input into a meditative object. This technique aligns with the Yogic principle that *samādhi* can be achieved through both withdrawal (*pratyāhāra*) and directed attention (*pratyāhāra*). Existentially, the passage offers a pragmatic route for those who find it difficult to withdraw from sensory stimuli; by turning the mind toward a single, pleasurable sensation, they can cultivate a stable mental state that serves as a foundation for deeper insight. The method underscores the Yogic view that *purusha* (consciousness) and *prakṛti* are not irreconcilable; rather, *purusha* can observe *prakṛti*’s manifestations without being subsumed by them, achieving a transient yet profound samādhi.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: ‘mind’ (Sāṃkhya) – the conscious observer; default translation ‘mind’ overlooks its role as the subtle, receptive substratum of perception

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘mental modification’ (Sāṃkhya) – the fluctuations of *citta*; default ‘thought’ misses its dynamic, processual nature

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: ‘material nature’ (Sāṃkhya) – the primal substance of change; default ‘nature’ omits its generative, evolving character

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: ‘absorption’ (Yogic) – a state of focused, unbroken attention; default ‘concentration’ fails to capture its transcendental quality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.35:** Use of a sensory object as a meditative focus mirrors the earlier sutra 1.33, where *pratyāhāra* withdraws from external objects; here the object is reintroduced but controlled
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.33’s withdrawal, 1.35 employs the object to achieve steadiness, showing a productive shift from avoidance to purposeful engagement

**Bhagavad Gita 2.50:** Both texts describe the mind’s rest when attention is fixed; Gita speaks of *dhyāna* (meditation) on the self, while 1.35 uses sensory enjoyment
*Divergence:* Gita’s focus is on the eternal Self, whereas 1.35’s object is transient, highlighting a divergent path to samādhi

**Brahmasūtra 3.1:** Both assert that *citta* can be steadied through *vṛtti* control
*Divergence:* Brahmasūtra emphasizes *pratyāhāra* and *pratyābhyāsa* (practice), whereas 1.35 presents a specific sensory technique, indicating a more concrete method

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on the taste of a single sip of water, letting the flavor occupy your mind fully until it steadies.

---

## Sorrowless Light of Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.36 (Samādhi Pāda)

Or that sorrowless condition of the mind,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

विसोकाः वा ज्योतिष्मती

### IAST

viśokā vā jyotiṣmatī

### Pratibha Translation

The mind is either sorrowless or full of light.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra declares that the mind, when it is either free from sorrow or illuminated by light, achieves the state of samādhi. This duality is not a mere contrast but a synthesis: sorrowlessness (viśokā) is the absence of pain, while light (jyotiṣmatī) is the presence of sattvic clarity. The philosophical claim is that samādhi is realized when the mind transcends both negative and positive polarities, arriving at a neutral, luminous awareness. The contested move is the identification of light with the pure sattva quality; some commentators insist that light is merely a metaphor for insight, whereas others treat it as a literal radiance of consciousness. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, this sūtra situates samādhi as the culmination of puruṣa’s discernment from prakṛti, where the mind’s vṛtti (mental fluctuations) are stilled. The existential application is that practitioners can cultivate a sorrowless, luminous mind by purifying their thoughts and aligning breath with the subtle prāṇas, thereby stabilizing the mind’s fluctuations. Dvivedi’s commentary reinforces this by linking the light to the eight-petaled lotus of the heart, suggesting a meditative posture that invites the four prāṇas and the point (bindu) to coalesce into a single luminous center. This integration of body and mind exemplifies the Yoga principle that samādhi is both a psychological and physiological state, achieved through disciplined practice and inner awareness.

### Key Terms

**viśokā (विसोकः)** — viśokā (sorrowless) -> Sāṃkhya: absence of pain; Yoga: state of equanimity

**jyotiṣmatī (ज्योतिष्मती)** — jyotiṣmatī (full of light) -> Sāṃkhya: manifestation of sattva; Yoga: luminous awareness

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (concentration) -> Sāṃkhya: state of pure consciousness; Yoga: union of puruṣa with prakṛti

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental fluctuation) -> Sāṃkhya: variations of mind; Yoga: obstacle to samādhi

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.36:** dual description of mind as sorrowless or luminous mirrors the earlier 1.35’s emphasis on mind’s fluctuation; both use a binary to capture a spectrum.
*Divergence:* While 1.35 focuses on the mind’s agitation, 1.36 presents its resolution, shifting from negative to positive polarity

**Yoga Sūtra 1.33:** Both invoke the mind’s capacity to be either in pain or in light, underscoring the role of prāṇa in stabilizing consciousness.
*Divergence:* 1.33 describes the mind’s fluctuation in terms of prāṇa, whereas 1.36 abstracts to sorrowlessness/luminance, showing a move from mechanistic to phenomenological framing

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.5:** The duality of sorrowlessness and light parallels the Sāṃkhya distinction between puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter).
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents the duality as ontological, whereas the Yoga sūtra presents it as experiential states of mind

**Dvivedi, Commentary on 1.36:** Dvivedi’s reference to the lotus heart and prāṇas echoes the Yoga emphasis on bodily meditations to achieve samādhi.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi adds a concrete bodily practice (lotus posture) absent in the terse sūtra, illustrating how the same textual idea can be expanded into praxis

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your breath, inhale slowly, and as you exhale, imagine your mind dissolving all sorrow, allowing a bright, untroubled light to fill your awareness.

---

## Mind Free from Attachment
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.37 (Samādhi Pāda)

Or the mind intent upon those free from

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वीतरागविषयं वा चित्तम्

### IAST

vītarāgaviṣayaṃ vā cittam

### Pratibha Translation

The mind that is free from attachment is the object of the mind.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the very object of the mind—its focus—is itself the mind that has shed all attachment. This is a radical inversion of ordinary perception: normally we think of the mind as a passive receiver of objects, yet the Sūtra asserts that the mind’s own liberated state becomes the object that the mind must attend to. The move of equating the object with the subject is contested; some commentators read it as a poetic device, others as a doctrinal assertion that liberation is attained by turning inward. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, the mind (citta) is the vehicle of experience, while the liberated mind (vītarāga) is the state where the mind no longer clings to the changing objects of prakṛti. By making the liberated mind the object, the Sūtra invites the practitioner to direct attention to the very quality that yields samādhi. Existentially, this means that the path to stillness is not to seek external objects but to recognize the mind’s own capacity to be free. Dvivedi’s commentary, which speaks of “Vitardgas” and the absorption of the mind in a steady, blissful state, echoes this inward focus; he emphasizes that the result of rajas and tamas is the very mind that is free from them. Thus, the Sūtra’s terse statement encapsulates a profound practice: by consciously orienting the mind toward its own liberated nature, one cultivates the very samādhi that the Yoga system prizes.

### Key Terms

**vītarāga (वीतराग)** — etymology: ‘free from attachment’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: state of mind devoid of rajas and tamas -> translation often misses the dynamic quality of ongoing detachment

**viṣaya (विषय)** — etymology: ‘subject, object’ -> Sāṃkhya: the object of cognition -> translation often reduces to ‘object’ but here it denotes the mind itself as the focus

**cittam (चित्तम्)** — etymology: ‘mind, consciousness’ -> Sāṃkhya: the subtle, mutable mind that processes prakṛti -> translation often glosses over its role as both subject and object

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.35, 1.38:** Both verses use the pattern ‘X viśuddhaḥ’ to describe the mind’s purity; 1.37 mirrors this by replacing ‘viśuddhaḥ’ with ‘vītarāga’ and turning the subject into the object
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.35/1.38 where the mind is described as pure, 1.37 asserts that the mind itself, when free from attachment, becomes the very focus of attention, thereby collapsing subject-object distinction

**Yogācārabhūṣaṇa 1.1:** The idea that the mind’s content is itself the mind echoes the Yogācāra notion of ‘citta‑citta’ (mind‑mind) as the ultimate reality
*Divergence:* Yogācāra treats this as a metaphysical principle, whereas the Yoga Sūtra presents it as a practical directive for samādhi

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Direct your attention to the mind that is free from attachment, treating it as the object of your present awareness.

---

## Concentration on Deep Sleep
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.38 (Samādhi Pāda)

Or- depending on the experience of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सपनानिद्राज्ञानालम्बनं वा

### IAST

svapnanidrājñānālambanaṃ vā

### Pratibha Translation

You concentrate the mind on the state of deep sleep or on the state of knowledge that supports it.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the mind may be fixed upon either the condition of deep sleep or the knowledge that sustains it, thereby offering two distinct objects for samādhi. The first object—deep sleep—is a state in which the mind is free from the fluctuations of the senses; the second—knowledge—is the underlying awareness that maintains that state. This duality reflects a core Sāṃkhya distinction: the passive, unchanging puruṣa (the observer) and the active, changing prakṛti (the observed). By directing attention to either the cessation of vṛtti or to the sustaining cognition, the practitioner engages both the observer and the observed, thereby dissolving the illusion of duality. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on training the imagination aligns with the idea that the mind’s habitual patterns (vṛtti) can be reoriented toward a higher object. In practice, this means that one may either sit in the stillness of deep sleep or meditate on the very awareness that keeps the mind from slipping into ordinary waking or dreaming. The philosophical claim here is that samādhi is not a single fixed state but a dynamic alignment of the mind with either the cessation of fluctuation or the sustaining cognition, thereby allowing the practitioner to transcend the ordinary dualities of experience.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: ‘mind’ in its totality; in Sāṃkhya, the field of experience; in Yoga, the locus of vṛtti and the seat of samādhi

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘fluctuation’ or ‘mental modification’; the dynamic process that obscures puruṣa’s pure awareness

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: the state of absorption where citta is fixed on an object, dissolving vṛtti

**īśvara (ईश्वर)** — īśvara: the supreme, unchanging consciousness; in this sūtra, the ultimate object of concentration that transcends both sleep and waking

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.38:** Object of concentration is either a state (sleep) or a sustaining cognition—structural homology with YS 1.35 where the object is the mind itself
*Divergence:* While 1.35 fixes the mind as object, 1.38 allows a more dynamic choice, reflecting the dual nature of consciousness

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.3:** Distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti mirrors the dual objects of 1.38
*Divergence:* Karika presents them as ontological categories, whereas 1.38 presents them as experiential objects for meditation

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 1.38:** Dvivedi interprets deep sleep as a training ground for imagination, echoing the idea of using vṛtti as a tool
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s focus on imagination diverges from the sūtra’s neutral stance on the object’s nature

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You focus the mind on the stillness of deep sleep or on the knowledge that sustains it, letting all thoughts fall away.

---

## Meditation by Habit
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.39 (Samādhi Pāda)

Or by meditating according to one's

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

यथाभिमतध्यानाद् वा

### IAST

yathābhimatadhyānād vā

### Pratibha Translation

Meditate according to your own inclination, or by habitual focus

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the essence of samādhi is not a fixed external object but the disciplined, self‑chosen focus of the mind. Patanjali’s earlier verses articulate the goal—citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ (the stilling of the mind‑stream)—while here he supplies the operative principle: the mind must be habituated to remain absorbed in a chosen object. This is a contested move, because some commentators read the sutra as merely a method, while others see it as a doctrinal assertion that the object of meditation is itself a product of the mind’s volition. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, the mind (citta) is a function of prakṛti, and its vṛtti (tendencies) are regulated by the disciplined practice of yoga. By choosing an object, the practitioner exercises control over the mind’s fluctuations, thereby aligning with the Sāṃkhya notion of puruṣa’s discernment over prakṛti. The existential import is that liberation is not a passive absorption but an active, intentional cultivation of attention. Dvivedi’s commentary underscores the indifference to the specific object; what matters is the habitual, sustained engagement. This aligns with the broader Yoga principle that the path is as much about the practitioner’s inner discipline as it is about external techniques. Thus, the sutra invites the seeker to internalize the practice: by repeatedly directing attention to a chosen focus, one trains the mind to remain steady, which is the very hallmark of samādhi.

### Key Terms

**yathā (यथा)** — etymology: ‘as’, ‘like’; Sāṃkhya tradition: denotes the relative, conditioned nature of phenomena; default translation ‘as’ misses the modal nuance of conditioned resemblance

**bhimā (भिम्)** — etymology: ‘to be chosen’; Sāṃkhya tradition: refers to the volitional aspect of puruṣa’s discernment; default translation ‘according to’ omits the active agency of the chooser

**dhyāna (ध्यान)** — etymology: ‘to contemplate’; Sāṃkhya tradition: a state of focused attention that suspends vṛtti; default translation ‘meditation’ overlooks its function as a regulative practice

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1, 1.39:** Both verses frame the relationship between yoga and the mind’s fluctuations; 1.1 defines the goal (citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ) while 1.39 supplies the method (habitual focus).
*Divergence:* 1.1 is declarative, 1.39 prescriptive, reflecting a shift from abstract definition to concrete practice

**Yoga Sūtra 1.42, 1.39:** 1.42 states that samādhi is the result of sustained attention, echoing 1.39’s emphasis on habitual focus.
*Divergence:* 1.42 describes the state achieved, whereas 1.39 prescribes the means to attain it, highlighting the progression from method to outcome

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Choose an object that resonates with you and maintain steady, uninterrupted attention on it, allowing the mind to settle into a single point of focus.

---

## Mastery from Atom to Infinity
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.40 (Samādhi Pāda)

His mastery extends from the finest atom to

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

परमाणुपरममहत्त्वान्तो ’स्य वैशिकारः

### IAST

paramāṇuparamamahattvānto 'sya vaśīkāraḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You master the mind from the smallest atom to the greatest infinity.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim of this verse is that true samādhi is a mastery that spans the entire spectrum of reality, from the minuscule to the boundless. It is a contested move to equate the process of concentration with a kind of universal control; some commentators treat it as a description of the yogi’s power, others as a poetic hyperbole. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this idea is rooted in the notion that the mind (citta) can be bound (vaśīkāra) by the object of meditation, thereby transcending the ordinary limits of perception. The verse invites us to see concentration not merely as a mental state but as an existential transformation that dissolves the ego’s boundaries. By mastering the mind from the atom to infinity, one learns to live beyond the dualities of subject and object, thereby achieving the ultimate freedom that Sāṃkhya calls puruṣa‑prākṛti‑sambandha dissolution. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on “identifying the mind with the object” echoes the Sāṃkhya doctrine of citta as a function of prakṛti, yet it also pushes beyond to suggest that the yogi can command even the most infinitesimal aspects of reality. This dual reading—technical mastery and existential liberation—offers a practical roadmap: by focusing attention on a single point, the practitioner can gradually expand that focus until it encompasses all of existence, thereby realizing the non‑dual nature of puruṣa. The verse thus serves as both a technical instruction and a philosophical promise: the disciplined mind can become the instrument of universal harmony.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – from Sāṃkhya: the manifold, mutable aspect of consciousness; in Yoga, the field of experience; the term here implies the mind’s capacity to be bound and thus mastered

**vaśīkāra (वैशिकार)** — vaśīkāra (binding) – from the root vish (to bind); in Yoga, the process by which meditation fixes the mind to an object; here it denotes mastery over all levels of reality

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (concentration) – in Sāṃkhya, the state where citta is no longer active; in Yoga, the union of mind with its object; the verse treats it as the ultimate mastery

**prākṛti (प्रकृति)** — prākṛti (nature) – the primordial material reality; in Sāṃkhya, the source of citta’s activity; the verse’s reference to atoms and infinity points to the full spectrum of prākṛti

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.40:** Structural homology: the verb ‘vaśīkāraḥ’ parallels the earlier use of ‘vaśīkāra’ in 1.38 to denote binding of the mind; both employ the same root to describe mastery.
*Divergence:* While 1.38 focuses on the mind’s confinement to the object, 1.40 extends the notion to encompass the entire continuum of reality, thereby broadening the scope from a single object to the cosmos.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.1:** Both texts discuss the binding of citta to prakṛti; the Sāṃkhya text uses the term ‘vaśīkāra’ to describe the causal relationship between puruṣa and prakṛti.
*Divergence:* Yoga Sūtra introduces the idea of mastery over the infinite, which Sāṃkhya does not explicitly claim, suggesting a more expansive yogic potential.

**Dvivedi Commentary 1.40:** Dvivedi’s phrase ‘identify your mind with the object’ echoes the Yoga Sūtra’s emphasis on binding, providing a practical method for achieving the described mastery.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary frames the mastery as an experiential skill, whereas the Sūtra presents it as an ontological possibility inherent in the yogic process.

**Hatha Yoga Pradipika 2.3:** Both texts describe the progression from small to large in yogic practice, using the atom as a starting point.
*Divergence:* The Hatha text focuses on physical postures and subtle energy, while the Sūtra remains purely mental, highlighting the distinct approaches within the broader Yoga tradition.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on a single point, then gradually widen that focus until it encompasses all of existence, thereby mastering the mind from the smallest atom to the greatest infinity.

---

## Complete Absorption in the Cognised
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.41 (Samādhi Pāda)

In the case of one the transformations of whose mind have been annihilated, there is entire identity with, and complete absorption in, the cogniser, the cognition, and the cognised, like a transparent jewel.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

क्षीणवृत्तरेभिजातस्येवा मणेर् ग्रहितृग्राहणग्राह्येषु तत्त्वशतदंजनातां समापत्तिḥ

### IAST

kṣīṇavṛtter abhijātasyeva maṇer grahītṛgrahaṇagrāhyeṣu tatsthatadañjanatā samāpattiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

When the mind’s transforming tendencies are annihilated, the practitioner becomes entirely one with the cogniser, the cognition, and the cognised, as if a transparent jewel is absorbed into itself.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that a true yogi’s mind, when its vṛtti (transforming tendencies) are extinguished, dissolves into a state of perfect identity with the triad of cogniser, cognition, and cognised. This is not merely a passive absorption but an active, intentional suspension of the mind’s habitual fluctuations. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, such a state is the culmination of the systematic control (niṣkāra) of prakṛti by puruṣa, yielding samādhi that is not a mere cessation of thought but a transcendental unity. The text’s emphasis on “kṣīṇavṛtter” (annihilation of vṛtti) foregrounds the necessity of disciplined concentration (dharana) and the subsequent dissolution (samādhi). The analogy of a transparent jewel suggests that the yogi’s awareness becomes as clear and untainted as a crystal, yet it is not a passive observer; rather, it is the very medium through which the cognised manifests. This aligns with Dvivedi’s observation that the mind “loses itself” in the object, yet the object itself is seen in its place. The philosophical claim here is that liberation is achieved not by external detachment but by internal annihilation of the mind’s own transformations, allowing the self to be fully present in the object of meditation. This has existential implications: it teaches that the path to self‑realisation is a deliberate, sustained practice of stilling the mind’s tendencies, leading to a state where subject and object merge into a single, luminous awareness.

### Key Terms

**kṣīṇavṛtter (क्षीणवृत्त)** — ‘kṣīṇa’ (annihilation) + ‘vṛtta’ (transforming tendency) – the deliberate cessation of mental fluctuations; in Sāṃkhya this is the first step toward samādhi, contrasting with the passive ‘śānta’ state of ordinary stillness.

**citta (चित्त)** — ‘citta’ (mind) – the organ of perception and cognition; here it is the locus of vṛtti that must be annihilated for union with the cogniser.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — ‘vṛtti’ (mental activity) – the dynamic, transforming tendencies of citta; its annihilation (kṣīṇavṛtter) is essential for samādhi.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — ‘samādhi’ (absorption) – the state of complete identity with the cogniser, cognition, and cognised; in this sutra it is the result of annihilating vṛtti.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.41:** Explicit linkage of annihilated vṛtti to identity with cogniser, cognition, cognised
*Divergence:* Unlike later sutras that separate the three as distinct objects, here they are merged into a single experience of absorption.

**Yoga Sūtra 1.42:** Continuation of the theme of absorption into the cognised
*Divergence:* 1.42 narrows focus to the cognised alone, whereas 1.41 presents a triadic unity.

**Yoga Sūtra 1.43:** Further elaboration on the nature of the absorbed state
*Divergence:* 1.43 introduces the idea of the mind becoming the object, a development from the mere identity expressed in 1.41.

**Yoga Sūtra 1.40:** Preceding sutra discusses the cessation of vṛtti
*Divergence:* 1.40 describes the process of annihilation, while 1.41 states the resulting state, highlighting the causal chain.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You sit quietly, focus your attention on a single object, and consciously let all mental fluctuations cease, allowing your awareness to become fully one with that object.

---

## Concentrated Mind and Meaning
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.42 (Samādhi Pāda)

The argumentative condition ( of the con- centrated mind) is that which is mixed with thoughts of word, meaning, and understanding.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तत्र शब्दार्थज्ञानविकलपैः संकीर्णा स्वितर्का समापत्तिḥ

### IAST

tatra śabdārthajñānavikalpaiḥ saṃkīrṇā savitarkā samāpattiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

There, the concentrated mind, mixed with the word, its meaning, and understanding, narrows to a single point of insight.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that samādhi is not merely a state of stillness but an active, focused engagement of the mind with the semantic content of a word. It claims that the mind’s concentration is inseparable from the triad of word (śabda), meaning (artha), and understanding (buddhi). This triadic engagement is the decisive move that transforms ordinary cognition into a refined, single-pointed awareness. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, the mind’s vṛtti (mental fluctuations) are tamed when the citta (consciousness) is directed toward a specific semantic object, thereby allowing puruṣa to experience a direct, non-dual insight. The Dvivedi commentary’s distinction between gross and subtle samādhi aligns with this claim: the gross form involves the external Bhutas, while the subtle form engages the internal ToMmatras and indriyas. The sutra’s emphasis on the “mixed” nature of the mind’s condition underscores that samādhi is not a passive withdrawal but an active, intentional focus that dissolves the vṛtti. This has existential implications: by mastering the triadic focus, one can transcend the endless cycle of thoughts and achieve a stable, self‑realizing awareness that is the ultimate goal of yoga practice.

### Key Terms

**samādhi (समाधि)** — etymology: ‘to settle’ -> tradition: a state of intense concentration where mind is unified -> default translation ‘concentration’ misses the active, focused engagement with meaning

**citta (चित्त)** — etymology: ‘mind’ -> tradition: the storehouse of impressions and consciousness -> default translation ‘mind’ omits its role as the locus of vṛtti and the site of samādhi

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘fluctuation’ -> tradition: the mind’s wandering states that must be subdued -> default translation ‘fluctuation’ neglects its dynamic causal role in generating ignorance

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘person’ -> tradition: the pure, unchanging consciousness that observes vṛtti -> default translation ‘self’ fails to convey its distinct ontological status
