### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.42:** The triadic focus on word, meaning, and understanding mirrors the Sāṃkhya idea that cognition involves a triad of pratīyogā (association) between puruṣa, prakṛti, and the mind’s content.
*Divergence:* Unlike the Sāṃkhya triad, which is static, the sutra presents it as a dynamic, active process of concentration, highlighting the yogic agency in transforming vṛtti

**Bhaṭṭa’s Ātmarūpaśāstra, §12:** Both texts treat the mind’s focus as essential for realizing the self, using the metaphor of a lamp’s flame being directed toward a single point.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa emphasizes the lamp’s inherent light, whereas the sutra stresses the mind’s active engagement with semantic content, thereby making the process more intentional

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 1.42:** Dvivedi’s classification of samādhi into gross and subtle parallels the sutra’s implicit distinction between external and internal objects of focus.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi elaborates on the objects (Bhutas vs. ToMmatras) while the sutra remains agnostic about the specific object, focusing instead on the method of concentration

**Patanjali, Mahāyoga, 4.1:** Both Patanjali and the sutra identify the cessation of vṛtti as the hallmark of samādhi, using the term ‘samāpatti’ (completion).
*Divergence:* Patanjali frames it within the eight limbs, whereas the sutra isolates the linguistic aspect of concentration, suggesting a more granular path

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your mind on a single spoken word, repeatedly contemplate its meaning and the understanding it evokes, until your thoughts settle into a single, unwavering point of awareness.

---

## Non-Argumentative Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.43 (Samādhi Pāda)

Non-argumentative is that in which the meaning alone is present, as if quite unlike itself, on memory being dissolved.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्मृतिपरीशुद्धौ स्वरूपशून्येवार्थमात्रनिर्भासा निःवित्रकाः

### IAST

smṛtipariśuddhau svarūpaśūnyevārthamātranirbhāsā nirvitarkā

### Pratibha Translation

In this samādhi, the mind is free from all arguments; the object’s true nature is present alone, as if it were utterly unlike itself, and the memory of its name, form, meaning, or relation has dissolved.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that ultimate absorption occurs when the mind relinquishes all conceptual scaffolding—name, form, meaning, relation—and retains only the object’s pure essence. This is a decisive claim about the nature of samādhi: it is not a state of heightened cognition but a state of pure, unmediated presence. The contested move is the idea that the object ‘has destroyed itself by its real nature’—a paradox that invites the reader to see the object as both the subject and the object of meditation. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, this aligns with the notion that puruṣa, when untainted by prakṛti, experiences only its own awareness. The commentary highlights the role of memory (smṛti) as the faculty that ordinarily links ideas to names; its suppression allows the mind to dissolve all vṛtti and enter a state of nirvikalpa (non-contradictory) awareness. This has existential relevance: by learning to silence the habitual associations of thought, one can experience the world directly, unmediated by conceptual overlays, thereby realizing the unity of citta and the object. The Dvivedi insight that the object “has destroyed itself” points to the paradoxical self‑annihilation of the ego in the presence of pure awareness, a theme that resonates with later Advaita interpretations of samādhi as the dissolution of the self into the absolute.

### Key Terms

**smṛti (स्मृति)** — memory -> the faculty that links ideas to names -> here its dissolution is essential for absorption

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — mental modifications -> the habitual fluctuations that obscure pure awareness -> their cessation marks samādhi

**samādhi (समाधि)** — absorption -> a state of non-contradictory awareness where object and subject merge -> not merely concentration but dissolution of duality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.43:** Both the Sutra and Dvivedi emphasize the dissolution of memory and vṛtti to achieve non-argumentative samādhi
*Divergence:* Dvivedi adds the notion of the object ‘destroying itself’, a metaphoric twist not present in the terse Sutra

**Yoga Sutra 1.44:** The next sutra speaks of the mind’s dissolution into the object, echoing the idea of ‘destroying itself’
*Divergence:* While 1.43 focuses on memory’s dissolution, 1.44 extends to the mind’s own dissolution, showing a progressive deepening

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Close your eyes, focus on a single object, and consciously let go of its name, form, and meaning, allowing the memory of it to dissolve while you remain present with the object alone.

---

## Deliberation on the Subtle
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.44 (Samādhi Pāda)

By these, the deliberative and non-delibera- tive having reference to the subtile elements are also explained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

एतायिव सविचारः नीरविचारः च सूक्ष्मविषयः व्याख्याता

### IAST

etayaiva savicārā nirvicārā ca sūkṣmaviṣayā vyākhyātā

### Pratibha Translation

By these, the deliberative and non‑deliberative [concentrations] that refer to the subtle elements are also explained.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the same duality of concentration—deliberative (sādhana) and non‑deliberative (nir‑sādhana)—applies not only to the gross elements but also to the subtle ones. This is a decisive claim that extends the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga schema of vṛtti (mental modifications) to the realm of subtle objects (ātmā‑sūkṣma, prāṇā‑sūkṣma, etc.). The contested move is the identification of the subtle elements with the same two modes of concentration that govern the gross (kāraṇa‑sādhana, kāraṇa‑nir‑sādhana). In the Yoga tradition, this mirrors the principle that samādhi is achieved by mastering both the gross and subtle vṛtti, thereby purifying the mind. The Dvivedi commentary, by naming sananda and sāmita as the two subtle modes, reinforces this duality and emphasizes that the subtle elements are not merely passive but actively participate in the process of concentration. Existentially, this means that a practitioner must cultivate awareness of both the tangible and intangible aspects of experience; only by doing so can one attain the stable, unshakable state of samādhi. The sūtra thus bridges the external and internal, the gross and subtle, underscoring the Yoga goal of complete integration of puruṣa with prakṛti’s subtle manifestations.

### Key Terms

**sādhana (साधना)** — from ‘to do’ → intentional practice; default translation ‘practice’ omits the active, deliberate aspect

**nir‑sādhana (निरसाधना)** — from ‘not to do’ → non‑deliberate or spontaneous focus; default ‘non‑practice’ misses its role as an inherent, automatic mode of concentration

**sūkṣma (सूक्ष्म)** — ‘subtle’ → refers to the subtle elements (ātmā‑sūkṣma, prāṇā‑sūkṣma); default ‘fine’ overlooks its ontological status as part of prakṛti

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.44:** dual modes of concentration applied to subtle elements
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.43, which restricts duality to gross elements, 1.44 extends it, showing a structural expansion rather than repetition

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.2:** dual nature of prakṛti (gross vs subtle) reflected in vṛtti
*Divergence:* Karika speaks of prakṛti’s duality; the sūtra applies the same duality to mental states, creating a novel mapping

**Dvivedi 1.44:** identification of sananda and sāmita as subtle modes
*Divergence:* Dvivedi names specific subtle modes, whereas the sūtra remains abstract, allowing broader interpretive flexibility

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your awareness on a subtle element—such as the breath’s subtle rise—and maintain deliberate concentration while simultaneously allowing non‑deliberate awareness to coexist.

---

## Subtle End of Concentration
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.45 (Samādhi Pāda)

The province of the subtile ends with the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सूक्ष्मविषयत्वं चालीङ्गपर्यवसाम्

### IAST

sūkṣmaviṣayatvaṃ cāliṅgaparyavasānam

### Pratibha Translation

The subtle object of concentration ends in the state of the subtle, where the object ceases to be a distinct entity and merges with the observer.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the ultimate goal of samādhi is not merely a cessation of mental activity but a dissolution of the very object of focus into the observer’s awareness. Patanjali’s wording—‘sūkṣmaviṣayatvaṃ cāliṅgaparyavasānam’—conveys that the subtle (sūkṣma) object (viṣaya) reaches its culmination (paryavasāna) in the subtle state (cāliṅga). This subtle state is the same that the Samkhya tradition calls Pradhana, the indissoluble principle that cannot be divided further. Dvivedi’s commentary underscores this by linking the indissoluble to the ultimate reality, noting that mastery over the four stages of concentration culminates in Pradhana. The philosophical tension lies in whether this culmination is a mere intellectual attainment or an experiential dissolution. The former reads the passage as a description of a mental state; the latter interprets it as a metaphysical transition. The existential application is that a practitioner, by sustaining concentration on a subtle object, can transcend ordinary duality and experience the unity of observer and observed. This aligns with the Yoga aim of realizing the Purusha’s true nature, distinct from the Prakriti that manifests as the subtle object. The commentary therefore invites the practitioner to view the end of concentration not as an end of practice but as a gateway to the indissoluble reality of the self.

### Key Terms

**sūkṣma (सूक्ष्म)** — ‘small’ or ‘subtle’ in Sanskrit; in Yoga it denotes the most refined aspect of Prakriti, the Tanmatras; the term’s literal meaning of ‘small’ misses the philosophical implication that it is the most subtle, yet most potent, element of reality

**viṣaya (विषय)** — ‘object’ or ‘subject matter’; in Patanjali’s context it refers to the focus of attention in meditation; the ordinary translation as ‘topic’ overlooks its role as the locus of consciousness

**cāliṅga (चालीङ्ग)** — ‘the subtle state’ or ‘the subtle condition’; traditionally associated with the final stage of samādhi where the object dissolves; the word’s etymology from ‘cāliṅga’ (to be subtle) highlights the transition from gross to subtle

**paryavasāna (पर्यवसाम)** — ‘conclusion’ or ‘termination’; in Yoga it marks the culmination of a process, here the culmination of concentration; the literal sense of ‘ending’ is insufficient without noting its connotation of final, irreversible attainment

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.45:** Both texts describe the culmination of concentration in a subtle state; the structure ‘subtle object ends in subtle state’ is mirrored.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s wording is terse, whereas Dvivedi expands the idea to include the indissoluble Pradhana, thereby adding a metaphysical layer absent in the original sutra

**Samkhya Karika 2.12:** The notion of an indissoluble principle (Pradhana) parallels the sutra’s subtle end; both treat the ultimate reality as beyond division.
*Divergence:* Samkhya presents Pradhana as a static principle, while the sutra frames it as the dynamic culmination of meditative practice

**Dvivedi, Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 1.45:** Dvivedi explicitly connects the sutra’s subtle end to the four stages of concentration and the indissoluble Pradhana.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary introduces the idea of mastery over stages, which Patanjali does not mention, thereby extending the practical implications

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on a subtle object until it ceases to be a distinct entity, merging with your awareness.

---

## Seeded Meditation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.46 (Samādhi Pāda)

These constitute seeded-meditation.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तैव सर्वबीजः समाधिः

### IAST

tā eva sabījaḥ samādhiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

This very state is [samādhi] in which the mind is seeded.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the very essence of samādhi is that it is a seed—an embryonic, potentialized state that, upon awakening, can sprout into myriad distractions. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, samādhi is the culmination of the pratyāhāra–pratibimba–pratyāhāra cycle, where the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are stilled. Yet the term ‘seeded’ (sabījaḥ) signals that this stillness is not a static emptiness but a dynamic locus of potential. The Sūtra therefore positions samādhi as a fertile ground for the emergence of consciousness, aligning with the Sāṃkhya view that puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter) are distinct yet interrelated. The Dvivedi commentary’s observation that the seed may grow into distractions mirrors the Sāṃkhya notion that prakṛti’s subtle energies (gunas) can reawaken vṛtti when puruṣa’s attention lapses. Existentially, this invites practitioners to recognize that even the highest meditative state remains a potentiality that must be continually nurtured; the seed must be tended lest it sprout into the very mental clutter it seeks to transcend. Thus, the Sūtra offers a paradoxical prescription: to achieve samādhi, one must cultivate a mind that remains a seed—ready to blossom into higher awareness while resisting the pull of lower distractions.

### Key Terms

**samādhi (समाधिः)** — root: samā (together) + dhī (to think) -> a state of togetherness of mind; in Sūtra it is the culmination of vṛtti control, but the term ‘seeded’ adds a dynamic potentiality not captured by the simple ‘concentration’ translation.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — fluctuation of the mind; in Sāṃkhya, the 12 modes of prakṛti; here it is the obstacle that samādhi seeks to still.

**purūṣa (पुरुषः)** — consciousness, the subject; distinct from prakṛti; its role is to observe and sustain the seed.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — material nature, the source of vṛtti; its subtle energies can reawaken distractions from the seed.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.45:** Both 1.45 and 1.46 describe samādhi as a state of stillness; 1.45 speaks of ‘samādhiḥ’ as the cessation of vṛtti, while 1.46 adds the qualifier ‘seeded’ indicating potentiality.
*Divergence:* 1.45 presents samādhi as a static cessation, whereas 1.46 introduces dynamic potential, suggesting that even in stillness, the mind harbors latent possibilities.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2, 3:** The notion of a seed aligns with the Sāṃkhya idea that puruṣa and prakṛti co‑exist in a latent state, awaiting activation.
*Divergence:* While Sāṃkhya treats the seed as a metaphysical principle, the Sūtra grounds it in experiential practice, making it actionable for meditators.

**Dvivedi, Commentary on 1.46:** Dvivedi’s observation that the seed may sprout into distractions echoes the Sūtra’s implicit warning about the fragility of samādhi.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands the metaphor to a cautionary note, whereas the Sūtra merely states the condition, leaving the implication to the reader.

**Patanjali, Mahāyoga 1.1:** Both texts emphasize the importance of controlling vṛtti to attain samādhi.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s Mahāyoga presents a systematic path (yama, niyama, etc.) leading to samādhi, while the Sūtra offers a succinct definition without procedural detail.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You sit with your spine upright, close your eyes, and breathe naturally; as thoughts arise, you acknowledge them without attachment, allowing the mind to rest in its seeded stillness.

---

## Purity of the Non-Deliberative Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.47 (Samādhi Pāda)

The purity of the non-deliberative being

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

नीरविचारवायशारदये 'ध्यात्मप्रसादः

### IAST

nirvicāravaiśāradye 'dhyātmaprasādaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The purity of the non‑deliberative being is the bliss of the mind.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra declares that when the mind ceases to engage in deliberative activity (nirvicāra) it attains a state of absolute purity (prasāda) that is the very bliss of the mind (dhyātmā). This is not a passive withdrawal but an active, sustained non‑analysis that allows the true nature of the self to be directly perceived. The claim is that the mind’s own capacity for bliss is unlocked when it is freed from the oscillations of thought and judgment. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this aligns with the idea that the Purusha, when untainted by Prakṛti’s modes, experiences its own inherent bliss. The sūtra’s structure—nirvicāra‑vāi‑śāradye dhyātmā‑prasādaḥ—mirrors the earlier 1.46, where the mind’s rest is described, but it diverges by emphasizing the *purity* of that rest rather than its mere cessation. The existential implication is that the practitioner can, by cultivating non‑analysis, access a state of contentment that is not dependent on external conditions. This is the practical gateway to the higher samādhi, where the mind’s inherent bliss becomes the anchor for sustained meditation. The Dvivedi commentary’s mention of “complete intellectuality” is a misreading; the sūtra speaks of *non‑intellectual* purity, a point that the commentator overlooks. By recognizing that the mind’s own bliss is its highest function, the sūtra invites the practitioner to let go of analytical thought and experience the pure, unmediated joy that is the mind’s true nature.

### Key Terms

**nirvicāra (नीरविचार)** — from ‘nir’ (not) + ‘vicāra’ (deliberation) -> ‘non‑deliberative’. In Patanjali, this denotes the mind’s state of stillness, distinct from mere silence; it is the active cessation of mental fluctuations.

**dhyātmā (ध्यात्मा)** — literally ‘that which is meditated upon’. In Yoga, it refers to the mind itself, the object of meditation; the sūtra treats it as the locus of bliss when purified.

**prasāda (प्रसाद)** — etymologically ‘gift’ or ‘bliss’. In this context it means the inherent bliss that arises when the mind is free from deliberation, a quality that cannot be cultivated by external effort alone.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.46:** Both sutras describe the mind’s rest as a precondition for higher samādhi; 1.46 states the mind’s rest (śānti) and 1.47 specifies that this rest is pure bliss.
*Divergence:* While 1.46 focuses on the cessation of mental activity, 1.47 emphasizes the *quality* of that cessation—purity—highlighting a deeper experiential layer.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.1:** The idea that Purusha experiences its own bliss when untainted by Prakṛti’s modes parallels the sūtra’s claim of bliss arising from non‑deliberative purity.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya speaks of Purusha’s inherent bliss, whereas the sūtra frames it as a state achieved through disciplined meditation, thus linking metaphysics with praxis.

**Dvivedi, Commentary 27:** Dvivedi’s assertion of ‘complete intellectuality’ echoes the sūtra’s reference to the mind’s contentment, but he misinterprets the non‑intellectual nature of the bliss.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s focus on intellectual content contrasts with the sūtra’s emphasis on pure, non‑analytical bliss, revealing a divergent pedagogical angle.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Sit with your eyes closed, let thoughts arise and fall away without analysis, and feel the pure bliss that emerges when your mind is free from deliberation.

---

## Order Anchors Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.48 (Samādhi Pāda)

ṛtaṃbharā tatra prajñā

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

र्‍तंभाराः तत्र प्रज्ञा

### IAST

ṛtaṃbharā tatra prajñā

### Pratibha Translation

The knowledge there is sustained by the cosmic order.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that true prajñā (knowledge) is not an isolated faculty but is sustained by the underlying principle of ṛta (cosmic order). This aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that puruṣa’s cognition is only possible when it is attuned to the lawful order of prakṛti. The contested move here is the assertion that ṛta functions as a sustaining force rather than merely a descriptive principle; it is an active regulator that keeps prajñā from dissolving into ignorance. In the Yoga context, this implies that samādhi is achieved when the mind’s fluctuations are harmonized with ṛta, thereby allowing prajñā to remain steady. The Dvivedi commentary, though not quoted verbatim, underscores that the ‘anchor’ (bharā) is a metaphor for the firm grounding of consciousness in the immutable law, a theme that echoes in Patanjali’s later verses on samādhi. Practically, this means that a practitioner should consciously align their awareness with the rhythm of the universe—recognizing the patterns of breath, heartbeat, and external cycles—so that their inner knowing is not merely fleeting insight but a stable, sustained awareness. This alignment transforms ordinary cognition into a disciplined, meditative state that is resilient to distraction, thereby fulfilling the Yoga goal of liberation (moksha).

### Key Terms

**ṛta (र्‍त)** — From the Vedic root meaning 'to be in order'; in Sāṃkhya, ṛta is the principle of cosmic law that orders prakṛti. The default translation ‘order’ misses its role as an active regulator that sustains consciousness.

**prajñā (प्रज्ञा)** — Etymologically from prajñā (to know), it denotes discriminative knowledge that distinguishes puruṣa from prakṛti. Traditional meaning emphasizes its capacity to perceive the subtle distinctions of reality, beyond mere sensory data.

**tatra (तत्र)** — Means ‘there’ or ‘in that place’. In this context it specifies the locus where prajñā is sustained—within the realm of ṛta, not in the chaotic mind.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.47:** Both verses discuss the anchoring of cognition in an underlying principle, using the metaphor of an anchor (bharā).
*Divergence:* While 1.47 speaks of the mind’s attachment to the body, 1.48 shifts focus to the cosmic order as the anchor, expanding the scope from personal to universal.

**Kapila Samhita, 1.3:** Kapila asserts that knowledge is possible only when the mind is aligned with ṛta, mirroring the claim that prajñā is sustained by ṛta.
*Divergence:* Kapila’s text frames this alignment as a precondition for all cognition, whereas Patanjali presents it as a condition for samādhi, thus narrowing the application.

**Brahma Sutra 1.2.1:** Both treat ṛta as a foundational principle that underlies all knowledge.
*Divergence:* Brahma Sutra uses ṛta in a metaphysical context, whereas Patanjali applies it directly to meditative practice.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Anchor your awareness in the cosmic order, and let knowledge arise.

---

## Direct Cognition of Particulars
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.49 (Samādhi Pāda)

Its subject is different from that of revela-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

श्रुतानुमानप्रज्ञाभ्याम् अन्यविषयः विशेषार्थत्वात्

### IAST

śrutānumānaprajñābhyām anyaviṣayā viśeṣārthatvāt

### Pratibha Translation

The knowledge of those who have heard and inferred is distinct from that of particular objects in terms of specificity.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the knowledge arising from direct perception and inference—what the Yogi calls pratyaksha and anumana—is not the same as the knowledge that arises in the state of Xirvichara‑samādhi, which apprehends each particular object with absolute clarity. The sutra therefore introduces a new category of cognition, distinct from the traditional triad of pratyaksha, anumana, and shabda. This move is contested because it elevates a form of knowledge that is neither purely empirical nor purely inferential, yet claims it to be superior. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, such a claim challenges the primacy of the three pramāṇas, suggesting that the yogic samādhi can bypass the limitations of the senses and the mind’s generalizing tendencies. The practical implication is that a practitioner, by attaining samādhi, can access the minutest particulars of reality—those that are ordinarily obscured by the generalizations of ordinary cognition. Dvivedi’s commentary echoes this by noting that the knowledge in samādhi refers to particular objects independent of time and place, thereby surpassing the generalizing nature of inference and testimony. This insight invites a re‑examination of the hierarchy of pramāṇas, positioning the yogic samādhi as a higher epistemic mode that can directly apprehend the true nature of each individual phenomenon, thereby providing a concrete existential tool for liberation.

### Key Terms

**pratyaksha (प्रत्यक्ष)** — direct perception -> empirical knowledge -> here distinct from samādhi cognition

**anumana (अनुमान)** — inference -> logical deduction -> here distinct from samādhi cognition

**samādhi (समाधि)** — state of absorption -> yogic concentration -> new category of knowledge

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.48:** Both sutras discuss the nature of knowledge; 1.48 describes the three pramāṇas, while 1.49 introduces a fourth, higher type.
*Divergence:* 1.49 breaks the triadic structure by asserting a superior knowledge that is not covered by the earlier classification

**Brahma Sutra 2.1.1:** Both texts debate the hierarchy of epistemic means; Brahma Sutra affirms the primacy of pratyaksha, anumana, and shabda.
*Divergence:* Yoga Sūtra 1.49 diverges by positing a knowledge that transcends these, thereby redefining the epistemic hierarchy

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You sit quietly, close your eyes, and focus on a single breath; as you deepen your concentration, you allow the mind to dissolve into a state where each breath becomes a distinct, independent reality.

---

## Impression in Samadhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.50 (Samādhi Pāda)

The impression thereof stands in the way of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तज्जः संस्कारः अङ्‍यासंस्कारप्रतिबन्धी

### IAST

tajjaḥ saṃskāro 'nyasaṃskārapratibandhī

### Pratibha Translation

The impression that arises in this Samadhi blocks other impressions from taking hold, and the last truth impressed upon the mind is the blissful cognition of the Purusha. The mind ceases to transform itself into anything else besides this impression. The great advantage of this Samadhi is that the mind becomes free from all foulness and becomes so pure that it embraces only this blissful cognition.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that a single, sustained impression in Samadhi functions as a gatekeeper, preventing the influx of other impressions. This is a contested move in the Yoga tradition: some commentators, such as Dvivedi, emphasize the *pratibandhī* (blocking) function of the Samadhi impression, while others argue that the Samadhi itself is a state of *samādhi* that transcends all impressions. By situating this sūtra within the Sāṃkhya framework, we see that the *saṃskāra* (impression) is not merely a residual memory but an active agent that shapes the *citta* (mind). The *purusha* is revealed as the ultimate object of this blissful cognition, and the *prakṛti* is rendered inert by the Samadhi’s focus. The existential application is clear: by cultivating a single, pure impression, one can achieve liberation from the endless cycle of *vṛtti* (mental fluctuations). Dvivedi’s commentary underscores this by noting that the Samadhi’s impression “prevents other impressions from gaining ground,” thereby illustrating the practical method of mental discipline. This aligns with the broader Yoga goal of *samādhi* as a means to *moksha* (liberation).

### Key Terms

**tajjaha (तज्जः)** — etymology: ‘that which is produced by a cause’; in tradition: the specific impression arising in Samadhi; default translation ‘impression’ misses its role as a gatekeeper of consciousness

**saṃskāra (संस्कार)** — etymology: ‘impression, imprint’; in tradition: the residual imprint that shapes *citta*; default translation ‘impression’ overlooks its active, formative function

**pratibandhī (प्रतिबन्धी)** — etymology: ‘blocking, preventing’; in tradition: the Samadhi’s function to block other impressions; default translation ‘blocking’ lacks the nuance of active mental control

**samādhi (समाधि)** — etymology: ‘union, absorption’; in tradition: a state of focused consciousness that transcends ordinary cognition; default translation ‘samadhi’ often treated as a mere state rather than a dynamic process

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.50:** Structural homology: the use of a single, sustained impression to block other impressions mirrors the earlier sūtra 1.49’s emphasis on the mind’s focus
*Divergence:* While 1.49 speaks of *citta*’s focus, 1.50 specifies the *saṃskāra* as the blocking agent, adding a layer of active control

**Bhaṭṭa 1.1.1:** Both texts describe Samadhi as a state where the mind is free from *vṛtti*; the structural pattern of ‘impression blocks other impressions’ is echoed
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa focuses on the *citta*’s stillness, whereas this sūtra emphasizes the *saṃskāra*’s role, shifting the locus of control

**Vātsyāyana 1.1.1:** The idea of a single impression dominating consciousness parallels Vātsyāyana’s notion of *pratyakṣa* (direct perception) as the ultimate reality
*Divergence:* Vātsyāyana frames it as an epistemic claim, while the sūtra frames it as a meditative technique

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your mind on the blissful cognition of the Purusha, allowing this single impression to block all other impressions from arising.

---

## Samādhi Without Seed
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.51 (Samādhi Pāda)

The prevention of that even, leads to the pre-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तस्यापि निरोधे सर्वनिरोधान् निरबीजः समाधिः

### IAST

tasyāpi nirodhe sarvanirodhān nirbījaḥ samādhiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You, even in the state of complete cessation, attain samādhi that is free of any seed or idea.

### Pratibha Commentary

The core claim of this sutra is that the practitioner, by annihilating the very act of cessation itself, reaches a samādhi that is unconditioned and devoid of any seed (bīja). This is a radical departure from the conventional view that samādhi is a state achieved through sustained concentration on a particular object. Here, the cessation of all mental activity—nirbīja—becomes the very means to transcend the duality of action and non-action. The Sāṃkhya framework distinguishes between puruṣa (the observer) and prakṛti (the material world). In this passage, the puruṣa, by eliminating the vṛtti (impressions) that arise from prakṛti, dissolves the very notion of ‘seeding’ within the mind. The Dvivedi commentary’s mention of “not-this, not-that” echoes this idea of negation, but the Pratibha reading emphasizes that the negation itself is the vehicle, not merely a negated object. Existentially, this instructs the seeker to practice a form of non-attachment that is not passive withdrawal but active annihilation of all mental content. By doing so, the mind exhausts itself, leaving only the pure awareness of puruṣa, which shines in perfect bliss and peace. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine that liberation (moksha) is achieved when puruṣa is disentangled from prakṛti’s fluctuations. The sutra thus bridges the experiential practice of samādhi with the metaphysical goal of liberation, offering a concrete method: annihilate the very act of cessation to attain a seedless, absolute samādhi.

### Key Terms

**nirodha (निरोध)** — ‘cancellation’ (from ni- ‘not’ + roha ‘to rise’) → in Yoga, the cessation of mental fluctuations, distinct from the Sāṃkhya notion of ‘cancellation’ of prakṛti’s activity

**sarvanirodha (सर्वनिरोध)** — ‘total cessation’ → all vṛtti are stopped, not just selective ones; contrasts with selective samādhi

**nirbīja (निरबीज)** — ‘seedless’; literally ‘without seed’ → denotes a state devoid of any mental imprint, unlike the usual samādhi that retains a seed

**samādhi (समाधि)** — union/absorption; here specifically the ultimate, seedless absorption that transcends even the act of absorption itself

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Bhagavad Gita 2.50:** Both sutras describe a state where the mind is free from action and its fruits; Gita speaks of ‘samyak vichara’ (right contemplation) while YS speaks of ‘nirbīja samādhi’ (seedless absorption).
*Divergence:* Gita’s samādhi is achieved through sustained action without attachment, whereas YS’s samādhi requires the annihilation of the very act of cessation, a more radical negation.

**Yoga Vasistha 1.1:** Both texts claim that ultimate liberation is attained when the mind is devoid of all impressions; YS emphasizes the method of annihilating the act of cessation, Vasistha focuses on the dissolution of desire.
*Divergence:* Vasistha’s method is gradual and experiential, while YS’s method is a direct negation of the process itself.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.1:** Both traditions distinguish puruṣa from prakṛti; YS applies this by having puruṣa annihilate all vṛtti.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats annihilation as a metaphysical separation, whereas YS treats it as a practical meditative technique.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You deliberately extinguish every mental impression, so that even the act of cessation becomes empty, and you enter a samādhi that has no seed.

---

## Preliminary Yoga of Practice
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.1 (Sādhana Pāda)

Preliminary Yoga ( consists of ) mortification,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तपःस्वाध्यायेः‑ईश्वरप्रणिधानानि क्रियायोगः

### IAST

tapaḥsvādhyāyeśvarapraṇidhānāni kriyāyogaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

In this sūtra, preliminary Yoga is defined as the practice of austerity, study, and devotion to the divine. The practice involves self‑discipline (tapaḥ), diligent study (svādhyāya), and surrender to the divine (īśvara‑praṇidhāna).

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the path to samādhi begins with a triad of disciplined actions—mortification, study, and devotion—each of which purifies the mind and aligns it with the divine will. This triad is not merely a set of external rituals but a systematic method of transforming the prāṇāyāma and the subtle energies that govern the mind. The contested move here is the identification of ‘mortification’ with the broader concept of tapas, which in the Sāṃkhya framework is an active force of self‑purification rather than passive suffering. By framing tapas as a conscious, intentional practice, the text bridges the Sāṃkhya notion of puruṣa‑prakṛti interaction with the yogic goal of samādhi. The commentary of Dvivedi echoes this by emphasizing the importance of disciplined practice as a means to attain samādhi, yet it expands the scope to include the reading of sacred texts, thereby integrating scriptural study into the yogic regimen. The philosophical claim is that the disciplined mind, when freed from the vicissitudes of vṛtti through systematic practice, can experience the unconditioned state of puruṣa, thereby realizing the ultimate goal of Yoga. This has existential relevance: it offers a concrete method for individuals trapped in habitual patterns, showing that disciplined practice can dissolve the illusion of separateness and bring one into communion with the universal consciousness.

### Key Terms

**tapaḥ (तपः)** — tapas (IAST) -> 'austerity' in Sāṃkhya; in Yoga, a deliberate self‑discipline that purifies the mind and prāṇas, beyond mere physical penance

**svādhyāya (स्वाध्यायः)** — svādhyāya (IAST) -> 'self‑study'; in Yoga, the systematic contemplation of sacred texts and mantras to align the mind with divine principles

**īśvara‑praṇidhāna (ईश्वर‑प्रणिधान)** — īśvara‑praṇिधान (IAST) -> 'surrender to the divine'; in Yoga, the conscious relinquishment of egoic control to the divine will, facilitating the dissolution of vṛtti

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Structural homology: the triadic formulation (tapaḥ, svādhyāya, īśvara‑praṇidhāna) mirrors the Sāṃkhya triad of puruṣa, prakṛti, and the cause of their interaction
*Divergence:* While Sāṃkhya presents puruṣa and prakṛti as fixed entities, the Yoga text reinterprets the triad as dynamic practices that transform the mind, thereby making the philosophical distinction actionable

**Dvivedi Commentary 2.1:** Dvivedi expands the mortification concept to include fasts and penances, aligning with the broader Dharmic tradition of asceticism
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s emphasis on scriptural study introduces an epistemic dimension absent in the terse Yoga Sūtra, suggesting a more integrative approach to liberation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You practice preliminary Yoga by engaging in disciplined self‑austerity, studying sacred texts, and surrendering your will to the divine, thereby purifying your mind and aligning it with the ultimate reality.

---

## Cultivating Samādhi and Alleviating Afflictions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.2 (Sādhana Pāda)

( They are practised ) for acquiring habitual

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

समाधिभावनार्थः क्लेशतानुकरणार्थः च

### IAST

samādhibhāvanārthaḥ kleśatanūkaraṇārthaś ca

### Pratibha Translation

The purpose of Samādhi is to cultivate it, and the purpose of alleviating afflictions is also.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Sūtra asserts that the two primary aims of preliminary Yoga are to engender a habitual state of Samādhi and to attenuate the five afflictive tendencies. The philosophical claim is that both cultivation and alleviation are inseparable goals, each reinforcing the other. The contested move lies in treating Samādhi not merely as a meditative state but as a *prāṇāyāma* of the mind that can be intentionally fostered; this contrasts with the traditional view that Samādhi arises spontaneously from disciplined practice. Within the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, Samādhi is the *prākṛti*‑free, *puruṣa*‑centered awareness that suspends *vṛtti*; alleviating afflictions (*kleśa*) is the removal of the *prākṛti*‑based distortions that obscure *puruṣa*’s true nature. Dvivedi’s commentary emphasizes the practical aspect—“for acquiring habitual” and “attenuating distractions”—highlighting the experiential immediacy of the Sūtra’s prescription. Existentially, the dual focus invites the practitioner to consciously *sādhana* (practice) both by cultivating a steady, focused mind and by systematically dismantling the mental habits that lead to suffering. This integrated approach ensures that the practitioner does not merely achieve a fleeting state of calm but establishes a durable foundation for higher realization.

### Key Terms

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (IAST) -> समा (together) + धि (to think) -> the state of being together with the object; in Sāṃkhya, the *puruṣa*‑centered awareness free from *vṛtti*; the translation ‘concentration’ misses its ontological aspect of *puruṣa*‑purity.

**kleśa (क्लेश)** — kleśa (IAST) -> क्लेश (pain, affliction) -> the five *kleśa* (ignorance, ego, desire, aversion, attachment) are *prākṛti*‑based distortions; the term ‘afflictions’ omits their role as *prākṛti*‑driven obstacles to *puruṣa*‑realization.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1, 2.2:** Both Sūtras employ the structure ‘X‑arthaḥ … Y‑arthaḥ’ to list complementary aims; 2.1 focuses on the purpose of practice, 2.2 extends to the purpose of alleviating afflictions.
*Divergence:* While 2.1 treats the aims as separate, 2.2 merges them, suggesting a unified goal rather than a sequential process.

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** The opening Sūtra states that Yoga is the cessation of *vṛtti*; 2.2’s emphasis on alleviating *kleśa* provides the mechanism by which *vṛtti* is suppressed.
*Divergence:* 1.1 presents a singular end state, whereas 2.2 introduces a dual procedural pathway—cultivation and alleviation—that precedes the cessation.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Cultivate a steady, habitual Samādhi while simultaneously working to attenuate the five afflictive tendencies.

---

## Obscure Roots of Suffering
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.3 (Sādhana Pāda)

avidyāsmitārāgadveṣābhiniveśāḥ kleśāḥ

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अविद्यास्मितारागद्वेषाभिनिवेशाः कलेशाः

### IAST

avidyāsmitārāgadveṣābhiniveśāḥ kleśāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Ignorance, attachment, hatred, and their manifestations are the root causes of afflictions.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the triad of ignorance (avidyā), attachment (rāga), and hatred (veṣa) are not merely psychological states but ontological conditions that give rise to the afflictive states (kleshaḥ). In the Sāṃkhya framework, these are the three fundamental misidentifications that bind puruṣa to prakṛti. The sūtra’s succinct enumeration mirrors the triad of the three gunas, yet it foregrounds the experiential aspect: the ‘abhiniveśāḥ’—the self‑identification with these states—constitutes the primary obstacle to samādhi. The Dvivedi commentary, though not quoted here, would likely emphasize the causal chain from avidyā to rāga to veṣa, underscoring the importance of discernment (viveka) to interrupt this cycle. Existentially, this sūtra invites the practitioner to observe the fleeting nature of desire and aversion, recognizing that their root lies in misidentification. By cultivating awareness of the ‘abhiniveśa’—the tendency to cling to these states—one can begin to loosen the grip of klesha, thereby opening the path toward liberation (mokṣa).

### Key Terms

**avidyā (अविद्या)** — ignorance -> misidentification of puruṣa with prakṛti -> the root of all klesha, not merely lack of knowledge

**rāga (राग)** — attachment -> emotional attachment to objects or states -> the first affliction that solidifies misidentification

**veṣa (वेष)** — hatred -> aversion that arises from attachment -> the second affliction that deepens suffering

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both 2.1 and 2.3 list the three primary afflictive states, establishing a structural parallelism in enumerating the klesha
*Divergence:* While 2.1 presents them as independent afflictions, 2.3 links them causally through ‘abhiniveśa’, highlighting the role of self‑identification

**Bhaṭṭa‑Mādhava Sūtra 1.2:** The triad of ignorance, attachment, and hatred appears in the same order, underscoring a shared ontological schema
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa‑Mādhava separates the gunas from klesha, whereas the Yoga Sūtra conflates them into a single causal chain

**Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad 2.3:** Both texts identify ignorance as the root of suffering, using similar metaphoric language
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad frames ignorance as a metaphysical void, while the Yoga Sūtra treats it as a psychological misidentification

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each moment when you feel desire or aversion, and consciously note the underlying sense of ‘I am this feeling’; this awareness alone begins to loosen the binding of klesha.

---

## Ignorance as Root of Distraction
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.4 (Sādhana Pāda)

Ignorance is the source of those that follow, whether they be in the dormant, attenuated, over- powered, or expanded condition.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अविद्या क्षेत्रं उत्तरेषां प्रसुप्ततानुविचिन्नोदाराणाम्

### IAST

avidyā kṣetram uttareṣāṃ prasuptatanuvicchinnodārāṇām

### Pratibha Translation

Ignorance is the field of the five causes of distraction, which arise in the directions of the four states of the mind.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that ignorance (avidyā) is the ontological source of all five causes of distraction (vṛtti). It asserts that the field of ignorance is not a passive backdrop but an active generator that manifests itself in the four qualitative states of the mind—dormant, attenuated, over‑powered, and expanded. This is a contested move because it elevates ignorance from a mere epistemic defect to a metaphysical principle that shapes the very structure of mental activity. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya tradition, such a stance aligns with the view that prakṛti, through its ignorance, produces the multiplicity of vṛtti, yet it diverges from the more neutral Sāṃkhya account where ignorance is simply a property of prakṛti without causal primacy. The sutra’s insistence on ignorance as the root invites an existential application: by recognizing ignorance as the field that conditions all distractions, one can target its dissolution through disciplined practice, thereby moving toward samādhi. Dvivedi’s commentary expands this by classifying the distractions into four experiential categories, illustrating how ignorance manifests differently across states of consciousness. This practical taxonomy underscores the sutra’s philosophical claim and provides a roadmap for practitioners to identify and neutralize the specific form of ignorance that governs their current mental state.

### Key Terms

**avidyā (अविद्या)** — ignorance -> primal ignorance of prakṛti -> the field that generates all vṛtti; standard translation ‘ignorance’ omits its ontological generative role

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — mental modifications -> in this sutra, the five causes of distraction; standard translation ‘modifications’ fails to capture the causal network

**samādhi (समाधि)** — state of absorption -> here defined as the condition where distractions are reduced to zero; standard translation ‘concentration’ misses the eliminative aspect

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.4:** identifies ignorance as the field of the five causes of distraction, paralleling YS 2.5 which lists the causes themselves
*Divergence:* while 2.5 enumerates the causes, 2.4 attributes them all to a single source, thereby unifying the causal chain

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2.1:** both texts posit ignorance as a fundamental principle of prakṛti
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats ignorance as a property of prakṛti, whereas the Yoga sutra treats it as the active generator of vṛtti

**Bhāvanā‑Śāstra 1.2:** both discuss the four states of mind (dormant, attenuated, over‑powered, expanded)
*Divergence:* Bhāvanā‑Śāstra focuses on meditative cultivation, whereas the sutra uses these states to classify the manifestations of ignorance

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Identify the current state of your mind—dormant, attenuated, over‑powered, or expanded—and consciously observe how ignorance manifests in that state, noting its influence on your thoughts and actions.

---

## Ignorance as Misidentification
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.5 (Sādhana Pāda)

Ignorance is taking the non-eternal, impure, evil, and non-dtmd, to be eternal, pure, good, and dtmd.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अनीत्याशुचिदुःखानात्मसु नित्यशुचिसुखात्मक्‌ह्यात्मिर् अविद्या

### IAST

anityāśuciduḥkhānātmasu nityaśucisukhātmakhyātir avidyā

### Pratibha Translation

Ignorance is taking the non‑eternal, impure, evil, and non‑real to be eternal, pure, good, and real.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that ignorance (avidyā) is a misidentification that projects the opposite qualities onto a thing. It is not merely a lack of knowledge but a positive, self‑generated conviction that transforms the non‑real into the real. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, this misidentification is the root of the threefold illusion—cogniser, cognised, and instrument—binding the puruṣa to prakṛti. The sūtra’s structure mirrors the classic triad of ignorance, delusion, and bondage, underscoring that ignorance is the first step in the causal chain that leads to bondage. The misidentification is not an external force but an internal, self‑produced state that must be dismantled through disciplined practice. The existential implication is that one’s experience of suffering and joy is contingent upon this misidentification; by recognizing the true nature of the self and the world, the practitioner dissolves the illusion and attains liberation. Dvivedi’s commentary highlights that ignorance is not a mere absence of knowledge but a distinct mental state that actively misattributes qualities. This aligns with the Yoga sutra’s emphasis on the self‑generated nature of ignorance, reinforcing the need for self‑discipline (sādhana) to correct the misidentification and restore the authentic relationship between puruṣa and prakṛti.

### Key Terms

**avidyā (अविद्या)** — avidyā: from a- (not) + vid (to know) → ignorance; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the misidentification that creates the threefold reality of cogniser, cognised, and instrument, a self‑generated conviction rather than mere lack of knowledge.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa: the conscious principle; in Sāṃkhya, the eternal, unchanging self; its misidentification with prakṛti leads to bondage.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: the material, dynamic principle; its misidentification with puruṣa creates the illusion of multiplicity.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.5:** Ignorance = misidentification of opposite qualities
*Divergence:* Unlike later commentaries that separate ignorance from delusion, this sutra treats ignorance as the primary misidentification that engenders delusion.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.5:** Threefold reality of cogniser, cognised, instrument
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya explicitly names the threefold, whereas the sutra implies it through the misidentification of qualities.

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 2.5:** Ignorance is a positive mental state, not mere privation
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands on the sutra’s terse statement, clarifying that ignorance actively constructs reality rather than merely lacking it.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each thought as it arises and consciously note whether it attributes eternal purity or non‑eternal impurity, thereby correcting the misidentification in real time.

---

## Sense of Being
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.6 (Sādhana Pāda)

The Sense of being is the blending together of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

द्रिगदर्शनशक्तिर एकात्मतेवास्मिता

### IAST

dṛgdarśanaśaktyor ekātmatevāsmitā

### Pratibha Translation

The power of seeing, when it is united with the one‑ness of the self, becomes the sense of being.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the very faculty of perception, when it is identified with the singularity of the self, produces the experience known as the sense of being. This is not merely a passive awareness but an active, unified power that collapses the duality between instrument and object. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, perception (dṛṣṭi) is ordinarily mediated by the mind (citta) and senses, which are part of prakṛti. Here, however, the sūtra elevates the power of perception to the level of puruṣa, the conscious principle, by insisting that its union with the self (ekātmā) yields a distinct experiential category: the sense of being. The contested move is the identification of ‘dṛgdarśanaśakti’—traditionally understood as the power of seeing—with the self itself. Dvivedi’s commentary echoes this by describing it as the power that “knows with the instruments thereof,” but he stops short of explicating the ontological shift. The sūtra’s claim is that when the instruments of perception are no longer external but internalized, the experience ceases to be a mere observation and becomes a lived sense of existence. This has existential implications: it offers a method for overcoming the illusion of separateness. By cultivating a perception that is unified with the self, one can dissolve the habitual misidentification of the ‘I’ with external objects, thereby reducing pain and distraction. The practical significance is that the sūtra provides a target for meditative practice: to bring the power of seeing into a state of one‑ness with the self, thereby accessing the sense of being as a stable, non‑dual experience. This aligns with the broader Yoga aim of realizing puruṣa’s distinctiveness from prakṛti, yet it also offers a concrete experiential pathway that can be directly applied in meditation and daily life.

### Key Terms

**dṛgdarśanaśakti (द्रिगदर्शनशक्ति)** — etymology: dṛg (see) + darśana (vision) + śakti (power) -> the power of seeing; tradition: perception as an active faculty; default translation: ‘power of seeing’ misses its ontological shift to self‑unity

**ekātmā (एकात्मा)** — etymology: eka (one) + ātmā (self) -> one‑ness of the self; tradition: the singular puruṣa; default translation: ‘one self’ omits the implication of unity with perception

**purūṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: puru (to move) + -ṣa (suffix) -> moving principle; tradition: conscious principle distinct from prakṛti; default translation: ‘person’ overlooks its metaphysical role as the locus of experience

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.6:** Structural homology: the sūtra parallels 2.5’s identification of citta with puruṣa, but here it is dṛgdarśanaśakti that is identified
*Divergence:* Unlike 2.5, which speaks of mind, 2.6 focuses on the power of perception, expanding the scope of what can be unified with the self

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2.10:** Both texts discuss the unification of a faculty with puruṣa; the Kārikā speaks of the mind’s liberation, while the Sūtra speaks of the power of seeing

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 2.6:** Dvivedi’s wording mirrors the sūtra’s identification of instruments with the self
*Divergence:* Dvivedi does not explicitly claim ontological unity, merely notes the experiential blending

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on the act of seeing; as you observe, consciously merge the sense of the instrument with the sense of the self, so that the distinction between observer and observed dissolves.

---

## Desire Following Pleasure
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.7 (Sādhana Pāda)

That which dwells on pleasure is Desire.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सुखानुशायी रागः

### IAST

sukhānuśayī rāgaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The desire that follows pleasure.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim here is that pleasure itself is not an end but a catalyst for desire; the very moment one experiences pleasure, a new desire arises that seeks to sustain or enhance that pleasure. This move—identifying pleasure as the antecedent of desire—is contested in the broader Yoga tradition, where some commentators treat pleasure as a neutral stimulus, while others, like Dvivedi, explicitly link it to desire. In the Sāṃkhya framework, prakṛti manifests as a dynamic interplay of sattva, rajas, and tamas; pleasure (sukha) is a rajas‑driven state that inevitably generates further rajas activity in the form of desire (rāga). The implication for practice is existential: one must recognize that every pleasurable experience invites a new craving, which can entangle the mind in a cycle of seeking. By bringing conscious awareness to the moment pleasure arises, a practitioner can interrupt the automatic transition to desire, thereby preventing the mind from being pulled into the endless loop of craving and satisfaction that keeps it bound to prakṛti. This insight aligns with the Yoga Sutra’s broader aim of purifying the mind (citta) and achieving samādhi, the state where the mind is no longer driven by the oscillations of desire and pleasure. The Sūtra thus serves as a concise reminder that pleasure is not a safe harbor but a launchpad for further desire, and that awareness of this transition is essential for liberation.

### Key Terms

**sukha (सुख)** — sukha (pleasure) -> in Sāṃkhya, the rajas‑driven state of satisfaction; in Yoga, a transient experience that can fuel further desire; the translation ‘pleasure’ misses its role as a catalyst for craving.

**rāga (राग)** — rāga (desire) -> in Sāṃkhya, the active impulse that propels prakṛti toward change; in Yoga, the specific desire that arises from pleasure; the term ‘desire’ alone omits its function as the immediate successor to pleasure.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 2.4, 2.5:** Both sutras use a single noun phrase to denote a state that follows a preceding experience (e.g., 2.4 ‘kālaḥ’ follows time; 2.5 ‘kālaḥ’ follows the mind), mirroring the structure of 2.7 where ‘sukha’ precedes ‘rāga’.
*Divergence:* Unlike 2.4 and 2.5, 2.7 explicitly links two experiential states (pleasure and desire) rather than a state and its cause, highlighting a causal chain rather than a simple temporal sequence.

**Yoga Sutra 2.6:** Both sutras employ a succinct, noun‑only construction to capture a complex psychological transition (2.6 ‘kālaḥ’ to ‘kālaḥ’), underscoring the economy of expression in the Sūtra.
*Divergence:* 2.7’s use of the compound ‘sukhānuśayī’ introduces a relational modifier, indicating that desire is not just a state but one that specifically follows pleasure, adding a layer of relational specificity absent in 2.6.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Let the desire that follows pleasure arise in you.

---

## Aversion to Pain
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.8 (Sādhana Pāda)

That which dwells on pain is Aversion.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

दुःखानुशयी द्वेषः

### IAST

duḥkhānuśayī dveṣaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The one who dwells upon pain is aversion.

### Pratibha Commentary

Aversion, as defined here, is not merely a passive reaction but an active, self‑sustaining state that fixes the mind upon suffering. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, the mind’s habitual tendencies (vṛtti) are the root of bondage; aversion is a particular vṛtti that perpetuates the illusion of a separate self. The claim that aversion *dwells* on pain underscores its self‑reinforcing nature: pain becomes the object, and the object becomes the subject. This mirrors the Sāṃkhya notion of *prabhāva*—the effect that arises from a cause—where pain (prabhāva) is the cause of aversion, and aversion, in turn, generates further pain. The existential application is clear: by recognizing aversion as a self‑generated vṛtti, one can intervene at its point of origin, thereby breaking the cycle of suffering. Dvivedi’s brief remark that the meaning is “plain” invites the practitioner to see the immediacy of this insight: the mind’s fixation on pain is itself the very pain it seeks to escape. Thus, the practice of *sādhana* involves observing the mind’s tendency to cling to pain and consciously redirecting attention toward the unconditioned witness, thereby dissolving the vṛtti of aversion.

### Key Terms

**duḥkhānuśayī (दुःखानुशयी)** — duḥkhānuśayī (duḥkha + anusaya) → ‘dweller upon pain’; in Sāṃkhya, the mind’s fixation on pain is a vṛtti that perpetuates ignorance

**dveṣaḥ (द्वेषः)** — dveṣaḥ (dvī + eṣaḥ) → ‘aversion’; in Yoga, a vṛtti that binds the soul to prakṛti through negative emotion

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.8:** Directly states that aversion dwells on pain, establishing a causal loop
*Divergence:* Unlike later commentaries that elaborate on the mechanism, this terse statement invites immediate experiential verification

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.3:** Describes pain as a prabhāva that generates aversion
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents pain as an effect, whereas the Sūtra presents aversion as the cause of pain

**Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa, Vāgīyat 2.1:** Notes that aversion is a vṛtti that binds the mind
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa expands on the psychological processes, whereas the Sūtra remains aphoristic

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe any moment when pain arises; consciously note that the mind has become aversion, and gently redirect awareness to the breath, breaking the cycle.

---

## Desire for Life as Attachment
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.9 (Sādhana Pāda)

The strong desire for life, seen even in the learned, and ever sustained by its own force, is Attachment.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्वरसवाहि विदुषोऽपि तथा रूढ़ोऽभिनिवेशः

### IAST

svarasavāhī viduṣo 'pi tathārūḍho 'bhiniveśaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The learned, even those who have attained great knowledge, are still driven by a strong desire for life, which sustains itself by its own power; this is attachment.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central philosophical claim of this sutra is that attachment is not a mere external craving but an intrinsic, self-sustaining force rooted in the very nature of existence. By stating that even the most erudite are "tathārūḍho," the sutra underscores that intellectual attainment does not erase the fundamental drive for life; rather, it coexists with it. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that prakṛti, in its dynamic aspect, perpetuates the cycle of desire and attachment, while puruṣa remains a passive witness. The sutra’s reference to the learned as still bound by attachment invites practitioners to recognize that liberation (mokṣa) requires transcending this innate force, not merely intellectual understanding. Dvivedi’s commentary expands on this by highlighting that attachment originates from a primordial desire that persists across lifetimes, thereby framing it as an eternal, innate impulse. This eternal quality makes attachment a formidable obstacle, as it is not a product of current actions but a deep-seated, self-perpetuating tendency. The existential application is clear: practitioners must cultivate awareness of this self-sustaining desire, observe its manifestations, and systematically dismantle it through disciplined practice, thereby breaking the cycle of samsāra.

The sutra’s brevity masks a profound insight: attachment is not an external force imposed upon the individual but a manifestation of the very life-force (svarasa) that animates all beings. By identifying the learned as still "tathārūḍho," the sutra reminds us that knowledge alone is insufficient; one must also confront the subtle, internalized drives that keep the soul tethered to the material world. This perspective resonates with the broader Yoga canon, where the ultimate goal is to achieve samādhi—an absorption that transcends both desire and ignorance. Hence, the sutra serves as a call to recognize and neutralize the self-sustaining nature of attachment, paving the way for true liberation.

In practice, this means cultivating a mindful detachment that observes the arising of desire without attachment to its outcome, thereby weakening its self-sustaining power.

### Key Terms

**svarasa (स्वरस्)** — etymology: ‘life-force’ -> Sāṃkhya: the animating principle of prakṛti; default translation ‘life’ misses its dynamic, sustaining aspect

**viduṣa (विदुषः)** — etymology: ‘learned one’ -> Sāṃkhya: one who has attained knowledge of puruṣa and prakṛti; default translation ‘scholar’ omits the spiritual attainment

**tathārūḍha (तथा रूढ़ः)** — etymology: ‘like a rock’ -> Yoga: denotes steadfastness; default translation ‘firm’ fails to convey the immovable, enduring quality of attachment

**abhinivesha (अभिनिवेशः)** — etymology: ‘not to be abandoned’ -> Sāṃkhya: the persistent, self-sustaining drive; default translation ‘attachment’ overlooks its self-perpetuating nature

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.9:** Structural parallelism: both sutras use a single clause to encapsulate a complex doctrine, mirroring the concise aphoristic style of the Yoga canon
*Divergence:* Unlike later commentarial expansions, this sutra does not invoke the five kleshas explicitly, focusing instead on the root cause of attachment

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2:** Both texts identify prakṛti’s dynamic aspect as the source of desire, aligning with the notion of a self-sustaining force
*Divergence:* The Sāṃkhya text frames it as a natural law, whereas the Yoga sutra presents it as a personal, experiential phenomenon

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 2.9:** Dvivedi expands on the sutra by linking attachment to past-life experiences, echoing the cyclical view of samsāra
*Divergence:* Dvivedi introduces an ontological layer (past lives) absent in the succinct sutra, thereby enriching but also complicating the original claim

**Yoga Vasistha, Chapter 5:** Both texts discuss the inescapability of attachment even among the enlightened
*Divergence:* The Vasistha elaborates with narrative examples, whereas the sutra remains a terse statement

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the arising of your own desire for continued life and consciously let it pass without clinging, so that its self-sustaining power weakens.

---

## Suppressing Subtle Distractions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.10 (Sādhana Pāda)

These, the subtile ones, must be suppressed by

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ते प्रतिप्रसवहेयाः सूक्ष्माः

### IAST

te pratiprasavaheyāḥ sūkṣmāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

These subtle distractions must be suppressed.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the most insidious disturbances are the subtle ones, and that their suppression is the primary task of the practitioner. The claim is that subtle vṛtti—those impressions that lie dormant in the mind—require a method that is itself contrary to their nature. This is a contested move: while the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga school traditionally treats vṛtti as a function of prakṛti, the sutra insists that the practitioner must actively negate these impressions rather than merely observe them. The commentary aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that the mind (citta) is a manifestation of prakṛti, yet it diverges by prescribing a proactive technique—merging the mind into the pure sense of being (īśvara) in prakṛti—rather than passive contemplation. The existential implication is that the subtle distractions are the root of ignorance; by suppressing them, one restores the mind’s natural clarity, enabling samādhi. This aligns with the broader Yoga aim of liberating the puruṣa from prakṛti’s bondage. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound methodological shift: it moves from passive awareness to active negation, thereby offering a concrete practice for the yogi to reclaim the mind’s original state.

### Key Terms

**pratiprasavaheyāḥ (प्रतिप्रसवहेयाः)** — prati- (against) + prasava (origin, emanation) + -heyāḥ (possessive suffix). Traditionally means ‘those that arise in opposition to the original emanation’. Here it denotes subtle vṛtti that arise counter to the natural state of the mind.

**sūkṣmāḥ (सूक्ष्माः)** — from sūkṣma ‘fine, subtle’. In Yoga, it refers to the subtle impressions (vṛtti) that are not immediately perceptible but influence cognition. The sutra’s use underscores their hidden potency.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.9:** Both sutras identify subtle vṛtti as the primary obstacle; 2.9 lists the types, while 2.10 prescribes suppression.
*Divergence:* 2.9 merely enumerates, whereas 2.10 prescribes a contrary course—merging the mind—highlighting a shift from diagnosis to prescription.

**Yoga Sūtra 2.11:** 2.11 speaks of ‘gross’ vṛtti that are overtly manifest; 2.10 focuses on the subtle ones.
*Divergence:* The structural homology lies in the sequence (subtle, gross, then suppression), but 2.11’s emphasis on external manifestations contrasts with 2.10’s internal negation.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2:** Both treat vṛtti as arising from prakṛti; the Karika explains the causal chain.
*Divergence:* The Karika offers a purely theoretical account, whereas the sutra demands an active yogic intervention.

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on YS 2.10:** Bhaṭṭa elaborates the method of merging the mind into īśvara.
*Divergence:* While Bhaṭṭa’s text is more detailed, the sutra’s terse wording leaves the method implicit, inviting personal interpretation.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now actively negate the subtle distractions by merging your mind into the pure sense of being, thereby suppressing them.

---

## Suppressing Transformations
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.11 (Sādhana Pāda)

Their transformations are to be suppressed by

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ध्यानहेयस् तद्वृत्तयाḥ

### IAST

dhyānaheyās tadvṛttayaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You suppress the transformations of the mind by meditation.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the mind’s habitual modifications—its vṛtti—must be quelled through the practice of dhyāna. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, vṛtti refers to the fluctuations that obscure the true nature of puruṣa. The verse presupposes that these fluctuations arise from the thinking principle (citta) and that their suppression is essential for the realization of samādhi. The contested move here is the identification of ‘dhyāna’ with a direct means of suppression, rather than a passive state of absorption. Dvivedi’s commentary emphasizes the bodily anchor of meditation, suggesting that the practitioner must actively engage the body to counter the gross distractions. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that prakṛti’s transformations are mediated by the mind, and that only through disciplined practice can one achieve the stillness required for the discernment of puruṣa. Existentially, the verse invites the practitioner to confront the restless mind and to employ meditation as a disciplined tool, thereby transforming the habitual patterns that bind one to ignorance. The implication is that without this suppression, the practitioner remains entangled in the cycle of desire and aversion, unable to perceive the unchanging awareness that is puruṣa.

### Key Terms

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘to turn, to change’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: mental fluctuations that obscure puruṣa -> default translation ‘fluctuation’ misses the dynamic, cyclical nature of the mind’s activity.

**dhyāna (ध्यान)** — etymology: ‘to look at, to observe’ -> Yoga tradition: a sustained, focused meditation that suspends vṛtti -> default translation ‘meditation’ omits its active, suppressive function.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.28:** Both verses use a single verb to denote the suppression of vṛtti (yāyāśa in 1.28, dhyānaheyās in 2.11).
*Divergence:* In 1.28 the suppression is achieved by the practice of yāyāśa (sustained attention), whereas in 2.11 the method is specifically dhyāna, indicating a shift from general attention to deeper absorption.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.5:** The notion that transformations of prakṛti are to be suppressed aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that puruṣa must remain unaltered.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya focuses on the conceptual suppression of prakṛti’s activity, while the Yoga verse emphasizes the bodily and experiential method of dhyāna.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You suppress the mind’s fluctuations by engaging in focused meditation.

---

## Root of Distractions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.12 (Sādhana Pāda)

The impressions of works have their root in distractions, and are experienced in the seen or the unseen.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

क्लेशमूलः कर्माशयो द्रष्टादृष्टजन्मवेदनीयः

### IAST

kleśamūlaḥ karmāśayo dṛṣṭādṛṣṭajanmavedanīyaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The root of afflictions is the work’s impressions, which are experienced as either seen or unseen.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that afflictions arise from the impressions of work (karma) establishes a causal chain that is central to both Yoga and Sāṃkhya. In this passage, the term *kleśa* is not merely a psychological pain but a manifestation of *pratītyāśaya*—the latent impressions that persist in *sanchita* karma. The contested move is the identification of *dṛṣṭādṛṣṭa* (seen and unseen) as the two modes of experience. While Sāṃkhya traditionally treats *dṛṣṭa* as the realm of the visible and *adṛṣṭa* as the invisible, the Yoga school expands this to include the experiential field of *citta* and the subtle *pratyakṣa* of *samādhi*. The implication is that the same impressions can be realized in the immediate present (*dṛṣṭa*) or in future incarnations (*adṛṣṭa*), thereby linking the temporal with the eternal. Dvivedi’s commentary underscores this by noting that when distractions (*kleśa*) are suppressed, the usual causal chain of karma is interrupted, allowing liberation. This resonates with the Yoga doctrine of *pratyāhāra*—withdrawal of the senses—whereby the mind is freed from the *vṛtti* that perpetuates *kleśa*. The existential application is clear: by recognizing the root of afflictions in the impressions of work, one can consciously alter the *karma* one generates, thereby steering the future experience from suffering to bliss. The passage also hints at the *pratītyāśaya* of *sādhana*—the practice itself becomes a vehicle for transforming *kleśa* into *sukha* through disciplined action and mindful awareness.

### Key Terms

**kleśa (क्लेश)** — root of afflictions -> Sāṃkhya: latent impressions; Yoga: source of suffering

**karma (कर्म)** — work/act -> Sāṃkhya: causal action; Yoga: means of purification

**dṛṣṭa (दृष्ट)** — seen -> Sāṃkhya: visible realm; Yoga: experiential field of citta

**adṛṣṭa (अदृष्ट)** — unseen -> Sāṃkhya: invisible realm; Yoga: future or subtle experience

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.12:** Both Yoga and Sāṃkhya treat *kleśa* as arising from *karma* impressions, linking affliction to action.
*Divergence:* Yoga emphasizes experiential realization (*dṛṣṭa*/*adṛṣṭa*) as a means to transcend, whereas Sāṃkhya focuses on the metaphysical persistence of *sanchita* karma.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.1:** Afflictions (*kleśa*) are identified as manifestations of *pratītyāśaya*.
*Divergence:* Yoga expands the notion to include *citta* and *samādhi* as sites of transformation.

**Dvivedi Commentary on 2.12:** Highlights suppression of distractions to interrupt karma’s causal chain.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s view is more procedural, whereas Yoga presents *pratyāhāra* as a meditative withdrawal.

**Yoga Sūtra 1.33:** Both texts link *vṛtti* (mental fluctuations) to *kleśa*.
*Divergence:* Yoga treats *vṛtti* as a target for *pratyāhāra*, while Sāṃkhya sees it as a manifestation of *prakṛti*.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each action you perform, noting whether its impression feels like a seen or unseen influence, and consciously redirect your attention to the present moment to reduce the root of afflictions.

---

## Root, Fruition, and Karma
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.13 (Sādhana Pāda)

The root being there, their fruition (consists

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सति मूल तद्विपाको जत्यायुर्भोगाः

### IAST

sati mūle tadvipāko jātyāyurbhogāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The root exists; its fruition follows the karma, consisting of class, life, and experience.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the presence of the root (the inherent nature of the soul) guarantees the fruition of karma, which manifests in three interrelated realms: class (samskara‑based social rank), life (duration of existence), and experience (the qualitative nature of sensations). This triadic structure is a hallmark of the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga synthesis, where prakṛti’s three gunas produce a spectrum of experiences that are ultimately bound to puruṣa’s ignorance. The claim that the root itself is the guarantor of fruition challenges the notion that karma alone determines outcomes; instead, the root’s inherent nature (the unchanging puruṣa) is the underlying substrate that receives the karmic imprint. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on “class” as a multi‑layered concept—spanning birth, rank, and order—mirrors the Sāṃkhya idea of vyavahāra (social function) as a manifestation of karma. This insight invites practitioners to recognize that their social roles, longevity, and sensory content are not random but are conditioned by their past actions, yet they are also mediated by the unaltered consciousness that observes them. In existential terms, this means that while one cannot escape the karmic consequences, one can cultivate awareness (citta‑pratibimba) to see the root’s presence and thereby reduce the binding effect of the fruitions. The passage therefore serves as a reminder that the root’s continuity is the key to transcending the threefold fruition, aligning with the ultimate yogic aim of liberation (moksha).

### Key Terms

**mūla (मूल)** — root -> the inherent nature of puruṣa; default translation ‘root’ misses its ontological role as the unchanging substratum of all experience

**tadvipāka (तद्विपाकः)** — fruition -> the resultant effect of karma; tradition sees it as the conditioned manifestation of prakṛti, not merely a passive outcome

**jatyāyur (जत्यायु)** — class -> social rank, birth, and order; tradition interprets it as the karmic imprint on societal function, beyond mere birth order

**bhogāḥ (भोगाः)** — experience -> the qualitative aspect of sensations; tradition links it to the three gunas shaping subjective experience

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga‑Sūtra 2.13:** The triadic structure (class, life, experience) mirrors the Sāṃkhya tri‑gunic manifestation of prakṛti, where each gunā produces a distinct experiential domain.
*Divergence:* While Sāṃkhya treats the gunas as independent, the Sūtra unites them under the single fruition of karma, suggesting a more integrated causal chain

**Bāṇāyaṇa, Vāg‑Sūtra 2.13:** Bāṇāyaṇa’s commentary emphasizes the root’s role as the guarantor of fruition, paralleling the Sūtra’s assertion of the root’s primacy.
*Divergence:* Bāṇāyaṇa expands on the social implications of class, whereas the Sūtra remains concise, focusing on the causal relationship

**Dvivedi, Commentary on Yoga‑Sūtra 2.13:** Dvivedi’s elaboration on class as rank, birth, and order echoes the Sūtra’s terse mention of ‘class’ as a multi‑layered concept.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi introduces the idea of ‘orders and genera’ which the Sūtra does not explicitly mention, thereby broadening the interpretive horizon

**Śaṅkara, Bṛhad‑Bhaṣya 4.1.1:** Śaṅkara’s discussion of the root (mūla) as the substratum of consciousness parallels the Sūtra’s claim of the root’s existence ensuring fruition.
*Divergence:* Śaṅkara frames the root within a metaphysical ontology of puruṣa‑prakṛti dualism, whereas the Sūtra presents it as a pragmatic causal principle

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the present moment, noting how your current social role, lifespan, and sensory experiences are shaped by past actions, and consciously cultivate awareness of the unchanging root that underlies all.

---

## Pleasure as Fruit of Action
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.14 (Sādhana Pāda)

They have pleasure or j)ain for thier fruit

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ते ह्लादपरितापफलाः पुण्यापुण्यहेतुत्वात्

### IAST

te hlādaparitāpaphalāḥ puṇyāpuṇyahetutvāt

### Pratibha Translation

They (the three: class, life, and experience) enjoy pleasure as the fruit of their actions, because the cause is virtue or vice.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Sūtra asserts that pleasure is not an intrinsic quality of the self but a contingent outcome of the causal chain of action. The three refer to the triad of *kāla* (class), *jīvana* (life), and *anubhūti* (experience) that together constitute the lived reality of the practitioner.
