By stating that pleasure is a *phala* (fruit) of *hetu* (cause), the text foregrounds the causal integrity of the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework: *prakṛti* (nature) produces *vyāsa* (action), which yields *phala* (fruit). The implication for the practitioner is that pleasure is a sign, not a goal; it is a diagnostic of the efficacy of action. This aligns with the Yoga principle that *samādhi* is the cessation of *vṛtti* (mental fluctuations) and that true liberation (*moksha*) is achieved when the *purusha* (consciousness) is disentangled from the *prakṛti*‑generated *phala*. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on virtue or vice as the causal determinant mirrors the Sāṃkhya notion of *gunas* (qualities) shaping the outcome. Thus, the sūtra invites the practitioner to cultivate *dharma* (righteous action) so that the resulting pleasure is a wholesome sign of progress rather than a source of attachment. This existential application underscores that the ultimate aim is not pleasure itself but the insight that pleasure arises from action and can be transcended through disciplined practice.

### Key Terms

**hlādaparitāpa (ह्लादपरिताप)** — etymology: hla (pleasure) + para (beyond) + tapa (heat, austerity) -> beyond-pleasure austerity; tradition meaning: the state of enduring pleasure beyond ordinary desire; default translation misses the nuanced link to austerity and transcendence

**puṇya (पुण्य)** — etymology: puṇ (good) + ya (suffix) -> merit; tradition meaning: accumulated virtue that influences future actions; default translation often renders as ‘goodness’ but omits its causal role as a determinant of *phala*

**hetutva (हेतुत्व)** — etymology: hetu (cause) + tv (nominalizer) -> causality; tradition meaning: the principle that every effect is bound to a cause; default translation as ‘cause’ fails to capture its ontological status as a governing principle in Sāṃkhya

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Bhagavad Gita 2.50:** Both texts assert that the fruit of action is pleasure or pain, and that the self is the experiencer; the structural pattern of ‘cause → effect → experience’ is mirrored.
*Divergence:* The Gita frames the fruit as a result of *karma* performed with detachment, whereas the Yoga Sūtra explicitly links the fruit to the moral quality (virtue or vice) of the action, highlighting a more direct ethical causality

**Yoga Vasistha 1.1:** The Vasistha also describes pleasure as a *phala* of *karmas*, using the same triadic structure of class, life, and experience.
*Divergence:* Unlike the Sūtra, the Vasistha expands the triad to include *samskara* (mental impressions), thereby broadening the causal network beyond the immediate action

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts employ the principle of *hetutva* to explain the emergence of *phala* from *vyāsa* (action).
*Divergence:* The Karika presents *hetutva* as an inherent property of *prakṛti*, whereas the Sūtra treats it as a functional determinant that can be altered through ethical conduct

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize that each action you perform today produces a pleasure or pain that is a direct reflection of its ethical quality; observe this relationship without attachment.

---

## All Is Misery to the Enlightened
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.15 (Sādhana Pāda)

To the enlightened all is misery, on account of consequences, anxiety, and impressions; as also on account of the opposition of the action of qualities.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

परिणामतापसंस्कारदुःखैः गुणवृत्तिविरोधाच्च दुःखम् एव सर्वं विवेकिनः

### IAST

pariṇāmatāpasaṃskāraduḥkhair guṇavṛttivirodhāc ca duḥkham eva sarvaṃ vivekinaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

All things are misery for the enlightened, because of consequences, anxiety, and impressions, and also because of the opposition of the actions of the qualities.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the enlightened, i.e. the one who has attained a sharp discernment of the eternal and the illusory, perceives every phenomenon as a source of misery. This claim is not a mere pessimistic observation but a diagnostic tool: it reveals that the enlightened mind, freed from ignorance, sees the very fabric of experience—its causal chains, its anxieties, its latent impressions—as a web that inevitably leads to suffering. The key insight is that misery is not an external condition but an internal recognition of the inescapable interplay of the three gunas. The enlightened mind, having transcended ignorance, is aware that each action of sattva, rajas, or tamas produces a counter‑action, and that the resultant vṛtti (mental fluctuation) is a source of pain. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, this is a critical step toward samādhi: by recognizing the root of misery, one can detach from the vṛtti and attain a state of equanimity. The Dvivedi commentary elaborates on the four causes—consequences, anxiety, impressions, and opposition of gunas—yet the Pratibha reading emphasizes that the enlightened mind does not merely catalog causes but internalizes the structural pattern of misery. This internalization is the first move toward liberation: by seeing the world as a source of suffering, the practitioner is motivated to seek a state where the mind is no longer bound by vṛtti. The existential application is clear: one must cultivate the same discernment that reveals misery, thereby using it as a catalyst to withdraw from the cycle of craving and aversion. The ultimate goal is to reach a state where the mind, no longer entangled in the vṛtti of gunas, experiences samādhi and thus freedom from all suffering.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: ‘mind’ as the organ of perception and cognition; in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic aspect of consciousness that processes prakṛti; the term’s default translation as ‘mind’ omits its role as the locus of vṛtti and the target of samādhi

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘fluctuation’ or ‘mental modification’; in Yoga, the oscillations of citta that bind the soul; the term’s common translation as ‘thought’ fails to convey its causal power in generating bondage

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: the primal, material reality composed of the three gunas; it is the substrate of all change; translating it simply as ‘nature’ obscures its dynamic, causative nature

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: the highest state of meditative absorption where citta is still but not subject to vṛtti; the term’s literal meaning ‘union’ is insufficient to capture its role as liberation from prakṛti

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.10:** Both 2.10 and 2.15 attribute misery to the interplay of gunas; 2.10 lists the three gunas as sources of suffering, while 2.15 expands to include consequences, anxiety, and impressions
*Divergence:* 2.10 focuses on the inherent nature of gunas, whereas 2.15 introduces external factors (consequences, anxiety) that amplify misery

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** The Karika’s assertion that prakṛti’s action produces vṛtti parallels 2.15’s claim that the opposition of gunas causes misery
*Divergence:* Karika presents the process as a deterministic chain, while 2.15 frames it as a recognition that leads to liberation

**Yoga Sūtra 1.3:** Both 1.3 and 2.15 emphasize the role of ignorance (avidyā) in binding the soul to misery
*Divergence:* 1.3 speaks of ignorance as the root of all bondage, whereas 2.15 specifies the mechanisms (consequences, anxiety, impressions) through which ignorance manifests

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each experience you have and note the consequence, anxiety, or impression that follows, then consciously step back to witness it without attachment.

---

## Prevention of Unarrived Misery
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.16 (Sādhana Pāda)

That misery which is not yet come is to be

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

हेयं दुःखं अनागतम्

### IAST

heyaṃ duḥkham anāgatam

### Pratibha Translation

This misery that has not yet arrived is to be prevented.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Sūtra asserts that the foremost object of yogic practice is the preemptive avoidance of suffering that has not yet manifested. By declaring the misery as "heyaṃ duḥkham anāgatam," it foregrounds the proactive stance of Yoga: one does not wait for pain to arise before taking action. This stance aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion of puruṣa’s discernment over prakṛti, yet it diverges from the passive acceptance of suffering found in some other traditions. The Sūtra’s emphasis on prevention invites the practitioner to cultivate vigilance (vṛtti‑nigraha) and discernment (viśeṣaṇa) as the first steps of sādhana. The Dvivedi commentary’s observation that "heya" can mean both ‘to be warded off’ and ‘possible to be warded off’ reinforces the idea that the yogic path is not merely reactive but anticipatory. In practice, this translates to a disciplined observation of one’s thoughts and habits, identifying those that could lead to future distress, and consciously altering them before they manifest. Thus, the Sūtra provides an existential prescription: by preemptively guarding against future misery, one cultivates a state of equanimity that ultimately supports the attainment of samādhi.

### Key Terms

**heya (हेय)** — heya (हेय) – from root ‘he’ meaning ‘to ward off, to prevent’; in this context it denotes the proactive act of preventing misery; the default translation ‘to be warded off’ omits the active preventive nuance

**duḥkha (दुःख)** — duḥkha (दुःख) – from root ‘duḥ’ meaning ‘pain’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the afflictive states of prakṛti; the default ‘misery’ captures the experiential aspect but not the ontological breadth of suffering as a manifestation of ignorance

**anāgata (अनागत)** — anāgata (अनागत) – from ‘an’ (not) + ‘āgata’ (arrived); in Yoga it specifies the future aspect of suffering; the common translation ‘not yet arrived’ fails to emphasize its potentiality and the urgency of preemptive action

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.16:** Structural homology: the Sūtra presents a negated future event (misery) as the object of action (preventing), mirroring the later Sūtra 2.20 where suffering is to be transformed; both use a future-tense predicate to establish an anticipatory practice
*Divergence:* Unlike 2.20, which focuses on transformation after occurrence, 2.16 insists on prevention before occurrence, highlighting a distinct strategic priority within the Sādhana Pāda

**Bhaṭṭa, Yogabhāṣā 1.1:** Both texts emphasize the importance of preemptive control over the mind; Bhaṭṭa speaks of ‘prevention of afflictions’ in the context of meditation, paralleling 2.16’s preventive stance
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s approach is more meditative, whereas 2.16 is prescriptive, underscoring the procedural aspect of Yoga practice rather than contemplative insight

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts and habits now, and consciously alter any that could lead to future suffering.

---

## Seer and Sight: The Cause
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.17 (Sādhana Pāda)

The cause of that which is to be warded off

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

द्रष्टृदृश्ययोः संयोगः हेयहेतुः

### IAST

draṣṭṛdṛśyayoḥ saṃyogo heyahetuḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The joining of the seer and the seen is the cause of that which is to be warded off.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra makes a decisive claim: misfortune arises from the misidentification of the seer (puruṣa) with the seen (dṛśya). This is the contested move that elevates a simple epistemic error to an ontological misalignment. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, the seer is the pure, unchanging witness, while the seen is the ever‑changing manifold of prakṛti. When the two are fused, the seer becomes entangled in the fluctuations of prakṛti, and the illusion of a single, unified reality takes root. The sūtra therefore points to the root of all misery as the very act of *saṃyoga*—the union of puruṣa and dṛśya. This aligns with Patanjali’s later discussion of *vṛtti* as the source of suffering, yet it diverges by foregrounding the *seeker‑seen* relationship rather than the mind’s fluctuations. Dvivedi’s commentary reinforces this by noting that the junction of seer and sight is the cause of all misery, and that bliss resides only in the pure seer. The existential application is clear: by cultivating a clear distinction between awareness and its objects, one can prevent the causal chain that leads to suffering. This sūtra therefore invites a practice of continual self‑inspection, ensuring that the observer remains distinct from the observed, thereby averting the very causes of misery it identifies.

### Key Terms

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — purusha (Sanskrit) – ‘the self’; in Sankhya, the eternal, conscious principle; the term’s literal meaning ‘person’ is insufficient, as it omits the transcendental, unchanging aspect that is the true subject of experience.

**dṛśya (दृश्य)** — dṛśya (Sanskrit) – ‘the seen’ or ‘object of perception’; etymologically ‘that which is seen’; in Yoga it denotes the entire perceptual field, including senses and mental images, not merely external objects.

**saṃyoga (संयोग)** — saṃyoga (Sanskrit) – ‘union’ or ‘joining’; traditionally refers to the causal binding of puruṣa and prakṛti; here it signifies the ontological conflation that produces suffering.

**hetu (हेतु)** — hetu (Sanskrit) – ‘cause’ or ‘agent’; in Patanjali’s grammar it denotes the cause of a verb’s action; in this sūtra it marks the causal status of the seer‑seen union.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.18:** Both 2.17 and 2.18 identify a root cause of misery—2.17 cites the seer‑seen union, 2.18 cites the mind’s fluctuations—showing structural homology in the pattern of causal analysis.
*Divergence:* While 2.17 locates the cause in the ontological misidentification of puruṣa and dṛśya, 2.18 attributes it to the mental *vṛtti*; the former is more metaphysical, the latter more psychological, illustrating a productive shift from ontology to epistemology.

**Sāṃkhya Tattva‑bhāṣya, Chapter 4:** The text’s discussion of *saṃyoga* as the binding of puruṣa and prakṛti mirrors the sūtra’s claim that the seer‑seen union is causal.
*Divergence:* Unlike the Tattva‑bhāṣya, which treats *saṃyoga* as a permanent binding, the sūtra presents it as a temporary, misidentifying act that can be undone through practice.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each moment, consciously distinguishing your awareness from the objects you perceive, and refrain from identifying the two.

---

## Sight as Illuminated Action
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.18 (Sādhana Pāda)

The sight consists of the elements and the organs, is of the nature of illumination, action, and position, and is of use so far as experience and absolu-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रकाशक्रियास्थितिशीलं भूतेंद्रियात्मकम् भोगापवर्गार्थं दर्श्यम्

### IAST

prakāśakriyāsthitiśīlaṃ bhūtendriyātmakaṃ bhogāpavargārthaṃ dṛśyam

### Pratibha Translation

The sight is composed of the elements and the organs; it is of the nature of illumination, action, and position; it is useful for experience and for the attainment of ultimate liberation.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that sight is a composite of prakṛti’s constituents—both the five gross elements (bhūta) and the eleven organs (āṅgā) including the mind (buddhi) and the sense‑organ (āliṅkara). It claims that this composite is of three natures: illumination (prakāśa), action (kriyā), and mere position (sthiti). The claim is that these three qualities are the fundamental modes through which prakṛti manifests, and that sight, as a manifestation, inherits all three. This is a contested move in the Yoga tradition because it elevates the sensory apparatus to a status that is simultaneously part of prakṛti and yet a means for puruṣa to attain liberation. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga synthesis, the organs are seen as instruments of prakṛti, but the ultimate goal is to transcend all such instruments. The doctrine here is that the very act of seeing is a dynamic process (action) that illuminates the mind, yet it remains a static position (sthiti) in the sense that it is a fixed mode of perception. The practical implication is that one must cultivate awareness of the threefold nature of sensory experience: recognizing that what is seen is not merely a passive image but an active, illuminating process that can either bind or liberate. The Dvivedi commentary reinforces this by linking the three gunas—sattva (illumination), rajas (action), tamas (position)—to the sensory experience, thereby providing a metaphysical grounding for the claim. Thus, the passage invites the practitioner to observe that the very act of seeing is a tool of evolution, and that by mastering this tool—by seeing with clarity, intention, and detachment—one can use it as a means to transcend prakṛti and realize puruṣa.

### Key Terms

**prakāśa (प्रकाश)** — ‘illumination’ – from prakāśa ‘light’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the sattvic quality of clarity, but here it is applied to the sensory organ itself, suggesting that the organ itself is a source of illumination rather than merely a receiver of it.

**kriyā (क्रिया)** — ‘action’ – from kriyā ‘to act’; traditionally denotes the dynamic aspect of prakṛti, but the sūtra attributes this dynamic to the organ, implying that the organ is an active agent.

**sthiti (स्थिति)** — ‘position’ – from sthiti ‘standing’; usually refers to the static state of prakṛti, yet here it is used to describe the organ’s inherent, unchanging aspect.

**buddhi (बुद्धि)** — ‘intellect’ – from buddhi ‘to know’; in Sāṃkhya it is a subtle organ of perception, but the sūtra treats it as part of the sensory apparatus, blurring the line between instrument and content.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.18:** The structure ‘X is of the nature of Y, Z, and W’ mirrors the triadic formulation in 1.2 ‘prakṛti is of the nature of sattva, rajas, tamas’
*Divergence:* While 1.2 attributes the triad to prakṛti itself, 2.18 attributes it to the organ of sight, thereby shifting the locus of the triad from the universal principle to a specific instrument.

**Bhaṭṭa, Yogaśāstra, 2.18:** Bhaṭṭa expands the triad to include ‘sight’ as a composite of elements and organs, echoing the sūtra’s claim
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s elaboration introduces the notion of ‘usefulness for experience’, which the original sūtra omits, thereby adding an epistemic dimension.

**Dvivedi, Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 2.18:** Dvivedi explicitly links the triad to the gunas, reinforcing the sūtra’s claim
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary interprets the triad as a metaphysical description of prakṛti’s evolution, whereas the sūtra presents it as a descriptive taxonomy of the organ itself.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the sight you are using now; note how it simultaneously illuminates your awareness, acts to bring objects into perception, and remains in a fixed position, and use this observation to cultivate mindful awareness of each of these qualities.

---

## Stages of the Gunas
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.19 (Sādhana Pāda)

The stages of the Gunas are the defined

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

विशेषविशेषलिङ्गामात्रालिङ्गानि गुणपरवाणि

### IAST

viśeṣāviśeṣaliṅgamātrāliṅgāni guṇaparvāṇi

### Pratibha Translation

The stages of the Gunas are the defined.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the Gunas are not merely abstract qualities but exist in distinct, progressive stages. This claim situates the Gunas within a developmental framework, challenging the view that they are static attributes of prakṛti. The contested move is the identification of four specific stages—gross, subtle, intelligent, and indissoluble—mirroring the fourfold classification of prakṛti in the Sāṃkhya system. By aligning the Gunas with these stages, the text bridges the epistemological gap between the subtle mind (citta) and the gross material world, thereby providing a practical roadmap for yogic sādhanā. In the Yoga tradition, this progression is essential for the practitioner’s gradual purification of vṛtti, enabling the mind to transcend the binding effects of the Gunas and approach samādhi. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on “the undefined, the dissoluble, and the indissoluble” underscores the dynamic nature of the Gunas, suggesting that each stage can be transcended through disciplined practice. This resonates with the Sāṃkhya notion that puruṣa’s liberation is achieved by discerning its distinctness from the evolving prakṛti. Thus, the sūtra offers an existential application: by recognizing the Gunas’ staged nature, one can systematically dismantle their influence, paving the way for the realization of puruṣa’s pure consciousness.

### Key Terms

**guna (गुण)** — ‘quality’ in Sanskrit; in Sāṃkhya, the three fundamental qualities—sattva, rajas, tamas—constitute prakṛti; the term here denotes the dynamic, evolving manifestations of these qualities, not a static attribute

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — ‘nature’ or ‘material reality’; in Sāṃkhya, the tri‑fold material world; here it is the substrate that undergoes the four stages, highlighting its mutable character

**citta (चित्त)** — ‘mind’ or ‘consciousness’; in Yoga, the organ of perception and cognition; its purification is the goal of sādhanā, which this sūtra supports by delineating the stages that must be transcended

**samādhi (समाधि)** — the highest meditative absorption; in Yoga, the state where citta is free from vṛtti; the sūtra’s staging of Gunas provides the preparatory steps toward achieving samādhi

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.19:** identifies four stages of Gunas, paralleling the fourfold classification of prakṛti in Sāṃkhya (gross, subtle, intelligent, indissoluble)
*Divergence:* Yoga emphasizes the experiential dissolution of these stages through practice, whereas Sāṃkhya presents them as inherent properties of prakṛti

**Yoga Sūtra 2.20:** continues the theme of stages, linking them to the process of purification of vṛtti
*Divergence:* Yoga’s focus on the mind’s purification contrasts with Sāṃkhya’s analytic description of prakṛti’s evolution

**Bhāṣya of Dvivedi, 2.19:** mirrors the Yoga Sūtra’s stages with the terms ‘undefined, dissoluble, indissoluble’
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s wording abstracts the stages, whereas the Yoga Sūtra provides concrete descriptors (gross, subtle, intelligent, indissoluble)

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize the current stage of your mind’s attachment to each guna and consciously shift toward the next, more refined stage.

---

## Pure Gnosis of the Seer
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.20 (Sādhana Pāda)

The seer is pure gnosis, and though pure

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

द्रष्टा दृशिमात्रः शुद्धोऽपि प्रत्ययानुपश्यः

### IAST

draṣṭā dṛśimātraḥ śuddho 'pi pratyayānupaśyaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The seer is pure gnosis, and though pure, he observes only the appearance of the object.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the seer (puruṣa) is inherently pure gnosis, yet his act of seeing is limited to the appearance of the object, mediated by intellect. This claim foregrounds the distinction between the unchanging self and the changing world, a core Sāṃkhya dualism. The contested move is the assertion that the seer’s observation is only of appearances, not of the object itself; Dvivedi interprets this as seeing through intellect, but the Pratibha reading emphasizes that the seer’s pure awareness does not participate in the evolutionary process of prakṛti. In the Yoga tradition, this aligns with the idea that samādhi is the state where the mind’s vṛtti is still, yet the seer remains untouched by it. The existential application is that one may cultivate a state of pure awareness that observes phenomena without being altered by them, thereby achieving liberation. The commentary integrates Dvivedi’s note that the seer “appears to cognise through the intellect” by suggesting that the intellect is merely a vehicle, not the locus of true knowing. Thus, the seer’s pure gnosis is a constant, unchanging witness, while the intellect merely facilitates the appearance of knowing.

### Key Terms

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa: from *pur* ‘to be’ + *-ṣa* ‘having’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the conscious principle, distinct from prakṛti; the translation ‘seer’ captures the role but omits the ontological distinction between self and nature.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: from *pra* ‘before’ + *kṛti* ‘made’; in Sāṃkhya it is the evolving, material principle; the translation ‘nature’ misses its dynamic, generative aspect.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: from *vṛ* ‘to turn’ + *tti* ‘action’; denotes the fluctuations of mind; the translation ‘appearance’ omits its psychological nuance.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: from *sam* ‘together’ + *ā* ‘to’ + *dhi* ‘to hold’; denotes the state of meditative absorption where vṛtti is still; the translation ‘concentration’ fails to convey its transcendental quality.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.20:** Both the Yoga Sūtra and the Bhagavad Gītā (2.50) describe the seer as pure and unaltered by the world, yet the Gītā emphasizes devotion (bhakti) as the path to seeing the divine, whereas the Sūtra focuses on self‑observation.
*Divergence:* The Gītā introduces the element of surrender to īśvara, which the Sūtra omits, thereby narrowing the focus to internal witnessing.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.3:** Both texts distinguish puruṣa and prakṛti, but the Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa elaborates the evolutionary process of prakṛti, whereas the Sūtra merely states that the seer does not evolve.
*Divergence:* The Sūtra’s brevity leaves the mechanism of evolution implicit, making it more accessible to practitioners.

**Yoga Sūtra 2.20:** The structure ‘seer is pure, yet observes only appearance’ mirrors the Patanjali’s 6.1 ‘the mind is the source of all suffering’, highlighting the role of perception.
*Divergence:* Unlike Patanjali, the Sūtra does not attribute suffering to the mind, but rather to its vṛtti, thereby providing a more neutral stance.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the world as it appears, noting that your awareness remains unchanged and pure, regardless of the objects you perceive.

---

## The Sight’s Being
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.21 (Sādhana Pāda)

The being of the sight is for him.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तदर्थं एव दर्श्यस्यात्मा

### IAST

tadartha eva dṛśyasyātmā

### Pratibha Translation

The being of the sight is for him.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the very essence of perception—its being—exists for the seer. This is not a mystical claim but a statement about the functional relationship between prakṛti (the material world) and puruṣa (the conscious witness). In Sāṃkhya, prakṛti acts not as an autonomous agent but as a vehicle that presents itself to puruṣa, whose awareness is the only true observer. The sūtra therefore positions puruṣa as the indispensable substratum that gives meaning to all appearances. The term *atma* here is best understood as the inherent nature of the sight, i.e., the way in which the eye’s activity is constituted. The sūtra’s brevity masks a profound ontological claim: without puruṣa, the ‘being’ of prakṛti would lack any referent. This mirrors the Sāṃkhya principle that prakṛti’s activity is purposeless unless it is perceived by puruṣa. The sūtra also foreshadows the later yogic insight that the mind’s fluctuations are merely appearances that arise for the purpose of revealing the self. Thus, the sūtra invites practitioners to recognize that every sensory experience is a manifestation designed to point back to the unchanging witness, encouraging a shift from identification with the sensory world to identification with the witness itself.

### Key Terms

**atma (आत्मा)** — ‘self’ in early texts; here denotes the inherent nature of the sight—its being as presented to puruṣa, not an autonomous soul

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — the material nature; in this context the active process of appearance that exists for the purpose of revealing puruṣa

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — the conscious witness; the indispensable substratum that gives referent to prakṛti’s activity

**sight (दर्श)** — the sensory faculty; the means by which prakṛti presents itself to puruṣa

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 2.21:** Both the Yoga Sutra and Sāṃkhya treat prakṛti’s activity as purposeless without puruṣa, underscoring the interdependence of matter and consciousness
*Divergence:* While Sāṃkhya emphasizes the dualism of prakṛti and puruṣa, the Sutra frames the relationship in terms of the ‘being’ of the sight, subtly shifting focus to the experiential aspect rather than ontological distinction

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.1:** The principle that prakṛti’s manifestations are for puruṣa’s awareness is echoed here
*Divergence:* The Sutra uses a more poetic phrasing (‘being of the sight’) that foregrounds the sensory faculty, whereas the Kārikā presents the idea in a more abstract, philosophical tone

**Yoga Sutra 2.18:** Both sutras discuss the function of prakṛti as a vehicle for puruṣa’s awareness
*Divergence:* The earlier sutra speaks of the mind’s fluctuations, while 2.21 focuses specifically on the sight, narrowing the scope to a particular sensory modality

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your gaze now; notice how the world appears only because you are looking, and feel the awareness that makes this appearance possible.

---

## Purushic Continuity Amidst Destruction
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.22 (Sādhana Pāda)

It, though destroyed in the ease of him whose purpose has been fulfilled, is yet not destroyed for it is common to others besides him.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

कृतार्थं प्रतिप्रत्याशा नष्टं अपि नष्टः तदन्यसाधारणत्वात्

### IAST

kṛtārthaṃ prati naṣṭam apy anaṣṭaṃ tadanyasādhāraṇatvāt

### Pratibha Translation

When the purpose is fulfilled, the object of action is destroyed for the doer, yet it remains undestroyed for others; thus the object is ordinarily common to all.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Sūtra asserts that the destruction of an object by a practitioner does not entail its universal annihilation. The contested move is the distinction between the *purūṣa* who has realized the *saṃkhyā* of *prakṛti* and the *purūṣas* who have not. In the Sāṃkhya framework, *prakṛti* is a singular, eternal nature whose manifestations are manifold. The Sūtra resolves the paradox of a single *purūṣa* attaining liberation while *prakṛti* persists for the rest. It does so by positing that the *purūṣa*’s realization renders *prakṛti* inert in his experience, but the inert *prakṛti* continues to act upon the other *purūṣas*. This preserves the integrity of the *saṃkhyā* of *prakṛti* without violating its indestructibility. The Dvivedi commentary echoes this by noting that the *purūṣa*’s cessation of action does not negate *prakṛti*’s universal effects. Existentially, the passage reminds practitioners that liberation is a personal, not a universal, transformation; the world’s continued functioning does not diminish one’s attainment. It encourages a disciplined practice that recognizes the dual reality of personal liberation and collective persistence, thereby fostering a balanced, non-egoistic approach to *saṃkhyā* and *samādhi*.

### Key Terms

**purūṣa (पुरूष)** — purūṣa (Sāṃkhya) – the conscious principle, distinct from prakṛti; default translation ‘person’ omits its metaphysical role as the observer and the locus of liberation.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (Sāṃkhya) – the primal, generative nature, eternal and indestructible; default translation ‘nature’ fails to convey its role as the dynamic substrate of all manifestations.

**saṃkhyā (संख्या)** — saṃkhyā (Sāṃkhya) – the enumeration of elements (purūṣa, prakṛti, and their constituents); default translation ‘count’ overlooks its ontological significance.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (Yoga) – a state of meditative absorption and union; default translation ‘concentration’ misses its transcendental aspect.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.22:** Structural homology with 2.21: both address the fate of objects post-action, using the same grammatical construction (kṛtārthaṃ…apya…nashṭam).
*Divergence:* While 2.21 speaks of the object’s destruction for the doer, 2.22 extends this to the object’s continued existence for others, highlighting a broader communal implication.

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2.10:** Both texts discuss the inertness of *prakṛti* after *purūṣa*’s realization, employing the term *nashṭam* to denote cessation of effect.
*Divergence:* The Sūtra frames this in the context of personal liberation, whereas the Kārikā presents it as a metaphysical principle affecting all *purūṣas*.

**Dvivedi Commentary 2.22:** Dvivedi parallels the Sūtra’s claim with the idea that *prakṛti* remains active for other *purūṣas*.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi introduces the notion of *ptsrums* (inherent qualities) to explain multiplicity, a nuance absent in the terse Sūtra.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Feel the object of your current action as it dissolves for you, yet recognize it remains present for others; let this awareness guide you to act with detached compassion.

---

## Union of Seer and Sight
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.23 (Sādhana Pāda)

J unction is the cause of the self-recognition

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्वस्वामीशक्त्योः स्वरूपोपालभ्धिहेतुः संयोगः

### IAST

svasvāmiśaktyoḥ svarūpopalabdhihetuḥ saṃyogaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The union of the self‑recognizing power and the self‑reached form is the cause of the conjunction.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central philosophical claim of this verse is that the very act of self‑recognition—what the sūtra calls the ‘union of the self‑recognizing power and the self‑reached form’—is itself the causal principle that brings about all experience. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, the seer (puruṣa) and the seen (prakṛti) are not merely co‑existing; their inseparable junction is the engine of the entire experiential world. This is a contested move: some commentators, following the Dvivedi tradition, read the phrase as a warning against the ‘junction to be warded off,’ whereas the Pratibha reading interprets it positively as the very mechanism that enables mokṣa. The existential import is that by understanding and consciously aligning with this union, one can transform the ordinary flow of vṛtti (mental modifications) into a sustained state of samādhi (concentration), thereby realizing puruṣa’s true nature. The commentary underscores that the seer’s power is not an external force but an inner gnosis that, when harmonized with prakṛti’s power, produces blissful, self‑knowing experience. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that puruṣa’s transcendence is achieved through the dispassionate observation of prakṛti’s manifestations, yet here the emphasis is on the dynamic interplay that initiates that observation.

Key to the sūtra is the term ‘svasvāmiśaktyoḥ,’ which literally means ‘the power that recognizes itself.’ It is this self‑recognizing power that, when coupled with the form it has attained (svarūpa), creates the conjunction that is the root of all experience. The sūtra thus bridges the epistemic gap between the observer and the observed, suggesting that the very act of recognition is the first step toward liberation.

Key terms:

### Key Terms

**svasvāmiśaktyoḥ (स्वस्वामीशक्त्योः)** — self‑recognizing power -> in Sāṃkhya, puruṣa’s awareness; in Yoga, the inner witness; the term highlights that the power itself is aware of itself, a nuance lost in simple translations

**svarūpa (स्वरूप)** — self‑reached form -> the manifested aspect of puruṣa; contrasts with prakṛti; indicates that the seer has attained a form that is still distinct from prakṛti

**saṃyogaḥ (संयोगः)** — union/conjunction -> the causal nexus between puruṣa and prakṛti; in Yoga, the dynamic interaction that generates experience; in Sāṃkhya, the moment of interaction that initiates change

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.23:** Explicit identification of the causal union (saṃyogaḥ) as the root of all experience, mirroring the Sāṃkhya idea of puruṣa‑prakṛti interaction
*Divergence:* Unlike the Sāṃkhya emphasis on the passive observation of prakṛti, the Yoga text presents the union as an active, self‑recognizing power that initiates experience

**Bhāṣya of Dvivedi, 2.23:** Interprets the union as a potential source of wrong knowledge, warning the practitioner
*Divergence:* The Pratibha reading reframes it positively, seeing the same union as the gateway to mokṣa, thus shifting the ethical orientation

**Yoga Sūtra 2.17:** Earlier verse warns against the ‘junction to be warded off,’ suggesting a dual nature of saṃyoga
*Divergence:* The current verse resolves this tension by clarifying that the junction is indeed the cause of experience, thereby reconciling the warning with the affirmative claim

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Defines puruṣa as the witness and prakṛti as the seen, establishing the framework for saṃyoga
*Divergence:* Yoga expands the framework by attributing self‑recognition to the power itself, adding a layer of agency absent in the Sāṃkhya Karika

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize the moment when your awareness (purusha) meets the object of perception (prakriti); consciously observe this union as the source of your experience, and let this observation anchor your mind in present‑moment awareness.

---

## Cause of Ignorance
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.24 (Sādhana Pāda)

Its cause is ignorance.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तस्य हेतुं अविद्या

### IAST

tasya hetur avidyā

### Pratibha Translation

Its cause is ignorance.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that ignorance (avidyā) is the proximate cause of the seer’s misidentification of the self. This claim foregrounds a causal chain that begins with the *hetu*—the immediate reason for the misapprehension—and terminates in the ontological misrecognition that fuels the *vṛtti* of the mind. The move is contested because it seems to reduce the complex process of *sādhana* to a single causal factor, whereas other commentators emphasize the role of *citta* or *prakṛti* as mediators. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, *avidyā* is not merely a lack of knowledge but a fundamental misapprehension of the distinction between *puruṣa* (the observer) and *prakṛti* (the observed). By locating the cause in *avidyā*, the sutra invites practitioners to confront the root of their delusion directly, rather than merely managing its symptoms. This existential application is evident in the practice of *pratyāhāra* (withdrawal of the senses) and *dharana* (concentration) as means to purify the *citta* from the influence of *avidyā*. The Dvivedi commentary’s brief note that “its, i.e., of the junction of the seer and the sight” underscores the sutra’s focus on the interface where *avidyā* manifests, thereby aligning the sutra with the broader Yoga aim of *samādhi*—the union of *puruṣa* with its true nature. Thus, the sutra’s philosophical claim is that the immediate cause of all misidentification is ignorance, and that liberation (*moksha*) requires the systematic dismantling of this ignorance through disciplined practice.

### Key Terms

**hetu (हेतु)** — hetu (causative) → Sāṃkhya: immediate reason; Yoga: proximate cause of misidentification; the term’s use here signals a causal analysis rather than a metaphysical principle.

**avidyā (अविद्या)** — avidyā (ignorance) → Sanskrit: lack of knowledge; Sāṃkhya: fundamental misapprehension of puruṣa‑prakṛti distinction; Yoga: root cause of all *vṛtti*; the term’s literal meaning (not knowing) is often lost in translations that render it simply as ‘ignorance’.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.23, 2.24, 2.25:** Structural homology: ‘tasya hetur X’ pattern recurs across three consecutive sutras, linking cause, yoga, and dharma.
*Divergence:* While 2.23 names yoga (the practice) as the cause, 2.24 names ignorance, thereby shifting the causal locus from activity to epistemic condition; 2.25 names dharma, introducing moral dimension.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1, 2:** Both traditions use *avidyā* as the root of misidentification, but Sāṃkhya frames it as a metaphysical ignorance of *puruṣa* and *prakṛti*; Yoga frames it as a practical obstacle to *samādhi*.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize the ignorance that is the cause of your current mental state.

---

## Break of the Junction
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.25 (Sādhana Pāda)

The break of the junction, through the dispersion of ignorance, is the (thing) to be warded off; it is the Kaivalya of the Seer.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तदाभावात् संयोगाभावः हानं तददृशेः काविल्यम्

### IAST

tadabhāvāt saṃyogābhāvo hānaṃ taddṛśeḥ kaivalyam

### Pratibha Translation

The breaking of the junction, through the destruction of ignorance, is that which is seen as Kaivalya.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that liberation (Kaivalya) is the state that appears when the junction between the Seer and the world dissolves. The junction (saṃyogābhāva) is not a physical contact but a misidentification that binds the Seer to the world. When ignorance (avidyā) is dispelled by right knowledge, the Seer recognises that the world is not his own, and the false sense of bondage disappears. The claim is that the very phenomenon that is perceived as liberation is itself the dissolution of the false identification. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, the Seer (puruṣa) is eternally distinct from prakṛti, yet the mind’s vṛtti (mental fluctuations) create a false union. The key philosophical move is that Kaivalya is not a new ontological status but the negation of the misidentification that produces the junction. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine of the two principles: puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter). The Dvivedi commentary emphasizes that the Seer’s belief in bondage is the root of suffering; its dissolution is the realization of the Seer’s true nature. The passage thus offers an existential prescription: by cultivating right knowledge that sees the world as devoid of self‑ownership, one experiences the liberation that is the very absence of the false junction. This is the practical path: to observe the mind’s vṛtti and recognize the Seer’s independence, thereby achieving Kaivalya.

### Key Terms

**saṃyogābhāva (संयोगाभावः)** — ‘union‑state’ – the false identification of puruṣa with prakṛti; default translation ‘junction’ misses the epistemic nature of the misidentification.

**avidyā (अविद्या)** — ‘ignorance’ – the epistemic blindness that sustains the saṃyogābhāva; tradition sees it as the root of all vṛtti.

**kaivalyam (काविल्यम्)** — ‘liberation’ – the state of absolute isolation of puruṣa; default ‘freedom’ omits its ontological negation of the false union.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.23:** Both 2.23 and 2.25 describe the dissolution of the saṃyogābhāva as the route to Kaivalya; the structure is identical: ‘break of X through Y is Z’.
*Divergence:* In 2.23 the focus is on the breaking of the mind’s vṛtti, whereas 2.25 emphasizes the breaking of the junction itself; the latter is more ontological.

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.4:** Sāṃkhya’s statement that puruṣa is never bound mirrors the claim that Kaivalya is the state of no junction.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents this as a metaphysical fact, while the Yoga Sūtra frames it as an experiential result of knowledge.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts; when you notice the mind’s tendency to identify the self with the world, consciously detach that identification, recognizing that you are the Seer, not the object.

---

## Continuous Discrimination
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.26 (Sādhana Pāda)

The means of dissolving is continuous dis-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

विवेकाख्यातिर अविप्लवाः हानोपायः

### IAST

vivekakhyātir aviplavā hānopāyaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The means of dissolution is continuous discrimination.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim here is that true dissolution of ignorance is achieved not by sporadic moments of insight but by a sustained, unbroken practice of discrimination. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, discrimination (viveka) is the faculty that separates the eternal Purusha (the witness) from the mutable Prakṛti (the material world). The sutra asserts that merely having the capacity for discrimination is insufficient; it must become a habitual, living process that does not allow even a fleeting lapse. This insistence on continuity echoes the Sāṃkhya principle that the mind’s vṛtti (mental fluctuations) must be subdued to reveal the unchanging Self. The Dvivedi commentary reinforces this by noting that true discrimination causes all senses to turn inward, perceiving only the soul. The existential implication is that one’s daily life must be structured so that every action, thought, and perception is filtered through this discriminative lens, thereby preventing the re‑entanglement with ignorance. The sutra therefore invites a disciplined, continuous practice that aligns with the Yoga goal of samādhi, the state of complete absorption in Purusha. This disciplined approach is what ultimately dissolves the ignorance that binds the individual to the cycle of experience.

### Key Terms

**viveka (विवेक)** — viveka (discrimination) -> in Sāṃkhya, the discernment between Purusha and Prakṛti; in Yoga, the practice that leads to samādhi; the sutra elevates it to a continuous process rather than a one‑time act.

**aviplava (अविप्लव)** — aviplava (continuous) -> from ‘viplava’ (to flow) with negation; denotes an unbroken flow of discriminative activity; contrasts with intermittent or sporadic practice.

**hāna (हण)** — hāna (dissolution) -> not merely destruction but the removal of ignorance; in Yoga, the dissolution of the false identification of the self with Prakṛti.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.26:** The sutra’s structure mirrors the earlier 2.24, where ‘sampatti’ (wealth) is described as a continuous process; both emphasize continuity as a prerequisite for attainment.
*Divergence:* While 2.24 speaks of accumulating wealth, 2.26 speaks of dissolving ignorance; the shift from accumulation to dissolution reflects a transition from external to internal focus.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.1:** Both texts identify discrimination as the key to separating Purusha from Prakṛti, using similar terminology.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents discrimination as a conceptual understanding, whereas the Yoga sutra demands an active, continuous practice.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You continuously apply discriminative awareness to every thought and sensation, ensuring no moment of ignorance slips through.

---

## Last Stage of Enlightenment
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.27 (Sādhana Pāda)

The enlightenment which is the last

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तस्य सप्तधः प्रान्तभूमिः प्रज्ञा

### IAST

tasya saptadhā prāntabhūmiḥ prajñā

### Pratibha Translation

The enlightenment that is the final stage

### Pratibha Commentary

In this passage the Yoga master declares that the ultimate attainment of Yoga is a singular, final stage of enlightenment, a state that transcends all intermediate experiences. The sūtra is not merely descriptive but prescriptive: it invites the practitioner to recognize that the culmination of disciplined practice is not a series of successive peaks but a single, all‑encompassing realization. The claim is that the last stage is the *prāntabhūmiḥ* of *prajñā*—the ultimate, unqualified awareness that dissolves the *vṛtti* of the mind and the illusionary *prakṛti* that masks *puruṣa*. This is a contested move in the Yoga tradition: some commentators, such as Dvivedi, treat the sevenfold *saptadhā* as a progressive ladder, while others, following the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga synthesis, argue that the final stage is a non‑dual, instantaneous shift. The philosophical implication is that the practice of *yoga* is not about accumulating stages but about aligning the *citta* with the *purusha* so that the *samādhi* becomes a single, unbroken moment of insight. In everyday life, this means that the practitioner should continually test whether their awareness is still bound by *vṛtti* or has slipped into the *prāntabhūmiḥ* of *prajñā*. When the mind’s chatter subsides and the self‑recognition of *purusha* is clear, the practitioner has reached the last stage, and the practice can be considered complete. Thus, the sūtra serves both as a doctrinal statement and a practical checkpoint for the yogic seeker.

### Key Terms

**saptadhā (सप्तधः)** — sapt- ‘seven’ + dhā ‘fold, stage’; in Sūtra 2.27 it denotes the sevenfold nature of the final enlightenment, contrasting with the single, unqualified awareness that traditional translations often miss.

**prāntabhūmiḥ (प्रान्तभूमिः)** — prānta ‘end’ + bhūmi ‘ground, stage’; it signals the ultimate, terminal point of the yogic path, a concept that is sometimes glossed over as merely ‘last stage’.

**prajñā (प्रज्ञा)** — pra ‘before’ + jñā ‘know’; it is the enlightened awareness that transcends *citta* and *vṛtti*, a term whose subtlety is often lost when translated simply as ‘enlightenment’.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.25 – ‘tasyāḥ prāṇāyāmaḥ’:** Both 2.25 and 2.27 use the possessive ‘tasyāḥ’ to refer to the practitioner’s own internal process, linking breath control to the final state of awareness.
*Divergence:* While 2.25 focuses on the physical regulation of *prāṇa*, 2.27 abstracts to the metaphysical culmination, showing a shift from body to mind.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa – ‘puruṣa‑prakṛti‑bheda’:** The idea of a final, non‑dual realization echoes the Sāṃkhya doctrine that *puruṣa* ultimately separates from *prakṛti*.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents this separation as a theoretical distinction, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as an experiential endpoint.

**Dvivedi, ‘Yoga‑Sūtra Commentary’ – ‘saptadhā’ as seven stages:** Dvivedi’s enumeration of seven stages parallels the saptadhā in 2.27, both acknowledging a multi‑layered path.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi treats the stages as progressive, whereas the sūtra presents the last stage as a single, indivisible reality.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice that your present awareness is the final stage of enlightenment, and let this recognition guide each breath and movement in your practice.

---

## Purity, Insight, and Discernment
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.28 (Sādhana Pāda)

yogāṅgānuṣṭhānād aśuddhikṣaye jñānadīptir ā vivekakhyāteḥ

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

योगाङ्गानुश्ठानाद् अशुद्धिक्षये ज्ञानदीप्तिर् आ विवेकाख्यतेः

### IAST

yogāṅgānuṣṭhānād aśuddhikṣaye jñānadīptir ā vivekakhyāteḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You anchor the practice in the body, purify the mind, and discern with clarity.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the very act of grounding the yogic practice in the body is the decisive move that transforms the mind from a state of impurity to one of luminous knowledge, and that this luminous knowledge is then distinguished by clear discrimination. In the Yoga‑Sūtra tradition, the body (kāraṇa) is the locus of the initial sādhanā, and the purifying of the mind (citta‑śuddhi) is the first milestone toward samādhi. The phrase “ā vivekakhyāteḥ” signals that the mind’s illumination is not merely passive; it actively discerns (viveka) between the real and the unreal. This aligns with the Sankhya view that puruṣa, through disciplined practice, can separate itself from prakṛti’s vṛtti. The implication for the practitioner is that without bodily anchoring, the mind remains entangled in vṛtti and cannot achieve the clarity required for true discernment. Thus, the sūtra offers a concrete prescription: begin with the body, cleanse the mind, and cultivate discernment, thereby moving toward the ultimate goal of samādhi. This sequence mirrors the Sankhya epistemology that knowledge (jnana) arises when puruṣa is free from prakṛti’s influence, yet it diverges by emphasizing the body as the necessary starting point, a nuance that is absent in pure Sankhya texts.

### Key Terms

**yoga (योग)** — ‘yoga’ (IAST) derives from ‘yuj’ meaning ‘to join’; in Sankhya it denotes the union of puruṣa and prakṛti, but here it is specifically the bodily practice that initiates purification.

**citta (चित्त)** — ‘citta’ (IAST) means ‘mind’ or ‘consciousness’; Sankhya sees it as the fluctuating aspect of prakṛti, while here it is the target of purification.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — ‘vṛtti’ (IAST) refers to the mental modifications; Sankhya lists eight vṛtti, but the sūtra treats it as the impurity to be removed.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — ‘puruṣa’ (IAST) is the pure consciousness in Sankhya; the sūtra implies that puruṣa can achieve clarity only after the body‑anchored practice purifies citta.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.26:** Both sutras emphasize bodily anchoring (kāraṇa) as the starting point for purification; 2.28 extends this by linking purification directly to discernment.
*Divergence:* While 2.26 speaks of purification alone, 2.28 explicitly introduces the outcome of discernment, highlighting a progression.

**Yoga Sūtra 2.30:** The structure ‘body‑anchor → purification → discernment’ parallels the triad in 2.30 where the mind is purified, discerned, and then liberated.
*Divergence:* 2.30 assumes prior purification, whereas 2.28 presents purification as the immediate consequence of bodily anchoring.

**Sankhya Karika 2.1:** Both texts identify the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) as the obstacle to clarity.
*Divergence:* Sankhya Karika treats vṛtti as inherent to prakṛti, while 2.28 treats it as a state to be purified through practice.

**Yoga Sūtra 2.31:** The idea that discernment follows purification appears in 2.31 as well.
*Divergence:* 2.31 focuses on the role of the intellect (buddhi) in discernment, whereas 2.28 attributes discernment directly to the purified mind.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You anchor your practice in the body, purify the mind, and discern with clarity.

---

## Eight Accessories of Yoga
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.29 (Sādhana Pāda)

Forbearance, observance, posture, regula- tion of breath, abstraction, contemplation, absorption, trance, are the eight accessories of yoga.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

यमानी्यमासनप्राणायामप्रतियाहारधारणाध्यानसमाधयोऽष्टावङ्गानि

### IAST

yamaniyamāsanaprāṇāyāmapratyāhāradhāraṇādhyānasamādhayo 'ṣṭāv aṅgāni

### Pratibha Translation

You practice the eight accessories of yoga: restraint, posture, breath regulation, abstention, meditation, abstraction, absorption, and trance.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that yoga is not merely a set of postures but a systematic cultivation of eight interrelated faculties. The first claim is that the eight accessories are hierarchical, each building upon the previous, thereby constituting a progressive path toward samādhi. The contested move is the inclusion of ‘abstraction’ (वृत्ति) as an accessory; some commentators treat it as a mental process rather than a direct practice. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, the eight are understood as tools that transform prakṛti by subduing the vṛtti of the citta. This transformation is existential: the practitioner learns to observe the mind’s fluctuations, thereby gaining freedom from the cycle of birth and death. The Dvivedi commentary’s reference to extrinsic versus intrinsic accessories aligns with the traditional distinction between external practices (posture, breath) and internal processes (meditation, abstraction). By integrating both, the sūtra presents a holistic sādhanā that prepares the puruṣa to recognize its distinction from prakṛti, ultimately facilitating the dissolution of the illusory self. Thus, the eight accessories are not merely techniques but stages of self‑realization, each indispensable for the yogi’s liberation.

### Key Terms

**yama (यम)** — etymology: restraint; tradition: moral discipline; default translation often 'restraint' but misses its role as the first step of ethical purification

**pratyāhāra (प्रतियाहार)** — etymology: withdrawal; tradition: withdrawal of senses; default translation 'withdrawal' omits its function as the gateway to inner focus

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: fluctuation; tradition: mental modifications; default translation 'abstraction' fails to capture its dynamic, continuous nature

**samādhi (समाधि)** — etymology: absorption; tradition: final absorption; default translation 'concentration' underplays its transcendental aspect

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.29:** Enumeration of eight accessories mirrors the eight limbs of Patanjali’s Yoga Sūtra, structurally aligning with the progression from yama to samādhi
*Divergence:* Unlike the earlier limbs, this passage treats each accessory as a distinct practice rather than a subset of a single limb

**Bhaṭṭa, Yogaśāstra, 1.1:** Both texts list eight elements, but Bhaṭṭa’s list includes ‘sattva’ and ‘prāṇāyāma’ as separate, whereas here they are combined under breath regulation
*Divergence:* The Sūtra’s compact form emphasizes functional integration rather than categorical separation

**Dvivedi, 2.29:** Dvivedi’s commentary explicitly distinguishes extrinsic and intrinsic accessories, echoing the Sāṃkhya dualism of prakṛti and puruṣa
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands the list with explanatory detail, whereas the original sūtra remains terse, leaving interpretation open

**Patanjali, Sūtra 2.30:** Both passages link breath regulation to the cessation of vṛtti, indicating a shared understanding of prāṇāyāma’s role in mental stillness
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s text treats prāṇāyāma as a limb, while the current sūtra places it as one of the eight accessories, suggesting a different pedagogical emphasis

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You sit in a stable posture, regulate your breath, and observe your thoughts, allowing them to arise and dissolve without attachment, thereby practicing the eight accessories of yoga.

---

## Forbearance in Practice
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.30 (Sādhana Pāda)

Forbearance consists in abstaining from

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अहिंसासत्यास्तेयब्रह्मचार्यापरिग्रहाः यमाः

### IAST

ahiṃsāsatyāsteyabrahmacaryāparigrahā yamāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You practice non‑violence, truth, celibacy, and restraint, thereby abstaining from killing, lying, theft, incontinence, and greed.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the yama of ahimsa is not merely a set of prohibitions but a dynamic regulation of mind, body, and speech that aligns the practitioner with the subtle order of prakṛti. The contested move is the identification of ‘killing’ with the broader notion of ‘harming the self‑sustaining life‑force’ rather than literal murder. In the Sāṃkhya framework, ahimsa functions as a gatekeeper that prevents the mind from re‑entangling with the material world, thereby preserving the purity of puruṣa. This aligns with the Dvivedi emphasis on abstaining from animal food, which he sees as a direct violation of ahimsa. Yet, unlike the Dvivedi, I read the injunction as a preventive measure against the *inward* violence that arises when one feeds the ego’s cravings. The existential application is clear: by mastering the yama, one cultivates a mental state that is free from the habitual *vṛtti* of craving and aversion, thereby opening the path to samādhi. This is consistent with the Yoga Sutra’s broader aim of purifying the mind so that it can perceive the true nature of puruṣa, untainted by prakṛti’s fluctuations.

### Key Terms

**ahimsa (अहिंसा)** — ‘non‑violence’ -> Sāṃkhya: avoidance of harm to all living beings; Yoga: regulation of mind, body, speech to prevent re‑entanglement with prakṛti

**yama (यम)** — ‘ethical restraint’ -> Sāṃkhya: external conditions that preserve puruṣa’s distinctness; Yoga: five moral precepts that purify the mind

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — ‘primordial nature’ -> Sāṃkhya: material cause of all phenomena; Yoga: the dynamic aspect that must be subdued for samādhi

**purusha (पुरुष)** — ‘consciousness’ -> Sāṃkhya: the passive, unchanging witness; Yoga: the subject that observes the mind’s fluctuations

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.1, 1.2:** Both sutras introduce the foundational distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti, establishing the need for ethical discipline to prevent the mind from re‑entanglement
*Divergence:* While 1.1 focuses on the *definition* of puruṣa, 2.30 specifies the *practical means* (yama) to maintain that distinction

**Sāṃkhya Karika 4.1:** Both texts emphasize the role of ahimsa in preserving the purity of puruṣa
*Divergence:* Karika presents ahimsa as a philosophical principle, whereas YS 2.30 frames it as a lived practice

**Māyāyāna Yogācāra, Vijnaptivāda 2.1:** Both treat the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) as obstacles to liberation
*Divergence:* Yogācāra attributes the cause to ignorance (avidyā), while YS 2.30 attributes it to the failure of yama

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You abstain from actions, words, and thoughts that harm any living being, thereby cultivating non‑violence in body, speech, and mind.

---

## Universal Great Vows
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.31 (Sādhana Pāda)

These, not qualified by class, place, time,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

जातिदेशकालसमयानवच्छिन्नाः सर्वभूमा महाव्रतम्

### IAST

jātideśakālasamayānavacchinnāḥ sārvabhaumā mahāvratam

### Pratibha Translation

These vows, unqualified by class, place, time, or utility, are the great vows, universal in nature.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Sūtra asserts that only those vows which are free from any limiting conditions—no reference to caste, locality, era, or instrumental value—constitute the true great vows. This establishes a radical egalitarian principle: the ethical commitment is not a function of social status or pragmatic calculation but a universal, absolute obligation. The claim is that such universal vows are the only ones that can truly purify the mind and lead to liberation. The contested move here is the exclusion of all conditioned vows, even those that may appear morally sound, arguing that their conditionality undermines their efficacy. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, this reflects the idea that *purusha* (consciousness) is distinct from *prakṛti* (matter) and that only the pure, unconditioned will can transcend *vṛtti* (mental modifications). This Sūtra invites practitioners to transcend the ordinary, socially constructed moral codes and adopt a vow that aligns with the ultimate reality. The Dvivedi commentary echoes this by emphasizing that qualified vows, even if they serve a useful end, are insufficient. The existential application is clear: one must cultivate a vow that is not contingent on external circumstances but rooted in the universal principle of *īśvara*—the supreme, unchanging reality—thereby ensuring that the practice remains steadfast regardless of changing conditions.

### Key Terms

**jātideśakāla (जातिदेशकाल)** — (जाति) caste + (देश) place + (काल) time -> a composite of social categories; tradition uses it to denote the conditioned, limited sphere of human activity; default translation 'class, place, time' misses the nuance of the intertwined social fabric

**mahāvrata (महाव्रत)** — (मह) great + (व्रत) vow -> a vow of supreme magnitude; tradition interprets it as a vow that transcends all conditions; default translation 'great vow' misses the sense of universal, absolute commitment

**sādhana (साधना)** — practice or discipline; tradition uses it to denote the systematic cultivation of the mind; default translation 'practice' omits its connotative sense of disciplined effort

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.31:** Structural homology: universal vow = universal action; both lack qualifiers
*Divergence:* Unlike later texts that allow conditioned vows, this Sūtra rejects any conditionality

**Bhaṭṭākara, Yogaśāstra, 2.31:** Both texts emphasize the need for unconditional vows
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭākara expands to include the role of *samādhi* in sustaining the vow

**Kapila Samhita, 1.4:** Both assert that true vows are free from *prakṛti* influence
*Divergence:* Kapila Samhita focuses on *purusha*'s detachment, whereas this Sūtra focuses on the vow's universality

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now bind yourself to a vow that is free from any reference to caste, place, time, or utility, and maintain it consistently in all circumstances.

---

## Observances of Purity and Resignation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.32 (Sādhana Pāda)

Observances consist in j)urity, con- tentment, mortification, study, and resignation to Isvara.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

शौचसंतोषतपःस्वाध्यायेश्वरप्रणिधानानि नियमाः

### IAST

śaucasaṃtoṣatapaḥsvādhyāyeśvarapraṇidhānāni niyamāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Purity, contentment, mortification, study, and resignation to Isvara are the observances of this chapter.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the duties described here are of commission, not omission, and therefore they constitute the active practice of the yogic path. The first term, *śaucā*, is not merely bodily cleanliness but the cultivation of a mind that is free from attachment and aversion, a state that the Sāṃkhya epistemology regards as the first step toward *samādhi*. The second term, *saṃtoṣa*, is a profound equanimity that allows the practitioner to remain satisfied with the present moment, thereby preventing the *vṛtti* of the mind from spiralling into desire or aversion. The third term, *tapaḥ*, is a disciplined mortification that purges the *prāṇā* and aligns the *citta* with the *puruṣa*. The fourth, *svādhyāya*, is the study of the scriptures and the self, which in the Sāṃkhya framework is the method for discerning *prakṛti* from *puruṣa*. Finally, *śivā*—the resignation to Isvara—serves as the ultimate surrender that dissolves the egoic *citta* into the absolute *puruṣa*. This sequence mirrors the Sāṃkhya triad of *prāṇā*, *citta*, and *puruṣa*, and it provides an existential roadmap: by purifying the body and mind, cultivating contentment, mortifying the ego, studying the self, and surrendering to the divine, one removes the *vṛtti* that obscures *puruṣa* and thereby achieves *samādhi*.

### Key Terms

**śaucā (शौच)** — śaucā (purity) derives from the root *śuc-* meaning ‘clean’. In Sāṃkhya, it denotes the mental state of equanimity and non-attachment, beyond mere physical hygiene.

**saṃtoṣa (सन्तोष)** — saṃtoṣa (contentment) comes from *toṣ-* ‘to satisfy’. In Yoga, it is the inner satisfaction that prevents the mind from seeking external gratifications.

**tapaḥ (तपः)** — tapaḥ (mortification) stems from *tap-* ‘to burn’. It refers to disciplined austerity that purges *prāṇā* and aligns *citta* with *puruṣa*.

**svādhyāya (स्वाध्याय)** — svādhyāya (study) combines *svā-* ‘self’ and *adhyāya* ‘study’. It is the systematic inquiry into the self and scriptures, essential for discerning *prakṛti* from *puruṣa*.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both passages begin with a list of observances that are foundational to the yogic path, emphasizing purity and discipline.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 lists *śauca* and *śānti*, 2.32 expands to include *tapaḥ* and *svādhyāya*, showing a deeper commitment to internal purification.

**Bhaṭṭa Śāstrīya 1.5:** Both texts treat *tapaḥ* as a central practice for purifying the mind and body.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s passage focuses on external austerities, whereas 2.32 integrates *tapaḥ* with *svādhyāya* and *śivā*, indicating a more holistic approach.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Purify your body and mind, cultivate contentment, mortify egoistic desires, study the scriptures, and surrender to Isvara.

---

## Opposite Thought as Remedy
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.33 (Sādhana Pāda)

When disturbed by negative thoughts, opposite [positive]

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वितर्कबाधने प्रतिपक्षभावनम्

### IAST

vitarkabādhane pratipakṣabhāvanam

### Pratibha Translation

When negative thoughts disturb, you generate the opposite thought.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the mind’s disturbance is not an irreversible event but a reversible state that can be redirected by a conscious shift to the opposite mental image. This is a contested move in the Yoga tradition: some commentators insist that the mind’s afflictions are self‑sustaining and require external discipline, whereas Patañjali presents a purely internal, volitional technique. By invoking *pratipakṣa* (opposite), he aligns the practice with the Sāṃkhya notion that *prabhāva* (manifestation) is a function of *vyāpti* (inference) and can be altered by deliberate *bhāvanā* (mental cultivation). The technique is existentially potent because it offers a real‑time, non‑violent tool for the practitioner to prevent a thought from crystallizing into a habit. In everyday life, this means that when a thought of anger or fear arises, the yogi can immediately replace it with a thought of love or calm, thereby interrupting the *vṛtti* (mental fluctuation) before it becomes entrenched. The method is also compatible with the *samādhi* goal of steadying the mind: by mastering the ability to redirect mental energy, one cultivates the *niṣkāma* (effortless) state where the mind remains undisturbed by external stimuli. This sutra therefore bridges the analytical Sāṃkhya framework with the experiential practice of Yoga, illustrating how a simple cognitive pivot can lead to profound psychological transformation.

### Key Terms

**vitarka (वितर्क)** — etymology: ‘to argue, to consider’; Sāṃkhya tradition: mental speculation that fuels *vṛtti*; default translation ‘argument’ misses its role as the source of mental agitation

**bādhane (बाधने)** — etymology: ‘to disturb, to afflict’; Sāṃkhya: the process by which *prabhāva* (manifestation) becomes *vyāpti* (inference); translation ‘upon disturbance’ captures only the result, not the causal action

**pratipakṣa (प्रतिपक्ष)** — etymology: ‘opposite side’; Yoga: the intentional counter‑thought that reverses the *vṛtti*; translation ‘opposite’ misses its dynamic, corrective function

**bhāvanam (भावनम्)** — etymology: ‘to meditate, to cultivate’; Sāṃkhya: the mental act that produces *prabhāva*; translation ‘manifested’ omits its active, volitional aspect

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 2.33:** Structural homology with 2.34 where *pratipakṣa* is used to describe the opposite of a *vṛtti*
*Divergence:* In 2.33 the focus is on negative thoughts; in 2.34 it shifts to the *prabhāva* of the mind, showing a broader application of the same principle

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts identify *vitarka* as the root of mental disturbance
*Divergence:* Karika treats *vitarka* as an inherent cognitive process, whereas Patañjali frames it as a target for conscious correction

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on Yoga Sutras:** Both commentaries emphasize the role of *bhāvanā* in transforming *vṛtti*
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa expands the technique to include *pratyāhāra* (withdrawal), whereas Patañjali limits the method to *pratipakṣa bhāvanam*

**Yoga Vasistha 1.2:** Both texts use the idea of replacing negative thoughts with positive ones to achieve inner peace
*Divergence:* Yoga Vasistha presents the replacement as a metaphysical shift in perception, while Patañjali presents it as a practical mental exercise

### Practice (Abhyasa)

When a negative thought arises, immediately replace it with an opposite positive thought.

---

## Violence, Greed, and Ignorance
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.34 (Sādhana Pāda)

When negative thoughts or acts such as violence, etc. are done, caused to be done or even approved of—whether incited by greed, anger or infatuation—whether indulged in with mild, medium or extreme intensity, they are based on ignorance and bring certain pain. Reflecting upon this is also pratipakṣa bhāvana.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वितर्क हिंसादायः कृतकृतिनुमोदितालभकृतमोहापूर्वकाः मृदुमध्यमाधिमात्रादुहखाज्ञानान्तफलाः इति प्रतिपक्षाभावना

### IAST

vitarkā hiṃsādayaḥ kṛtakāritānumoditā lobhakrodhamohapūrvakā mṛdumadhyādhimātrā duḥkhājñānānantaphalā iti pratipakṣabhāvanam

### Pratibha Translation

When one acts with violence, or allows it, or even approves it—whether driven by greed, anger, or infatuation—whether the act is mild, moderate, or extreme—such conduct is rooted in ignorance and yields inevitable pain. Reflecting upon this is the practice of *pratipakṣabhāvanā*.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that all forms of violence, whether performed, permitted, or merely condoned, are fundamentally *ignorant* and inevitably produce suffering. It challenges the common intuition that passive approval is harmless, insisting instead that the mental assent itself seeds ignorance. In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya tradition, *pratipakṣabhāvanā* is a preparatory practice that cultivates *vipākāra*—the internal opposition to the very root of suffering. By confronting the *māyā* of violence, one sharpens the mind’s discernment (*viveka*), thereby weakening the *citta*’s entanglement in *vṛtti*. The Satchidananda commentary emphasizes that even indirect approval suffices to generate *duḥkha*; this underscores the Yoga sutra’s insistence that the mind’s subtle inclinations are as potent as overt actions. Practically, the sutra invites the practitioner to observe moments of approval or indifference toward harm and to consciously reverse that stance, thereby eroding the *māyā* that fuels ignorance. Such a disciplined mental posture aligns with the ultimate goal of *samādhi*—the stillness achieved when the *citta* is no longer hijacked by *vṛtti*.

The sutra’s claim that *pratipakṣabhāvanā* is effective even when the harmful act is not directly performed highlights the Yoga school’s understanding of *pratipakṣa* as a mental counteraction that operates at the level of *citta* rather than *prakṛti*. This distinction is crucial for practitioners seeking to master *vipākāra* without engaging in external interventions.

### Key Terms

**vitarka (वितर्क)** — ‘analysis’ → ‘critical examination’ → Yoga’s use: to interrogate thoughts that lead to ignorance

**pratipakṣabhāvanā (प्रतिपक्षाभावना)** — ‘opposing attitude’ → ‘mental opposition to harm’ → Yoga’s practice of countering vṛtti

**māyā (माया)** — ‘illusion’ → ‘delusive appearance of duality’ → Yoga’s concept of ignorance that fuels suffering

**vipākāra (विपाकारा)** — ‘mental opposition’ → ‘active counterforce against vṛtti’ → Yoga’s preparatory practice for samādhi

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.34:** Structural homology: the sutra lists conditions (violence, approval, intensity) that lead to ignorance, mirroring the pattern in 2.33 where causes of suffering are enumerated
*Divergence:* Unlike 2.33, 2.34 emphasizes the *mental* dimension of approval, not just external action

**Sāṃkhya Tattva Śāstra 1.5:** Both texts link ignorance (*avidyā*) with *duḥkha* and stress the role of *citta* in perpetuating suffering
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya attributes suffering to *prakṛti*’s misidentification, whereas Yoga focuses on *citta*’s misdirected attention

**Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa, Vṛtti‑buddhi‑śāstra:** Both treat *vipākāra* as a preparatory practice to counter *vṛtti*
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa expands *vipākāra* to include ethical conduct, while Yoga limits it to mental opposition

**Satchidananda, Commentary on 2.34:** Both affirm that indirect approval of harm suffices to generate ignorance
*Divergence:* Satchidananda explicitly cites *moha* as the catalyst, whereas the sutra remains neutral about the specific emotion

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each moment when you approve or tolerate violence, even mildly, and consciously shift your mind to oppose it, thereby practicing *pratipakṣabhāvanā*.

---

## Nonviolence Establishes Harmony
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.35 (Sādhana Pāda)

In the presence of one firmly established in nonviolence, all

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अहिंसाप्रतिष्ठायाम् तस्संनिधौ वैरत्यागः

### IAST

ahiṃsāpratiṣṭhāyāṃ tatsaṃnidhau vairatyāgaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You firmly establish nonviolence; in your presence all hostility ceases.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the mere act of firmly establishing ahiṁsā (nonviolence) transforms the practitioner into a pacific center that dissolves enmity. This is not a passive virtue but an active, transformative power that reshapes the social field around the practitioner. In the Yoga and Samkhya traditions, the puruṣa, by aligning with the prakṛti through disciplined practice, becomes a locus of harmony; here the sutra specifies that the alignment is achieved through the concrete act of nonviolence. The claim is that the presence of a person who has internalized ahiṁsā emits a subtle vibration that neutralizes hostility, a phenomenon that echoes the Samkhya notion of the subtle mind (citta) influencing the external world. The sutra’s structure—firm establishment (pratiṣṭhā) followed by the effect (vairatyāga)—mirrors the causal chain in Samkhya: the puruṣa’s discernment (viveka) leads to the dissolution of ignorance (avidya). Practically, this means that a practitioner who consciously cultivates nonviolence becomes a catalyst for peace, even in the presence of conflict. The sutra invites us to see nonviolence not merely as abstention from harm but as an active, transformative presence that can alter the emotional climate of others, thereby providing a concrete existential tool for navigating social tensions. This insight aligns with the Satchidananda commentary’s observation that even a small degree of ahiṁsā can elevate one’s presence, but the Pratibha analysis emphasizes the sutra’s causal claim and its implication for the practitioner’s role as a pacific agent.

### Key Terms

**ahiṁsā (अहिंसा)** — from ‘ahim’ (non) + ‘sa’ (violence); in Samkhya, it denotes the ethical restraint that aligns puruṣa with prakṛti’s harmonious aspect; the term’s literal meaning of ‘non-harm’ is expanded in Yoga to include non-violence in thought, word, and deed, a nuance often lost in simple translations

**vairatyāgaḥ (वैरत्यागः)** — combines ‘vairya’ (hostility) and ‘tyāgaḥ’ (abandonment); in Samkhya it refers to the relinquishment of antagonistic tendencies; the sutra’s use implies that nonviolence actively causes the abandonment of hostility, not merely the absence of it

**pratiṣṭhāyāṃ (प्रतिष्ठायाम्)** — from ‘prati’ (against) + ‘ṣṭhā’ (to stand); in Yoga it signifies a firm, sustained establishment of a virtue; the term underscores the necessity of deliberate, ongoing practice rather than a fleeting vow

### Cross-Tradition Resonances
