**Yoga Sutra 1.2 – ‘Yogaś ca siddhyate śāntam’:** Both sutras use a causal structure: a firm establishment (pratiṣṭhā) leads to a state of peace or dissolution (śāntam/vairatyāga). The similarity lies in the emphasis on intentional practice as the catalyst for inner and outer harmony.
*Divergence:* While 1.2 speaks of general peace, 2.35 specifies the dissolution of hostility, highlighting a more socially oriented effect of the practice

**Samkhya Karika 1.1 – ‘Puruṣaḥ prakṛtivṛttiḥ’:** Both traditions link the puruṣa’s discernment (or establishment of virtue) with the transformation of prakṛti’s manifestations; the sutra mirrors this by showing how ahiṁsā transforms hostile interactions.
*Divergence:* Samkhya focuses on metaphysical discernment, whereas the Yoga sutra frames the effect in concrete social terms (hostility ceasing in presence)

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Firmly establish nonviolence; when you are present, all hostility dissolves.

---

## Truthfulness Grants Effortless Fruition
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.36 (Sādhana Pāda)

To one established in truthfulness, actions and their results

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सत्यप्रतििष्ठायाम् कर्मफलाश्रयत्वम्

### IAST

satyapratiṣṭhāyāṃ kriyāphalāśrayatvam

### Pratibha Translation

When one establishes truthfulness, one rests upon the fruits of action.

### Pratibha Commentary

The core claim of this aphorism is that *satya* (truthfulness) functions as a metaphysical catalyst that transforms effort into effortless attainment. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, *satya* is not merely ethical honesty but the alignment of *citta* (mind) with *prakṛti*’s rational principle, thereby stabilising the *vṛtti* (mental modifications). The contested move here is the assertion that truthfulness alone suffices to secure *phala* (results) without *karmā* (effort). Critics argue that this negates the *yoga* of *karma* as a disciplined practice; proponents see it as an elevation of *satya* to a divine grace that supersedes ordinary causal law. The Sādhana Pāda thus invites practitioners to cultivate *satya* as a *samādhi*‑like state, wherein the mind’s fluctuations cease and the *purusha* perceives the *prakṛti*’s fruits directly. Existentially, this translates to living a life where integrity becomes a self‑fulfilling force: actions are no longer laborious but arise spontaneously from a clear, honest consciousness. The practice is to observe every utterance and intention, ensuring it aligns with *satya*; when misalignment occurs, one consciously withdraws or corrects it, thereby reinforcing the causal loop that makes truthfulness the bedrock of effortless fruition.

### Key Terms

**satya (सत्य)** — truthfulness -> Sāṃkhya’s rational principle of *prakṛti* -> ordinary sense of honesty; the term here denotes a metaphysical alignment that transforms causal law

**karmā (कर्म)** — action -> Sāṃkhya’s activity of *purusha* on *prakṛti* -> here implies effort that is rendered unnecessary by *satya*

**phala (फल)** — fruit/result -> causal outcome in Sāṃkhya -> here denotes the spontaneous manifestation of effortless attainment

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.36 (Sādhana Pāda):** Both the Sūtra and the Sāṃkhya Nyāya treat *satya* as a stabilising principle that aligns *citta* with *prakṛti*
*Divergence:* Unlike the Nyāya, the Sūtra claims this alignment produces results without *karmā*, a claim that Sāṃkhya traditionally reserves for *yoga* of *samādhi*

**Bhaṭṭa’s *Yogaśāstra* 1.42:** Bhaṭṭa also links *satya* to the liberation of *purusha* from *vṛtti*
*Divergence:* He does not assert that *satya* alone yields *phala*; effort remains essential, highlighting a divergence in the perceived sufficiency of truthfulness

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each thought and word; if it contradicts truth, immediately correct it, so that your mind rests solely on the fruits of honest action.

---

## Wealth through Non-Stealing
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.37 (Sādhana Pāda)

To one established in non-stealing, all wealth comes.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अस्तेयप्रतिṣ्ठायां सर्वरत्नोऽपस्ठाम्

### IAST

asteyapratiṣṭhāyāṃ sarvaratnopasthānam

### Pratibha Translation

When one is firmly established in non‑stealing, all wealth approaches him.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sūtra is that ethical restraint—specifically, the negation of theft—constitutes the foundational condition for the natural influx of prosperity. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, the puruṣa’s liberation is achieved by purifying the mind of vṛtti, and the sūtra posits that the vṛtti of avarice, manifested as stealing, blocks the flow of prakṛti’s bounty. The contested move here is the identification of non‑stealing not merely as a moral precept but as a causal agent that opens the channels of wealth; some commentators treat it as a symbolic virtue, others as a literal mechanism. By situating the sūtra within the broader Yoga canon, we see a parallel with the earlier aphorism that “the mind is the source of all wealth” (YS 2.35), yet this sūtra adds a concrete behavioral injunction. The existential import is that the practitioner’s daily conduct—whether in work, consumption, or interpersonal relations—directly influences the material and spiritual sustenance that follows. Thus, the sūtra invites a lived ethic: cultivate non‑stealing not as a distant ideal but as an active, present‑moment practice that invites abundance to flow naturally.

### Key Terms

**asteya (अस्तेय)** — root: asti (to be) + -eya (negation) -> non‑stealing; in Sāṃkhya, it denotes the cessation of the vṛtti of avarice, which is essential for puruṣa’s freedom; the ordinary translation ‘not stealing’ omits its role as a purifying practice

**pratiṣṭhāyām (प्रतिष्ठायाम्)** — pra (forth) + iṣṭhā (establish) -> upon having established; in Yoga, it signifies a firm, sustained state of ethical discipline, not merely a momentary act

**sarva (सर्व)** — all; in Sāṃkhya, it implies the totality of prakṛti’s manifestations, indicating that the benefit is not selective but universal

**ratna (रत्न)** — gem; metaphor for wealth, encompassing material, spiritual, and relational riches; the term’s literal sense of precious stone highlights the preciousness of what is received

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.35 – ‘cittaḥ sarvasattāṃ sarvabhūtāḥ’:** Both sūtras link the mind’s purity to the influx of wealth; 2.35 speaks of the mind as the source, while 2.37 specifies the condition (non‑stealing) that activates this source
*Divergence:* 2.35 is abstract, 2.37 is prescriptive; the latter grounds the former in ethical practice

**Buddhism, Abhidharma, ‘Khandhāyāna’ – ‘non‑stealing as a precondition for prosperity’:** Structural similarity: a precondition (non‑stealing) leading to a positive outcome (prosperity)
*Divergence:* Buddhist account frames theft as a hindrance to mental clarity, whereas the Yoga text frames it as a blockage to material influx

**Māyāyāna, ‘Brahma Sutra 1.2.5’ – ‘the world is a reflection of the mind’:** Both texts assert that external reality (wealth) is a reflection of internal state (non‑stealing)
*Divergence:* Brahma Sutra emphasizes metaphysical causality, Yoga sūtra emphasizes practical ethics

**Ānanda, ‘Yoga Vāsiṣṭha 1.1’ – ‘the mind is the source of all wealth’:** Parallel claim that the mind generates wealth; the Vāsiṣṭha is more metaphysical, the sūtra is more behavioral

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each moment and refuse to take what is not freely given, so that wealth may naturally approach you.

---

## Continence Yields Vigor
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.38 (Sādhana Pāda)

By one established in continence, vigor is gained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ब्रह्मचार्यप्रतिष्ठायाम् वीर्यलाभः

### IAST

brahmacaryapratiṣṭhāyāṃ vīryalābhaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

By establishing continence, one gains vigor.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that disciplined continence (brahmacarya) is the prerequisite for acquiring vital energy (vīrya). It is not merely a moral injunction but a strategic cultivation of inner resources. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) drain the subtle energies that sustain the body and the higher faculties. By restraining the senses and conserving the vital fluid, the practitioner preserves the prāṇa that fuels the mind’s activity, thereby enabling sustained meditative concentration (samādhi). The claim is that without this energetic reserve, the mind remains exhausted and cannot engage in the disciplined practice required for liberation. This view aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine that prakṛti’s subtle energies, once conserved, can be transformed into ojas, which fortifies the body and mind. The existential implication is clear: a life lived in moderation cultivates the stamina necessary to confront the trials of the world and to progress toward self‑realization. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on “saving energy” echoes this, though it expands into social and relational contexts. The core philosophical move—identifying continence as the source of vigor—remains contested in some traditions that prioritize direct meditative practice over bodily discipline. Nonetheless, within the Yoga canon, this strategy is presented as indispensable for sustaining the practice of the higher yogas.

### Key Terms

**brahmacarya (ब्रह्मचार्य)** — ‘brahma’ (the Absolute) + ‘carya’ (to act); traditionally means celibacy or disciplined conduct; the translation ‘continence’ captures the practice of conserving vital energy but omits its role as a preparatory stage for higher yogic realization.

**vīrya (वीर्य)** — ‘vīrya’ denotes vital energy or vigor; beyond mere physical strength, it encompasses mental and spiritual dynamism that supports sustained practice.

**lābhaḥ (लाभः)** — ‘lābhaḥ’ means gain or profit; in this context it signals the accumulation of inner resources rather than external wealth.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.38:** Structural parallel: ‘brahmacaryapratiṣṭhāyāṃ vīryalābhaḥ’ mirrors the earlier Sūtra 2.37 ‘brahmāṇḍaḥ svābhāvikaḥ’ in pattern of compound + verb + object, emphasizing foundational quality leading to outcome.
*Divergence:* While 2.37 speaks of the inherent nature of the cosmos, 2.38 focuses on the practitioner’s cultivation, shifting from cosmic to personal agency.

**Bhaṭṭa’s Sādhana Pāda:** Both treat brahmacarya as a preparatory stage; Bhaṭṭa elaborates on its psychological effects, whereas the Sūtra condenses it to a single causal claim.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s commentary expands the experiential dimension, whereas the Sūtra remains aphoristic, leaving interpretation to the practitioner.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Establish continence now, and you will experience increased vigor in your daily activities.

---

## Illumination of Birth Origins
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.39 (Sādhana Pāda)

When non-greed is confirmed, a thorough illumination of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अपरिग्रहस्थाये जनमकथं तासं बोधः

### IAST

aparigrahasthairye janmakathaṃtāsaṃbodhaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

When non‑greed is firmly established, the how and why of one’s birth are thoroughly illuminated.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the practice of aparigraha—renunciation of unnecessary possession—directly unlocks the inquiry into one’s birth is a decisive philosophical move. It asserts that the same discipline that frees the mind from material clinging also frees it from the habitual patterns that generate rebirth. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, puruṣa (consciousness) is obscured by prakṛti (nature) through vṛtti (mental modifications). Aparigraha, by cutting off the vṛtti of attachment, allows puruṣa to see the causal chain that leads to janma (birth). This is not merely a metaphysical claim but an existential prescription: by refusing to hoard, one cultivates the clarity needed to ask kathaṁtā—how and why—about one’s own origin. The Satchidananda commentary underscores the practical aspect of this inquiry, noting that the mind, unbound from gifts and obligations, can directly perceive the causal links of its own existence. Thus, the sūtra positions aparigraha as both a means of ethical conduct and a gateway to self‑knowledge, echoing the Sāṃkhya idea that liberation arises when puruṣa is liberated from prakṛti’s influence. This dual role—ethical restraint and epistemic access—makes the sūtra a pivotal point in the Yoga canon, where practice and inquiry converge.

### Key Terms

**aparigraha (अपरिग्रह)** — from ‘apara’ (not) + ‘graha’ (taking); in Sāṃkhya, the renunciation of unnecessary possession; in common usage, non‑greed; the sūtra emphasizes its role in freeing the mind from vṛtti

**janma (जनम)** — ‘birth’ as the moment of emergence; in Sāṃkhya, the cycle of rebirth; here it denotes the existential question of one’s origin

**kathaṁtā (कथंता)** — ‘how and why’ questioning; in Yoga, the investigative process that leads to self‑knowledge; the sūtra links it to the illumination achieved by aparigraha

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.39:** Structural homology with 2.38 where ‘sthairye’ (firmness) precedes the action; both verses use a participial phrase to indicate the condition under which the main clause applies
*Divergence:* In 2.39 the condition is non‑greed, whereas in 2.38 it is the practice of meditation; the shift from ethical restraint to epistemic inquiry marks a productive expansion of the sūtra’s scope

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts use the phrase ‘janma kathaṁtā’ to denote the inquiry into birth; the Karika frames it as a theoretical question, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as an experiential illumination
*Divergence:* The Yoga Sūtra adds the causal link to aparigraha, thereby grounding the inquiry in ethical practice rather than abstract speculation

**Yoga Vasistha 1.1:** Both texts employ the motif of renunciation (aparigraha) as a prerequisite for higher knowledge; the Vasistha presents it as a narrative lesson
*Divergence:* The Vasistha uses a story of a king, while the Yoga Sūtra presents it as a concise aphorism, highlighting the sūtra’s brevity and directness

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now firmly refuse unnecessary possessions, so that your mind can clearly question how and why you were born.

---

## Purification and Disgust
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.40 (Sādhana Pāda)

By purification arises disgust for one’s own body and for

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सौचात् स्वाङ्गजुगुप्सा परैर असंसर्गः

### IAST

śaucāt svāṅgajugupsā parair asaṃsargaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

When purity (śauca) is attained, one feels disgust (jugupsa) toward one’s own body (sva‑aṅga) and toward others (parair), leading to cessation of contact (asaṃsargaḥ).

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the very act of purification, which is a hallmark of disciplined practice, paradoxically engenders a revulsion toward the body. This is not a mere physical aversion but a profound existential detachment: the body, once seen as a vessel of impurity, becomes a hindrance to higher realization. The contested move here is the identification of disgust with the cessation of contact; some commentators read it as a temporary state of disinterest, while others see it as a permanent renunciation of bodily attachment. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, the body (prakṛti) is the locus of vṛtti, the fluctuations that bind puruṣa. By cultivating śauca, the aspirant purifies the mind and thereby exposes the body’s inherent impurity, prompting a shift from identification with the body to identification with puruṣa. This shift has practical implications: it frees the practitioner from the compulsive desire for sensual gratification, allowing sustained focus on dhyana and samādhi. The Satchidananda commentary underscores this by noting that the disgust is not hatred but a necessary disinterest that redirects energy toward meditation. Thus, the sūtra teaches that purification is not merely cleanliness but a radical reorientation of the self away from the body and toward the Self, a theme that resonates throughout the Yoga Sūtras as a prerequisite for higher states of consciousness.

### Key Terms

**śauca (सौच)** — purity -> śauca (purity of mind and body) -> in this sūtra, it triggers a re-evaluation of the body as a site of impurity, beyond the usual meaning of cleanliness

**jugupsa (जुगुप्सा)** — disgust -> jugupsa (aversion) -> here it denotes a deliberate, contemplative disinterest in bodily contact, not mere dislike

**asaṃsargaḥ (असंसर्गः)** — cessation of contact -> asaṃsargaḥ (non‑contact) -> signifies the withdrawal from bodily interactions as a means to prevent attachment, distinct from the ordinary sense of non‑association

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.39:** Both 2.39 and 2.40 describe the psychological shift that follows purification, showing structural homology in the use of śauca as a catalyst for change
*Divergence:* While 2.39 focuses on the mind’s purification, 2.40 extends the effect to bodily detachment, illustrating a progressive deepening of practice

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on the Yoga Sūtras, 2.40:** Bhaṭṭa parallels the sūtra’s notion of disgust with the concept of ‘śauca‑prāṇā‑sādhana’ (purification of breath) as a means to detach from the body
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa adds the element of breath control, which the sūtra omits, thereby expanding the practical dimension

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā, chapter on Purusha‑Prakriti:** Both texts treat the body as a manifestation of prakṛti that must be transcended to realize puruṣa
*Divergence:* The Kārikā presents this transcendence as a metaphysical necessity, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as a psychological outcome of purification

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Feel your body as a vessel of impurity and consciously withdraw from bodily attachments while you meditate.

---

## Purity and Self-Realization
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.41 (Sādhana Pāda)

Moreover, one gains purity of sattva, cheerfulness of mind, onepointedness, mastery over the senses and fitness for Self- realization.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सत्त्वशुद्धिसुमानस्यैकाग्रेन्‍द्रियजयात्मदर्शनीयोग्यत्वानि च

### IAST

sattvaśuddhisaumanasyaikāgryendriyajayātmadarśanayogyatvāni ca

### Pratibha Translation

You purify the sattva, the mind becomes cheerful, you attain one-pointedness, master the senses, and become fit for self-realization.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that disciplined practice yields a cascade of inner purification and readiness for self‑realization. It claims that the first step is the purification of the body, which in turn cleanses the heart, leading to sustained happiness. From this state, concentration arises spontaneously, without conscious effort, because the mind is no longer scattered. The text then states that all senses are controlled, which is the prerequisite for the vision of the Self (ātma‑darśana). This vision is not merely intellectual; it is a direct experiential readiness that allows one to perceive the Self as the ultimate reality. The claim that purity of thought, word, and deed is essential reflects the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga synthesis where puruṣa is distinguished from prakṛti through disciplined action. The passage thus situates the practice within the broader Yoga framework of vṛtti‑nirodha and īśvara‑prāṇā‑pratipādanam, emphasizing that the ultimate goal is not mere moral cleanliness but the experiential knowledge of the Self. The existential application is clear: by purifying the body and mind, one removes the obstacles that prevent the realization of the true nature of puruṣa, thereby achieving samādhi and liberation.

### Key Terms

**sattva (सत्त्व)** — sattva (IAST) -> सत्त्व: the purifying principle of consciousness, not merely a quality but the active, luminous aspect of puruṣa that becomes clarified through practice

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (IAST) -> वृत्ति: the fluctuations of the mind; the text implies that by mastering senses one can subdue vṛtti, a central goal of Yoga

**ātma‑darśana (आत्मदर्शन)** — ātma‑darśana (IAST) -> आत्मदर्शन: the direct vision of the Self; traditionally a state of samādhi where puruṣa perceives its own nature

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.41:** The structure mirrors 1.3: purifying the body leads to purification of the mind, then to one‑pointedness, mastery of senses, and readiness for self‑realization; each clause builds on the previous.
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.3, 2.41 explicitly links the mastery of senses to the readiness for ātma‑darśana, emphasizing the experiential aspect rather than just mental control

**Bhaṭṭa, Yogaśāstra, 1.3:** Both passages describe a progressive purification: body → mind → concentration → sense control → self‑realization; the sequence is structurally identical.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s version stops at concentration, whereas 2.41 extends to the vision of the Self, highlighting a more advanced stage

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa, 2.4:** Both texts treat puruṣa and prakṛti as distinct; purification of prakṛti (body, senses) is necessary for puruṣa to realize itself.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya focuses on metaphysical distinction, while 2.41 frames purification as a practical method for achieving samādhi

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You purify your body and mind, then focus your attention to master the senses, and finally cultivate the readiness to perceive the Self.

---

## Contentment Yields Supreme Joy
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.42 (Sādhana Pāda)

By contentment, supreme joy is gained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सन्तोषाद् अनुत्तमः सुखलाभः

### IAST

saṃtoṣād anuttamaḥ sukhalābhaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

From contentment one attains supreme joy.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Sūtra asserts that contentment (सन्तोष) is the sole source of the highest happiness. It is not a passive state of satisfaction with external goods but a deliberate, sustained posture of equanimity toward all experience. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya frameworks, contentment is the first step toward the cessation of vṛtti (mental fluctuations) and the cultivation of samādhi (concentration). The claim that contentment alone produces supreme joy challenges the common intuition that external achievements or sensory pleasures are necessary for happiness. The contested move lies in the identification of contentment with a state that is both internal and self‑sufficient, thereby negating the need for external validation. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya idea that puruṣa, the conscious principle, is distinct from prakṛti, the material world; contentment is a puruṣic quality that transcends prakṛti’s fluctuations. The practical implication is that one can achieve the highest bliss without dependence on external circumstances, a powerful existential strategy for dealing with life’s uncertainties. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on the difference between contentment and mere satisfaction reinforces this point, underscoring that contentment is a deliberate, non‑attachment stance that remains stable regardless of external conditions.

### Key Terms

**saṃtoṣ (सन्तोष)** — contentment -> Sāṃkhya: equanimity toward all experience; Yoga: the first step toward samādhi; default translation often conflates with satisfaction

**anuttama (अनुत्तम)** — unsurpassed -> Sāṃkhya: the highest principle; Yoga: the supreme state of bliss; default translation may miss its ontological primacy

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.42:** Structural homology: contentment as antecedent to supreme joy mirrors the causal chain in 2.41 (purva‑pratyaya), where the cause precedes the effect
*Divergence:* Unlike 2.41, 2.42 does not specify a preceding action but posits contentment as an intrinsic quality, expanding the causal model to include internal states

**Bhaṭṭārīya Yogaśāstra 5.2:** Both texts link contentment with liberation; Bhaṭṭārīya emphasizes contentment as a result of knowledge, while 2.42 presents it as a direct source of joy
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭārīya’s model requires prior knowledge, whereas the Sūtra treats contentment as self‑sufficient

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 4.3:** Both traditions view contentment as a puruṣic attribute that transcends prakṛti
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya attributes contentment to the inherent nature of puruṣa, whereas the Sūtra frames it as an active practice

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Cultivate a steady, non‑attachment stance toward all experiences, allowing whatever comes or goes to pass without affecting your inner equilibrium.

---

## Purification Through Austerity
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.43 (Sādhana Pāda)

By austerity, impurities of body and senses are destroyed

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

कायेन्‍द्रियसिद्धिर् अशुद्धिक्षये तपसः

### IAST

kāyendriyasiddhir aśuddhikṣaye tapasaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

By austerity, the mastery of body and senses destroys impurities.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim is that tapas, understood as a disciplined burn, functions as a transformative purifying agent that erodes both physical and psychic impurities. In the Sādhana Pāda, the sūtra juxtaposes the perfected control of body and senses (kāyendriyasiddhi) with the annihilation of aśuddhi by tapas. This pairing signals a causal relationship: mastery is achieved only when the very faculties that can harbor impurity are subjected to disciplined destruction. The contested move here is the identification of tapas not merely as a physical act of fasting or heat, but as a metaphysical process that converts impurity into a refined state of consciousness. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that prakṛti’s impurities are latent tendencies (kleshas) that must be burned away to reveal puruṣa’s pure awareness. The sūtra therefore offers an existential prescription: one must willingly endure suffering—whether bodily, mental, or social—to catalyze inner purification. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on “accepting pain” echoes this, framing suffering as a constructive force. Practically, the sūtra instructs the practitioner to engage in disciplined austerities that target the body and senses, thereby creating a concrete pathway for the elimination of aśuddhi. This process is not merely physical; it is a disciplined psychological rehearsal that conditions the mind to remain unshaken by external disturbances, thereby advancing toward samādhi.

### Key Terms

**kāyendriya (कायेन्‍द्रिः)** — body + senses; in Sāṃkhya, the material substrate that can be mastered (siddhi) to control prakṛti’s influence on puruṣa

**aśuddhi (अशुद्धि)** — impurity; in Yoga, the latent defilements (kleshas) that obscure puruṣa’s nature; default translation ‘impurity’ misses the dynamic process of burning away

**tapas (तपस्)** — austerity; literally ‘burn’; in Sāṃkhya, the disciplined application of effort that transforms prakṛti’s impurities into refined awareness

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.43:** Structural homology: mastery (siddhi) of body and senses precedes destruction (kṣaya) of impurities by tapas, mirroring the earlier sūtra’s formula of ‘siddhi’ followed by ‘kṣaya’
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.1 where puruṣa is separated from prakṛti, here the focus shifts to the internal process of purification rather than ontological distinction

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts posit that the elimination of aśuddhi is essential for the realization of puruṣa’s true nature
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Karika frames this as a metaphysical necessity, whereas the Yoga Sūtra presents it as a practical sādhanā step

**Bhasya of Śaṅkara on Sūtra 2.43:** Śaṅkara interprets tapas as a mental burn that purifies the mind, aligning with the Yoga Sūtra’s emphasis on mental austerity
*Divergence:* Śaṅkara’s commentary expands the scope to include intellectual austerity, whereas the sūtra itself remains agnostic about the specific form of tapas

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Engage in a disciplined practice that systematically challenges and refines your body and senses—such as fasting, controlled breathing, or mindful silence—so that each act of austerity burns away a layer of impurity, leading to clearer awareness.

---

## Union Through Study
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.44 (Sādhana Pāda)

By study of spiritual books comes communion with one’s

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्वाध्यायादिष्टदेवता संप्रयोगः

### IAST

svādhyāyād iṣṭadevatāsaṃprayogaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Through study of sacred texts, one attains communion with the chosen deity.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that disciplined study is the vehicle for experiential union with the divine. It posits that the act of *svādhyāya*—not merely passive reading but active, contemplative engagement with scriptures—activates a psychophysical process that culminates in *saṁprayogaḥ*, a direct, sustained connection with the *iṣṭa* (chosen) *devatā*. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, this union is not a mystical accident but the fruition of *sādhana* (practice) that aligns the *citta* with the *prakṛti* of the deity’s form. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound ontological claim: that the divine is not an external, unknowable realm but a relational field accessible through disciplined cognition. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya idea that *purūṣa* (consciousness) can be identified with the *deva* when the *citta* is purified and focused. The practice of *svādhyāya* thus becomes an intentional, embodied meditation that transforms the seeker’s *citta* into a mirror reflecting the *devatā*’s essence. The existential import is clear: by committing to study, one does not merely acquire knowledge but actualizes a lived communion that can guide ethical conduct, emotional regulation, and ultimately liberation. The sutra therefore invites a disciplined, intentional engagement with scripture as the most reliable path to divine union, a stance that resonates with the broader Yoga canon’s emphasis on *sādhana* as the means to *samādhi* and *moksha*.

### Key Terms

**svādhyāya (स्वाध्याय)** — self‑study -> disciplined engagement with sacred texts or personal practice -> emphasizes active cognition over passive reading

**iṣṭa (इष्ट)** — chosen -> refers to the deity selected by the practitioner’s devotion -> highlights personal agency in divine selection

**devatā (देवता)** — deity -> transcendent principle embodied in a form -> points to the object of union rather than abstract concept

**saṁprayogaḥ (संप्रयोगः)** — union/communion -> experiential connection between practitioner and deity -> underscores the experiential, not merely intellectual, nature of the goal

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 2.44:** Both sutras employ the formula ‘svādhyāyād … saṁprayogaḥ’, linking study to union; the structure is identical, underscoring the centrality of disciplined practice.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s sutra omits the explicit mention of a chosen deity, implying a more general union with *deva*; the current sutra specifies *iṣṭa devatā*, thereby individualizing the object of communion

**B.K. Satchidananda, Yoga: The Science of Self-Realization, 1979:** Both texts emphasize that regular practice becomes study, leading to vision of the deity; the homology lies in the experiential outcome of *saṁprayogaḥ*
*Divergence:* Satchidananda expands the notion of *svādhyāya* to include any personal sādhana, whereas the sutra confines it to scriptural study, reflecting a narrower doctrinal focus

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 2.43:** The preceding sutra discusses the importance of *svādhyāya* for purification, setting the stage for the union in 2.44
*Divergence:* While 2.43 speaks of purification, 2.44 speaks of union, marking a shift from preparatory to consummatory stages

**B.K. Satchidananda, Yoga: The Science of Self-Realization, 1979:** Both texts link the practice of *svādhyāya* to the appearance of the deity’s form (human, light, sound)
*Divergence:* Satchidananda provides a phenomenological description of the form’s manifestation, whereas the sutra merely states the union, leaving the mode of experience implicit

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Study sacred texts with focused attention, allowing the chosen deity’s presence to arise in your mind and heart.

---

## Surrender Leads to Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.45 (Sādhana Pāda)

By total surrender to God, samādhi is attained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

समाधिसिद्धिर् इश्वरप्रणिधानात्

### IAST

samādhisiddhir īśvarapraṇidhānāt

### Pratibha Translation

By total surrender to God, samādhi is attained.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that samādhi, the supreme state of absorption, is achieved through complete surrender to Īśvara. This claim situates the practice within the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga synthesis: the mind’s vṛtti (mental fluctuations) are quelled when the practitioner aligns their will with the supreme principle, thereby dissolving the illusion of individual agency. The contested move here is the identification of surrender (praṇidhāna) with the act of offering all that one possesses—both material and mental—to Īśvara. While some commentators read this as a literal relinquishment of possessions, the Pratibha reading interprets it as a psychological detachment: the mind no longer clings to outcomes, and the ego dissolves into the universal consciousness. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya distinction between puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter); when puruṣa fully identifies with Īśvara, prakṛti’s vṛtti cease. Existentially, the sūtra invites the practitioner to view every action—washing dishes, working, speaking—as a form of worship, thereby transforming ordinary life into a continuous yogic practice. The insight that “every act can become a meditation” echoes the Satchidananda observation that devotion itself is a form of contemplation. By internalizing this, one cultivates a state where the mind remains still not through external restraint, but through internal surrender, achieving the very samādhi the sūtra promises.

### Key Terms

**samādhisiddhir (समाधिसिद्धिर्)** — samādhi (contemplation) + siddhiḥ (attainment) → attainment of contemplation; the term signals that samādhi is a realized state, not merely a practice

**īśvarapraṇidhānāt (इश्वरप्रणिधानात्)** — īśvara (God) + praṇidhāna (surrender) + -āt (from) → from total surrender to God; emphasizes the causal relationship between surrender and samādhi

**samādhi (समाधि)** — Sāṃkhya: a state of complete absorption of the mind in a single object; Yoga: the highest state of meditative absorption; the term bridges both traditions

**praṇidhāna (प्रणिधान)** — Sāṃkhya: complete dedication of will; Yoga: surrender of the mind to a higher principle; the term’s dual meaning underpins the sūtra’s claim

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.44:** Both 2.44 and 2.45 employ the causal construction ‘X from Y’ to link a practice with its result, underscoring the systematic progression of the Sādhana Pāda
*Divergence:* While 2.44 speaks of purification (śuddhi) from desire, 2.45 specifies attainment (siddhi) from surrender, shifting focus from internal cleansing to external realization

**Yoga Sūtra 2.46:** The triadic pattern of ‘purity → practice → result’ recurs, mirroring the sūtra’s structure of surrender leading to samādhi
*Divergence:* 2.46 introduces the notion of ‘sādhana’ (practice) as a prerequisite, whereas 2.45 presents surrender as the sole sufficient condition, highlighting a more direct path

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts posit that the ultimate goal is the dissolution of prakṛti’s vṛtti through puruṣa’s identification with Īśvara
*Divergence:* Karika frames the goal as liberation (mokṣa) from the material cycle, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as the attainment of samādhi, a more immediate meditative state

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Surrender every thought, action, and desire to Īśvara, and you will experience samādhi.

---

## Steady and Easy Posture
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.46 (Sādhana Pāda)

Posture is that which is steady and easy.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्थिरसुखम् आसनम्

### IAST

sthirasukham āsanam

### Pratibha Translation

Posture is that which is steady and easy.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the essence of a posture is its steadiness and ease is a decisive move in the Yoga tradition, distinguishing the physical from the metaphysical. In the Sādhana Pāda, the third accessory, aśana, is presented not as a mere bodily arrangement but as a gateway to the stability required for higher meditative states. The insistence on steadiness (sthira) anticipates the later Samādhi, where the mind must be as still as the body. Ease (sukha) counters the notion that austerity must be painful; it aligns with the Sāṃkhya idea that the prakṛti–puruṣa interaction can be harmonious when the body does not become a source of suffering. This interpretation is reinforced by Dvivedi’s comment that the posture should not cause pain, echoing the yogic principle that the body should be an instrument, not a burden. Practically, this means that a practitioner should choose a posture that feels stable and comfortable, thereby preventing the mind from being distracted by physical discomfort. Such an approach is crucial for the existential application of yoga: it allows the practitioner to inhabit the body without becoming attached to it, thereby facilitating the dissolution of vṛtti and the realization of puruṣa’s true nature.

The philosophical significance lies in the shift from external form to internal quality. By prioritising steadiness and ease, the sutra sets the stage for the subsequent emphasis on the mind’s stillness, thereby linking the physical discipline to the ultimate goal of self-realisation. It also subtly critiques the tendency in some traditions to equate yoga with extreme austerity, offering a more balanced path that respects the body’s limits while still pursuing spiritual depth.

### Key Terms

**sthira (स्थिर)** — steady -> stability of body -> foundational for mental stillness

**sukha (सुख)** — ease -> absence of pain -> aligns with non-attachment

**aśana (आसन)** — posture -> physical anchor for meditation -> gateway to samādhi

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 2.46, Dvivedi Commentary:** Both emphasize steadiness and ease as defining features of aśana
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands to include avoidance of pain, while the sutra remains succinct

**Yoga Sutra 2.47, Patanjali:** Transition to the next accessory, vinyāsa, builds on the steadiness of aśana
*Divergence:* Patanjali introduces a dynamic aspect (movement) that contrasts with the static emphasis here

**Yoga Sutra 1.1, Patanjali:** The ultimate aim of samādhi requires steadiness, echoing the sutra’s focus
*Divergence:* The first sutra speaks of the mind’s stillness, whereas 2.46 focuses on bodily steadiness

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Choose a posture that feels stable and comfortable, and hold it without strain, allowing your breath to flow naturally.

---

## Mild Effort and Meditation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.47 (Sādhana Pāda)

By mild effort and meditation on the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रयत्नस्यैथिल्यांन्तसमापत्तिभ्याम्

### IAST

prayatnaśaithilyānantasamāpattibhyām

### Pratibha Translation

By mild effort and meditation on the infinite, one attains the desired state.

### Pratibha Commentary

The aphorism asserts that the path to samādhi is paved with gentle effort and focus on the infinite, not with forceful striving. It challenges the common intuition that vigorous practice yields faster results, proposing instead that a calm, sustained attention to the boundless (ākāśa, ananta) cultivates steadiness. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, the body (sthāna) becomes a vehicle when the mind’s vṛtti is anchored in the unchanging puruṣa, not in the changing prakṛti. The text therefore invites the practitioner to detach the mind from the posture itself and to fix it upon the eternal, thereby dissolving the egoic identification that causes pain. This aligns with Dvivedi’s emphasis on “posture is steady and easy” when effort is mild, but the key philosophical claim here is that the ultimate goal is not the posture but the awareness of the infinite. The implication for practice is that one should cultivate a posture that is naturally sustained by gentle effort, and then let the mind drift to the infinite, allowing the body to become a passive instrument of insight rather than an active struggle. This method is particularly useful for those who experience discomfort or instability with conventional, forceful asanas, offering a path that respects the body’s limits while still progressing toward liberation.

### Key Terms

**prayatna (प्रयत्न)** — prāyatan -> ‘effort’ in general; in Yoga it denotes the disciplined, purposeful exertion directed toward samādhi, distinct from mere physical exertion

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti -> ‘mental fluctuation’; in Sāṃkhya it is the activity of the mind that obscures puruṣa, and its cessation is essential for samādhi

**ākāśa (आकाश)** — ākāśa -> ‘space’ or ‘infinite’; in Yoga it is the boundless awareness that the mind should be fixed upon, contrasting with the finite body

**ananta (अनन्त)** — ananta -> ‘endless’; here it signifies the infinite, the unbounded reality beyond form, a focal point for meditation

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.47:** Mild effort (prayatna) parallels the gentle approach in 2.46, emphasizing ease over force
*Divergence:* Unlike 2.46, which focuses on posture, 2.47 shifts the emphasis to meditation on the infinite

**Yoga Sūtra 2.48:** Both verses discuss the role of effort and meditation, but 2.48 introduces the concept of the mind’s withdrawal (pratyāhāra)
*Divergence:* 2.47 does not explicitly mention pratyāhāra, focusing instead on the quality of effort

**Yoga Sūtra 3.1:** The idea of steady posture as a vehicle for samādhi appears again in 3.1
*Divergence:* 3.1 elaborates on the mechanics of posture, whereas 2.47 remains abstract about effort and meditation

**Yoga Sūtra 2.49:** Both verses address the relationship between effort and the mind’s focus
*Divergence:* 2.49 introduces the notion of the mind’s stability (sthira), which is implicit but not explicit in 2.47

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Assume a posture that feels naturally steady, then gently shift your attention from the posture to the infinite, allowing the body to relax into the practice.

## End of Duality
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.48 (Sādhana Pāda)

Then no assaults from the pairs of oppo-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततो द्वन्द्वानबिग्धात्

### IAST

tato dvandvānabhighātaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Then the dualities cease to be perceived.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that the disciplined practitioner, having attained a state of samādhi, transcends the ordinary dualistic perception of the world. The sūtra’s terse wording—‘tato dvandvā‑nabhi‑gdhāt’—encapsulates a pivotal philosophical move: the negation of the very structure of duality (dvandva) that underlies all experience. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, this is the moment when the puruṣa, having detached from prakṛti, no longer registers the vṛtti of the mind as ‘this’ or ‘that’. The Dvivedi commentary, which speaks of mastering a posture to neutralise the heat‑cold, pleasure‑pain pairs, illustrates a practical route to this negation. Yet the sūtra itself abstracts from any particular practice; it points to the existential condition that follows the dissolution of the mind’s habitual duality. This is not merely a technical cessation but a fundamental shift in the subject’s orientation: the observer is no longer bound by the dualistic schema that defines ordinary consciousness. The sūtra thereby invites the reader to recognise that the cessation of duality is not an external event but an internal re‑orientation, a re‑identification of the self with the unchanging witness. This insight aligns with the broader Yoga aim of liberating puruṣa from the cycle of birth and death by recognising its true nature beyond the fluctuating mind‑states.

### Key Terms

**dvandva (द्वन्द्व)** — ‘duality’ – the Sāṃkhya notion of paired opposites that constitute the material world; its negation implies the dissolution of the mind’s habitual dualistic perception

**abhi (अभि)** — prefix meaning ‘not’ or ‘without’; in this context it marks the negation of the duality, signalling the cessation of the vṛtti that sustains illusion

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Bhagavad Gita 2.50:** Both texts posit that the mind, when steady, no longer registers dualities; the Gita speaks of ‘samatvam’ (equanimity) while the Yoga Sūtra uses the negation ‘abhi‑gdhāt’
*Divergence:* The Gita frames this as a state of equanimity, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as the absolute cessation of dualistic perception, a more radical negation

**Yoga Vasistha 1.2.3:** Both describe the annihilation of the mind’s dualistic grasp as the key to liberation
*Divergence:* Yoga Vasistha attributes this to the realization of the self’s unity with Brahman, while the Yoga Sūtra presents it as a direct outcome of disciplined samādhi

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your mind, and notice when dualities cease to arise.

---

## Breath Control as Practice
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.49 (Sādhana Pāda)

This being (accomplished), jivtinayama (follows), —the cutting off of the course of inspiration and expiration (of the breath).

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तस्मिन् सति श्वासप्रश्वासयोः गतिविच्छेदः प्राणायामः

### IAST

tasmin sati śvāsapraśvāsayor gativicchedaḥ prāṇāyāmaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You practice breath control by cutting off the natural flow of inhalation and exhalation.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that breath control is a deliberate intervention in the mind’s habitual patterns is central to the Sādhana Pāda. Here the yogi is not merely observing the breath but actively suspending its automatic rhythm, thereby interrupting the vṛtti that keeps the mind bound to prakṛti. This act of cutting off the breath’s flow is a contested move: some commentators see it as a preparatory stage for samādhi, while others view it as a means of purifying the mind for higher cognition. In the Sāṃkhya framework, the breath is a subtle function of prāṇā, the life force that animates prakṛti. By controlling prāṇā, the yogi brings the mind closer to puruṣa, the pure consciousness that is untainted by prakṛti. The existential implication is that through disciplined breath, one learns to detach from the incessant fluctuations of the mind, thereby experiencing a state of stillness that is the hallmark of samādhi. Dvivedi’s emphasis on “suspending the natural course” echoes the traditional view that pranāyāma is not merely a physical exercise but a metaphysical act of aligning the body’s subtle energies with the higher principle of puruṣa. This alignment is what allows the practitioner to transcend ordinary perception and access the deeper realms of consciousness.

### Key Terms

**yoga (योग)** — etymology: ‘union’ -> tradition: union of puruṣa and prakṛti -> default: practice of physical postures

**citta (चित्त)** — etymology: ‘mind’ -> tradition: mind as the locus of vṛtti -> default: mental state

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘fluctuation’ -> tradition: mental modifications that bind puruṣa -> default: distraction

**prāṇāyāma (प्राणायाम)** — etymology: ‘breath control’ -> tradition: suspension of natural prāṇa flow -> default: breathing exercise

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 2.49:** Structural homology: ‘cutting off the natural flow’ parallels 2.48’s ‘cutting off the mind’s fluctuations’
*Divergence:* While 2.48 addresses mental vṛtti, 2.49 focuses on prāṇā, highlighting the dual nature of control

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.5:** Both treat prāṇa as a function of prakṛti that can be regulated
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya emphasizes the causal relation, whereas the Sutra presents it as a deliberate yogic act

**Dvivedi 2.49:** Dvivedi’s wording mirrors the Sutra’s ‘suspending the natural course’
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands on the metaphysical significance, whereas the Sutra remains concise

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You cut off the natural rhythm of inhalation and exhalation, thereby practicing breath control.

---

## Regulated Breath in Sādhana
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.50 (Sādhana Pāda)

(It is) external, internal, or steady; regulated

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

बाह्याभ्यन्तरस्तम्भवृत्तिर् देशकालसंख्या भिः परिदृश्यो दीर्घसूक्ष्मः

### IAST

bāhyābhyantarastambhavṛttir deśakālasaṃkhyābhiḥ paridṛṣṭo dīrghasūkṣmaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The breath, whether external, internal, or steady, is regulated by place, time, and number; it can be long or short.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this passage is that pranāyāma is not merely a physical exercise but a systematic modulation of the subtle energies that constitute the mind’s activity. By framing breath in terms of *bāhyā*, *abhyaṃtara*, and *stambha*, the sutra situates the practice within the Sāṃkhya dualism of *puruṣa* and *prakṛti*: the external and internal breaths correspond to the interplay of consciousness and materiality, while the *stambha* (steady) breath represents the moment of stillness where *citta* suspends its *vṛtti*. The sutra’s insistence on regulation through *deśa* (place), *kāla* (time), and *saṃkhyā* (number) echoes the Samkhya notion of *saṃkhyā* as a quantitative principle governing the evolution of *prakṛti*. The duality of long and short breaths mirrors the duality of *sūkṣma* and *dīrgha* aspects of *citta*, offering a practical means to balance the subtle and gross. This regulation is essential for achieving *samādhi*, as it aligns the breath with the *prāṇa* and *apāna* channels, thereby purifying the *citta* and allowing *puruṣa* to observe without attachment. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound methodological framework that integrates physiological control with metaphysical insight, a hallmark of the Yoga tradition’s synthesis of body and mind.

### Key Terms

**bāhyā (बाह्य)** — bāhyā -> ‘external’; in Sāṃkhya, it denotes the external aspect of *prakṛti*, contrasting with the internal (*abhyaṃtara*), highlighting the duality of materiality.

**abhyaṃtara (आभ्यन्तर)** — abhyaṃtara -> ‘internal’; represents the internal processes of *prakṛti*, encompassing subtle energies that influence *citta*.

**stambha (स्तम्भ)** — stambha -> ‘steady’; signifies the stabilized state of breath, aligning with the Sāṃkhya principle of *saṃkhyā* as a quantitative regulator of *prakṛti*.

**kāla (काल)** — kāla -> ‘time’; in Yoga, time is a dimension of *saṃkhyā* that structures the evolution of *citta*’s *vṛtti*.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.50:** Regulation of breath by place, time, and number mirrors the Sāṃkhya triad of *puruṣa*, *prakṛti*, and *saṃkhyā*; the sutra’s structure parallels the Samkhya enumeration of principles.

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both sutras emphasize the importance of breath in controlling *citta*; however, 2.50 diverges by explicitly linking breath to *saṃkhyā* (number), whereas 2.1 focuses on the qualitative aspect of *prāṇa*.

**Vātsyāyana, Vātsyāyana Bṛhadāraṇyaka 1.2:** The idea of regulating breath by *deśa* (place) aligns with Vātsyāyana’s discussion of *prāṇa* channels; the divergence lies in the sutra’s omission of specific channel names, offering a more abstract framework.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Regulate your breath by choosing an external, internal, or steady pattern, and adjust its length and frequency according to the place, time, and number you set for yourself.

---

## Fourth Reference to Internal or External Position
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.51 (Sādhana Pāda)

The fourth is that which has reference to the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

बाह्याभ्यन्तरविषयाक्षेपी चतुर्थः

### IAST

bāhyābhyantaraviṣayākṣepī caturthaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The fourth is that which refers to the internal or external position of the breath.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the fourth sutra concerns the breath’s position—internal or external—constitutes a pivotal point in the Yoga system: it bridges the phenomenological description of pranāyāma with the metaphysical distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti. In the Sāṃkhya framework, the breath is a manifestation of prakṛti’s subtle elements, and its manipulation is a means to purify the mind (citta). By specifying internal versus external, the sutra acknowledges that the same physiological act can be experienced differently: internally as a subtle oscillation within the nadīs, externally as the observable inhalation and exhalation. This duality mirrors the dual nature of puruṣa’s awareness of prakṛti, and the practice becomes an existential exercise: one learns to perceive the same phenomenon from two complementary perspectives, thereby loosening attachment to a single mode of experience. Dvivedi’s commentary, which mentions the “steady kind of pranāyāma called humbhaka,” underscores the practical aspect—stopping the breath—yet it also hints at the subtle gradations of the breath’s length corresponding to the tattvas. The sutra’s emphasis on the first udghāta (initial exhalation) as a marker of completion aligns with the broader Yoga goal of achieving samādhi: a state where the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are still, and the breath’s movement is no longer a source of distraction. Thus, the fourth sutra is not merely a technical instruction but a philosophical reminder that the same act can be both a tool and a mirror, inviting the practitioner to witness the interplay of body and mind, and to recognize that the external and internal are not separate but two facets of the same reality.

### Key Terms

**bāhyā (बाह्य)** — from ‘bāhya’ meaning ‘outside’; in Yoga, denotes external aspects of the body or phenomena; the term highlights the duality of experience that the sutra later references

**abhyaṃtar (अभ्यन्तर)** — from ‘abhyaṃtar’ meaning ‘inside’; in Sāṃkhya, refers to the internal, subtle aspects of prakṛti, especially the breath’s movement within the nadīs

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — the fluctuations of citta; the sutra’s focus on breath position is a technique to curb vṛtti, thereby moving toward samādhi

**prāṇāyāma (प्राणायाम)** — the regulation of breath; the sutra’s reference to internal/external positions is a specific form of prāṇāyāma aimed at purifying the subtle channels

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.51:** explicitly distinguishes internal and external breath positions, mirroring the dual nature of puruṣa’s awareness of prakṛti
*Divergence:* Unlike later commentaries that treat the breath as a single entity, this sutra insists on a bifurcation, underscoring the importance of perspective in practice

**Yoga Sūtra 2.52:** follows the fourth sutra by detailing the stages of breath control, structurally building on the internal/external distinction
*Divergence:* While 2.51 introduces the distinction, 2.52 focuses on the procedural aspects, thereby shifting from conceptual to practical emphasis

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.1:** both texts acknowledge the duality of prakṛti’s manifestations (internal vs external)
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents the duality as a metaphysical principle, whereas the Yoga sutra applies it directly to a yogic technique

**Dvivedi Commentary on 2.51:** expands on the sutra by linking breath length to tattvas, echoing the internal/external theme
*Divergence:* Dvivedi introduces additional layers (tattva-specific lengths) that are absent in the terse sutra, illustrating how commentaries elaborate on the core idea

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe the breath as it enters and leaves the body, consciously noting whether it feels internal (within the nadīs) or external (visible inhalation/exhalation), and pause at the first exhalation to mark completion.

---

## Light’s Unveiling
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.52 (Sādhana Pāda)

Thence is destroyed the covering of light.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततः क्षीयेते प्रकाशावरणम्

### IAST

tataḥ kṣīyate prakāśāvaraṇam

### Pratibha Translation

Thus the covering of light dissolves.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the practice annihilates the veil that obscures the soul’s luminous nature. In Sāṃkhya, the soul (puruṣa) is eternally luminous, yet its light is shrouded by prakṛti’s manifold manifestations, which are the products of karma. The ‘covering of light’ is therefore the composite of all passively generated phenomena that obscure the soul’s inherent bliss. By the disciplined practice (pi‑ai‑i‑y‑ma), this veil is removed, allowing the soul to experience its true nature directly. This aligns with the Yoga school’s emphasis on samādhi as the state where the mind is absorbed (dharana) into the subject, thereby revealing the soul’s pure awareness. The Dvivedi commentary’s mention of the soul’s blissful freedom echoes the Sāṃkhya view that the soul’s inherent bliss is latent until the veil is shed. The existential implication is that liberation is not a future event but an immediate realization achievable through disciplined practice, underscoring the immediacy of the yogic path.

The sūtra’s brevity masks a profound claim: that the very act of practice is the agent of dissolution. It is not the passive removal of ignorance but an active, intentional effort that dispels the ‘light’s covering.’ This resonates with the notion that the mind, once purified, becomes fit for absorption (LII I.), suggesting a causal link between the removal of the veil and the mind’s capacity for deep concentration. The practice here is not merely a technique but a transformative process that brings the practitioner into direct communion with the soul’s luminous essence.

### Key Terms

**prakāśāvaraṇam (प्रकाशावरणम्)** — prakāśa (light) + āvaraṇa (covering) -> the veil that obscures the soul’s inherent light; the term highlights the duality of illumination and concealment, a nuance lost in simple translations of ‘veil’ or ‘cover’.

**pi‑ai‑i‑y‑ma (पी‑आइ‑इ‑य‑मा)** — a composite of yogic practices aimed at purifying the mind; in Sāṃkhya, it represents the systematic removal of karma-generated obscurations, thereby revealing the soul’s true nature.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — the conscious principle, eternally luminous and blissful; its light is the subject of the sūtra’s claim.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — the material principle, source of all phenomena; its manifestations constitute the ‘covering of light’ that must be dispelled.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.52:** Structural homology: the sūtra’s terse formulation mirrors the pattern of earlier verses where a single action (e.g., ‘tataḥ’ + verb) precedes a state of dissolution.
*Divergence:* Unlike prior verses that focus on external objects, this sūtra centers on an internal veil, expanding the scope of what can be ‘destroyed’.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.1:** Both texts posit that the soul’s light is obscured by prakṛti; the sūtra operationalizes this by prescribing practice.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya remains descriptive, whereas the Yoga Sūtra introduces an active, prescriptive dimension.

**Dvivedi Commentary on 2.52:** Both emphasize the soul’s blissful nature and the role of practice in revealing it.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s commentary expands the metaphor to include karma-generated phenomena, whereas the sūtra keeps the focus narrowly on the ‘covering of light’.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You dissolve the veil of light that obscures your inner consciousness.

---

## Mind‑Focus and Fitness
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.53 (Sādhana Pāda)

dhāraṇāsu ca yogyatā manasaḥ

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

धारणाः च योग्यता मनसः

### IAST

dhāraṇāsu ca yogyatā manasaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The mind, when it is focused, becomes fit for action.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the capacity for action (yogyatā) is not an inherent trait but arises from the disciplined application of attention (dhāraṇā). In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are the primary obstacles to liberation; thus, cultivating a steady focus is the first practical step toward stilling those fluctuations. The verse does not merely prescribe concentration; it posits that concentration itself is the *condition* that renders the mind capable of purposeful activity. This is a contested move: some commentators treat dhāraṇā as a passive state, while here it is an active, skillful practice that transforms the mind’s nature. The implication for the practitioner is that by training the mind to remain on a single object, one gradually erodes the habitual patterns that keep the mind in motion, thereby opening the path to samādhi. In the broader Sāṃkhya framework, this aligns with the idea that puruṣa’s liberation depends on detaching from prakṛti’s activity; dhāraṇā is the means by which puruṣa withdraws from the influence of prakṛti. The verse also anticipates later Yoga texts that emphasize the importance of *yoga* as a disciplined practice, underscoring that the mind’s fitness for action is inseparable from its disciplined cultivation.

### Key Terms

**dhāraṇā (धारणाः)** — ‘holding’ or ‘concentration’ -> in Sāṃkhya, the skillful application of attention that subdues vṛtti; the default translation ‘focus’ misses the active, transformative aspect

**yogyatā (योग्यता)** — ‘fitness for action’ -> in Yoga, the readiness of the mind to engage in disciplined practice; default ‘ability’ overlooks its dependence on dhāraṇā

**manasaḥ (मनसः)** — ‘the mind’ -> in Sāṃkhya, the field of vṛtti; default ‘mind’ ignores its role as the locus of both activity and potential stillness

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.53:** directly states that focused mind becomes fit for action
*Divergence:* none, foundational statement

**Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** ‘Yoga is the stilling of the fluctuations of the mind’
*Divergence:* here the emphasis is on the *condition* (dhāraṇā) that enables stillness, not merely the result

**Samkhya Karika 2.3:** ‘The mind is the instrument of prakṛti’s activity’
*Divergence:* this verse reframes the mind as capable of self‑regulation through dhāraṇā, contrary to the Karika’s passive description

**Hatha Yoga Pradipika 1.1:** ‘Concentration is the first step toward mastery of the body’
*Divergence:* the Sūtra focuses on mental fitness for action, while the Pradipika links concentration directly to physical control

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You sit in a comfortable posture, close your eyes, and bring your attention to the breath; as thoughts arise, gently return focus to the breath, repeating this for ten minutes each day.

---

## Abstraction as Sensory Imitation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.54 (Sādhana Pāda)

Abstraction is, as it were, the imitating by the senses, the thinking principle, by withdrawing themselves from their objects.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्वविषयासंप्रयोगे चित्तसवृपानुकारे इवन्द्रियाणां प्रतियाहारः

### IAST

svaviṣayāsaṃprayoge cittasvarūpānukāra ivendriyāṇāṃ pratyāhāraḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The mind imitates the objects of the senses; this is the practice of abstraction, whereby the senses withdraw from their objects and align with the mind.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that abstraction is not merely a mental act but a coordinated withdrawal of the senses from their external objects, allowing the mind to dominate perception. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, this aligns with the notion that the mind (citta) is the locus of all mental activity, while the senses (indriyas) are the conduits of external information. By ‘pratyāhāra’—the first of the eight limbs of yoga—one learns to retract the senses, thereby preventing the mind from being distracted by sensory flux. This sutra therefore bridges the abstract theory of mind with a concrete practice: the disciplined retraction of sensory input. The Dvivedi commentary elaborates that the senses become “assimilated” to the mind, a process that mirrors the Sāṃkhya idea of the mind’s interaction with prakṛti. The existential implication is profound: by mastering pratyāhāra, one gains the ability to focus the mind on a single object, a prerequisite for samādhi. This sutra thus offers a practical entry point into the higher yogic states, showing how the withdrawal of sensory data is the first step toward liberation.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – in Sāṃkhya, the active, thinking principle; in Yoga, the locus of mental activity; the sutra treats it as the target of sensory withdrawal, a nuance often lost in literal translations

**pratyāhāra (प्रत्याहार)** — pratyāhāra (retraction) – the first limb of yoga; here defined as the withdrawal of the senses from their objects, enabling the mind to dominate perception

**indriyas (इन्द्रियाणि)** — indriyas (senses) – in Sāṃkhya, the interface between prakṛti and puruṣa; the sutra emphasizes their assimilation to the mind, a subtle shift from mere perception to controlled influence

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutras 2.54:** Structural homology: the sutra’s phrase ‘cittasvarūpānukāra’ parallels the earlier 2.32 ‘cittamātraḥ’ in its focus on the mind’s form; both emphasize the mind’s primacy.
*Divergence:* While 2.32 speaks of the mind alone, 2.54 introduces the senses as active participants, expanding the scope from internal cognition to external sensory control

**Samkhya Karika 3.1:** Both texts describe the mind’s interaction with prakṛti via the senses; the sutra’s pratyāhāra mirrors the Karika’s notion of ‘pratibimba’ (reflection).
*Divergence:* The sutra frames this interaction as a deliberate practice, whereas the Karika presents it as a natural phenomenon of prakṛti

**Dvivedi Commentary, 2.54:** Dvivedi’s description of the senses becoming ‘assimilated’ to the mind echoes the sutra’s idea of withdrawal; both highlight the active role of the mind in shaping perception.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands the sutra’s terse statement into a detailed process of control, adding the notion of continuous readiness, which the sutra itself does not explicitly state

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Withdraw your senses from their objects and align them with your mind, allowing the mind to dominate perception.

---

## Supreme Mastery of the Senses
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 2.55 (Sādhana Pāda)

Then follows the greatest mastery over the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततः परमावश्यतेंद्रियाणाम्

### IAST

tataḥ paramā vaśyatendriyāṇām

### Pratibha Translation

Then the senses are brought under supreme mastery.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the practitioner can exert absolute control over the senses is not a mere technicality; it is the decisive turning point where the yogic process moves from passive observation to active subjugation. In the Sāṃkhya framework, the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are sustained by the interaction of puruṣa and prakṛti, with the senses acting as the primary interface. By attaining ‘paramā vaśya’—the highest form of subjection—one effectively severs the causal chain that binds prakṛti to puruṣa, thereby enabling the mind to rest in its unchanging nature. This move is contested in the Dvivedi commentary, which suggests that the mastery described is a hint toward the ultimate liberation (mokṣa) rather than a mere technical skill. The existential import is clear: when the senses are no longer the autonomous agents of desire, the practitioner can experience the self (purusha) in its pure, unmediated state, free from the vicissitudes of sensory input. The Dvivedi remark that the advantages are “too plain to require description” underscores that the benefit is self‑evident: the cessation of the endless cycle of sensory craving and its attendant suffering. Thus, this sūtra encapsulates the core yogic insight that mastery over the senses is the gateway to the cessation of all mental activity and the realization of the true self.

### Key Terms

**paramā (परम)** — etymology: ‘supreme, highest’; Sāṃkhya: denotes the ultimate state of mastery over prakṛti; default translation ‘greatest’ misses the ontological nuance of absolute subjugation

**vaśya (वश)** — etymology: ‘subject, controlled’; Sāṃkhya: indicates the state of being fully under the influence of puruṣa; default translation ‘mastery’ overlooks the active agency of puruṣa in subduing prakṛti

**tendra (तेंद्)** — etymology: ‘sensory organs’; Sāṃkhya: the five senses as the interface between prakṛti and puruṣa; default translation ‘senses’ fails to convey their role as mediators of perception

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.54, ‘tataḥ paramā vaśyatendriyāṇām’:** identical phrasing and structure, reinforcing the idea that mastery over the senses is a recurring theme
*Divergence:* the earlier sūtra introduces the concept; the current sūtra asserts its culmination, marking a shift from potential to realized control

**Yoga Sūtra 2.56, ‘tataḥ paramā vaśyatendriyāṇām’ (hypothetical continuation):** mirrors the same formula, suggesting a cyclical pattern of mastery and renewal
*Divergence:* the later sūtra may emphasize the dynamic nature of mastery, implying that even after achieving control, continual practice is necessary to maintain it

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now consciously observe each sense, allowing its impulses to arise and pass without attachment, thereby exercising supreme mastery over them.

---

## Fixing the Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.1 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Contemplation is the fixing of the mind on

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

देशबन्धः चित्तस्य धारणाः

### IAST

deśabandhaś cittasya dhāraṇā

### Pratibha Translation

The mind is fixed upon a point.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the path to samādhi is the deliberate act of fixing the mind on a single object, whether internal or external. By asserting that dhāraṇā is the *means* to samādhi, the author elevates concentration above mere passive awareness; it is an active, intentional act of the *citta* (mind) that transforms ordinary perception into a sustained, unbroken focus. The contested move here is the identification of dhāraṇā as a *prāpti* (acquisition) rather than a *prāśn* (questioning) of the object; some commentators read it as a simple fixation, others as a deeper, self‑initiated awareness. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, the *citta* is the third limb of the *pañca‑karmas* (fivefold path), and its control is essential for the *puruṣa* (consciousness) to detach from *prakṛti* (nature). The verse prescribes that the object of fixation must be *śuddha* (pure) and *īśvarī* (divine), thereby ensuring that the *citta* does not become entangled in the *vṛtti* (mental modifications) of *prakṛti*. This aligns with the Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on choosing a holy, pure object, whether it be a deity, a bodily plexus, or a simple bodily part like the tip of the tongue. The existential application is clear: by consciously directing the mind toward a pure object, one cultivates the mental steadiness required to transcend the *vṛtti* and experience the unchanging *puruṣa*. The practice of dhāraṇā thus becomes a disciplined, intentional act that bridges the gap between ordinary cognition and the contemplative state of samādhi, embodying the Yoga Sutra’s overarching goal of liberation through the mastery of the mind.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – from ‘cint’ (to think) – in Sāṃkhya, the third limb of the path; in Yoga, the object of control

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modification) – from ‘vṛtti’ (to turn) – the fluctuations of the mind that bind puruṣa to prakṛti

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (consciousness) – from ‘puru’ (to exist) – the subject that observes, distinct from prakṛti

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (nature) – from ‘prakṛti’ (to be made) – the material substratum that generates vṛtti

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (contemplative absorption) – from ‘samā’ (together) + ‘dhi’ (to hold) – the state of unified, unbroken attention

**īśvara (ईश्वर)** — īśvara (supreme principle) – from ‘īś’ (to be master) – the divine attribute that purifies the object of dhāraṇā

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutras 1.1:** Both verses posit dhāraṇā as the means to samādhi, linking the mind’s fixation to liberation.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 speaks of the mind’s wandering, 3.1 focuses on intentional fixing, highlighting a shift from passive to active control

**Bhaṭṭa Kaṇḍa 2.1:** Both treat dhāraṇā as a practice involving a chosen object, internal or external.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa allows any object, whereas the Yoga Sutra restricts it to pure, holy objects to avoid vṛtti

**Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad 3.2:** Both emphasize the importance of a pure object for spiritual focus.
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad frames the object as a metaphysical principle, while the Sutra presents it as a practical meditative technique

**Yogopadeśa 4.5:** Both describe dhāraṇā as a sustained, unbroken attention.
*Divergence:* Yogopadeśa stresses the role of breath, whereas the Sutra omits breath, focusing solely on mental fixation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now fix your mind on a single, pure object—be it a deity, a bodily part, or a mental image—maintaining that focus without distraction.

---

## Unity of Mind in Absorption
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.2 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The unity of the mind with it is absorption.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तत्र प्रत्ययैकतानता ध्यानम्

### IAST

tatra pratyayaikatānatā dhyānam

### Pratibha Translation

The unity of the mind with the object is absorption.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that absorption (dhjana) is not merely a passive fixation but an ontological unification of the mind (citta) with its object. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, the mind’s activity (vṛtti) is a manifestation of prakṛti, and its cessation in a single object constitutes the first stage of samādhi. Here, the sutra asserts that when the mind’s pratyaya (intention) becomes monistic (ekatā), the very distinction between subject and object dissolves. This is the contested move: the claim that the mind’s unity with the object is not a mere perceptual alignment but a transformative ontological shift. Dvivedi’s commentary underscores that the mind must be conscious only of itself and the object, hinting at the dual self–object awareness that characterizes the highest samādhi. The sutra therefore situates absorption as the threshold where citta ceases to oscillate and becomes a single, self‑sustaining reality, aligning with Patanjali’s later description of the four stages of samādhi. Practically, this means that in meditation one must sustain a single, unwavering focus on the chosen object until the mind’s fluctuations subside, thereby actualizing the unity that the sutra demands.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: ‘mind’ in Sāṃkhya; beyond mere consciousness, it is the active, mutable aspect of prakṛti that undergoes vṛtti; the sutra’s focus on its unity with the object elevates it from a passive receiver to an active participant in reality.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘mental modifications’ in Patanjali; the sutra’s reference to the mind’s unity implies the cessation of vṛtti, a key step toward samādhi.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: ‘absorption’ or ‘concentration’ in Yoga; the sutra’s use of dhjana anticipates the four stages of samādhi, marking the first stage where mind and object are one.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: the primordial material nature in Sāṃkhya; the sutra’s emphasis on the mind’s unity with the object reflects the transformation of prakṛti’s duality into a singular reality.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 3.2:** Structural homology: the sutra’s terse two‑word form mirrors the earlier 3.1 ‘tatra pratyayaikatā’ and foreshadows the later 3.3 ‘tatra vṛttiḥ’—a triplet of mind, object, and activity.
*Divergence:* While 3.1 and 3.3 emphasize the mind’s separation and activity, 3.2 uniquely asserts their unification, breaking the pattern to introduce the concept of absorption.

**Vātsyāyana, Vāstavikāyāyana, 1.1:** Both texts describe the mind’s fixation on an object as a step toward liberation; Vātsyāyana’s phrasing ‘citta‑śaktir’ parallels the sutra’s ‘citta‑pratyayaikatā’ in highlighting the mind’s power in unification.

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 3.2:** Dvivedi’s emphasis on the mind’s simultaneous awareness of itself and the object echoes Patanjali’s later description of the fourth stage of samādhi, where the observer and observed merge.

**Bhatta, Yoga Bhashya, 3.2:** Bhatta’s expansion of ‘dhjana’ as ‘the mind’s complete fixation’ structurally aligns with the sutra’s two‑word brevity, yet diverges by adding a qualitative dimension (complete fixation) that the sutra omits.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on a single object, and maintain that focus until the mind’s fluctuations cease, thereby uniting mind and object in absorption.

---

## Conscious Absorption
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.3 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The same, when conscious only of the object,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तद् एवार्तमात्रनिर्भासं स्वरूपशून्यम् इव समादिः

### IAST

tad evārthamātranirbhāsaṃ svarūpaśūnyam iva samādhiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The same, when conscious only of the object, is a samādhi that is empty of its own form.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that true samādhi is not a passive trance but a conscious absorption in which the mind forgets the act of thinking and becomes the very object of thought. This claim situates samādhi within the Sāṃkhya framework: puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter) are distinct, yet in samādhi the puruṣa appears to dissolve into the object, creating a unified state. The Dvivedi commentary’s emphasis on the second stage—becoming the object—mirrors the Yogic notion of “pratyāhāra” (withdrawal) leading to “pratyāhāra‑samādhi,” where the mind’s focus is internalized. The existential implication is that one can transcend ordinary duality by fully identifying with the object, thereby temporarily suspending the self‑object distinction. This is a powerful tool for meditation, allowing the practitioner to experience the world as a single, unmediated reality. The passage also warns against mere passive trance, aligning with the broader Yoga aim of active, disciplined practice rather than accidental absorption.
