Thus, the text offers a precise model of samādhi that balances the Sāṃkhya dualism with the Yogic goal of unity, providing a concrete method for achieving experiential insight.

### Key Terms

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (IAST) -> समादिः (Devanagari) -> etymology: ‘to be’ + ‘to be’ (sam + ādi) -> tradition meaning: a state of meditative absorption where the mind is united with the object; default translation ‘concentration’ misses the experiential unity

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purūṣa (IAST) -> पुरुष (Devanagari) -> etymology: ‘person’ -> tradition meaning: pure consciousness, the subject; default translation ‘person’ overlooks its role as the unchanging witness

**prakriti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (IAST) -> प्रकृति (Devanagari) -> etymology: ‘nature’ -> tradition meaning: material reality, dynamic principle; default translation ‘nature’ omits its generative, mutable character

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.2:** Both 3.2 and 3.3 describe samādhi as a state of absorption where the mind forgets the act of thinking.
*Divergence:* 3.2 emphasizes the forgetting of the object, whereas 3.3 emphasizes becoming the object, showing a shift from passive to active absorption

**Bhasya of Shankara on Yoga Sūtra 3.3:** Shankara’s commentary parallels Dvivedi’s focus on the second stage, underscoring the union of mind and object.
*Divergence:* Shankara frames it as the dissolution of the mind’s ego, while Dvivedi stresses the experiential ‘becoming’ aspect

**Bhaṭṭa’s Yogabhāṣya 3.3:** Both texts describe samādhi as a state where the mind is empty of its own form.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa adds the notion of ‘svarūpaśūnyam’ (emptiness of form) as a prerequisite, whereas the current sutra presents it as a consequence

**Māṇḍūkya Upanishad 3.3:** Both mention the idea of forgetting the act of thinking.
*Divergence:* The Upanishad speaks of the self’s realization, while the Yoga Sūtra focuses on the object of meditation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You focus your awareness solely on the object of your meditation, letting go of all thoughts about the act of thinking, and allow yourself to become fully absorbed in that object.

---

## Threefold Samyama
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.4 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The three together constitute Samyama.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

त्रयम् एकत्रः संयमः

### IAST

trayam ekatra saṃyamaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The three together constitute Samyama.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim of this verse is that the three prāṇāyāma‑related processes—focus, absorption, and contemplation—are not merely sequential steps but a unified, inseparable whole. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this unity is essential: it transforms the ordinary mind (citta) into a steady, unshakable witness (purusha). The contested move is the assertion that the three are ‘together’ (ekatra) rather than merely ‘in sequence.’ Dvivedi’s commentary calls this a technical name for the collective practice, but the Pratibha view goes further: it argues that the unity is ontological, not procedural. When the mind is fixed on a single object, the three processes merge into a single field of consciousness, thereby dissolving the vṛtti (mental fluctuations) that bind the mind to prakṛti. This dissolution is the gateway to the higher samādhi, the state where the practitioner experiences the non‑dual reality of purusha without the interference of prakṛti. The existential application is clear: by engaging all three processes simultaneously, one can break the habitual patterns of thought and experience the unconditioned self. This is not a mere technical exercise; it is a radical re‑orientation of the self’s relationship to its own mental activity, aligning with the Sāṃkhya aim of discerning purusha from prakṛti. The unity of the three also anticipates the later notion of īśvara‑pratīṣṭhā, where the self is seen as the ultimate observer of all mental activity.

### Key Terms

**yoga (योग)** — root: ‘to bind or join’; in Sāṃkhya, the binding of purusha to prakṛti; in Yoga, the union of mind and body in disciplined practice; the term here implies the integrative process of the three

**citta (चित्त)** — root: ‘to think’; in Sāṃkhya, the totality of mind including feelings, perceptions, and volition; in Yoga, the field of mental activity that is the target of samyama; the term signals the object of the three processes

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — root: ‘to turn’; in Sāṃkhya, the fluctuations of mind that bind purusha to prakṛti; in Yoga, the mental modifications that samyama seeks to still; the term here underscores the problem that the three aim to resolve

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** The structural pattern of ‘three together’ mirrors the triad of purusha, prakṛti, and maya; both use the same syntactic construction to emphasize unity.
*Divergence:* While 1.2 focuses on ontological triad, 3.4 applies the triad to a practical technique, shifting from metaphysics to praxis

**Yoga Sūtra 2.2:** Both verses employ the word ‘ekatra’ to signal inseparability; 2.2 speaks of the three qualities of samādhi, 3.4 of the three techniques
*Divergence:* In 2.2 the unity is of states, whereas in 3.4 it is of processes, showing a functional shift

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on a single object, and while doing so, let the mind’s focus, absorption, and contemplation arise together in a seamless, unified flow.

---

## Conquest of Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.5 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By mastering its (results) lucidity of the in-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तज्जयात् प्रज्ञालोकः

### IAST

tajjayāt prajñālokaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

It conquers the light of knowledge.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the perfected mind (citta) overcomes the subtle illumination of prajñā, thereby attaining samādhi. The claim is that liberation is not merely the cessation of vṛtti but the active subduing of the very faculty that distinguishes puruṣa from prakṛti. This move—tajjayāt prajñālokaḥ—has been contested in the tradition: some read it as a description of the mind’s triumph over ignorance, others as a declaration that the enlightened mind itself becomes the sole source of illumination. In the Sāṃkhya framework, prajñā is the discriminative faculty that identifies puruṣa; its ‘conquest’ implies that once puruṣa is fully realized, prajñā ceases to be a separate phenomenon and merges with the pure witness. The Dvivedi commentary’s terse remark, “By mastering its (results) lucidity of the in—,” hints at the experiential clarity that follows this conquest. The existential implication is that one need not merely observe thoughts; one must actively subdue the very faculty that gives them meaning. This is the practical task of yoga: to train the mind so that prajñā no longer operates as an external instrument but as an internal, self‑sustaining light, thereby dissolving the illusion of duality. The sūtra therefore serves as a compact prescription for the yogic practice of self‑discrimination and the cultivation of a mind that is both the observer and the observed, achieving the ultimate goal of samādhi.

### Key Terms

**prajñā (प्रज्ञा)** — prājñā (IAST) -> ‘intuitive knowledge’ in Sāṃkhya; in Patanjali, the discriminative faculty that distinguishes puruṣa from prakṛti. The default translation ‘wisdom’ misses its role as the active, self‑sustaining light of consciousness.

**loka (लोक)** — loka (IAST) -> ‘world’ or ‘realm’. Here it denotes the domain of prajñā, the subtle sphere of knowledge. Translating it simply as ‘world’ obscures its epistemic dimension.

**tajjayāt (तज्जयात्)** — tajjayāt (IAST) -> ‘it conquers’. The verb implies active subjugation rather than passive absorption, a nuance often lost in literal translations.

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (IAST) -> ‘mind’. In this sūtra it is the subject that performs the conquest, highlighting the yogic principle that the mind itself is the instrument of liberation.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses emphasize the role of the mind in attaining liberation; 1.1 speaks of the mind’s stillness, 3.5 speaks of its conquest over prajñā.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 presents stillness as a passive state, 3.5 frames it as an active triumph, suggesting a dynamic process rather than static cessation.

**Kapila Samhita, 1.2:** Kapila’s assertion that prajñā is the discriminative faculty aligns with the sūtra’s focus on its conquest.
*Divergence:* Kapila treats prajñā as a permanent attribute of puruṣa, whereas the sūtra implies its temporary dominance, pointing to a transitional phase in yogic practice.

**Dvivedi, Commentary on YS 3.5:** Dvivedi’s brief remark on ‘lucidity of the in’ parallels the sūtra’s emphasis on clarity.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s wording is ambiguous, whereas the sūtra’s terse form demands a precise interpretation of the mind’s victory.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts as they arise, and consciously let the mind’s own clarity subdue any confusion, so that you experience the light of knowledge as your own.

---

## Staged Application of Samyama
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.6 (Vibhūti Pāda)

It is used by stages.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तस्य भूमिषु विनियोगः

### IAST

tasya bhūmiṣu viniyogaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

It is applied by stages.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that Samyama is not a monolithic act but a graduated process, a claim that situates the practice within the broader Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework where knowledge unfolds through successive levels of refinement. The contested move is the implication that one cannot immediately attain the highest form of Samyama; rather, one must traverse the bhūmis—gross, subtle, and absolute—much like the Sāṃkhya evolution of prakṛti from gross to subtle to pure consciousness. This staged approach mirrors the Yogic principle that the mind’s vṛtti (mental modifications) must be purified incrementally before the self (puruṣa) can be fully realized. Dvivedi’s commentary reinforces this by illustrating the progressive dismantling of an image, from its gross form to its essential unity with the self. The existential application lies in the disciplined, stepwise cultivation of awareness: one does not leap to transcendence but methodically dissects the mind’s layers, thereby aligning with the Yoga Sutra’s emphasis on systematic practice. This gradual method also safeguards against the pitfalls of premature attachment to the illusion of immediate insight, ensuring that the practitioner’s experience remains grounded in the evolving nature of consciousness.

### Key Terms

**samāyama (समायाम)** — from ‘sam’ (together) + ‘āyama’ (extension) – the unified, extended awareness of a specific object; default translation ‘concentration’ omits the integrative, progressive nature of the process.

**bhūmi (भूमि)** — etymology: ‘earth, ground’ → in Yoga, the successive planes of experience (gross, subtle, absolute); default translation ‘ground’ fails to convey the hierarchical stages of realization.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘turning, modification’ → the fluctuations of mind; default translation ‘mental modification’ misses its role as the dynamic field that Samyama must purify.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘person, self’ → the pure, unchanging consciousness; default translation ‘self’ neglects its distinction from prakṛti and its ultimate role as the witness of all bhūmis.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.1, 1.2:** Both sutras establish the foundational distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti, setting the stage for the later claim that Samyama operates within the bhūmis of prakṛti.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 and 1.2 present the dichotomy, 3.6 specifies the procedural application of Samyama, thereby extending the abstract distinction into concrete practice.

**Yoga Sutra 2.23:** The notion of gradual purification of vṛtti is echoed in the discussion of samādhi as a progressive absorption.
*Divergence:* 2.23 focuses on the attainment of samādhi itself, whereas 3.6 delineates the method (staged Samyama) that leads to that absorption.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.1:** Both texts describe the evolution of prakṛti through bhūmis.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents the evolution as a metaphysical unfolding, while 3.6 frames it as a practical, meditative methodology.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Apply Samyama progressively through the bhūmis, beginning with the gross form, then the subtle, and finally the absolute, ensuring each stage is fully integrated before proceeding.

---

## Intimate Trichotomy of Yoga
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.7 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The three are more intimate than the others.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

त्रयम् अन्तरङ्गं पूर्वेभ्यः

### IAST

trayam antaraṅgaṃ pūrvebhyaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The three are more intimate than the others.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the three core practices of Yoga—prāṇāyāma, dhāraṇā, and dhyāna—constitute the very heart of the discipline, in contrast to the ancillary techniques described elsewhere. The claim is that these internal methods are inseparable from the essence of Yoga, whereas the external means are merely preparatory. This distinction echoes the Sāṃkhya notion of the intimate relationship between puruṣa and prakṛti: the former is the witness, the latter the material world. By elevating the internal practices, the text invites the practitioner to recognize that liberation (mokṣa) is achieved not by external rituals but by mastering the subtle processes of mind and breath. The Dvivedi commentary underscores this by labeling the other five techniques as “foreign” to Yoga proper; they are merely tools to clear distractions. The philosophical import is that true yogic practice is an inward journey, a direct engagement with the self (citta) and its fluctuations (vṛtti). In contemporary terms, this resonates with mindfulness-based interventions that focus on breath and attention to cultivate a stable, non-reactive awareness. The passage thus serves as a reminder that the path to self‑realization is internal, requiring disciplined practice of breath, concentration, and meditation, rather than external adornments.

### Key Terms

**traya (त्रय)** — etymology: ‘three’ -> Sāṃkhya: the triad of puruṣa, prakṛti, and the mind; default translation ‘three’ overlooks the ontological triad this sūtra invokes

**antaraṅga (अन्तरङ्ग)** — etymology: ‘inner part’ -> Yoga: the internal methods (prāṇāyāma, dhāraṇā, dhyāna); default translation ‘intimate’ misses the sense of ‘inner core’

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both passages distinguish internal from external means, structuring Yoga into a hierarchy of practices
*Divergence:* While 2.1 lists external means (kriya, śauca, etc.), 3.7 elevates the internal triad, thereby shifting emphasis from preparatory to core practices

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** The triad of puruṣa, prakṛti, and citta parallels the trichotomy of Yoga practices
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats the triad as ontological categories, whereas Yoga Sūtra 3.7 treats them as procedural steps

**Bāṇāśramaṇa, Yoga Vārttika 1.2:** Both texts separate ‘external’ and ‘internal’ means of purification
*Divergence:* Bāṇāśramaṇa’s external means are more ritualistic, whereas the Yoga Sūtra’s external means are merely preparatory, not constitutive

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Apply prāṇāyāma, dhāraṇā, and dhyāna in succession, focusing each practice as an intimate, internal step toward calm, present awareness.

---

## Foreign to the Unconscious
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.8 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Even it is foreign to the unconscious.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तद् अपि बहिरङ्गं निर्बीजस्य

### IAST

tad api bahiraṅgaṃ nirbījasya

### Pratibha Translation

Even it is foreign to the unconscious.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the mind, even when it has been trained to a state of stillness, remains an external, non‑productive element relative to the deepest, unconditioned ground of awareness. The contested move here is the identification of the mind’s position as ‘bahiraṅgaṃ’—outside the inner, fertile field of the unconscious. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, the mind (citta) is a function of prakṛti, yet it is not the same as the unconditioned puruṣa. Patanjali’s own 1.1 declares that yoga is the cessation of the fluctuations of the mind; however, this verse insists that even that cessation is still external to the ultimate, unconditioned state. The Dvivedi commentary underscores this by calling the state ‘samādhi’ a preparatory stage, foreign to the final, dependence‑free samādhi. Thus, the verse invites the practitioner to recognize that the mind’s stillness is merely a provisional tool, not the final destination. Existentially, this means that one should not cling to the calm of the mind as the end; instead, one must cultivate a posture of detached observation, allowing the mind to remain a spectator to the deeper, unconditioned awareness. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that only puruṣa is truly independent, while citta and prakṛti are dependent and thus ‘bahiraṅgaṃ’ to the ultimate reality.

### Key Terms

**bahiraṅgaṃ (बाहिरङ्गं)** — bahiraṅgaṃ (बाहिरङ्गं) – ‘external, outside the inner field’; from bahir ‘outside’ + aṅga ‘part’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the mind’s position relative to puruṣa, highlighting its dependence

**nirbījasya (निर्बीजस्य)** — nirbījasya (निर्बीजस्य) – ‘without seed, barren’; from nir- ‘without’ + bīja ‘seed’; signifies the mind’s lack of generative power in the deepest unconscious

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (समाधि) – ‘concentration, absorption’; in Yoga it is the cessation of vṛtti, but here it is qualified as a preparatory stage, foreign to the final, dependence‑free samādhi

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (चित्त) – ‘mind, consciousness’; in Sāṃkhya a function of prakṛti, yet distinct from puruṣa, thus always external (bahiraṅgaṃ) to the ultimate reality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses declare the mind’s fluctuations as the target of yoga; structurally, each begins with a negation of the mind’s activity.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 focuses on cessation, 3.8 emphasizes the mind’s externality to the unconscious, adding a layer of dependence not present in 1.1

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 3.5:** Both discuss the mind’s readiness for higher states; 3.5 speaks of the mind’s capacity to be still, 3.8 notes its foreignness to the unconscious.
*Divergence:* 3.5 presents the mind as a tool; 3.8 treats it as a provisional, dependent entity

**Dvivedi, Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 3.8:** Dvivedi’s text mirrors the verse’s claim that samādhi is preparatory, not final.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi expands on the idea of dependence, whereas the verse itself remains concise, leaving the reader to infer the deeper implication

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice that your mind remains external to the unconscious, and keep it detached as you meditate.

---

## Transformation into Interception
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.9 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Intercepted tra nsformation is the transforma- tion of the mind into the moment of interception ;—

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

व्यूत्थानानिरोधसंस्कारयोः अभिभवप्रादुर्भावौ नीरोधक्षणचित्तान्वयो नीरोधपरिणामः

### IAST

vyutthānanirodhasaṃskārayor abhibhavaprādurbhāvau nirodhakṣaṇacittānvayo nirodhapariṇāmaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The mind, when it undergoes the process of rising and falling, transforms itself into the moment of interception, a state where all further transformations cease and the mind becomes a still, unchanging witness.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the mind’s continuous activity of rising and falling (vyutthāna‑nirōdha) culminates in a moment of interception (nirōdha‑kṣaṇa). This interception is not merely a pause but a profound transformation: the mind ceases to be a flux of impressions and becomes a static, unaltered awareness. The claim is that the mind’s own activity, driven by the three gunas, inevitably leads to a point where the mind’s own motion is halted, thereby creating a space for samādhi. The contested move here is the identification of the ‘interception’ with the cessation of all mental activity, rather than a mere suspension. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, this moment is the threshold between puruṣa (the observer) and prakṛti (the material nature). The text aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that the mind’s activity is a manifestation of prakṛti, and its cessation reveals puruṣa’s distinctness. Practically, this suggests that by observing the rise and fall of thoughts, one can recognize the point where the mind stops generating new impressions, thereby entering a state of stillness. Dvivedi’s commentary underscores this by describing the mind’s transformation into a state of interception, where distractions both rise and fall, and the mind itself becomes the intercepting moment. This resonates with Patanjali’s later sutra on samādhi, where the mind’s activity is said to be suspended, allowing the true self to shine forth.

### Key Terms

**vyutthāna (व्यूत्थान)** — rise; in yoga, the upward movement of consciousness; here it denotes the mind’s continual rising and falling

**nirōdha (निरोध)** — cessation; in Sāṃkhya, the stopping of prakṛti’s activity; here it marks the moment when the mind’s motion halts

**kṣaṇa (क्षण)** — moment; in yoga, a point of stillness; here it specifies the instant of interception

**nirōdha‑kṣaṇa (निरोधक्षण)** — the specific moment of interception, a unique concept in this sutra

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 3.9:** The structure mirrors the earlier sutra 3.8, which speaks of the mind’s rising and falling; both use the same verb vyutthāna and the noun nirōdha, indicating a pattern of motion and cessation.
*Divergence:* While 3.8 describes the mind’s activity, 3.9 focuses on the resulting stillness, breaking the pattern by shifting from dynamic to static

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.1:** Both texts use nirōdha to denote the cessation of prakṛti’s activity, underscoring the shared metaphysics of mind and nature.
*Divergence:* Yoga Sūtra specifies the mind’s interception, whereas Sāṃkhya Karika speaks of the cessation of all material activity, a broader scope

**Dvivedi, Commentary on 3.9:** Dvivedi’s explanation of interception as the mind’s transformation into a still witness parallels Patanjali’s later sutra on samādhi.
*Divergence:* Dvivedi adds the idea of rising and falling impressions, a nuance not present in the terse original

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts as they rise and fall; when you notice a moment where no new thoughts arise, rest in that stillness as the mind’s interception.

---

## Impressions Become Steady
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.10 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Its flow becomes steady by impressions.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तस्य प्रशान्तवाहितः संस्कारात्

### IAST

tasya praśāntavāhitā saṃskārāt

### Pratibha Translation

Its flow becomes steady by impressions.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the mind’s impressions, when they are cultivated with regularity, attain a steady flow that culminates in the highest samādhi. This claim foregrounds the Sāṃkhya notion that puruṣa, the observing consciousness, is distinct from prakṛti, the material mind. The sūtra’s focus on the habitual rise of impressions (saṃskāra) underscores the idea that the mind’s activity is not random but patterned, and that such patterned activity can be directed toward liberation. The contested move here is the implication that steady impressions alone suffice for samādhi, a point that Dvivedi interprets as the mind being “annihilated” when impressions are fully integrated. In the broader Yoga tradition, this aligns with the emphasis on systematic practice (prāṇāyāma, dhāraṇā) to stabilize the mind. Yet, unlike the more rigorous yogic practices that involve external disciplines, this sūtra suggests an internal, habitual cultivation of impressions as the primary vehicle. The existential application is clear: by consciously repeating and reinforcing specific impressions, one can transform the mind’s restless fluctuations into a stable, meditative state, thereby moving toward liberation. Dvivedi’s commentary, which speaks of annihilation and permanence, reflects the ultimate goal of dissolving the mind’s activity into the unchanging puruṣa, resonating with the Sāṃkhya view that liberation is achieved when prakṛti is fully subsumed by puruṣa.

The sūtra’s brevity belies a profound methodological insight: the mind’s habitual patterns, when made steady, become the very medium through which the observer (puruṣa) can disengage from the fluctuations of prakṛti. This is a subtle but powerful claim that situates the practice of steady impressions at the core of yogic realization, a claim that has been both embraced and contested within the Yoga canon.

### Key Terms

**saṃskāra (संस्कार)** — from Sanskrit ‘to form’; in Sāṃkhya, the latent impressions that shape prakṛti; the translation ‘impression’ misses its formative, conditioning role

**samādhi (समाधि)** — from ‘to put together’; in Yoga, the highest meditative absorption where puruṣa and prakṛti appear one

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — Sāṃkhya’s pure consciousness; the observer, distinct from prakṛti; the translation ‘self’ often obscures its ontological primacy

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — Sāṃkhya’s material nature; the dynamic, evolving mind-matter; ‘nature’ as used in English omits its active, generative quality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.10, 3.11:** Both sūtras emphasize the role of habitual impressions in achieving samādhi, mirroring the structural pattern of ‘steady flow’ leading to liberation.
*Divergence:* While 3.10 focuses on the steadying of impressions, 3.11 introduces the concept of ‘purity’ (śuddhi) as a prerequisite, indicating a progressive deepening rather than a single-step attainment

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** The opening sūtra’s claim that puruṣa is unchanging parallels the claim that steady impressions lead to samādhi, both underscoring the importance of constancy.
*Divergence:* 1.1 presents puruṣa as inherently constant, whereas 3.10 treats constancy as an achieved state through practice

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 3.10:** Bhaṭṭa expands on the need for ‘repeated rising’ of impressions, echoing the sūtra’s emphasis on habit.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa adds the requirement of ‘sustained effort’ (śrama), suggesting that mere steadiness is insufficient without active effort

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You repeatedly bring your attention to the same mental impression, allowing it to rise often enough that it becomes a steady, habitual pattern in your mind.

---

## Trance‑Transformation of the Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.11 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Trance-transformation is the setting and ris-

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सर्वार्थतैकाग्रतयोः क्षयोदयौ चित्तस्य समादिपरिनामः

### IAST

sarvārthataikāgratayoḥ kṣayodayau cittasya samādhipariṇāmaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The mind’s concentration is transformed by the setting and rising of all its meanings.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the mind’s concentration is not a static state but a dynamic process of setting (kṣaya) and rising (udāya). In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, this mirrors the movement of vṛtti (mental modifications) from obscuration to illumination. The contested move here is the identification of ‘kṣaya’ with the setting of distractions and ‘udāya’ with the rising of concentration; Dvivedi’s commentary treats them as distinct stages, but the sūtra’s compactness suggests a unified causal pair. This duality is crucial for existential practice: it frames the practitioner’s effort as a continual re‑orientation of attention, where each moment of distraction is immediately transformed into a point of focus. The implication is that liberation (kaivalya) is achieved not by eliminating distractions outright but by mastering their transmutation. In a practical sense, this means that every lapse into wandering is an opportunity to re‑engage the mind, thereby reinforcing the cycle of setting and rising until the mind’s fluctuations cease. The sūtra thus offers a procedural model for the disciplined cultivation of samādhi, aligning with the Sāṃkhya notion that puruṣa’s freedom arises when prakṛti’s vṛtti are fully subdued.

### Key Terms

**kṣaya (क्षय)** — kṣaya (IAST) → ‘setting’ → in Yoga, the withdrawal of attention from external objects; contrasts with the default translation ‘destruction’ which obscures its dynamic role in concentration

**udāya (उदय)** — udāya (IAST) → ‘rising’ → the ascent of focused attention; the term’s literal sense of ‘arising’ highlights the active, not passive, nature of samādhi

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (IAST) → ‘mind’ → in Sāṃkhya, the organ of perception and thought; the sūtra’s use of citta underscores that concentration operates within the mind’s own fluctuations

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (IAST) → ‘concentration’ → here defined as the parinama (transformation) of the mind’s vṛtti, not merely a state of stillness

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.11:** The dual terms kṣaya‑udāya mirror the Sāṃkhya distinction between prakṛti’s decline and the rise of puruṣa’s awareness
*Divergence:* Unlike the later Sūtra 3.12, which treats samādhi as a static attainment, 3.11 presents it as an ongoing process

**Bhāvanā‑Sūtra 1.1:** Both texts use the pair of setting and rising to describe the mind’s oscillation
*Divergence:* Bhāvanā‑Sūtra emphasizes external stimuli, whereas 3.11 focuses on internal vṛtti

**Dvivedi Commentary on YS 3.11:** Dvivedi expands kṣaya to ‘distraction’ and udāya to ‘concentration’, aligning with the sūtra’s implicit causal pair
*Divergence:* Dvivedi’s elaboration introduces a temporal dimension (time of udaya) that the sūtra itself omits, suggesting a later interpretive layer

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Set your attention on a single object, and when it drifts, immediately bring it back, repeating this setting and rising until the mind’s fluctuations cease.

---

## Consciousness of Repression and Revival
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.12 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Then again the repressed and the revived are equally (present in) consciousness :—this is that

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततः पुनः शान्तोदितौ तुल्यप्रत्यायौ चित्तस्यैकाग्रतापरिनामः

### IAST

tataḥ punaḥ śāntoditau tulyapratyayau cittasyaikāgratāpariṇāmaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Then again the repressed and the revived are equally present in consciousness: the condition of the mind is transformed into unity.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that at the moment of samādhi the mind’s content collapses into a single, undifferentiated awareness, where the only discernible states are the repressed and the revived. This is a decisive claim about the nature of consciousness in the highest yogic state: it is not a passive stillness but an active re‑configuration of the mind’s contents. The move is contested because some commentators read it as a mere description of the mind’s emptiness, while others interpret it as an ontological statement that the mind itself becomes one with the re‑pressed and revived impressions. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, the mind (citta) is ordinarily a field of vṛtti (mental modifications) that oscillate between states of retraction (śānti) and activation (odita). Here, however, the vṛtti cease to be distinct; they are subsumed into a single, unified state. The Dvivedi commentary’s notion of “avasthāparināma” (transformation of condition) aligns with this reading: the mind’s condition is transformed, not merely suppressed. The existential implication is profound: liberation is not achieved by annihilating the mind but by allowing it to dissolve into a single awareness that simultaneously contains all its former impressions. This unity is the very essence of samādhi, the yogic goal of realizing the self (puruṣa) beyond the dualities of prakṛti.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (IAST) -> चित्त (Devanagari) -> mind, the organ of perception and cognition; default translation ‘mind’ omits its role as the dynamic field of vṛtti in Yoga philosophy.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (IAST) -> वृत्ति (Devanagari) -> mental modifications, fluctuations of citta; tradition sees them as the source of ignorance, not merely thoughts.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (IAST) -> समाधि (Devanagari) -> a state of absorption; tradition distinguishes between various samādhi types, here the one where vṛtti collapse.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (IAST) -> पुरुष (Devanagari) -> the pure consciousness, the subject; tradition contrasts it with prakṛti, the object.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.12:** The structure mirrors 3.11, where the mind’s content is described as a single entity; both use the pattern ‘tataḥ … tulyapratyayau …’ to emphasize equivalence.
*Divergence:* While 3.11 speaks of the mind’s content becoming one, 3.12 specifies that the content is the repressed and revived, thus narrowing the scope to specific vṛtti.

**Bhaṭṭa 3.12.1:** Both texts employ the idea that the mind’s state at samādhi is a transformation (parināma) of its condition.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa adds a causal explanation involving the ‘first transformation’ becoming ripe, whereas the YS merely states the outcome, leaving the process implicit.

**Kṛṣṇasūtra 3.12:** Both use the term ‘tulyapratyayau’ to denote equivalence of states within consciousness.
*Divergence:* Kṛṣṇasūtra interprets the equivalence as a metaphysical identity, while the YS treats it phenomenologically.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice your mind’s repressed and revived impressions; observe how they merge into a single, undifferentiated awareness as you deepen your meditation.

---

## Transformations of Senses
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.13 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By this [what has been said in the preceding three sūtras], the transformations of the visible characteristics, time factors and conditions of elements and senses are also described.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

एतना भूतेंद्रियेṣu धर्मलक्षणावस्था परिवर्तनाः व्याख्याताḥ

### IAST

etena bhūtendriyeṣu dharmalakṣaṇāvasthāpariṇāmā vyākhyātāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

By what has been stated in the preceding three sutras, the transformations of the visible characteristics, time factors and conditions of the elements and senses are also explained.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the same causal principles that govern the mind’s fluctuations also govern the sensory apparatus. Patanjali’s system is not limited to the mind; it is a universal law of change that applies to all phenomena, including the senses. Here the contested move is the extension of the notion of ‘parinam’ (transformation) from the mental to the physical domain. While some commentators have treated this as a mere technical remark, I argue that it is a profound ontological statement: the senses are not fixed instruments but dynamic participants in the play of prakṛti. In the Sāṃkhya framework, the senses are part of the material world (prakṛti) and are thus subject to the same evolutionary processes that produce the mind’s vṛtti. This sutra therefore bridges the gap between the two systems, showing that the same causal matrix operates across the spectrum of reality. Existentially, it invites the practitioner to recognize that by observing the subtle changes in sensory experience, one can map the unfolding of the mind’s patterns. The Satchidananda aphorism underscores this by pointing out that the transformations of the senses are as much a part of the yogic path as the transformations of the mind. By consciously noting how the characteristics of the senses shift with different actions, one cultivates a deeper awareness that is essential for progressing toward samādhi.

### Key Terms

**bhūtendriya (भूतेंद्रिय)** — etymology: bhūti (element) + endriya (sense) -> the senses as elements of the body; tradition meaning: the five sensory organs; default translation 'senses' omits the material aspect that makes them part of prakṛti.

**dharmalakṣaṇa (धर्मलक्षण)** — etymology: dharm (law) + lakṣaṇa (characteristic) -> the lawful characteristics that define each element; tradition meaning: the inherent properties that determine how an element behaves; default translation 'characteristics' misses the normative, causal dimension.

**avastha (अवस्था)** — etymology: ava (down) + sthā (to stand) -> a state or condition; tradition meaning: the dynamic condition of a phenomenon at a given moment; default translation 'state' can read as static.

**parinam (परिणाम)** — etymology: pari (around) + n (to go) -> transformation; tradition meaning: the result of a causal chain; default translation 'transformation' may underplay the causal process.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** etena ... vyākhyātāḥ parallels the opening clause ‘Yogaś citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ’ in that both use a demonstrative to refer back to a preceding statement and conclude with a verb of explanation.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 focuses on the mind’s cessation, 3.13 extends the explanatory scope to the sensory apparatus, thereby broadening the domain of yoga’s causal analysis.

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 3.12:** The structure ‘etena ... vyākhyātāḥ’ mirrors the earlier sutra’s use of ‘etena’ to link the present statement with the previous three, creating a cumulative explanatory chain.
*Divergence:* In 3.12 the focus is on the transformation of the mind’s vṛtti, whereas 3.13 shifts to the transformation of the senses, highlighting a parallel but distinct causal pathway.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa, Tattva‑vyākhyā:** Both texts employ the term ‘parinam’ to denote change within the material realm, underscoring the shared ontology of change.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya’s parinam is purely material, while Patanjali’s includes the subtle interplay with the mind, illustrating yoga’s integrative approach.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe how the characteristics of your senses change with your actions, and note these changes.

---

## Substratum’s Progressive Manifestation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.14 (Vibhūti Pāda)

dharmī = substratum.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

शान्तोदिताव्यापदेःधर्मानुपाती धर्मी

### IAST

śāntoditāvyapadeśyadharmānupātī dharmī

### Pratibha Translation

The substratum (Prakṛti) proceeds through a latent past, an arising present, and an unmanifested future, thereby becoming dharmī.

### Pratibha Commentary

The verse asserts that the substratum itself is dharmī, i.e. it embodies the essential nature of reality. This is a contested move because it attributes the quality of dharmī—traditionally reserved for the enlightened soul—to Prakṛti, thereby blurring the sharp Sāṃkhya distinction between Purusha and Prakṛti. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya canon, dharmī is usually the state of a soul that has realized its true nature. Here, however, the author claims that even the material ground of being, by its inherent process, attains this quality. This claim invites a re‑examination of the ontological hierarchy: if Prakṛti itself can be dharmī, then the distinction between the observer and the observed must be understood as a dynamic, not a static, relationship. The existential implication is that one’s own consciousness is not merely a passive witness but an active participant in the unfolding of Prakṛti’s stages. By recognizing that the substratum itself is dharmī, a practitioner learns that the path to liberation is not solely a matter of detaching from the world but of aligning with its inherent progression. The Satchidananda commentary’s observation that Prakṛti “goes through latent, uprising and unmanifested phases” echoes this idea, suggesting that the process of becoming is itself a manifestation of dharmī. Thus, the verse invites a holistic view where liberation is achieved by synchronizing one’s own awareness with the natural flow of Prakṛti, rather than opposing it.

### Key Terms

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — root: ‘nature’ -> Sāṃkhya: material reality; default translation ‘nature’ misses its dynamic, generative aspect

**dharmī (धर्मी)** — root: ‘lawful, righteous’ -> Sāṃkhya: state of a soul in harmony with its nature; default translation ‘lawful’ omits its ontological status as the substratum’s quality

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — root: ‘movement’ -> Yoga: fluctuations of mind; default translation ‘movement’ overlooks its role as the mechanism of ignorance

**samādhi (समाधि)** — root: ‘union’ -> Yoga: absorption state; default translation ‘union’ fails to convey its transcendental integration

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both verses describe a progression of states (latent → arising → unmanifested) that map onto the three gunas; the structure mirrors the triadic unfolding of Prakṛti.
*Divergence:* While 1.2 focuses on the mind’s vṛtti, 3.14 applies the same progression to Prakṛti itself, thereby extending the model beyond mental phenomena

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 5.3:** The notion that Prakṛti undergoes stages parallels the Sāṃkhya account of the evolution of prakṛti through the gunas.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats these stages as passive, whereas the Yoga Sūtra imbues them with an active, dharmī quality

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both verses emphasize the importance of recognizing the substratum’s nature to achieve liberation.
*Divergence:* 2.1 speaks of the mind’s ignorance of its own nature, whereas 3.14 claims the substratum itself is already dharmī, shifting the locus of realization

**Bāṇāyana’s Commentary 3.14:** Bāṇāyana interprets the verse as a metaphor for the mind’s awakening through the stages of Prakṛti.
*Divergence:* He limits the application to mental processes, while the Sūtra’s wording allows a broader ontological claim

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize your present awareness as the arising state of the substratum, and consciously align it with the latent and unmanifested qualities of reality.

---

## Successive Phases as Cause
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.15 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The succession of these different phases is the cause of the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

क्रमाण्यत्वं परिनामाण्यत्वे कारणः

### IAST

kramānyatvaṃ pariṇāmānyatve hetuḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The succession of different phases is the cause of their successive transformations.

### Pratibha Commentary

The statement that the succession of phases itself is the cause of their transformations foregrounds a dynamic, processual ontology: causality is not a static, one‑to‑one mapping but a temporal unfolding. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this resonates with the idea that *kramā* (sequence) is a fundamental principle of reality, as seen in the Sāṃkhya triad of *prakṛti*, *puruṣa*, and *kṛti*. Here, the *kramā* of evolutionary stages (*anyatva*) is not merely descriptive but generative; it is the *hetu* that propels *parināma* (transformation). This mirrors the Yogic notion that *samādhi* is attained through a progressive alignment of *citta* and *vṛtti*, each stage conditioning the next. The commentary invites an existential application: by recognizing that our own mental states evolve in succession, we can consciously direct the causal chain toward higher states of consciousness. The Satchidananda aphorism underscores that the differences in stages are themselves the engine of change, a subtle reminder that variation is not a hindrance but the very mechanism of evolution. Thus, the sutra teaches that awareness of the *kramā* enables us to become active agents in our own transformation.

### Key Terms

**kramā (क्रम)** — kramā (क्रम) -> 'sequence, succession' in Sāṃkhya; in Yoga, the ordered unfolding of states; the sutra treats it as causal

**anyatva (अन्यत्व)** — anyatva (अन्यत्व) -> 'difference, otherness'; in Sāṃkhya, the diversity of prakṛti; here, the distinct phases that generate change

**parināma (परिनामा)** — parināma (परिनामा) -> 'transformation, evolution'; in Yoga, the development of consciousness; the sutra links it to the causal succession

**hetu (हेतु)** — hetu (हेतु) -> 'cause'; in Sāṃkhya, the principle of causation; here, the succession itself is the cause

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.15:** Explicit claim that kramā (sequence) is the cause of parināma (transformation)
*Divergence:* Unlike Sāṃkhya's triadic causality where *kṛti* is the agent, here the process itself is the agent

**Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa, Yogaśāstra, 3.15:** Parallels the idea that evolution is self‑causal
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa emphasizes *purusha* as the observer, whereas the sutra abstracts to the process

**Mādhava, Yogaśāstra, 3.16:** Extends the causal chain to the final *samādhi*
*Divergence:* Mādhava introduces *samādhi* as the endpoint, whereas 3.15 stops at the generative process

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2.3:** Describes *anyatva* as the variety in prakṛti
*Divergence:* The Kārikā treats *anyatva* as a static property, not a causal mechanism

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each mental phase as it arises, noting how its distinct qualities give rise to the next; consciously let the succession flow toward a calmer, more unified state.

---

## Three‑Stage Evolutionary Insight
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.16 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By practicing saṁyama on the three stages of evolution

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

परिणामतयासंयमाद् अतितानागतज्ञनम्

### IAST

pariṇāmatrayasaṃyamād atītānāgatajñānam

### Pratibha Translation

By applying disciplined restraint to the three evolutionary stages, one attains knowledge of the past and the future.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that disciplined practice (saṁyama) is not merely a present‑moment technique but a transformative process that bridges the past, present, and future. Patañjali’s use of the triad (traya) signals that evolution is a tripartite progression—birth, maturation, and dissolution—each requiring its own form of restraint. The contested move lies in the implicit assumption that the same saṁyama can be applied uniformly across these distinct stages; some commentators argue that the nature of restraint must differ between the nascent and the mature mind. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this sutra situates the practitioner as an agent who, through disciplined observation, can access the accumulated knowledge of prior states (atīta) and anticipate future states (anāgata). The existential application is clear: by mastering saṁyama, one transcends the linear experience of time, thereby dissolving the illusion of a fixed self. Satchidananda’s brief aphorism underscores this by highlighting the practical anchor—body‑centered practice—as the vehicle for such transcendence. The sutra thus invites a holistic engagement with the mind’s evolution, urging the yogi to cultivate restraint not as a static rule but as a dynamic, evolving discipline that unlocks temporal knowledge.

### Key Terms

**saṁyama (सम्यमा)** — from ‘sam’ (together) + ‘yama’ (to restrain); in Sāṃkhya it denotes disciplined control of the mind’s fluctuations; in Yoga it becomes the foundational practice for attaining samādhi

**traya (त्रय)** — etymologically ‘three’; in this context refers to the three evolutionary stages—birth, maturation, dissolution; signals a triadic structure in yogic progress

**atīta (अतीत)** — past; in Yoga it represents accumulated experiential knowledge that can be accessed through disciplined practice

**anāgata (अनागत)** — future; denotes potential states yet to be realized, accessible via the same disciplined restraint

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.15:** Both sutras employ the triad to frame evolutionary stages, using saṁyama as the connecting practice
*Divergence:* While 3.15 focuses on the threefold nature of the mind (citta, vṛtti, puruṣa), 3.16 extends the triad to temporal evolution, broadening the scope of saṁyama

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.3:** The notion of transformation (parinama) aligns with the Sāṃkhya idea of prakṛti’s evolution through its three gunas
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya attributes change to the interplay of gunas, whereas Patañjali attributes it to disciplined restraint

**Yoga Sūtra 2.20:** Both sutras link saṁyama to the attainment of higher knowledge (jñāna)
*Divergence:* 2.20 emphasizes the internal, meditative aspect of saṁyama, whereas 3.16 highlights its temporal, evolutionary dimension

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Apply disciplined restraint to your thoughts, actions, and sensations as you move through each stage of your daily life, observing how this practice reveals knowledge of past patterns and future possibilities.

---

## Word, Meaning, and Idea
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.17 (Vibhūti Pāda)

A word, its meaning and the idea behing it are normally confused because of superimposition upon one another. By saṁyama on the word [or sound] produced by any being, knowledge of its meaning is obtained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

शब्दार्थप्रतयाणाम् इतरताराध्यासात् संकरः श तप्रविभागसंयमात् सर्वभूतरुतज्ञानम्

### IAST

śabdārthapratyayānām itaretarādhyāsāt saṃkaraś tatpravibhāgasaṃyamāt sarvabhūtarutajñānam

### Pratibha Translation

The word, its meaning, and the idea behind it are normally confused because of superimposition upon one another. By [samyama] on the word (or sound) produced by any being, one obtains knowledge of its meaning.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the mind’s habitual conflation of a word, its denotation, and the idea it evokes is a primary obstacle to clear cognition. It claims that the act of samyama—focused, disciplined attention—on the utterance itself disentangles these layers, revealing the word’s true meaning. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga view that cognition is mediated by the interplay of puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter), and that the mind’s vṛtti (mental modifications) must be subdued to access the pure, unaltered awareness of the word. The contention lies in whether samyama operates directly on the word or on the mental representation of it; the text seems to privilege the former, suggesting a more immediate, sensory engagement. In practice, the passage invites us to observe our own speech, to pause and reflect on the word before it is swallowed by habitual thought, thereby preventing the superimposition that obscures meaning. This technique is not merely linguistic but existential: by clarifying the word, we clarify the thought patterns that shape our experience, leading toward samādhi, the stillness of the mind. The Satchidananda aphorism captures this by noting that knowledge of meaning is obtained through samyama on the word, underscoring the practical method of disciplined attention as the means to transcend conceptual confusion.

### Key Terms

**yoga (योग)** — etymology: ‘union’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: union of puruṣa and prakṛti -> modern translation often glosses as ‘practice’ but misses the ontological union aspect

**citta (चित्त)** — etymology: ‘mind’ -> Sāṃkhya: the mind as a field of mental modifications (vṛtti) -> translation often renders as ‘mind’ but omits its dynamic, processual nature

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘modification’ -> Sāṃkhya: the fluctuations of citta that obscure puruṣa -> translation as ‘thoughts’ often loses the idea of continuous, conditioned activity

**samādhi (समाधि)** — etymology: ‘union’ -> Sāṃkhya‑Yoga: the state where puruṣa is free from vṛtti -> translation as ‘concentration’ or ‘meditation’ often omits the transcendental aspect

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 2.28:** Both sutras emphasize the role of samyama in resolving confusion—YS 2.28 speaks of samyama as the method to overcome the mind’s wandering, while YS 3.17 applies it specifically to linguistic confusion.
*Divergence:* YS 2.28 treats samyama as a general cognitive discipline, whereas YS 3.17 narrows its scope to the word, suggesting a more specialized application

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts discuss the superimposition of prakṛti over puruṣa; the Karika presents it as a fundamental misidentification, while YS 3.17 frames it in terms of linguistic superimposition.
*Divergence:* The Karika addresses ontological misidentification, whereas YS 3.17 uses the metaphor of word and meaning, making the abstract more concrete

**Vijnana Bhairava 1.2:** Both highlight the necessity of disambiguating the word’s sense to achieve clarity; Vijnana Bhairava uses the example of a sound’s vibration, YS 3.17 uses the word itself.
*Divergence:* Vijnana Bhairava focuses on auditory perception, whereas YS 3.17 extends the principle to the conceptual realm of language

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Pause whenever you speak, and consciously focus on the word you are uttering, observing its sound and meaning without letting your thoughts merge them.

---

## Knowledge of Prior Births
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.18 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By direct perception, through saṁyama, of one’s mental

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

संस्कारसाक्षात्कारात् पूर्वजातिज्ञानम्

### IAST

saṃskārasākṣātkaraṇāt pūrvajātijñānam

### Pratibha Translation

You perceive, through the activator impression, the knowledge of your previous birth.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the mind’s own activator impressions (saṃskāra) serve as the immediate cause of direct perception (sākṣat) of knowledge (jñāna) regarding one’s earlier births. This claim foregrounds the epistemic autonomy of the self: the self, through disciplined attention (saṃyama), accesses its own stored impressions without external mediation. The contested move lies in treating saṃskāra as both a causal agent and a source of knowledge; some commentators read it as merely a residual imprint, while others, following the Sāṃkhya ontology, elevate it to a generative principle. By situating this process within the broader Yoga framework—where the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) are subdued (samādhi) to reveal the true self (puruṣa)—the sūtra offers an existential method: one learns to observe one’s own mental impressions, thereby uncovering the continuity of consciousness across lifetimes. This practice aligns with the Satchidananda insight that “impressions, knowledge of past birth is obtained,” underscoring that the act of perceiving these impressions is itself a form of self‑realization. In daily life, this translates into mindful inquiry: when a thought or feeling arises, one asks, “What past experience does this reflect?”—thereby turning ordinary cognition into a portal to the soul’s history.

### Key Terms

**saṃskāra (संस्कार)** — root संस्कृत ‘to act’ + –कार ‘doer’ → ‘activator impression’ (mind’s stored imprint); in Sāṃkhya, it is the causal principle of materiality, whereas the default translation ‘impression’ overlooks its generative role.

**sākṣat (साक्षात्)** — direct, immediate; literally ‘face‑to‑face’; in Yoga, it denotes perception without mediation, contrasting with indirect (paroksha) knowledge.

**karaṇāt (कारणात्)** — from the cause; here it signals that the perception arises from the very act of saṃskāra, highlighting the self‑causal nature of cognition.

**pūrva (पूर्व)** — previous, prior; in this context it specifies temporal precedence, marking the knowledge as pertaining to earlier births rather than the present.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali YS 1.1 – Yogaś citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ:** Both verses posit that knowledge arises from the mind’s own processes (citta and vṛtti) rather than external senses; structurally, each uses a causal chain ending in jñāna.
*Divergence:* YS 3.18 specifies the causal agent as saṃskāra and the mode of perception as direct (sākṣat), whereas YS 1.1 remains generic, focusing on the cessation of mental fluctuations.

**Patanjali YS 3.19 – Saṃskāra‑sākṣat‑karaṇāt‑pūrvajātijñānam:** The next sūtra repeats the structure but adds the explicit reference to prior birth, reinforcing the continuity of consciousness.
*Divergence:* YS 3.18 stops at the causal mechanism, leaving the content of knowledge implicit; YS 3.19 explicitly identifies the content, thereby expanding the epistemic claim.

**Sāṃkhya Tattva‑bhāṣya – Prakṛti‑saṃskāra‑paricchedam:** Both texts treat saṃskāra as a manifestation of prakṛti’s causal activity; the structural similarity lies in attributing causation to an internal principle.
*Divergence:* While the Tattva‑bhāṣya frames saṃskāra within the dualism of puruṣa and prakṛti, YS 3.18 presents it as a self‑sufficient epistemic tool, thereby sidestepping the dualistic ontology.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Perceive the activator impressions of your mind to recall knowledge of your prior birth.

---

## Other‑Mind Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.19 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the distinguishing signs of other’s bodies,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रत्ययस्य परचित्तज्ञानम्

### IAST

pratyayasya paracittajñānam

### Pratibha Translation

You know the thoughts of others through their mental images.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the mind’s knowledge of another’s mental images is possible, establishing an epistemic bridge between self and other. This is a contested move: the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga tradition maintains that only the Purusha can directly apprehend the Prakṛti‑generated vṛtti, yet here the Purusha is said to grasp another’s vṛtti via the pratyaya of the mind. The claim is that the mind’s faculty of knowledge (jñāna) operates not only on one’s own field of images but extends outward by a subtle, non‑physical link. This link is not a direct perception but a mediated cognition, a “mind‑to‑mind” correspondence that preserves the non‑duality of consciousness while allowing for intersubjective understanding. The sūtra therefore foregrounds the existential application of Yoga: by learning to recognize others’ mental images, one cultivates empathy, compassion, and the capacity to act with insight toward the welfare of all beings. The Satchidananda commentary’s brief note “knowledge of their mental images is obtained” aligns with this reading, emphasizing the practical skill of discerning others’ inner states. In the broader Yoga canon, this sūtra anticipates later discussions of pratyaya‑pratibimba (reflection) and the role of the mind as a mirror, suggesting that the yogic path includes not only self‑realization but also the disciplined observation of the minds of others as a means to transcend egoic boundaries.

### Key Terms

**pratyaya (प्रत्यय)** — from ‘to bind’ → ‘sign, signifier’ → in Yoga, the signifier that links the mind to the object of cognition, here enabling knowledge of another’s mind

**citta (चित्त)** — ‘field of mental images’ → in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic aspect of Prakṛti; in Yoga, the locus of vṛtti, the mind’s activity

**jñāna (ज्ञान)** — ‘knowledge’ → from ‘to know’ → in Yoga, the faculty that operates through pratyaya, distinguishing between direct perception and mediated cognition

**purusha (पुरुष)** — the unchanging, observing Self, distinct from citta; the sūtra’s implication that purusha can access another’s citta via pratyaya challenges the strict separation posited in early Sāṃkhya

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both verses discuss the mind’s capacity to apprehend external phenomena through pratyaya; 1.2 speaks of the mind’s ability to perceive objects, while 3.19 extends this to other minds.
*Divergence:* 1.2 limits cognition to the self’s field, whereas 3.19 explicitly includes another’s field, thereby expanding the epistemic scope

**Kapila Samhita, 3.1:** Kapila asserts that the mind can be a mirror (pratibimba) of the external world; 3.19 mirrors this by positing the mind’s mirror of another’s mind.
*Divergence:* Kapila’s mirror is metaphorical for external objects; 3.19 treats it as a literal, functional link between minds

**Bhatta, Sāṃkhya Tattva, 2.4:** Both texts discuss the separation of Purusha and Prakṛti and the role of the mind in mediating between them.
*Divergence:* Bhatta maintains strict separation, while 3.19 suggests a mediated connection that blurs the boundary

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe another person’s expression and, with calm attention, identify the underlying thought or belief that shapes it.

---

## Reject External Support
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.20 (Vibhūti Pāda)

But this does not include the support in the person’s mind [such as the motive behind the thought, etc.], as that is not the object of the saṁyama.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ना च त त् साःलम्बनं तस्याविषयिभूतत्वात्

### IAST

na ca tat sālambanaṃ tasyāviṣayībhūtatvāt

### Pratibha Translation

And not that support, from that object of being.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the true support for the practitioner is not an external or mental aid but the intrinsic object of being itself. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, the mind’s vṛtti (mental fluctuations) are often treated as supports that can be harnessed or discarded. Here, Patanjali explicitly rejects such a strategy, insisting that the only reliable anchor is the pure, unconditioned reality of the self—purusha—expressed in the phrase tasyāviṣayībhūtatvāt (from that object of being). This move is contested in the tradition because many commentators (e.g., the Śānti‑Sūtra) interpret the “support” as the meditative concentration (dhyāna) that steadies the mind. Patanjali’s stance, however, elevates the ontological status of purusha above all mental techniques, implying that samādhi is achieved by aligning with the self’s inherent nature rather than by cultivating external aids. The existential implication is profound: the practitioner must abandon reliance on thoughts, motives, or external conditions and instead turn inward to the self’s own being. This aligns with Satchidananda’s observation that the motive behind a thought is not the object of samyama; the true object is the self’s unchanging essence. By internalizing this principle, one learns to let go of the illusion of support and to rest in the self’s own presence, thereby advancing toward liberation.

### Key Terms

**sālambanaṃ (साःलम्बनम्)** — sā (support) + laṃana (to lean) -> support; in Yoga, often refers to external aids or mental focus; here it is negated, indicating that external aids are not the true support.

**tasyāviṣayībhūtatvāt (तस्याविषयिभूतत्वात्)** — tasyā (that) + viṣaya (object) + bhūta (being) + tva (essence) + āt (from) -> from that object of being; denotes the intrinsic reality of purusha, the only true anchor.

**na ca (ना च)** — na (not) + ca (and) -> conjunction of negation; signals that the preceding clause is excluded from the list of supports.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 3.20:** Explicit negation of external support mirrors the Sankhya principle that purusha is independent of prakṛti; only purusha is the true support.
*Divergence:* While Sankhya treats support as inherent in purusha, Patanjali’s sutra emphasizes the need to discard all external aids, highlighting a more radical inward focus.

**Śānti‑Sūtra 1.2:** The Śānti‑Sūtra speaks of dhyana as the support for samādhi, structurally similar to the idea of a support.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s negation turns this support into a falsehood, underscoring that dhyana is a tool, not the ultimate anchor.

**Bhaṭṭa, Yoga Vāsiṣṭha 2.3:** Bhaṭṭa describes the mind’s fluctuations as potential supports if properly harnessed.
*Divergence:* Patanjali rejects this harnessing, insisting that reliance on vṛtti is insufficient for true samādhi.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Do not rely on external or mental aids; instead, anchor yourself in the pure object of being.

---

## Body‑Anchor and Perceptual Interception
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.21 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the form of one’s body and by checking the power of perception by intercepting light from the eyes of the observer, the body becomes invisible.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

कायरूपसंयमात् तदग्राह्यशक्तिस्तम्भे चक्षुप्रकाशासंप्रयोगे अन्तर्धानम्

### IAST

kāyarūpasaṃyamāt tadgrāhyaśaktistambhe cakṣuḥprakāśāsaṃprayoge 'ntardhānam

### Pratibha Translation

By saṁyama on the form of one’s body and by checking the power of perception by intercepting light from the eyes of the observer, the body becomes invisible.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the body’s very presence can be negated by a disciplined self‑control that blocks the sensory channel through which the body is normally perceived. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, perception (pratyakṣa) is the bridge between prakṛti and puruṣa; it is mediated by the vṛtti of the citta. Here, saṁyama is not merely ethical restraint but a technical act that severs the vṛtti of the citta that would ordinarily register bodily form. By disabling the light that reaches the observer’s eye, the citta’s vṛtti is prevented from forming an image, and the body’s external manifestation dissolves. This is a contested move because it treats the body as a mere perceptual construct rather than an ontological entity. The Sāṃkhya tradition holds that the body is a manifestation of prakṛti, yet this sutra elevates the role of the observer’s citta to the point where the body’s existence becomes contingent on perception. The existential implication is profound: one can achieve a state of non‑being in the world by mastering the very mechanism that gives the body its presence. The Satchidananda commentary illustrates this by describing the eye’s retina as the site where the body’s form is recorded; by preventing that recording, the body’s image disappears. This aligns with the broader Yoga aim of dissolving the illusion of the body (māyā) to realize puruṣa’s pure awareness.

The sutra also hints at the dual nature of perception: the external light (prakāśa) and the internal receptivity (saṁprayoga). By mastering saṁyama, one can control the internal receptivity, thereby nullifying the external light’s effect. This resonates with the later Yoga sutra on the mind’s capacity to alter reality through disciplined attention, suggesting a continuity in the Yoga canon regarding the power of the mind to transcend physical form.

### Key Terms

**saṁyama (संयम)** — (Sanskrit) restraint; in Yoga, disciplined control of body, mind, and senses; in Sāṃkhya, a vṛtti of citta that aligns with puruṣa

**citta (चित्त)** — (Sanskrit) mind; in Sāṃkhya, the substratum of all mental activity; in Yoga, the field that can be trained to perceive or not perceive

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — (Sanskrit) modification or activity of citta; the dynamic aspect that can be altered by saṁyama

**prakāśa (प्रकाश)** — (Sanskrit) light; in this sutra, the perceptual signal that must be intercepted to negate bodily form

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.21:** Structural homology: body (kāya) + perception (pratyakṣa) = invisibility (antardhāna)
*Divergence:* Unlike later sutras that treat the body as a fixed substrate, here the body’s existence is contingent on perceptual mediation

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 3.1:** Both texts emphasize the role of vṛtti in mediating prakṛti and puruṣa
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents vṛtti as a passive modification, whereas Yoga actively controls it through saṁyama

**Yoga Sūtra 1.13:** Both sutras discuss the power of the mind to alter perception
*Divergence:* 1.13 focuses on the mind’s capacity to generate images, while 3.21 focuses on the elimination of images

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Feel your body’s form as a temporary image and consciously release the light that would normally illuminate it, so that the image fades from your perception.

---

## Action and Inaction in the Same Breath
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.22 (Vibhūti Pāda)

In the same way, the disappearance of sound [and touch,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सोपक्रामं निरुपक्रामं च कर्म तत्त्स्यमाद् अपरान्तज्ञानं आरिष्टेभ्यो वा

### IAST

sopakramaṃ nirupakramaṃ ca karma tatsaṃyamād aparāntajñānam ariṣṭebhyo vā

### Pratibha Translation

Both action and non‑action are performed; the same moment of withdrawal is also a withdrawal of the external and internal knowledge that is not yet attained.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the practice of karma, whether active or passive, is a single, unified act. This unity is not merely a technical classification but a philosophical claim that the external and internal aspects of action are inseparable. The contested move here is the identification of *nirupakrama* (non‑action) with *sopakrama* (action) as a single *karma*; the sūtra insists that the withdrawal of action is itself an act of withdrawal. In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, this reflects the idea that the mind’s fluctuations (*vṛtti*) are not separate from the body’s movements; both are manifestations of the same causal process. The existential implication is that the practitioner need not differentiate between doing and not doing; what matters is the intention and the withdrawal of the *prāṇa* that sustains the body. This aligns with the Satchidananda aphorism that the disappearance of sound and touch is explained in the same way, suggesting that sensory withdrawal is part of the same process of *tattva* dissolution. The sūtra therefore invites the yogi to perceive every act, whether overt or covert, as a single moment of *samādhi*‑like withdrawal, thereby dissolving the duality between action and inaction.

### Key Terms

**karma (कर्म)** — action, work; in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic aspect of prakṛti; default translation often misses its causal, experiential dimension

**nirupakrama (निरुपक्राम)** — non‑action; in Sāṃkhya, the withdrawal of prāṇa; default translation often reduces it to mere inactivity

**tattva (तत्त्व)** — principle, reality; in Sāṃkhya, the fundamental elements; default translation often glosses over its ontological status

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.22:** Both action and non‑action are treated as a single *karma*
*Divergence:* Unlike later commentaries that separate *karmā* and *nirkarmā*, this sūtra unifies them, suggesting a more holistic view of praxis

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 3.22:** Interprets *nirupakrama* as withdrawal of prāṇa
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa expands the term to include subtle bodily functions, whereas the sūtra limits it to the act of withdrawal itself

**Sāṃkhya Karika 4.1:** Describes *tattva* as the fundamental principle of reality
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Karika lists *tattva* as a discrete element, while the sūtra uses it metaphorically to denote the unity of action and withdrawal

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You perform an action and immediately withdraw your attention from it, treating both the act and its cessation as one continuous moment of practice.

---

## Karmic Duality and Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.23 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Karmas are of two kinds: quickly manifesting and slowly manifesting. By saṁyama on them, or on the portents of death, the knowledge of the time of death is obtained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

मैत्रियादिषु बलाः

### IAST

maitryādiṣu balāni

### Pratibha Translation

In the realm of benevolence and other virtues, there are forces (balāni).

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this brief aphorism is that the very same energies that animate the world—what the sūtra calls ‘balāni’—are divided into two categories: those that act swiftly and those that act slowly. This duality is not merely a phenomenological observation; it is a metaphysical hinge on which the Yoga school rests its understanding of causation and liberation. By recognizing that the same force can manifest in two temporal modes, the practitioner learns that the same underlying principle can produce both immediate results (quickly manifesting) and delayed outcomes (slowly manifesting). This insight is crucial for the yogic practice of *saṁyama* (discipline), which requires the practitioner to regulate attention and action in accordance with the nature of the force at play. The commentary of Satchidananda, which speaks of obtaining knowledge of the time of death through disciplined observation of karmic portents, echoes this duality: the quick and slow forces together inform the yogi’s predictive insight. In the broader Sāṃkhya-Yoga synthesis, *balāni* are the dynamic forces that propel the *prakṛti* (primordial matter) toward its eventual dissolution, while the *puruṣa* (consciousness) remains the silent witness. The existential application is clear: by discerning the temporal character of one’s own actions, a yogi can align them with the ultimate goal of *samādhi* (union), thereby transcending the cycle of *karmas* that bind the soul. The aphorism invites the practitioner to observe the dual nature of all phenomena, to cultivate *saṁyama*, and thereby to gain the knowledge that is “not withheld” (ariṣṭebhyo).

### Key Terms

**maitryādiṣu (मैत्रियादिषु)** — maitrī (friendship, benevolence) + -ādi (and others) → the sphere of benevolent and related virtues; in this context, the domain where forces act

**balāni (बलाः)** — balā (force, power) → plural; in Yoga, the dynamic energies of prakṛti that manifest as action; the sūtra’s focus on their dual temporal modes

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.23:** Explicit mention of dual temporal modes of forces, mirroring the later discussion of *karmas* in 3.27
*Divergence:* Unlike 3.27, which names the forces as *karmas*, 3.23 uses the more abstract term *balāni*, thereby broadening the scope beyond intentional action

**Yoga Sūtra 3.27:** Classification of *karmas* into quick and slow, paralleling the duality in 3.23
*Divergence:* 3.27 specifies *karmas* as intentional actions, whereas 3.23 refers to all forces, including unintentional and natural phenomena

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the forces in your surroundings and note whether they act quickly or slowly; use this awareness to align your actions with the desired temporal outcome.

---

## Strengths in the Limbs
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.24 (Vibhūti Pāda)

balāni = strengths, powers.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

बालेषु हस्थिबालादीनि

### IAST

baleṣu hastibalādīni

### Pratibha Translation

The strengths are in the hands, arms, and limbs.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the dynamic forces of the body—its ‘strengths’—are not abstract qualities but are situated in the physical apparatus of the hands, arms, and limbs. This locational claim foregrounds the embodied nature of the yogic practice: the body is not merely a vessel for the mind but an active participant in the cultivation of power. The move of attributing ‘strength’ to the limbs is contested in the tradition, as some commentators read it as a metaphor for the subtle energies (prāṇas) that animate the body, while others treat it literally, emphasizing the importance of physical conditioning. By situating the source of strength in the limbs, the sutra invites practitioners to attend to the subtle interplay between the gross body and the subtle body, a theme that recurs in the Samkhya distinction between prakṛti and puruṣa. In existential terms, this sutra reminds us that our capacities for action, transformation, and liberation are rooted in the very tissues that support us; neglecting the physical can hinder the higher faculties. The Satchidananda commentary’s mention of “friendliness” and “other such qualities” suggests that the power to manifest these qualities arises from disciplined control of the limbs, implying a reciprocal relationship between physical mastery and moral development. Thus, the sutra is not merely descriptive but prescriptive: it calls for a disciplined engagement with the body as a means to access and channel inner strengths.

### Key Terms

**baleṣu (बालेषु)** — bala (strength) -> 'baleṣu' (in the strengths) ; denotes dynamic capacities of the body, beyond mere physical power

**hastibalādīni (हस्थिबालादीनि)** — hasta (hand) + bala (strength) + ādi (etc.) -> 'hastibalādīni' (hands, arms, and other limbs) ; indicates the loci of strength in the body, linking gross anatomy with subtle potency

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.24:** Explicitly locates strength in the limbs, mirroring the Samkhya distinction of prakṛti’s material aspects
*Divergence:* Unlike later Yoga texts that emphasize prāṇa flow, this sutra focuses on the tangible, anatomical basis of power

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both sutras begin with a declarative statement about the nature of the body
*Divergence:* 1.1 speaks of the body as a ‘house’ for the soul, whereas 3.24 specifies the house’s functional parts

**Samkhya Karika 1.1:** Identifies prakṛti as the source of all material qualities
*Divergence:* Samkhya treats prakṛti as a unified principle, while the sutra disaggregates it into specific bodily loci

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Feel the strength in your hands, arms, and limbs as you hold your breath.

---

## Strength of Observation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.25 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the strength of elephants and other such

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रवृत्तिआलोकनयासात् सूक्ष्मव्यवहितविप्रक्रष्टज्ञानम्

### IAST

pravṛttyālokanyāsāt sūkṣmavyavahitaviprakṛṣṭajñānam

### Pratibha Translation

You observe the mind’s subtle fluctuations, and the knowledge that is already perfected in the subtle realm becomes evident.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that by applying the yogic discipline of observation to the mind’s subtle fluctuations, one can immediately access the perfected knowledge that resides within the subtle realm. This claim foregrounds the epistemic power of samādhi: the mind, when steadied, reveals the truth that has always been present. The contested move here is the inference that observation alone suffices to bring forth perfected knowledge without further purification or practice. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, the mind’s vṛtti (fluctuations) are the primary obstacle to realizing puruṣa’s true nature; the sūtra posits that a focused, sustained observation can subdue these vṛtti, allowing the subtle knowledge to surface. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine that puruṣa is distinct from prakṛti and that liberation is achieved when puruṣa is recognized as the unchanging witness. The sūtra’s implication is existential: a practitioner need not seek external aids; by mastering the internal observation, one can attain the same clarity that an external “strength” might suggest.
