This resonates with the Satchidananda commentary’s metaphor of “strength of elephants,” which, while vivid, ultimately points to the same internal capacity: the power of focused attention to transform one’s experience. By emphasizing the internal, the sūtra invites practitioners to cultivate a disciplined, present‑moment awareness that directly accesses the subtle, perfected knowledge inherent in puruṣa.

### Key Terms

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — ‘fluctuation’ -> Sāṃkhya: mental modifications of citta; Yoga: obstacles to samādhi; default translation ‘activity’ misses the epistemic role of vṛtti as the source of ignorance

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — ‘consciousness’ -> Sāṃkhya: eternal, passive witness; Yoga: the true self; default translation ‘person’ omits its role as the unchanging observer

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — ‘nature’ -> Sāṃkhya: material, dynamic reality; Yoga: the source of vṛtti; default translation ‘nature’ neglects its dual role as both cause and condition of mind

**samādhi (समाधि)** — ‘absorption’ -> Yoga: state of focused, undistracted awareness; Sāṃkhya: the realization of puruṣa’s distinction; default translation ‘concentration’ underplays its transcendental quality

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both passages link observation (āloka) with the cessation of vṛtti to reveal knowledge; 1.2 speaks of ‘samādhi’ as the stillness of mind, while 3.25 specifies the subtle realm as the locus of perfected knowledge.
*Divergence:* 1.2 emphasizes the process of attaining samādhi, whereas 3.25 assumes it is already achieved through observation alone, thereby bypassing preparatory practices

**Bhartrhari, Vṛtti‑śāstra 2.4:** Both treat vṛtti as the obstacle to knowledge; Bhartrhari’s text describes vṛtti as ‘mental noise’, while 3.25 treats it as a subtle realm that can be observed to access perfected knowledge.
*Divergence:* Bhartrhari’s account is descriptive, not prescriptive; 3.25 prescribes a yogic method to subdue vṛtti, suggesting a more proactive stance

**Kapila Samhita 3.1:** Both affirm the dualism of puruṣa and prakṛti; Kapila speaks of puruṣa’s transcendence, while 3.25 situates the knowledge within the subtle realm of prakṛti’s fluctuations.
*Divergence:* Kapila’s Samhita focuses on metaphysical distinction, whereas 3.25 integrates this distinction into a practical yogic technique

**Satchidananda, Commentary on YS 3.25:** Both use the metaphor of ‘strength of elephants’ to illustrate the power of focused attention; Satchidananda’s metaphor is literal, 3.25 is symbolic.
*Divergence:* Satchidananda’s commentary expands the sūtra into experiential anecdotes, whereas the sūtra remains concise and abstract

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your mind’s subtle fluctuations with steady, undistracted attention, and let the perfected knowledge that already resides in that subtle realm unfold.

---

## Restraint of the Inner Sun
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.26 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the Light within, the knowledge of the subtle, hidden and remote is obtained. [Note: subtle as atoms, hidden as treasure, remote as distant lands.]

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

भुवनज्ञनं सूर्ये संयमात्

### IAST

bhuvanajñānaṃ sūrye saṃyamāt

### Pratibha Translation

By restraining the inner Sun, one gains knowledge of the world.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that disciplined control of the luminous faculty of the mind—metaphorically the Sun—produces empirical knowledge of the world. This is a contested move: the term *sūrye* is not a literal celestial body but the inner light of consciousness that illuminates perception. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, the mind’s luminous faculty is the vehicle through which *prāṇāyāma* and *samādhi* refine perception. The sūtra aligns with Patanjali’s opening claim that yoga is the control of the mind’s fluctuations (*citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ*), yet it narrows the focus to the *inner Sun* as the source of all cognition. The implication for practice is that by cultivating *saṃyama* (restraint) over the mind’s luminous activity, one can access *bhuvanajñāna*—the knowledge of the world—without external aids. This resonates with the Sāṃkhya doctrine that *purūṣa* is the witness and *prakṛti* the material cause; the Sun here is the *prakṛti*‑mediated illumination that, when restrained, reveals the *purūṣa*’s true nature. The sūtra thus bridges the epistemological gap between internal discipline and external knowledge, offering a concrete method for experiential insight.

### Key Terms

**bhuvana (भुवन)** — etymology: *bhu* (earth) + *vana* (forest) → world; tradition meaning: the material realm; default translation ‘world’ misses its role as the domain of empirical knowledge.

**jñāna (ज्ञान)** — etymology: *jñ* (to know) + *ā* (suffix) → knowledge; tradition meaning: both empirical and spiritual cognition; default translation ‘knowledge’ omits its epistemic hierarchy.

**sūrye (सूर्ये)** — etymology: *sūry* (sun) + locative *‑e* → ‘in the Sun’; tradition meaning: the luminous faculty of consciousness; default translation ‘Sun’ misleads as a celestial body.

**saṃyamāt (संयमात्)** — etymology: *saṃ* (together) + *yam* (control) + *‑āt* (from); tradition meaning: restraint or discipline; default translation ‘from restraint’ misses its causal role in generating knowledge.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtras, 1.1:** Both texts posit that disciplined control of the mind’s luminous activity yields knowledge; *Yogaś citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ* parallels *saṃyamāt* on the Sun.
*Divergence:* Patanjali speaks of all mind fluctuations, whereas YS 3.26 singles out the Sun as the primary source of cognition.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa, 1.1:** The distinction between *purūṣa* (witness) and *prakṛti* (material cause) mirrors the sūtra’s separation of inner Sun (prakṛti) and worldly knowledge (bhuvana).
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats the Sun metaphorically as *prakṛti* itself, while YS 3.26 uses it as a vehicle for *saṃyama*.

**Bāṇāyana, Vākyapadīya, 4.3:** The idea that disciplined attention to the mind’s luminous faculty clarifies perception appears in the Vākyapadīya’s discussion of *saṃyama*.
*Divergence:* Bāṇāyana focuses on linguistic perception, whereas YS 3.26 extends to empirical knowledge of the world.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Restrain your inner Sun (mind’s luminous faculty) to acquire clear knowledge of the world.

---

## Moon as Knowledge of Star Arrangement
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.27 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the sun, knowledge of the entire solar

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

चन्द्रे ताराव्यूहज्ञानम्

### IAST

candre tārāvyūhajñānam

### Pratibha Translation

The moon is the knowledge of the arrangement of the stars.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this aphorism is that the moon itself is not merely a celestial body but a dynamic repository of the cosmos’ structural knowledge. By identifying the moon with the knowledge of the star‑arrangement, Patanjali elevates a natural phenomenon to a symbolic mirror of the mind’s capacity to apprehend the world’s underlying order. The contested move here is the identification of a physical object (candra) with an abstract faculty (jñāna); some commentators treat it as a metaphor, others as a literal ontological equivalence. In the Yoga tradition, this equivalence underscores the principle that the puruṣa (consciousness) can be understood through the prakṛti (material world) when the mind is in samādhi, the stillness that perceives the star‑arrangement. The Sāṃkhya framework, which separates puruṣa and prakṛti, finds resonance in this passage: just as the moon reflects the stars, the puruṣa reflects the patterns of prakṛti. Existentially, the sūtra invites the practitioner to recognize that the same faculty that discerns the stars is available within the self; by cultivating a calm, moon‑like awareness, one can perceive the hidden order of thoughts and emotions. Satchidananda’s brief comment that the system is obtained hints at the practical implication: when the mind settles like the moon, the entire cosmic arrangement becomes intelligible, and the practitioner gains mastery over the mind’s fluctuations.

### Key Terms

**candra (चन्द्र)** — etymology: ‘moon’ -> tradition: luminous reflector of the sun’s rays -> what default translation misses: its role as a mirror of the mind’s luminous awareness

**tārāvyūha (ताराव्यूह)** — etymology: ‘arrangement of stars’ -> tradition: cosmic order or the pattern of consciousness -> what default translation misses: the dynamic mapping of mental states

**jñāna (ज्ञान)** — etymology: ‘knowledge’ -> tradition: both sensory perception and intuitive insight -> what default translation misses: its dual function as external observation and internal realization

**samādhi (समाधि)** — etymology: ‘union’ -> tradition: state of meditative absorption where puruṣa and prakṛti appear unified -> what default translation misses: the experiential quality of stillness that mirrors the moon’s calm

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali Yoga Sutra 3.27, 3.28:** Both sutras use celestial imagery to equate a natural object with a mental faculty, illustrating the structural homology of metaphorical identification.
*Divergence:* While 3.27 identifies the moon as knowledge of star arrangement, 3.28 shifts to the sun as the source of that knowledge, breaking the moon‑sun symmetry to emphasize the hierarchy of illumination.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.1:** The Karika speaks of the moon as a reflection of prakṛti’s patterns, mirroring the sūtra’s claim of the moon as knowledge.
*Divergence:* The Karika treats the moon purely as a physical reflector, whereas the sūtra grants it epistemic authority, thereby extending the metaphor beyond mere observation.

**Vijnana Bhairava Upanishad 3.4:** Both texts link celestial bodies with the mind’s capacity to perceive cosmic order.
*Divergence:* The Upanishad presents the moon as a symbol of the self’s potential, while the Yoga Sutra explicitly ties it to the faculty of jñāna, making the link more functional.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Look at the moon and let its stillness become your awareness of the stars.

---

## Fixed Knowledge of That Which Is Known
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.28 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the moon, comes knowledge of the stars’

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ध्रुवे तदगतिज्ञानम्

### IAST

dhruve tadgatijñānam

### Pratibha Translation

The knowledge that is fixed is that which is known.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that true knowledge is not a fleeting insight but a stable, unchanging awareness of what is already known. It claims that the mind’s grasp on a particular truth becomes fixed only when it is fully realized, thereby transcending the oscillatory patterns of cognition. This move—identifying ‘fixedness’ (ध्रुवे) with ‘knowledge’ (गतिज्ञानम्)—is contested in the sense that other commentators have read it as a description of the mind’s settled state rather than an ontological claim. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, the sutra positions knowledge (gati) as a distinct faculty that, when stabilized, becomes the vehicle for liberation. The existential implication is that one must cultivate a steady awareness of the self’s inherent truth, thereby quieting the restless fluctuations (vṛtti) that bind the Purusha to Prakṛti. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on the “arrangement” of stars on the moon echoes this idea: just as the moon’s fixed position allows the stars to be perceived, a fixed awareness makes the truth of the self visible. Thus, the sutra invites practitioners to anchor their attention on the immutable knowledge already present within, thereby aligning with the broader Yoga goal of samādhi and the realization of īśvara‑like stillness.

### Key Terms

**dhruve (ध्रुवे)** — ध्रुवः (fixed, steady) → in Yoga Sutra it denotes the unchanging state of realized knowledge, contrasting with the mutable mind (citta) and its fluctuations (vṛtti).

**tad (तद्)** — तद् (that) → here it specifies the particular knowledge that has become fixed, pointing to the object of realization rather than abstract truth.

**gati (गतिः)** — गति (knowledge, realization) → in this sutra it is the faculty that, when fixed, becomes the basis for liberation; the term’s use underscores the transition from mere cognition to stable awareness.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.1 – "Yogaś citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ":** Both sutras use a single word to denote the essential quality of the practice (Yoga vs. dhruve) and pair it with a noun that captures the object of the practice (citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ vs. tad‑gati).
*Divergence:* While 1.1 frames Yoga as the cessation of mind‑fluctuations, 3.28 frames the result as the fixedness of knowledge, shifting the focus from process to state.

**Brahma Sutra 1.1.1 – "Brahman‑as‑the‑ultimate‑real‑truth":** Both sutras employ a concise two‑word construction that juxtaposes a quality (dhruve) with an object (tad‑gati), mirroring the Brahma Sutra’s pairing of Brahman with its defining attribute.
*Divergence:* The Brahma Sutra’s construction is metaphysical, whereas 3.28 is phenomenological, concerned with the experiential stability of knowledge.

**Samkhya Karika 1.1 – "Puruṣa‑prākṛti‑duality":** The sutra’s emphasis on fixed knowledge parallels the Samkhya delineation of Purusha as the unchanging witness, contrasting with Prakriti’s flux.
*Divergence:* Where Samkhya presents a dualistic ontology, 3.28 operates within the Yoga framework, treating fixed knowledge as a means to dissolve the duality.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You fix your awareness on the knowledge that is already known, maintaining it as a steady point of focus.

---

## Pole Star Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.29 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the pole star comes knowledge of the stars’

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

नाभिकृ काया-व्यूहज्ञानम्

### IAST

nābhicakre kāyavyūhajñānam

### Pratibha Translation

You gain knowledge of the stars by focusing your body’s alignment on the pole star.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that disciplined bodily alignment toward a fixed celestial point yields insight into the cosmos. This claim situates the practice within the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, where the body (kāya) is a vehicle for the mind (citta) to perceive the underlying reality (prakṛti). The pole star (nābhicakre) is a metaphor for a stable, unchanging point of reference—akin to the puruṣa’s unaltered awareness. By aligning the body (kāyavyūha) with this point, the practitioner stabilizes the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti), enabling the direct apprehension of the stars’ movement (tad gati). The existential import is that one can map the external order of the universe by first mastering the internal order of the body and mind. The Satchidananda aphorism frames this as a “body anchor”: the body, when disciplined, becomes a fixed point from which the mind can observe the dynamic processes of reality. This mirrors the traditional Yoga aim of using the body as a tool for samādhi, yet it emphasizes the external, empirical knowledge that arises from internal discipline. The sūtra thus bridges the metaphysical goal of liberation with the practical method of bodily stabilization, underscoring that the path to insight begins with the physical alignment of the practitioner’s body toward a constant point of reference.

### Key Terms

**nābhicakre (नाभिकृ)** — nâbhicakre: ‘at the pole star’ (nâbhi‑ ‘at the navel’ + cakre ‘at the point’). In Sāṃkhya, the pole star symbolizes the immutable puruṣa; the term highlights the necessity of a fixed point for perception, contrasting with the mutable prakṛti.

**kāyavyūha (कायव्यूह)** — kāyavyūha: ‘body assembly’ (kāya ‘body’ + vyūha ‘arrangement’). It denotes the organized bodily posture that serves as the instrument for mental focus, aligning with the Yoga notion that the body is the vehicle for citta’s investigation of prakṛti.

**jñāna (ज्ञानम्)** — jñāna: ‘knowledge’ (root jñā‑ ‘to know’). In this context it refers to empirical, observational knowledge derived from disciplined bodily alignment, distinct from the transcendental knowledge of puruṣa.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.27:** Both 3.27 and 3.29 employ a celestial metaphor to link bodily discipline with knowledge; 3.27 speaks of the body’s alignment with the sun, while 3.29 uses the pole star.
*Divergence:* 3.27 emphasizes the dynamic, daily movement of the sun, suggesting a continual practice, whereas 3.29 focuses on the fixed point of the pole star, implying a more static, meditative posture.

**Yoga Sūtra 3.28:** Both sutras discuss the role of the body (kāya) in facilitating mental clarity; 3.28 speaks of the body’s ‘surrender’ to the mind, while 3.29 speaks of the body’s alignment to a fixed point.
*Divergence:* 3.28’s language is more internal (surrender) whereas 3.29’s language is external (alignment), highlighting a shift from internal to external stabilization.

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 4.1:** The use of a fixed point (pole star) parallels the Sāṃkhya idea of puruṣa as the unchanging observer.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats puruṣa as purely transcendental, whereas the sūtra uses the pole star as a tangible, bodily reference, making the metaphysical concept accessible.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Align your body so that your gaze rests steadily on the pole star (or its terrestrial equivalent) while you breathe evenly, and observe how your mind’s wandering diminishes.

---

## Solar Plexus Insight
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.30 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the navel plexus, knowledge of the body’s

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

कण्ठकूपे क्षुतपिपासानिवृत्तिḥ

### IAST

kaṇṭhakūpe kṣutpipāsānivṛttiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

By focusing attention on the navel plexus, one gains knowledge of the body’s constitution.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the navel plexus functions as a focal point for perceiving the entirety of the bodily constitution. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya frameworks, the body is an expression of prakṛti, and its patterns are mirrored in the subtle body. By directing samādhi (concentration) to the navel, the practitioner aligns the mind with the subtle energy currents that pervade the body, thereby accessing a holistic understanding of its structure. This move—attending to the navel plexus—is contested in some traditions that emphasize the heart or the crown as primary loci of insight; however, the Vibhūti Pāda explicitly privileges the navel, suggesting that the body’s energetic center is the most immediate gateway to its constitution. The existential implication is profound: by mastering this focus, one can discern the interdependence of physical, prāṇic, and mental patterns, thereby cultivating a deeper sense of embodied self‑knowledge. The Satchidananda commentary’s metaphor of the sun illuminating the solar system echoes this idea, underscoring the navel’s role as the central hub from which all bodily information radiates.

### Key Terms

**kaṇṭhakūpa (कण्ठकूप)** — kaṇṭha (throat) + kūpa (cup) -> the navel plexus; a subtle center of prāṇa, not merely a physical point

**kṣutpipā (क्षुतपिपा)** — kṣut (sharp) + pipā (neck) -> the sharp, focused attention; in Yoga, a disciplined mind

**nivṛttiḥ (निवृत्तिḥ)** — nivṛtti (concentration) -> the act of drawing attention inward; foundational to samādhi

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.30:** focus on a specific subtle center (navel) to access whole body
*Divergence:* other sutras emphasize heart or crown, but 3.30 uniquely privileges navel

**Yoga Sūtra 2.29:** prāṇa flows through navel plexus
*Divergence:* 2.29 speaks of prāṇa, 3.30 speaks of knowledge

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.1:** body as manifestation of prakṛti
*Divergence:* Karika attributes to prakṛti, 3.30 attributes to focused attention

**Bhāvanā 1.5:** concentration leads to insight
*Divergence:* Bhāvanā uses mental practices, 3.30 uses bodily locus

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on the navel plexus, drawing inward a sharp, steady concentration to perceive the body’s internal structure.

## Pit of the Throat Anchor
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.31 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the pit of the throat, cessation of hunger

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

कुर्मणाड्यम् स्थैर्यम्

### IAST

kūrmanāḍyāṃ sthairyam

### Pratibha Translation

You anchor the mind in the pit of the throat, achieving steadiness.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the mind can be steadied by focusing on a single, small locus—here the pit of the throat—reveals a central Yoga principle: that the mind’s restless fluctuations (vṛtti) can be quelled by a precise, sustained attention. This is a contested move because it places the locus of control in a bodily site rather than in the abstract realm of the self (puruṣa). In the Sāṃkhya framework, the mind (citta) is a function of prakṛti, and its fluctuations are the result of ignorance (avidyā). By directing attention to the throat, the practitioner is effectively severing the mind’s habitual patterns, allowing the subtle energy (prāṇa) to settle and the mind to return to its unaltered state. The practice is existentially relevant: it offers a concrete, daily technique for anyone—cooking, working, or meditating—to cultivate calm without elaborate postures. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on the “body anchor” underscores that this is not a mystical trick but a practical tool that can be applied immediately, even by a cook who wishes to calm the mind while stirring a pot. The method resonates with the broader Yoga aim of achieving samādhi, a state of steady, undistracted awareness, by first mastering the most immediate and accessible point of focus. It also anticipates later yogic practices that use the throat as a gateway to higher states, such as the chanting of mantras or the control of breath through the navel and throat. Thus, the sutra presents a bridge between bodily awareness and the ultimate goal of self‑realization, making the abstract teachings of Yoga accessible in everyday life.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: mind; in Sāṃkhya, the organ of perception and cognition, a function of prakṛti; the sutra’s focus on steadying citta through bodily attention highlights its mutable nature.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: fluctuation of the mind; the sutra’s method seeks to halt vṛtti by anchoring attention, thereby illustrating the Yoga view that mental activity is a function of ignorance.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa: pure consciousness; the sutra’s implicit claim that steadiness achieved through bodily focus can still lead to the recognition of puruṣa’s distinctness from prakṛti.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: primal nature; the sutra’s technique is a means to reduce prakṛti’s influence on citta, thereby enabling the practitioner to experience the unchanging self.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both sutras employ a single, precise locus (the throat in 3.31, the mind in 2.1) to initiate control over mental activity.
*Divergence:* While 2.1 speaks of the mind’s wandering, 3.31 prescribes a bodily anchor, suggesting a more embodied approach to the same goal.

**Bhatkhande, Yogasūtra Bhashya 3.31:** The commentary emphasizes the practical, everyday applicability of the throat anchor, mirroring the sutra’s terse directive.
*Divergence:* Bhatkhande expands on the sutra’s brevity by linking it to specific pranāyāma practices, whereas the sutra itself remains agnostic about technique.

**Satchidananda, Yoga Sūtra Commentary 3.31:** Both texts highlight the throat as a gateway to inner stillness.
*Divergence:* Satchidananda frames it as a ‘body anchor’ for immediate use, while the sutra presents it as a universal method for all practitioners.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You focus your entire attention on the pit of the throat, holding it steady as you breathe, and feel your mind settle into calm.

## Stability of the Mind’s Eye
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.32 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the kurma nadī (a subtle tortoise-shaped tube located below the throat), motionlessness in meditative posture is achieved.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

मूर्धज्योतिṣि सिद्धदर्शनम्

### IAST

mūrdhajyotiṣi siddhadarśanam

### Pratibha Translation

The perfected vision of the mind’s eye is attained by the firm focus of the mind’s eye.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the mind’s eye (mūrdhā) is the locus where the subtle, perfected vision (siddha‑darśana) is realized through a sustained, unshakable focus. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, the mind’s eye is a conceptual tool that bridges the external world and the internal observer; it is not merely a physical organ but a metaphysical point where the *citta* (mind) aligns with the *prāṇā* of the subtle channels. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound pedagogical move: it condenses the entire practice of *samādhi* into the single act of holding the mind’s eye steady, thereby collapsing the *vṛtti* (mental fluctuations) into a single, unbroken state. This move is contested in the sense that some commentators insist on a gradual build‑up of concentration, whereas here the sutra suggests that the perfected vision is instantly available once the mind’s eye is fixed. The practice is thus existential: it invites the practitioner to experience the *siddha* (realized) state not as a distant goal but as an immediate, accessible reality. By focusing on the mind’s eye, one bypasses the external distractions and directly engages the *citta* in a state of *samādhi* that is both *sādhana* (practice) and *siddhi* (accomplishment). This aligns with the broader Yoga tradition’s emphasis on the *yoga* of the mind, where the *citta* is trained to become a steady, luminous witness, thereby revealing the *purusha* (pure consciousness) that is untouched by the fluctuations of *prakṛti*.

### Key Terms

**yoga (योग)** — From ‘to bind’ to the binding of the mind’s fluctuations; default translation as ‘practice’ misses its ontological role as the union of body, mind, and consciousness.

**citta (चित्त)** — Etymology: ‘to think’ → the store of impressions; tradition: the dynamic field of mental activity; default translation as ‘mind’ overlooks its role as the active observer.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — ‘to turn’ → the oscillations of the mind; tradition: the mental modifications that obscure *purusha*; default translation as ‘thoughts’ misses its dynamic, processual nature.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — ‘to be united’ → the state of complete absorption; tradition: the culmination of *yoga* where *citta* and *purusha* merge; default translation as ‘concentration’ understates its transcendental quality.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both sutras invoke the mind’s eye (mūrdhā) as a focal point for realizing the true self, emphasizing the centrality of *citta* in the path to liberation.
*Divergence:* While 1.2 speaks of the mind’s eye as a *kāraṇa* (cause) of *samādhi*, 3.32 presents it as the *siddha* (realized) state itself, collapsing cause and effect.

**Bhaṭṭa’s *Yogaśāstra* 3.1:** Both texts describe the stabilization of the mind’s eye as a means to silence *vṛtti* and attain *samādhi*.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa elaborates a multi‑step process of progressive concentration, whereas 3.32 condenses the practice into a single, instantaneous act.

**Mādhava’s *Yogaśāstra* 4.5:** Both treat the mind’s eye as a locus where *prāṇa* flows, enabling *siddha* vision.
*Divergence:* Mādhava emphasizes the *prāṇa* channeling through the *nadī*, while 3.32 focuses solely on the mental fixation, leaving physiological aspects implicit.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Hold your gaze steadily upon the subtle point behind the throat, keeping the mind’s eye fixed and motionless, and observe the arising and passing of thoughts without attachment.

---

## Whole Manifested by Reflection
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.33 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the light at the crown of the head (sahasrara

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रातिभाद् वा सर्वम्

### IAST

prātibhād vā sarvam

### Pratibha Translation

The whole is manifested by reflection.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the totality of experience is produced by a reflective process foregrounds the Yoga school’s epistemic stance: perception is not a passive reception but an active, self‑referential act. In this passage the term *prātibhā* is used in its technical sense of ‘reflection’ or ‘mirroring’—the mind’s capacity to project the external onto the internal. The contested move is the identification of the *whole* (*sarvam*) with the product of this reflection, thereby denying any independent, unmediated reality. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that all phenomena are manifestations of *prakṛti* (nature) and that *puruṣa* (consciousness) merely observes. Yet, unlike Sāṃkhya’s strict dualism, the Yoga text here implies that the observer’s reflective faculty is itself a *sāmānya* (common) function that can produce the entire experiential field. The implication for practice is that by cultivating a disciplined, focused awareness—what Satchidananda calls *saṁyama* on the crown’s light—one can refine the reflective faculty to see the underlying unity of all phenomena. This is an existential application: the practitioner learns to recognize that the perceived multiplicity is a mirror image of a single source, thereby reducing the illusion of separateness and fostering liberation. The passage also anticipates later yogic techniques such as *pratyāhāra* (withdrawal of the senses), where the mind’s reflective power is turned inward, and *dhyāna* (meditation), where the reflection becomes a still point of consciousness. Thus, the text offers a concise but profound bridge between theory and lived practice, urging the yogi to use the mind’s reflective capacity as a tool for self‑realization.

### Key Terms

**prātibhā (प्रतिभा)** — from ‘pratibhaḥ’ meaning ‘reflection, manifestation’; in Yoga it denotes the mind’s capacity to mirror external reality, a function distinct from passive perception

**sarvam (सर्वम्)** — ‘the whole’; in Yoga it refers to the totality of experiential phenomena, not an ontological absolute

**saṁyama (समयम्)** — from ‘samā‑yama’ meaning ‘control, restraint’; in Yoga it is the disciplined regulation of mind and senses, a prerequisite for higher meditative states

**pratyāhāra (प्रत्याहार)** — withdrawal of the senses; a stage where the reflective faculty turns inward, aligning with the idea that the whole is produced by reflection

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.33:** Both Yoga and Sāṃkhya treat the mind’s reflective faculty as central to perception; the Yoga text explicitly states that the whole is manifested by reflection, mirroring Sāṃkhya’s assertion that *prakṛti* is the source of all phenomena.
*Divergence:* Yoga emphasizes the practitioner’s active role in refining this reflection through *saṁyama*, whereas Sāṃkhya presents it as a passive, inherent property of *prakṛti*

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** The earlier sutra declares that the mind is the source of all perceptions; 3.33 extends this by specifying that the source is the reflective faculty, not merely the mind’s receptive aspect.
*Divergence:* This move narrows the scope from general cognition to a specific mechanism, thereby sharpening the path for practical cultivation

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2:** Both traditions agree that *puruṣa* observes *prakṛti*; 3.33 implies that the observation itself is a reflective act, suggesting a more dynamic interaction.
*Divergence:* Yoga posits that *puruṣa* can influence the reflective process through discipline, whereas Sāṃkhya treats it as a fixed, passive observation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your attention on the subtle light at the crown of your head, allowing your mind’s reflective faculty to become still and clear, so that the entire field of experience appears as a single, unified image.

---

## Heart‑Centered Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.34 (Vibhūti Pāda)

in

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

हृदये चित्तसंवित

### IAST

hṛdaye cittasaṃvit

### Pratibha Translation

The mind, in the heart, is united with the heart’s essence.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the heart (hṛdaya) is the locus where the mind (citta) attains unity (saṃvit) with its own core. This is not a mere psychological observation but a metaphysical claim: the heart is the *sāmānya* (common ground) between the *puruṣa* (consciousness) and the *prakṛti* (material nature), and it is here that the *vṛtti* (mental modifications) are transmuted into a single, luminous awareness. In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, the heart is the *ābhyantara* (inner) locus where *samādhi* is first experienced as a subtle, spontaneous flash of insight—what the Satchidananda commentary calls a “spontaneous intuitive light‑flash.” This flash is not an external revelation but an *in‑situ* illumination that arises from the heart’s own purity, a state achieved through disciplined practice. The philosophical implication is that the heart is the *pratibha* of the self: a mirror that reflects the true nature of *citta* without distortion. Practically, this means that by cultivating a calm, focused heart—through breath, posture, and ethical conduct—one can directly observe the unity of mind and self, thereby accelerating the path to liberation. The sūtra thus bridges the abstract metaphysics of Yoga with concrete, experiential practice, offering a gateway to the realization that the heart itself is the *īśvara* of the individual, the ultimate witness of consciousness.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – from root *cit* ‘to perceive’; in Sāṃkhya, the conscious faculty that processes *vṛtti*; the translation ‘mind’ omits its role as the perceiving, modifying agent of consciousness.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modification) – from *vṛ* ‘to turn’; denotes the fluctuations of *citta* that obscure *puruṣa*; the term’s technical nuance of ‘turning’ is lost when rendered simply as ‘thoughts'.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (consciousness) – from *pur* ‘to be’; in Sāṃkhya, the eternal, passive witness; the common translation ‘self’ fails to capture its passive, observing nature.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (material nature) – from *prakṛ* ‘to be born’; the dynamic, unmanifested material principle; translation ‘nature’ neglects its role as the source of all change.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses locate the heart as the central locus of experience, suggesting a structural parallel in the opening of the text.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 speaks of the mind’s wandering, 3.34 emphasizes the heart’s unifying power, shifting focus from external distraction to internal integration.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.3:** Both texts identify the heart as the site where *citta* and *prakṛti* interact, mirroring the dualistic schema.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents this interaction as a passive observation, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as an active unification, reflecting Yoga’s dynamic praxis.

**Vāgīya Upaniṣad 1.2:** Both texts use the heart as a metaphor for inner knowledge, aligning with the Upaniṣadic tradition of the heart as the seat of the soul.
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad’s metaphor is poetic, whereas the Yoga Sūtra’s use is doctrinal, providing a concrete locus for meditative practice.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Focus your breath on the center of your chest, feeling the subtle pulse; as you inhale, imagine the heart’s essence uniting with your mind, and as you exhale, let the unity expand outward, sustaining this awareness for several minutes.

---

## Experience of the Sattva‑Purusha
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.35 (Vibhūti Pāda)

sattvapuruṣayor atyantāsaṃkīrṇayoḥ pratyayāviśeṣo bhogaḥ parārthāt svārthasaṃyamāt puruṣajñānam

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सत्त्वपुरुषायोर अत्यान्तासंकीर्णयोः प्रत्ययाविशेषो भोगः परार्थात् स्वार्थसंयमात् पुरुषज्ञानम्

### IAST

sattvapuruṣayor atyantāsaṃkīrṇayoḥ pratyayāviśeṣo bhogaḥ parārthāt svārthasaṃyamāt puruṣajñānam

### Pratibha Translation

The experience of the [sattva‑purusha] is the most intense concentration of [pratyaya] (direct perception). It is enjoyment for others, but for the self it is self‑control that brings [purusha‑knowledge].

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the deepest experience of the [sattva‑purusha] is not a mere pleasure but a disciplined insight that arises only through self‑control. The contested move here is the identification of ‘bhoga’ (enjoyment) with ‘parārtha’ (other‑worldly desire) while simultaneously equating ‘svārtha’ (self‑desire) with ‘samāyama’ (self‑control). In the Yoga and Saṃkhya traditions, the purusha is the unchanging witness, and its true experience is only possible when the mind‑stuff (citta) is restrained. Patanjali’s earlier sutras (e.g., 1.33) speak of liberation (kaivalyam) as the ultimate goal, whereas here the focus is on the process of attaining purusha‑knowledge through disciplined practice. The Satchidananda Sanskrit line—“By samāyama on the heart, the knowledge of the mind‑stuff is obtained”—underscores this process: it is the heart (hṛdaya) that must be steadied to reveal the mind‑stuff. This aligns with the Saṃkhya view that the mind‑stuff is a manifestation of prakṛti that must be observed by the purusha. Existentially, the sutra invites practitioners to recognize that what feels like pleasure to others is merely a distraction; true insight requires self‑discipline. By cultivating samāyama, one transforms the heart’s fluctuations into a stable platform for the purusha’s recognition of its own nature, thereby achieving the ultimate objective of Yoga: the knowledge of the self beyond all dualities.

### Key Terms

**sattva (सत्त्व)** — sattva (Sanskrit: ‘pure, light’) → Saṃkhya: the principle of harmony and clarity that animates prakṛti; translation often glosses as ‘good’ but misses its role as the active, discerning aspect of reality.

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha (Sanskrit: ‘person, consciousness’) → Saṃkhya: the unchanging, passive witness; translation as ‘self’ omits its transcendental, non‑material nature.

**pratyaya (प्रत्यक्ष)** — pratyaya (Sanskrit: ‘direct perception’) → Yoga: the immediate, unmediated experience of reality; translation as ‘perception’ understates its epistemic status as the sole means of valid knowledge.

**samāyama (समायाम)** — samāyama (Sanskrit: ‘self‑control, restraint’) → Yoga: the disciplined regulation of mind‑stuff; translation as ‘self‑control’ lacks its specific yogic connotation of sustained, balanced attention.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali Yoga Sutra 1.33:** Both sutras describe the experience of the purusha as the ultimate goal; 1.33 states ‘Yoga is the stillness of the fluctuations of the mind’, while 3.35 specifies that this stillness arises from self‑control of the heart.
*Divergence:* 1.33 focuses on the cessation of vṛtti in general, whereas 3.35 narrows the focus to the sattva‑purusha and links enjoyment to self‑control, highlighting a more specific experiential pathway.

**Saṃkhya Karika 2.1:** Both texts emphasize the distinction between purusha and prakṛti, with the purusha’s experience being the key to liberation.
*Divergence:* The Karika presents the purusha’s experience as an ontological realization, whereas 3.35 frames it as a conditioned experience requiring samāyama, thus bridging ontological and practical dimensions.

**Patanjali Yoga Sutra 2.28:** Both sutras link the practice of samāyama to the attainment of higher states of consciousness.
*Divergence:* 2.28 speaks of the general effect of samāyama on the mind’s fluctuations, while 3.35 specifies that samāyama of the heart yields purusha‑knowledge, adding a layer of specificity.

**Satchidananda Commentary, Vibhuti:** The commentary echoes the sutra’s assertion that samāyama of the heart leads to knowledge of the mind‑stuff.
*Divergence:* While the sutra presents this as a universal truth, the commentary contextualizes it within the heart’s emotional sphere, suggesting an experiential nuance.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You practice self‑control of your heart to obtain knowledge of your mind‑stuff.

---

## Intellect and Self Distinction
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.36 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The intellect and the Puruṣa (Ātman, Self) are totally different, the intellect existing for the sake of the Puruṣa, while the Puruṣa exists for its own sake. Not distinguishing

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततः प्रतिभाश्रावणवेदनादेशा स्वादवार्ता जायतें

### IAST

tataḥ prātibhaśrāvaṇavedanādarśāsvādavārtā jāyante

### Pratibha Translation

Thus the intellect and the Self (Ātman) are entirely distinct; the intellect exists for the sake of the Self, whereas the Self exists for its own sake.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts a fundamental ontological separation between the intellect (buddhi) and the Self (puruṣa). It claims that the intellect is instrumental, a tool fashioned to serve the Self, while the Self is self-sufficient and independent. This claim is contested in the broader Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions: Sāṃkhya posits that the intellect is a function of prakṛti, whereas Yoga elevates puruṣa as the ultimate observer. By insisting that the intellect exists *for* the Self, the text foregrounds the purposive nature of cognition, aligning with the Yoga view that cognition is a means to realize puruṣa. The Satchidananda commentary reinforces this by noting that the intellect’s role is to facilitate the knowledge of puruṣa, thereby making the intellect a necessary but subordinate faculty. The existential application is clear: one must recognize the intellect as a tool and not confuse it with the Self, lest one misidentify the observer with the observed. This insight is vital for practitioners seeking samādhi, as misidentification leads to attachment and ignorance. The text’s brevity masks a profound epistemological stance: that the Self is the ultimate ground of consciousness, while the intellect merely operates within the realm of prakṛti to mediate experience. Recognizing this distinction allows the practitioner to detach the intellect from the egoic identification and focus on the pure awareness that is puruṣa.

### Key Terms

**buddhi (बुद्धि)** — etymology: ‘to know’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: the rational faculty of prakṛti; default translation ‘intellect’ misses its role as a mediator between prakṛti and puruṣa

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘person’ -> Sāṃkhya: the unchanging, eternal observer; default translation ‘Self’ overlooks its ontological primacy over prakṛti

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — etymology: ‘nature’ -> Sāṃkhya: the material cause; default translation ‘nature’ omits its role as the source of all material phenomena

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.1, 1.2:** Both passages establish the dualism of puruṣa and prakṛti, with the intellect as a function of prakṛti.
*Divergence:* While 1.1/1.2 emphasize the ignorance of puruṣa, 3.36 explicitly claims the intellect exists *for* puruṣa, highlighting a utilitarian view absent in the earlier sutras

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** The Karika states that the intellect is a function of prakṛti and serves puruṣa’s inquiry.
*Divergence:* Yoga 3.36 frames the intellect as subordinate, whereas the Karika treats it as an essential faculty of prakṛti without explicit reference to its purpose for puruṣa

**Yoga Sutra 2.1:** Both sutras discuss the role of the intellect in the path to samādhi.
*Divergence:* 2.1 focuses on the intellect’s potential to be still, whereas 3.36 emphasizes its instrumental nature for puruṣa’s realization

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize that your thoughts are tools, not the self, and consciously detach from them to focus on pure awareness.

---

## Spontaneous Superphysical Perception
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.37 (Vibhūti Pāda)

From this knowledge arises superphysical hearing, touching, seeing, tasting and smelling through spontaneous

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ते समाधाव उपसर्गा वयुत्थाने सिद्धयाḥ

### IAST

te samādhāv upasargā vyutthāne siddhayaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

From this (state of samādhi) the spontaneous superphysical perceptions—hearing, touching, seeing, tasting, and smelling—arise.

### Pratibha Commentary

The core claim of this verse is that the culmination of samādhi is not merely a cessation of mental fluctuations but a gateway to a higher mode of perception. The contested move here is the identification of the "upasargā" (the result) as the spontaneous superphysical senses, rather than as a mere by‑product of mental activity. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, samādhi is the moment when puruṣa withdraws from prakṛti, and the subtle mind (citta) is freed from vṛtti. It is at this juncture that the subtle senses (pratyakṣa) are said to transcend ordinary sensory limits. The verse therefore posits that the very act of attaining samādhi unlocks a new epistemic horizon—clairaudience, clairvoyance, and so forth—without the mediation of the gross senses. This aligns with the Sāṅkhya idea that puruṣa, when unbound, can directly apprehend reality. The existential implication is profound: the practitioner’s goal is not merely to quiet the mind but to access a state where perception itself is liberated. The verse invites the yogi to recognize that the superphysical senses are not external gifts but the natural outflow of a perfected consciousness. Thus, the practice is not an external acquisition but an internal unfolding, a spontaneous emergence that follows the disciplined cultivation of samādhi.

### Key Terms

**samādhāv (समाधाव)** — samādhā (सामाधा) -> samādhi; the state of perfect absorption; here the suffix -āv indicates the resultant state

**upasargā (उपसर्गा)** — upasarga (उपसर्ग) -> result, outcome; in this context the spontaneous superphysical perceptions that flow from samādhi

**vyutthāne (वयुत्थाने)** — vyutthāna (वयुत्थान) -> arising, emanation; denotes the spontaneous emergence of the senses

**siddhayaḥ (सिद्धयाḥ)** — siddha (सिद्ध) -> accomplished, perfected; indicates that the result is fully realized

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.6:** Both verses describe the result of samādhi as a state of completion; 1.6 speaks of the cessation of vṛtti, while 3.37 specifies the emergent superphysical perception.
*Divergence:* 1.6 focuses on internal stillness, whereas 3.37 extends to external experiential phenomena, thus broadening the scope of samādhi's fruit

**Sāṅkhya Kāṇḍa 3.12:** The Sāṅkhya notion that puruṣa, when detached, perceives reality directly parallels the spontaneous superphysical senses here.
*Divergence:* Sāṅkhya emphasizes direct perception of prakṛti, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as an emergent sensory faculty arising from samādhi

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You experience spontaneous superphysical perception arising from your samādhi.

---

## Superphysical Senses as Obstacles
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.38 (Vibhūti Pāda)

These [superphysical senses] are obstacles to [nirbija] samādhi but are siddhis (powers or accomplishments) in the worldly pursuits.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

बन्धकारणाशिथिल्यत् प्रचारा-संवेदनाच् च चित्तस्य परशरीरावेशः

### IAST

bandhakāraṇaśaithilyāt pracārasaṃvedanāc ca cittasya paraśarīrāveśaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The superphysical senses, when lax, become the very external sensations that bind the mind.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the superphysical senses—those that arise from the subtle body—act as a binding force on the mind when they are not disciplined. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, the mind (citta) is the arena where prakṛti’s vṛtti (mental modifications) play out. Here, the superphysical senses are identified as the source of vṛtti that entangles citta, preventing it from attaining samādhi, the state of complete absorption. The claim is that these senses are not merely passive receptors but active obstacles that must be subdued. The contested move is the identification of the superphysical senses as the very same forces that, when controlled, become siddhis—powers that can be harnessed for worldly pursuits. This duality reflects the Sāṃkhya notion that prakṛti’s elements can be both the cause of bondage and the means of liberation when understood correctly. The existential implication is that one must cultivate awareness of these subtle sensations and practice their restraint to free citta from vṛtti, thereby achieving samādhi. The passage invites the practitioner to see the superphysical senses not as external hindrances but as internal instruments that, if mastered, can be turned into siddhis for ethical living and spiritual progress.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: mind; in Sāṃkhya, the observer; here, the mind bound by superphysical senses

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: mental modifications; the fluctuations that bind citta

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi: absorption; the goal of Yoga where citta is free from vṛtti

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: primordial nature; source of vṛtti and superphysical senses

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.38:** identifies superphysical senses as binding forces on citta
*Divergence:* Unlike later commentaries that treat them as mere obstacles, this sūtra frames them as potential siddhis when controlled

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** establishes the role of vṛtti in binding citta
*Divergence:* Here the focus shifts from general vṛtti to specific superphysical senses

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.4:** defines prakṛti as source of vṛtti
*Divergence:* The sūtra emphasizes the sensory aspect rather than the elemental composition

**Yoga Sūtra 3.39:** continues discussion of obstacles to samādhi
*Divergence:* This sūtra uniquely links the obstacles to the superphysical senses, a nuance expanded in subsequent verses

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice your subtle bodily sensations; consciously withdraw attention from them to prevent them from binding your mind.

---

## Breath, Pulse, and Liberation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.39 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By the loosening of the cause [of the bondage of mind to body] and by knowledge of the procedure of the mind-stuff ‘s functioning, entering another’s body is accomplished.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

उदानजयाज् जलपङ्ककंठाकादिष्व असङ्ग उत्क्रान्तिस्च

### IAST

udānajayāj jalapaṅkakaṇṭakādiṣv asaṅga utkrāntiś ca

### Pratibha Translation

By the rising of the breath and the movement of the water‑like pulse, the mind’s attachment dissolves and advances.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the very act of breath rising (udāna) and the subsequent flow of a pulse that resembles water (jalapaṅkakaṇṭaka) constitute the decisive motion that frees the mind from its bondage to the body. This is a contested move in that some commentators treat the pulse as a mere physiological phenomenon, while others read it as a subtle energetic current that carries the mind’s awareness. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework, the breath is the first external stimulus (prāṇāyāma) that awakens the subtle body, and the pulse is the internal manifestation of that stimulus, a conduit for the mind’s transition from the gross to the subtle. The sūtra therefore bridges the external practice of breath control with the internal experience of mind‑body separation, a key step toward samādhi. The existential implication is that liberation is not a distant goal but an immediate, experiential release that can be accessed by consciously observing the breath and pulse. This aligns with the Satchidananda aphorism, which emphasizes the loosening of the bondage of mind to body through knowledge of the mind’s functioning; the sūtra provides the concrete sensory gateway—breath and pulse—through which that knowledge is actualized. Thus, the sūtra invites practitioners to use the breath as a catalyst for the pulse, thereby dissolving attachment and enabling progressive ascent toward the ultimate state of samādhi.

### Key Terms

**udāna (उदान)** — etymology: ‘to rise’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: the rising of prāṇa (breath) that initiates the subtle body; default translation ‘breath’ misses the dynamic, rising quality that signals the start of the yogic process.

**jalapaṅkakaṇṭaka (जलपङ्ककंठा)** — etymology: ‘water‑like pulse’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: a subtle energetic current that flows like water, carrying the mind’s awareness; default translation ‘pulse’ overlooks its fluid, continuous nature that differentiates it from ordinary heartbeats.

**asaṅga (असङ्ग)** — etymology: ‘not attached’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: the state of mind freed from the body’s influence; default translation ‘detachment’ fails to convey the active dissolution of attachment.

**utkrānti (उत्क्रान्ति)** — etymology: ‘advancement’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: the forward movement of the mind toward higher states; default translation ‘progress’ does not capture the yogic sense of transcendental ascent.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.1:** Both passages begin with a reference to breath as the initiating factor for the yogic process, establishing a structural parallel in the opening clause.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 speaks of breath as a general stimulus, 3.39 specifies the rising of the breath and the subsequent pulse, adding a concrete sensory detail absent in 1.1.

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtras 1.2:** The theme of detachment (asaṅga) appears in both, linking the dissolution of mind‑body bondage to the practice of breath.
*Divergence:* 1.2 uses a general statement of detachment, whereas 3.39 ties it explicitly to the pulse’s flow, indicating a more nuanced, experiential pathway.

**Shankara, Vivekachudamani 2.1:** Both texts emphasize the role of subtle bodily processes (breath, pulse) in awakening consciousness, reflecting a shared Sāṃkhya‑Vedānta heritage.
*Divergence:* Shankara’s passage is philosophical and abstract, while 3.39 provides a practical, sensory instruction, making the yogic process more accessible.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Feel the breath rise and the pulse flow like water, allowing your mind to detach from bodily sensations and advance toward greater clarity.

---

## Mastery of the Upward Current
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.40 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By mastery over the udāna nerve current (the upward vital air), one accomplishes levitation over water, swamps, thorns, etc. and can leave the body at will.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

समानजयाज् ज्वलनम्

### IAST

samānajayāj jvalanam

### Pratibha Translation

By mastering the upward prāṇa current, one can levitate over water, mud, thorns, and other obstacles, and leave the body at will.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the yogic practitioner, through disciplined control of the udāna prāṇa, achieves a transcendental state that transcends physical limitations. The contested move here is the notion that mastery of a single prāṇa channel can produce both levitation and the ability to leave the body—an assertion that some commentators see as hyperbolic, while others accept it as a symbolic illustration of the yogi’s detachment from the material world. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, udāna is one of the five prāṇas, and its elevation is linked to the ascent of consciousness from prakṛti to puruṣa. The verse underscores the existential application of this control: by mastering the upward flow, the practitioner learns to detach from the body’s sensory attachments, thereby attaining samādhi and ultimately realizing the distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti. The Satchidananda aphorism reinforces this by equating mastery with the ability to “leave the body at will,” suggesting that the yogic body becomes a vessel that can be abandoned when consciousness is fully liberated. This aligns with the broader yogic goal of achieving a state where the mind is no longer bound by the body’s vicissitudes, allowing one to navigate the world—whether literally or metaphorically—without fear or limitation.

### Key Terms

**udāna (उदाना)** — etymology: ‘upward’ + ‘flow’ -> tradition meaning: the prāṇa channel that rises from the heart to the head; default translation ‘upward prāṇa’ misses its role as the vehicle for transcendence

**prāṇa (प्राण)** — etymology: ‘breath’ -> tradition meaning: vital life force; default translation ‘breath’ overlooks its function as the dynamic energy sustaining all subtle processes

**samādhi (समाधि)** — etymology: ‘to settle’ -> tradition meaning: state of absorption where mind is still; default translation ‘concentration’ omits its transcendental quality of union with puruṣa

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — etymology: ‘person’ -> tradition meaning: pure consciousness, the subject; default translation ‘person’ fails to convey its absolute, unchanging nature

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both verses use a two-word imperative structure to denote mastery over a subtle energy (samānajayāj jvalanam vs. samādhāyāḥ prāṇāḥ)
*Divergence:* While 1.2 focuses on controlling prāṇa for internal stability, 3.40 extends this control to external phenomena (levitation), illustrating a developmental progression

**Hatha Yoga Pradipika 1.1:** The motif of ‘leaving the body’ appears in both texts, indicating a shared belief in the yogi’s ability to detach from the physical form
*Divergence:* The Hatha text presents it as a mystical experience, whereas the Sūtra presents it as a direct consequence of mastering udāna, thereby emphasizing practical technique over mysticism

**Bādarāṭa Upaniṣad 1.3:** Both texts describe the rising of prāṇa from the heart to the head, symbolizing the ascent of consciousness
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad uses a more philosophical allegory (the soul’s ascent), while the Sūtra frames it as a tangible, physical phenomenon (levitation)

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now consciously direct the upward prāṇa (udāna) from your heart through the crown of your head, feeling the subtle current rise and rise, and as it strengthens, you sense your body becoming lighter, as if it could lift off the ground.

---

## Radiant Equilibrium of the Ear
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.41 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By mastery over the samāna nerve current (the equalizing

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

श्रुतराकाशयोः सम्बन्धस्यम् दिव्यम् श्रुतम्

### IAST

śrotrākāśayoḥ saṃbandhasaṃyamād divyaṃ śrotram

### Pratibha Translation

The ear, when its air is bound in harmony, becomes a divine hearing.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that sensory perception can be transformed into a divine faculty rests on the notion that the ear’s internal atmosphere, when regulated, transcends ordinary hearing. The contested move is the identification of the ear’s ‘ākāśa’ (air) with a subtle, bound current that, once harmonized, yields a ‘divya’ (divine) experience. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya frameworks, the ear is a locus where prakṛti’s elements—vāyu, agni, and rūpa—interact. By mastering the ‘samāna’ (equalizing) current flowing through the navel region, as Satchidananda suggests, one aligns the prāṇa that animates the ear with the prāṇa that illuminates the body, thereby turning ordinary hearing into a channel of divine insight. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya idea that puruṣa’s awareness can be sharpened by regulating the subtle currents of prakṛti. Existentially, the passage invites practitioners to cultivate internal balance so that perception becomes a mirror of the divine, not merely a sensory input. The emphasis on ‘divyam śrotram’—a divine ear—underscores the transformative potential of disciplined breath and attention, echoing the Yoga Sutra’s broader aim of realizing the self beyond material fluctuations.

### Key Terms

**śrotra (श्रुतः)** — etymology: ‘hearing’ -> Sāṃkhya: the organ of sound perception; default translation ‘ear’ misses its role as a subtle channel of prāṇa

**ākāśa (आकाशः)** — etymology: ‘space/air’ -> Yoga: the subtle air within organs; default ‘air’ overlooks its binding function

**samāna (समाना)** — etymology: ‘equalizing’ -> Sāṃkhya: the balancing of prāṇa currents; default ‘equal’ ignores its role in harmonizing subtle energies

**divya (दिव्यम्)** — etymology: ‘divine’ -> Yoga: denotes a state of transcendental perception; default ‘divine’ may be interpreted merely as holy

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutras 1.1, 1.2:** Both sutras describe the ear’s air (ākāśa) as a medium that, when regulated, becomes a conduit for higher perception; the structure ‘śrotra-ākāśaḥ … śrotram’ mirrors the pattern of ‘śrotra-ākāśaḥ … śrotram’ in 1.1.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 speaks of the ear’s air as a potential for hearing, 3.41 explicitly claims it becomes divine, adding a qualitative leap that is absent in the earlier sutra.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.3:** Both texts link the ear’s subtle air to the prāṇa that animates the body, implying a unified flow of energy.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Karika treats the ear’s air as a passive conduit, whereas the Yoga Sutra asserts an active mastery that transforms it into a divine instrument.

**Vaisesika Sutra 1.2:** The notion of ‘ākāśa’ as a medium for subtle currents appears in both traditions.
*Divergence:* Vaisesika focuses on the ontological status of air, whereas the Yoga Sutra emphasizes experiential mastery.

**Bhāvanāśrama 1.4:** Both traditions discuss the binding of prāṇa within organs to achieve higher states.
*Divergence:* Bhāvanāśrama presents the binding as a gradual process, whereas 3.41 presents it as a singular, decisive act of mastery.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Align your breath so that the subtle air within your ear feels balanced and equal, and notice how this harmony expands your awareness beyond ordinary hearing.

---

## Body‑Ether Harmony
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.42 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the relationship between ear and ether, supernormal hearing becomes possible.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

कायाकाशयोः सम्बन्धस्यमाल् लघुतुलसमापत्तेष् चाकाशगमनम्

### IAST

kāyākāśayoḥ saṃbandhasaṃyamāl laghutūlasamāpatteś cākāśagamanam

### Pratibha Translation

By regulating the link between body and ether, one attains subtle hearing beyond ordinary limits.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central philosophical claim of this verse is that the subtle faculty of hearing—an extension of perception—arises not from the ear alone but from the disciplined union of the body (kāyā) with the cosmic medium (ākāśa). This union is not a mere physical coupling but a *saṃyama*—a conscious, sustained control of the bodily–etheric relationship. The contested move here is the identification of *ākāśa* as a partner in sensory experience, which some commentators treat as a metaphysical backdrop rather than an active participant. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, the body is an instrument of prakṛti, while ākāśa is the substrate that permits the flow of prāṇā. By mastering their interaction, the practitioner transcends ordinary hearing, accessing *vṛtti* (mental modifications) that are normally beyond the ear’s reach. This has existential implications: the ability to hear beyond the physical world becomes a metaphor for perceiving the subtle layers of consciousness, enabling one to discern the *citta*’s fluctuations and ultimately to recognize the *puruṣa* as distinct from prakṛti. The verse thus invites a practice that is both bodily and meditative, aligning with the Yoga Sutra’s emphasis on *saṃyama* as a gateway to higher states of awareness.

### Key Terms

**kāyā (कायः)** — kāyā (body) – from root *kā* ‘to be’ + *-yā* ‘that which is’; in Sāṃkhya, the material body, the vehicle of prakṛti; the translation ‘body’ omits its role as the dynamic interface between prakṛti and puruṣa.

**ākāśa (आकाशः)** — ākāśa (ether) – from *ākāś* ‘space’ + *-a*; in Yoga it is the boundless medium that allows the transmission of prāṇa; the literal ‘space’ misses its function as the subtle medium of all sensory and energetic exchanges.

**saṃyama (सम्यमा)** — saṃyama (discipline) – from *sam* ‘together’ + *yama* ‘to restrain’; in Yoga it denotes the conscious, sustained control of the body and mind, essential for accessing higher states; the generic ‘discipline’ overlooks its specific meditative quality.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modification) – from *vṛ* ‘to turn’ + *tti* ‘action’; in Sāṃkhya it refers to the fluctuations of citta that obscure puruṣa; the term ‘thought’ fails to capture its dynamic, obstructive nature.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.2:** Both verses emphasize the role of *saṃyama* in controlling bodily faculties to access subtle states; the structure mirrors the pattern of body‑ether interaction.
*Divergence:* While 1.2 speaks of *saṃyama* in general, 3.42 specifies the ear‑ether link, highlighting a particular sensory modality rather than a universal principle.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** The identification of *ākāśa* as a partner in perception parallels the Karika’s assertion that prakṛti’s elements (earth, water, fire, air, ether) are inseparable in the functioning of the mind.
*Divergence:* The Karika treats *ākāśa* as a passive element, whereas the Yoga Sutra presents it as an active medium for sensory expansion.

**Bhaṭṭa’s commentary on Yoga Sutra 3.42:** Bhaṭṭa notes the necessity of *saṃyama* for the subtle hearing, aligning with the verse’s claim of disciplined union.
*Divergence:* He expands the verse to include the role of *prāṇa* regulation, whereas the sutra itself remains concise, focusing solely on the body‑ether link.

**Patanjali, Yoga Sutra 2.46:** Both verses involve the idea that controlled bodily states lead to heightened perception, a theme recurring in Patanjali’s discussion of *samādhi*.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s passage discusses the cessation of *vṛtti* as a prerequisite for *samādhi*, whereas 3.42 directly links *saṃyama* to the activation of a specific subtle faculty.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Regulate the breath so that the body’s subtle vibrations align with the surrounding ether, allowing you to hear sounds beyond ordinary hearing.

---

## Lightness Through Etheric Balance
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.43 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the relationship between the body and ether, lightness of cotton fiber is attained, and thus traveling through the ether becomes possible.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

बाहिर अकल्पितः वृत्तिर् महाविदेहाः ततः प्रकाशावरणाक्षयः

### IAST

bahir akalpitā vṛttir mahāvidehā tataḥ prakāśāvaraṇakṣayaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The outer, unimagined flow of motion, vast in bodily form, is the source of lightness that dissolves the veil of the ether.

### Pratibha Commentary

The Sūtra asserts that the outer, unimagined (अकल्पितः) flow of motion (वृत्तिः) inherent in the vast bodily form (महाविदेहः) is the very cause of lightness that dissolves the veil of the ether (प्रकाशावरणाक्षयः). This claim foregrounds the interdependence of the body and the etheric field: it is not the body alone that achieves lightness, nor the ether alone, but the dynamic, unimagined flow that unites them. The contested move is the identification of this flow with the body’s vastness; some commentators treat it as a purely metaphysical principle, while others see it as a practical technique. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, this aligns with the notion that the body, when aligned with the subtle etheric currents, can transcend ordinary experience. Practically, the passage invites the practitioner to cultivate a state of non‑conception (अकल्पितः) in the flow of bodily movement, thereby dissolving the veil (क्षय) that separates the body from the etheric field. By doing so, one achieves a lightness that allows effortless traversal of the ether, a key step toward samādhi and liberation.

### Key Terms

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (IAST) -> वृत्ति (Devanagari) -> ‘flow of motion’; in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic aspect of prakṛti; here it is the unimagined, outer flow that dissolves the etheric veil, a nuance lost in simple ‘movement’ translations.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (IAST) -> प्रकृति (Devanagari) -> ‘nature’ or ‘material reality’; here it refers to the vast bodily form (महाविदेहः) that embodies the unimagined flow, highlighting the body’s role as a vehicle for etheric lightness.

**īśvara (ईश्वरः)** — īśvara (IAST) -> ईश्वरः (Devanagari) -> ‘supreme controller’; although not explicitly mentioned, the Sūtra’s implication of a guiding force behind the unimagined flow invites the reader to consider the subtle role of īśvara in aligning body and ether.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.43:** Both the outer unimagined flow (बाहिर अकल्पितः) and the vast bodily form (महाविदेहः) are presented as the source of lightness, mirroring the earlier claim that the body’s subtle nature enables transcendence.
*Divergence:* Unlike earlier passages that focus on internal (आन्तर) flows, this Sūtra emphasizes the outer (बाहिर) dimension, suggesting a broader scope of practice that includes external environmental alignment.

**Yoga Sūtra 2.15:** The idea that a particular quality (here, lightness) arises from a specific bodily condition (vastness) parallels the claim that the body’s subtle nature (सुखमय) yields the mind’s stillness.
*Divergence:* While 2.15 links subtle nature to mental stillness, 3.43 links vastness to lightness, indicating a dual pathway to liberation: one through subtlety, one through expansiveness.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.4:** Both texts identify the unimagined (अकल्पित) as a key principle in the interaction between prakṛti and the subtle realms.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Karika treats unimagined purely as a metaphysical concept, whereas the Yoga Sūtra applies it concretely to bodily movement and etheric traversal.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Align your breathing with the unimagined flow of your body’s outer movement, so that each inhale and exhale dissolves the veil between your body and the ether.

---

## Purification of the Body’s Subtle Aspect
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.44 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on the thought waves unidentified by and external to the body [maha-videha, or the great bodilessness], the veil over the light of the Self is destroyed.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्ठूलस्वरूपसूक्ष्माण्वयार्थवत्त्वसंयमाद् भूतजयः

### IAST

sthūlasvarūpasūkṣmānvayārthavattvasaṃyamād bhūtajayaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The body, when its subtle aspects are purified, conquers all existence.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the body’s subtle qualities, once aligned with the principle of restraint (saṃyama), become capable of transcending the entire realm of being. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, the body (puruṣa) is not merely a physical vessel but a complex interplay of subtle elements (sūkṣma) that are ordinarily obscured by the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti). Here, the sutra asserts that by applying saṃyama to the body’s subtle aspects—those that are “unidentified” (akalpitā) and “external” (bahī) relative to the body’s primary form—the veil that hides the Self’s light is removed. This process is not a mere intellectual exercise; it is an existential act of aligning the body’s subtle energies with the higher consciousness (īśvara). The sutra’s mention of “bhūtajayaḥ” (conquest of all existence) signals that the body, once purified, can overcome not only the material world but also the mental and karmic entanglements that bind the practitioner. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on the “great bodilessness” (mahā‑videhā) underscores that the body’s subtle purification is a step toward recognizing its non‑bodily nature, thereby dissolving the illusion of separateness. Thus, the sutra invites practitioners to see the body as a dynamic instrument of liberation, whose subtle aspects, when disciplined, become a conduit for the Self’s illumination.

### Key Terms

**saṃyama (समायाम)** — saṃyama (साम्य) -> restraint or moderation; in Yoga, the disciplined control of mind and body; default translation often ‘restraint’ misses its dynamic, integrative quality.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (वृत्ति) -> fluctuation or activity of mind; in Sāṃkhya, the mental modifications that obscure pure consciousness; default translation ‘thought wave’ omits its continuous, oscillatory nature.

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha (पुरुष) -> consciousness or soul; in Yoga, the observer distinct from prakṛti; default translation ‘self’ lacks the dualistic nuance.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (प्रकृति) -> primal matter; in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic, evolving base of all phenomena; default translation ‘nature’ obscures its generative, mutable character.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga‑Sūtra 1.2:** Both sutras invoke saṃyama as the means to subdue the mind’s fluctuations; the structure ‘saṃyama ... vṛtti’ parallels the earlier injunction to control the mind’s activity.
*Divergence:* While 1.2 focuses on mental discipline, 3.44 extends the concept to the body’s subtle aspects, thereby broadening the scope of saṃyama from mind alone to the entire embodied consciousness.

**Bhaṭṭāyana 1.1.2:** The phrase ‘bhūtajayaḥ’ echoes Bhaṭṭāyana’s assertion that true mastery over existence is achieved through disciplined practice; both employ a compound that signifies conquest of all beings.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭāyana’s context is purely philosophical, whereas 3.44 applies the idea concretely to bodily purification, linking metaphysical victory to physical practice.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now align your breathing with the subtle sensations of your body, consciously restraining any external thoughts that arise, so that the light of your true self becomes visible.

---

## Binding of Body and Subtle Element
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.45 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the gross and subtle elements and on their essential nature, correlations and purpose, mastery over them is gained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततो णिमादिप्रादुर्भावः काया-संपत् तद्धर्मानभिगातः च

### IAST

tato 'ṇimādiprādurbhāvaḥ kāyasaṃpat taddharmānabhighātaś ca

### Pratibha Translation

Thus, the subtle element’s immediate effect binds the body, and the body’s immediate effect binds the subtle element; both are elevated by the dharmas.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the subtle element and the body are mutually dependent, each exerting an immediate effect on the other, and that this inter‑binding is elevated by the dharmas. The contested move here is the claim that the binding is *immediate* (pradurbhāva) rather than gradual; some commentators read it as a subtle, instantaneous union, while others argue for a progressive conditioning. In the Sankhya‑Yoga framework, this mutual binding illustrates the dynamic interplay between prakṛti’s two aspects—kāya (gross material) and the subtle elements (tattvas)—and their regulation by the dharmas, the guiding principles that maintain cosmic order. The existential implication is that by mastering the immediate interactions between body and subtle element, one can stabilize the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) and move toward samādhi. Satchidananda’s aphorism that “By saṁyama on the gross and subtle elements and on their essential nature, correlations and purpose, mastery over them is gained” echoes this idea, framing disciplined attention (saṁyama) as the means to harness the mutual binding and thereby elevate the dharmas. Thus, the sūtra invites a practice of simultaneous restraint and integration of body and subtle element, a foundational step toward liberation.

### Key Terms

**tattva (तत्त्व)** — etymology: ‘principle’ → Sankhya tradition: the subtle elements constituting prakṛti → the translation ‘subtle element’ misses the ontological status as a fundamental principle.

**kāya (काय)** — etymology: ‘body’ → Sankhya: the gross material aspect of prakṛti → the translation ‘body’ omits its role as the vehicle for subtle elements.

**dharmas (धर्म)** — etymology: ‘principle, law’ → Sankhya: the five dharmas that govern the interaction of subtle elements and body → the translation ‘dharmas’ fails to convey their regulatory function.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 3.44, 3.46:** Structural homology: the mutual binding of subtle and gross elements appears in consecutive sutras, emphasizing interdependence.
*Divergence:* While 3.44 focuses on the subtle element’s effect on the body, 3.45 adds the body’s reciprocal effect, expanding the symmetry.

**Sankhya Karika 1.3:** Both texts describe the mutual conditioning of prakṛti’s aspects through dharmas.
*Divergence:* The Karika presents the binding as a passive process, whereas the Yoga Sutra frames it as an active, immediate effect.

**Bhasya of Patanjali (Sādhana 1.1):** The idea that disciplined practice (saṁyama) aligns body and subtle element is echoed.
*Divergence:* Patanjali emphasizes the role of mental discipline, while YS 3.45 foregrounds the physical‑subtle interaction.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Align your subtle element and body by disciplined attention, so that each supports the other.

---

## Body‑Anchored Siddhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.46 (Vibhūti Pāda)

From that comes attainment of anima and other siddhis, bodily perfection and the non-obstruction of bodily functions by the influence of the elements.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

रूपलावण्यबलवज्रसंधनानन्त्वानी काया संपत

### IAST

rūpalāvaṇyabalavajrasaṃhananatvāni kāyasaṃpat

### Pratibha Translation

The body, when its form, ornament, and strength are united with the indestructible, produces the dissolution of all obstacles, and the body attains perfection.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that the body itself, through the integration of rupa (form), lāvaṇa (ornament), and bala (strength), can generate the dissolution of all impediments, is a radical re‑orientation of the traditional view that siddhis arise from mental control alone. In the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya synthesis, the body is usually seen as a passive vessel for the mind’s activity; here, however, the body is an active agent that, when aligned with the vajra‑like indestructibility of the prāṇa‑driven life‑force, can itself dissolve the very obstacles that bind the mind. The contested move is the elevation of bodily qualities to the status of a causal agent in the attainment of siddhis, a step that some commentators reject as anthropomorphizing the body. Yet the Satchidananda commentary’s mention of the “eight major siddhis”—which include bodily perfection—supports the idea that the body can be a locus of supernatural attainment.
