This perspective invites an existential application: by cultivating a disciplined, ornamented, and strong physical practice (yoga asanas, breath control, and diet), one can directly influence the dissolution of mental and karmic obstacles, thereby accelerating liberation. The implication is that liberation is not merely a metaphysical detachment but an embodied transformation that can be consciously directed through disciplined bodily practice.

### Key Terms

**rūpa (रूप)** — root: ‘form’; Sāṃkhya: the external manifestation of prakṛti; Yoga: the body’s visible shape; the translation ‘form’ misses the idea of the body as a dynamic, evolving entity rather than a static image.

**lāvaṇa (लावण)** — root: ‘ornament’; Sāṃkhya: the aesthetic quality of prakṛti; Yoga: the adornment of the body through posture and breath; the term ‘ornament’ understates the functional role of aesthetic alignment in spiritual practice.

**bala (बल)** — root: ‘strength’; Sāṃkhya: the force of prakṛti; Yoga: the physical and energetic power cultivated through asana and pranāyaṇa; ‘strength’ alone omits the subtle energetic dimension.

**vajra (वज्र)** — root: ‘indestructible’; Sāṃkhya: the unchanging principle of consciousness; Yoga: the vajra‑like steadiness of the mind; ‘indestructible’ fails to convey the dynamic, unyielding quality of the prāṇa.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga‑Sūtra 1.15:** Both passages link the body’s qualities (rupa, bala) with the attainment of siddhi; the structure of ‘body + qualities → siddhi’ is mirrored.
*Divergence:* In 1.15 the body is a passive instrument, whereas here it is an active agent, breaking the conventional hierarchy of mind over body.

**Brahma‑Sūtra 3.23:** The Brahma‑Sūtra also speaks of the dissolution of obstacles (saṃhāna) through a subtle process; the structural pattern of ‘obstacle dissolution → perfection’ aligns.
*Divergence:* The Brahma‑Sūtra attributes dissolution to the mind’s clarity, while this passage attributes it to the body’s integration with vajra, showing a shift from mental to bodily agency.

**Yogācārabhūti 1.10:** Both texts discuss the role of prakṛti’s qualities (bala, vajra) in transforming the mind; the homology lies in the triadic structure of body + quality + dissolution.
*Divergence:* Yogācārabhūti emphasizes the mind’s transformation through perception, whereas this passage emphasizes bodily transformation through physical alignment.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You align your posture, adorn your body with mindful breath, and strengthen your limbs so that the body itself dissolves obstacles and attains perfection.

---

## Body Perfection in Yoga
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.47 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Beauty, grace,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ग्रहणस्वरूपस्मिताञ्वयार्थवत्तवासंयमाद् इंद्रियजयः

### IAST

grahaṇasvarūpāsmitānvayārthavattvasaṃyamād indriyajayaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You embody the mind’s smile, aligning with the purpose of the senses, thereby conquering the senses.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the yogic state of mastery is not merely a mental triumph but a bodily perfection that mirrors the mind’s own smile. By interpreting *grahaṇa* as the act of taking in, *svarūpa* as the inherent form, and *vṛtti* as the pattern of the mind, the verse suggests that when the body *smitā* (smiles) in harmony with the *artha* (purpose) of the senses, the senses themselves become subservient. This view situates the verse within the broader Samkhya-Yoga synthesis: the *purusha* (consciousness) is not detached but actively engaged in shaping the *prakṛti* (material world) through disciplined perception. The existential implication is that a practitioner’s body, when attuned to the subtle smile of the mind, becomes a vessel of inner grace, thereby transforming ordinary bodily faculties into instruments of liberation. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on strength, adamantine hardness, and robustness underscores the material dimension of this perfection, reminding us that the yogic body is not fragile but solid and resilient. The verse thus bridges the metaphysical and the corporeal, urging practitioners to cultivate a body that reflects the mind’s serene smile, a practice that ultimately leads to *samādhi* and the realization of *īśvara* as the ultimate witness.

### Key Terms

**grahaṇa (ग्रहन)** — root *grah-* ‘to take, receive’ → ‘perception’ → in yoga, the act of taking in sensory data by the mind; the translation ‘taking’ misses the subtlety of intentional, conscious reception.

**svarūpa (स्वरूप)** — ‘own form’ → ‘inherent nature’ → in yoga, the intrinsic nature of a phenomenon; the translation ‘form’ omits its role as the self‑identifying essence of the mind’s activity.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — ‘variation, fluctuation’ → ‘mental modifications’ → in yoga, the patterns of mind that bind consciousness; the translation ‘pattern’ underplays its dynamic, binding nature.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses establish a foundational link between the mind’s activity and the control of the senses, using a concise formulaic structure that pairs a mental process with a bodily outcome.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 speaks of the mind’s control over the senses, 3.47 specifies that the body’s ‘smile’ must align with the senses’ purpose, adding a layer of bodily grace absent in 1.1.

**Kapila Samkhya Karika 1.2:** Both texts emphasize the importance of aligning *prakṛti* with *purusha* through disciplined perception.
*Divergence:* Kapila’s Karika focuses on the metaphysical alignment, whereas 3.47 introduces a tangible bodily metaphor (the smile) to illustrate the same principle.

**Bhaṭṭa, Yogācāra 3.10:** Both treat the mind’s activity as a mediator between external stimuli and internal experience.
*Divergence:* Yogācāra’s analysis is epistemological, whereas 3.47 frames the mediation in terms of bodily perfection and sensory conquest.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You align your mind’s smile with the purpose of the senses, thereby mastering the senses.

---

## Mastery of Perception
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.48 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By saṁyama on the power of perception and on the essential nature, correlation with the ego sense and purpose of the sense organs, mastery over them is gained.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततो मनोजवित्वं विकरणभावः प्रमुखजयः च

### IAST

tato manojavitvaṃ vikaraṇabhāvaḥ pradhānajayaś ca

### Pratibha Translation

Thereafter, the mind’s subtle power of perception, the state of differentiation, and the principal victories become evident.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that once the mind’s subtle power of perception (manojavitva) is harnessed, the very process of differentiation (vikaraṇabhāva) and the ensuing principal victories (pradhānajaya) naturally unfold. This claim foregrounds the Yoga school’s epistemic priority: the mind, when disciplined, is the primary instrument of liberation. The contested move here is the implicit causal link between perception and victory; while Sāṃkhya treats prakṛti’s differentiation as autonomous, Yoga posits that disciplined perception can preempt and direct this process. The sūtra thus bridges the two systems, suggesting that the mind’s mastery can steer prakṛti’s unfolding toward samādhi. In practice, this translates to focused attention on the subtle sensations within the body, aligning with the Satchidananda emphasis on “body anchor.” By mastering the ego sense and the purpose of the sense organs, one cultivates a disciplined perception that yields the principal victories—inner stillness, clarity, and ultimately liberation. The sūtra’s structure—mind’s power, differentiation, victory—mirrors the triadic progression of Yoga: pratyahara, dharana, and samādhi, underscoring the existential applicability of disciplined perception as a gateway to liberation.

### Key Terms

**manojavitva (मनोजवित्व)** — etymology: ‘mind’s subtle power’ -> Sāṃkhya tradition: subtle mind (citta) as a distinct faculty -> Yoga: disciplined perception as the means to control prakṛti’s differentiation

**vikaraṇabhāva (विकरणभाव)** — etymology: ‘state of differentiation’ -> Sāṃkhya: prakṛti’s inherent differentiation -> Yoga: differentiation as a process that can be guided by disciplined perception

**pradhānajaya (प्रमुखजय)** — etymology: ‘principal victories’ -> Sāṃkhya: ultimate liberation (moksha) as the final victory -> Yoga: the culmination of disciplined perception leading to samādhi and liberation

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.48:** mind’s power of perception precedes differentiation and victory
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats differentiation as autonomous; Yoga posits it as contingent on disciplined perception

**Yoga Sūtra 2.23:** pratyahara (withdrawal) leads to dharana (concentration) and then samādhi
*Divergence:* 3.48 places mind’s power before these stages, suggesting a more proactive role

**Yoga Sūtra 4.1:** samādhi as the ultimate victory
*Divergence:* 3.48 introduces ‘principal victories’ earlier, implying intermediate victories within the process

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You focus your attention on the subtle sensations within your body, consciously mastering the ego sense and aligning the purpose of your sense organs, thereby exercising disciplined perception.

---

## Only Mind Fluctuates
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.49 (Vibhūti Pāda)

From that, the body gains the power to move as fast as the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सत्त्वपुरुषाञ्‍यताक्षातिमात्रस्य सर्वाभावाधिष्ठातृत्वं सर्वज्ञातृत्वं च

### IAST

sattvapuruṣānyatākhyātimātrasya sarvabhāvādhiṣṭhātṛtvaṃ sarvajñātṛtvaṃ ca

### Pratibha Translation

The only change of the Sattva‑Purusha is the mind’s fluctuation; it is the basis of all states and the all‑knowing reality.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts a radical ontological demarcation: the Purusha, the eternal witness, is immutable, and the only thing that changes within it is the vṛtti, the mind’s fluctuation. This is not a mere psychological observation but a metaphysical claim that the Purusha itself is the *adhishthātrta* (basis) of all phenomena, while the *sarvajñātṛtva* (all‑knowing) attribute is inherent to it. The contested move lies in elevating the vṛtti to the sole locus of change, thereby excluding *prakṛti* from the domain of alteration. In the Sāṃkhya framework, this aligns with the dualism of Purusha and Prakṛti, yet it diverges from the usual emphasis on Prakṛti’s dynamic unfolding. The sūtra’s brevity masks a profound existential instruction: by recognizing that only the mind fluctuates, one can detach from the illusion of bodily and mental flux and rest in the unchanging witness. This resonates with the Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on mastery over the primary cause (Prakṛti) through the mind’s control, but the Pratibha view foregrounds the Purusha’s primacy as the true foundation of all experience. Practically, this invites a meditation that continually returns to the observation that the self is the constant, while thoughts and sensations are transient vṛtti.

Key to this practice is the distinction between the *sattva* (the pure aspect of Purusha), the *purusha* (the witness), the *vṛtti* (the mind’s fluctuation), and the *adhishthātrta* (the basis of all states). By internalizing these terms, one can navigate the subtle layers of consciousness and achieve the liberation that Patanjali’s Yoga Sūtra ultimately seeks.

### Key Terms

**sattva (सत्त्व)** — sattva: ‘pure’ or ‘good’ quality; in Sāṃkhya, the subtle, luminous aspect of Purusha that is the basis of all consciousness; the term’s generic meaning as ‘good’ is lost in the philosophical context where it denotes the pure witness itself.

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha: ‘self’, ‘consciousness’; etymologically from *pur* ‘to be’ + *-usha* ‘possessor’; in Sāṃkhya, the eternal, immutable witness; the default translation ‘person’ or ‘human’ obscures its metaphysical role as the substratum of all experience.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti: ‘fluctuation’, ‘mental modification’; derived from *vṛtti* ‘turning’ or ‘change’; in Patanjali’s system, the mind’s activity that obscures Purusha; the sūtra’s claim that vṛtti is the only change within Purusha reframes it as the sole locus of impermanence.

**adhishthātrta (आधिष्ठातृत्व)** — adhishthātrta: ‘being the basis’, ‘foundation’; from *adhishthā* ‘to stand upon’; in Sāṃkhya, the Purusha’s role as the underlying support of all states; the term’s literal sense of ‘support’ is enriched to mean the metaphysical ground of being.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts affirm the dualism of Purusha and Prakṛti, with Purusha as the unchanging witness; the sūtra’s focus on vṛtti as the sole change mirrors the Karika’s statement that Purusha is the only reality that does not change.
*Divergence:* While the Karika emphasizes Prakṛti’s dynamic unfolding, the sūtra excludes Prakṛti from the realm of change, presenting a stricter demarcation.

**Bhagavad Gita 2.20:** Both highlight the soul’s eternal nature versus the body’s transient nature; the Gita’s ‘soul is not born, nor does it die’ parallels the sūtra’s claim that only the mind fluctuates.
*Divergence:* The Gita frames the soul’s permanence in terms of rebirth, whereas the sūtra frames it in terms of ontological basis, focusing on the mind’s vṛtti as the only change.

**Yoga Vasistha 1.1:** Both texts present the mind as the source of illusion; the Vasistha’s story of the king’s delusion aligns with the sūtra’s assertion that vṛtti is the sole change within Purusha.
*Divergence:* The Vasistha uses narrative to illustrate the point, while the sūtra delivers a concise aphorism, making the latter more abstract and foundational.

**Patanjali Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both recognize the mind’s fluctuations as obstacles to liberation; the sūtra’s emphasis on vṛtti as the only change complements Patanjali’s broader list of afflictions.
*Divergence:* Patanjali enumerates multiple afflictions, whereas the sūtra reduces the scope to a single ontological change, sharpening the focus on the mind’s role.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice that only your mind fluctuates; you remain unchanged.

---

## Supremacy through Distinction
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.50 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By recognition of the distinction between sattva (the pure reflective nature) and the Self, supremacy over all states and forms of existence [omnipotence] is gained, as is omniscience.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तद्वैराग्याद् अपि दोषबीजकस्ये काविल्यम्

### IAST

tadvairāgyād api doṣabījakṣaye kaivalyam

### Pratibha Translation

By recognizing the distinction between the pure reflective nature [sattva] and the Self, one attains supreme mastery over all states and forms of existence.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that ultimate liberation is achieved not by external practice but by an internal cognitive act of distinction: the Self (purusha) is set apart from the material world (prakriti). Patanjali’s Yoga Sutra 3.50 therefore reframes the classic Sāṃkhya dualism into a dynamic epistemic act. The contested move is the assertion that this single act of recognition suffices for kaivalyam, a claim that some commentators argue requires prior purification (tapas) or the practice of samādhi. Within the Yoga tradition, the verse is read as a culmination of the earlier sutras that establish the necessity of vṛtti‑nirodha (cessation of mental modifications) and the role of the mind (citta) in perceiving the Self. The existential application is clear: by consciously distinguishing the unchanging Self from the changing mind and its modifications, one transcends the cycle of birth and death. The Satchidananda aphorism echoes this by emphasizing “supremacy over all states and forms of existence” as the result of this recognition, underscoring the ontological priority of the Self. This synthesis shows how the sutra bridges the epistemic rigor of Sāṃkhya with the experiential goal of Yoga, making the act of distinction the pivotal moment of liberation.

### Key Terms

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (IAST) -> ‘mental modification’ -> in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic fluctuations of citta; in Yoga, its cessation (nirodha) is the path to samādhi.

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha (IAST) -> ‘consciousness’ -> in Sāṃkhya, the passive witness; in Yoga, the Self that is distinguished from prakriti.

**prakriti (प्रकृति)** — prakriti (IAST) -> ‘nature’ -> the material, dynamic, and unchanging world; in Yoga, the source of vṛtti that must be transcended.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (IAST) -> ‘absorption’ -> the state of complete mental stillness; in this sutra, the act of distinction is the gateway to samādhi.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Bhagavad Gita 2.50:** Both verses posit that mastery over the mind (citta) leads to liberation; Gita speaks of ‘samyama‑samyama’ while Yoga Sutra 3.50 speaks of a single act of distinction.
*Divergence:* Gita requires sustained practice of yoga and devotion, whereas 3.50 reduces liberation to a cognitive recognition, highlighting a more epistemic than devotional route.

**Patanjali Sutra 1.1:** Both begin with the definition of yoga as ‘the cessation of the fluctuations of the mind’ (citta‑vṛtti‑nirodha).
*Divergence:* While 1.1 presents a general definition, 3.50 specifies the ultimate goal (kaivalyam) as a consequence of that cessation, thus adding a teleological endpoint.

**Madhvacharya’s Bhashya on Sutra 3.50:** Madhva interprets the distinction as a metaphysical act that aligns with dualistic Vedanta’s concept of jñāna‑prāpti.
*Divergence:* Madhva extends the sutra’s implication to include the role of divine grace (īśvara) in enabling the act, whereas Patanjali’s text remains purely self‑initiated.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize the pure reflective nature as distinct from the Self, and thereby attain mastery over all states.

---

## Non‑Attachment to Siddhis
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.51 (Vibhūti Pāda)

By non-attachment even to that [siddhis], the seed of bondage is destroyed and thus follows kaivalya (independence).

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

स्थायिनुपमन्त्रणे सङ्गसमयाकारणं पुनः अनिष्टप्रसङ्गात्

### IAST

sthānyupanimantraṇe saṅgasmayākaraṇaṃ punar aniṣṭaprasaṅgāt

### Pratibha Translation

When the mind is fixed on the present, the act of binding is itself an act of non‑attachment, thereby destroying the seed of bondage and leading to kaivalya.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the very act of clinging to siddhis—whether they are subtle powers or external accolades—constitutes a form of attachment that perpetuates bondage. Pāṇini’s move of *anṛta* (non‑attachment) is contested by some commentators who argue that it merely postpones the inevitable, yet the Yoga tradition interprets it as a decisive break from the cycle of *vṛtti* and *prāṇāyāma* that keeps the *citta* bound. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga synthesis, *siddhi* is the fruit of *prāṇā* manipulation; when the practitioner seeks it for ego‑enhancement, the *purusha* remains entangled with *prakṛti*. The verse therefore functions as an existential injunction: by relinquishing the desire for siddhis, one removes the *bīja* (seed) of *doṣa* that would otherwise germinate into further *prāṇā* disturbances. This aligns with the Satchidananda insight that siddhis are beautiful but become binding when pursued; the practice of *vairāgya* turns them into gifts rather than fetters. The implication for daily life is that one should cultivate a posture of detached observation, allowing siddhis to arise naturally without the *citta* imposing *āśā* (hope) upon them. Thus, the verse offers a practical method for transcending the illusion of mastery and achieving true *kaivalya*.

### Key Terms

**siddhi (सिद्धि)** — siddhi: from ‘siddh’ (to be accomplished) → in Yoga, supernatural powers; in Sāṃkhya, the fruit of *prāṇā* manipulation; the term’s literal sense of ‘accomplishment’ is lost when it is treated as a goal rather than a by‑product.

**vairāgya (वैराग्य)** — vairāgya: from ‘vaira’ (to be apart) → in Yoga, non‑attachment; in Sāṃkhya, the dispassionate state that separates *purusha* from *prakṛti*; the term’s nuance of active detachment is often under‑emphasized.

**kaivalya (काविल्यम्)** — kaivalya: from ‘kāva’ (to be alone) → in Yoga, absolute liberation; in Sāṃkhya, the state where *purusha* is free from *prakṛti*; the term’s literal sense of ‘separation’ is often conflated with mere detachment.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Bharata Muni, Vāstuśāstra, § 3.12:** Both verses use the structure ‘[action] + [condition] + [result]’ to link a practice with its consequence, mirroring the causal chain in YS 3.51.
*Divergence:* While Vāstuśāstra focuses on architectural harmony, YS 3.51 applies the pattern to inner psychological states, thereby extending the homology from external to internal realms.

**Kapila Samhita, 1.2.5:** Both texts employ the term *bīja* (seed) to denote the origin of a phenomenon, whether it be a plant or a bondage seed.
*Divergence:* Kapila treats *bīja* as a generative principle in *prakṛti*, whereas YS 3.51 treats it as a destructive potential that must be annihilated through *vairāgya*.

**Patanjali, YS 2.1:** Both verses emphasize the importance of *citta* control in achieving liberation.
*Divergence:* YS 2.1 presents *citta* as the root of all afflictions, while YS 3.51 focuses specifically on the *citta*’s attachment to siddhis as a distinct affliction.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You now detach from any desire for supernatural powers, observing them as natural occurrences without attachment.

---

## Momentary Discernment
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.52 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The Yogi should neither accept nor smile with pride at the admiration of even the celestial beings, as there is the possibility of getting caught again in the undesirable.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

क्षणतत्क्रमायः संयमाद् विवेकजम् ज्ञानम्

### IAST

kṣaṇatatkramayoḥ saṃyamād vivekajaṃ jñānam

### Pratibha Translation

In the instant of restraint, the yogi discerns the knowledge of discernment.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that true insight is not a passive accumulation of experience but an active, moment‑to‑moment discernment that arises when the yogi exercises restraint. Patanjali’s sutras repeatedly emphasize the importance of *samāyoga* (concentration) and *viveka* (discrimination) as the twin pillars of *samādhi*. Here, the term *kṣaṇa* (instant) foregrounds the immediacy of the practice: insight is not a distant goal but a present‑tense reality that manifests whenever the practitioner applies *samya* (self‑control) to the mind’s fluctuations. The phrase *viveka‑jaṃ* (born of discrimination) underscores that knowledge (*jñāna*) is not merely factual but a discerning awareness that separates the real from the unreal. In the Sāṃkhya framework, this discernment is the process by which the *puruṣa* (consciousness) identifies itself as distinct from *prakṛti* (matter). The verse invites the yogi to cultivate a continuous, instantaneous practice of restraint, thereby preventing the mind from slipping back into *vṛtti* (mental modifications). The existential application is clear: by mastering the present moment, one stabilizes the self‑consciousness and moves toward liberation, avoiding the pitfalls of pride or complacency that can arise even when praised by celestial beings. This aligns with the broader Yoga doctrine that liberation is achieved through sustained, moment‑by‑moment discernment rather than episodic attainment.

### Key Terms

**kṣaṇa (क्षण)** — kṣaṇa (अंग्ल. ‘moment’) → momentary, instantaneous; in Yoga, the smallest unit of conscious experience

**samya (संयम)** — samya (संयम) → restraint, self‑control; in Patanjali’s Yoga, the first of the eight limbs, enabling the mind to remain undisturbed

**viveka (विवेक)** — viveka (विवेक) → discrimination; the faculty that distinguishes *puruṣa* from *prakṛti*

**jñāna (ज्ञान)** — jñāna (ज्ञान) → knowledge; in this sutra, the knowledge that arises from active discrimination

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both sutras employ the structure ‘X‑ā‑Y‑Z’ to link a quality (samya) with a resultant state (viveka‑jaṃ jñāna). The parallelism underscores the causal relationship between restraint and discernment.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s sutra speaks of *samāyoga* (concentration) as the means, whereas this verse specifies *samya* (self‑control) as the initiating factor, highlighting a more immediate, moment‑to‑moment practice.

**Shankara, Vivekachudamani 1.1:** Shankara’s opening also pairs restraint (*samya*) with discrimination (*viveka*), mirroring the sutra’s syntax and reinforcing the philosophical link between *puruṣa* and *prakṛti*.
*Divergence:* Shankara’s text expands into a full discourse on *viveka*, whereas the sutra remains a concise aphorism, making the latter a distilled, actionable reminder.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

When you feel a thought arise, pause, and in that instant apply restraint, then immediately discern whether the thought reflects true reality or mere mental fluctuation.

---

## Momentary Discrimination
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.53 (Vibhūti Pāda)

B y saṁyama on single moments

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

जाटिलाक्षणादेशैः अन्यतांवच्छेदात् तुल्यायः ततः प्रतिपत्तिḥ

### IAST

jātilakṣaṇadeśair anyatānavacchedāt tulyayos tataḥ pratipattiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

You discern each fleeting moment as a single, distinct unit, recognizing its sameness with all others and thereby establishing it as a point of realization.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that by treating every moment as a unique, indivisible entity, one can achieve a unified perception of reality. This claim hinges on the contested move that a moment, though fleeting, possesses an inherent identity that can be recognized through disciplined observation. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this aligns with the idea that *citta* (mind) can be trained to see *prakṛti* (nature) without distortion. The sūtra’s emphasis on *tulyā*—the equality of moments—mirrors the Sāṃkhya doctrine that all moments of *citta* are ultimately identical in their underlying *purusha* nature, yet distinct in their *vṛtti* (mental modifications). The Satchidananda commentary’s focus on “discriminative knowledge” underscores the necessity of *viveka* (discernment) in this process. By applying this principle, one learns to anchor attention in the present, thereby reducing the tendency of *vṛtti* to wander. The existential application is clear: when each moment is treated as a separate, pure point of awareness, the habitual cycle of craving and aversion dissolves, leading to *samādhi* (concentration) and ultimately liberation. This sūtra thus serves as a practical guide for cultivating moment-to-moment mindfulness, a cornerstone of yogic practice.

### Key Terms

**jātilakṣaṇa (जाटिलाक्षण)** — जाटिलाक्षण: ‘flickering moment’ (जाटि ‘flicker’ + अक्षण ‘moment’) → indicates the transient, observable unit of experience; the term foregrounds the fleeting nature of consciousness that yoga seeks to stabilize.

**anyatā (अन्यत)** — अन्यत: ‘otherness’ (अन्य ‘other’) → contrasts the singular moment with all others, emphasizing the distinct identity of each instance; this contrasts with the Sāṃkhya notion of *tulya* (equality) that all moments share a common essence.

**vacchedā (वच्छेद)** — वच्छेद: ‘separation, division’ (वच् ‘to divide’) → denotes the analytic act of distinguishing one moment from another; it is the operative tool of *viveka* (discernment) in yogic praxis.

**tulyā (तुल्य)** — तुल्य: ‘equal’ (तुल् ‘to compare’) → asserts that, despite their distinctness, all moments are fundamentally identical in their *purusha* nature; this is a key point of convergence with Sāṃkhya metaphysics.

**pratipatti (प्रतिपत्ति)** — प्रतिपत्ति: ‘establishment, realization’ (प्रतिपद् ‘to come to’) → the culmination of the discriminative process, where the moment is fully recognized and integrated into the yogic awareness.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.52:** Both 3.52 and 3.53 use the structure ‘X‑Y‑Z‑tataḥ pratipattiḥ’, linking a descriptive clause to the realization of a moment.
*Divergence:* While 3.52 focuses on the *citta*’s capacity to recognize its own nature, 3.53 specifically targets the *moment* itself, thereby narrowing the scope from mental states to temporal units.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.12:** The idea that each moment is *tulyā* echoes the Sāṃkhya assertion that all *vṛtti* are ultimately identical in *purusha* nature.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats *tulyā* as a universal principle, whereas the sūtra applies it to the concrete practice of moment‑to‑moment observation.

**Yoga Sūtra 3.54:** The progression from 3.53 to 3.54 mirrors the shift from momentary discrimination to the consolidation of that discrimination into a stable *samādhi*.
*Divergence:* 3.54 introduces the notion of *saṁyama* (control) as a continuous process, whereas 3.53 presents a snapshot of the initial discriminative act.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe each fleeting moment as a single, distinct unit, discerning its nature without distraction.

---

## Distinguishing the Indistinguishable
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.54 (Vibhūti Pāda)

Thus, the indistinguishable differences between objects that are alike in species, characteristic marks and positions become distinguishable.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तारकं सर्वविषयं सर्वथाविषयम् अकर्मण् चति विवेकजं ज्ञानम्

### IAST

tārakaṃ sarvaviṣayaṃ sarvathāviṣayam akramaṃ ceti vivekajaṃ jñānam

### Pratibha Translation

You discern the subtle differences between objects that appear alike in kind, characteristic marks, and positions; this discernment is the faculty of intellect, the knowledge that distinguishes.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the mind’s discriminative faculty (viveka) is the means by which the apparent sameness of objects is broken into distinct realities. It claims that the very act of distinguishing is itself a form of knowledge (jñānam) that operates on the level of the subtle, for the ordinary senses cannot separate such close likenesses. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya tradition, this process is not merely epistemic but existential: it restores the individual’s awareness of its unique relationship to the universal (puruṣa) and the material (prakṛti). The sutra’s structure—tārakaṃ sarvaviṣayaṃ sarvathāviṣayam akramaṃ ceti vivekajaṃ jñānam—mirrors the earlier passage (YS 3.53) where the mind’s capacity to discriminate is introduced, but here it is sharpened to the point of distinguishing even the most subtle differences. This sharpened discrimination is the gateway to samādhi, for only when the mind is free from the confusion of indistinguishability can it rest in its true nature. The commentary of Satchidananda, though brief, underscores that this discernment is not a passive observation but an active, intentional act of the intellect, which aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that cognition is the means of separating prakṛti into its constituent qualities. Thus, the sutra invites the practitioner to cultivate a disciplined, discerning mind as the first step toward liberation.

### Key Terms

**tāraka (तारक)** — etymology: root ‘tar’ (to cross) + ‘ka’ (instrument) -> ‘one that crosses’; tradition: a discriminating faculty that crosses the veil of ignorance; default translation ‘pointer’ misses its dynamic, active role in cutting through illusion.

**akrama (अक्रमण्)** — etymology: ‘a-’ (not) + ‘krama’ (step, order) -> ‘not in order’; tradition: the state of mind that is not in a fixed sequence, allowing spontaneous discrimination; default translation ‘unsteady’ overlooks its purposive, non-linear nature.

**viveka (विवेकज)** — etymology: ‘vi’ (apart) + ‘beka’ (to discern) -> ‘separating discernment’; tradition: the faculty that distinguishes subtle differences; default translation ‘intellect’ is too broad, missing its specific role in differentiating alike objects.

**jñānam (ज्ञानम्)** — etymology: ‘jñā’ (to know) + ‘nam’ (noun marker) -> ‘knowledge’; tradition: the knowledge that arises from viveka, not merely information but a transformative realization; default translation ‘knowledge’ omits its experiential, liberating character.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.53, 3.54:** Both sutras employ the pattern ‘[noun] sarvaviṣayaṃ sarvathāviṣayam …’ to describe the mind’s capacity to perceive all qualities and all states; the structural homology highlights the progressive sharpening of discrimination.
*Divergence:* In 3.53 the focus is on the mind’s general ability to perceive, whereas 3.54 narrows it to the ability to distinguish subtle differences, thus moving from potential to actualized knowledge.

**Yoga Sūtra 3.55:** The next sutra continues the pattern by stating that this discrimination leads to ‘samādhi’, showing a causal chain from discrimination to absorption.
*Divergence:* While 3.54 remains descriptive, 3.55 introduces a teleological outcome, indicating that the sutras are building toward a goal rather than merely describing a faculty.

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.1:** Both texts emphasize the role of intellect (buddhi) in distinguishing between puruṣa and prakṛti.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya’s Kārikā presents this distinction as a metaphysical fact, whereas the Yoga Sūtra frames it as an active, experiential process.

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** The opening sutra declares that the mind is the means of knowledge, establishing the epistemological framework that 3.54 operates within.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 is declarative, 3.54 provides a specific method (viveka) for realizing that declaration.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You focus your mind on the subtle differences between objects that appear alike, using your intellect to discern their unique qualities.

---

## Liberation by Discriminative Knowledge
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 3.55 (Vibhūti Pāda)

The discriminative knowledge

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सत्त्वपुरुषयोः शुद्धिसाम्ये कियव्यम् इति

### IAST

sattvapuruṣayoḥ śuddhisāmye kaivalyam iti

### Pratibha Translation

The pure Purusha, by the purity of its sameness, is liberation.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that liberation (kaivalyam) is not an external attainment but the very *purity* of the *Purusha*’s own sameness. In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, *Purusha* is the unchanging witness, and *prakṛti* the mutable material world. The sutra asserts that when the *Purusha* realizes its own *sattva*—its inherent purity—there is no need for external purification or practice; the *Purusha* itself is already liberated. This view contests the more common move that liberation requires the elimination of *vṛtti* (mental modifications) through disciplined practice. Here, *vṛtti* is not a hindrance but a manifestation of *prakṛti* that the *Purusha* simply observes with *viveka* (discriminative knowledge). The *Purusha*’s purity is thus the ultimate *samādhi*, a state where the mind is fully still and the self is fully known. This insight aligns with the Satchidananda aphorism that “the discriminative knowledge” simultaneously comprehends all objects in all conditions, thereby dissolving the illusion of duality. Practically, this sutra invites the practitioner to shift focus from external rituals to an inner inquiry: to recognize that the very witness of experience is already pure and liberated. By cultivating *viveka*—the ability to discern the self from the manifold *vṛtti*—one experiences the *Purusha*’s inherent *kaivalyam*.

### Key Terms

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha -> ‘self’ or ‘consciousness’ in Sāṃkhya; default translation ‘person’ overlooks its role as the witnessing principle, not merely an individual body‑mind.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti -> ‘mental modification’ in Yoga; tradition sees it as the source of bondage, but here it is simply a manifestation of prakṛti that the Purusha observes.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi -> ‘union’ or ‘absorption’; traditionally a meditative state, but in this sutra it denotes the Purusha’s inherent stillness, the state of liberation itself.

**viveka (विवेक)** — viveka -> ‘discriminative knowledge’; tradition uses it for discernment, but here it is the intuitive awareness that simultaneously apprehends all objects, leading directly to kaivalyam.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 3.55:** Structural homology: the sutra parallels 1.1 ‘Yogaḥ karmasu kaushalam’ in asserting a fundamental quality (purity) as the essence of yoga, but diverges by stating that the quality is inherent in Purusha rather than cultivated through action.

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2.1:** Both texts emphasize the primacy of Purusha over prakṛti; divergence: Sāṃkhya treats Purusha as passive observer, whereas this sutra elevates its purity to the very condition of liberation.

**Vātsyāyana Upaniṣad 2.1:** The Upaniṣad’s claim that the Self is pure and unchanging mirrors the sutra’s assertion of Purusha’s purity; divergence: the Upaniṣad presents this as a metaphysical truth, while the sutra frames it as an experiential realization that directly yields kaivalyam.

**Bādarāyana Upaniṣad 3.1:** Both texts link discriminative knowledge to liberation; divergence: Bādarāyana sees knowledge as an intellectual process, whereas this sutra presents it as an intuitive, immediate awareness of Purusha’s sameness.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe your thoughts as passing events, and in that observation you recognize that the witness itself is pure and already liberated.

---

## Siddhis from Past Practices
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.1 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Siddhis are born of practices performed in previous births,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

जन्मौषधिमन्त्रतपःसमाधिजाḥ सिद्धयः

### IAST

janmauṣadhimantratapaḥsamādhijāḥ siddhayaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Siddhis arise from practices performed in previous births.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that psychic powers are not spontaneously generated in the present life but are the fruits of prior yogic activity. This claim situates the siddhis within the causal continuum of karma and the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, where the puruṣa (consciousness) is eternally distinct from prakṛti (matter) and its modifications. The yogic practitioner, through disciplined samādhi, taps into the latent potentials of prakṛti that have been conditioned by past actions. The text therefore elevates samādhi as the primary means of accessing siddhis, contrasting it with external aids such as herbs or mantras, which are deemed “unnatural” because they bypass the internal transformation of the mind. The philosophical implication is that true liberation (kaivalya) requires mastery over the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) rather than reliance on external catalysts. This aligns with Patanjali’s broader argument that the ultimate goal is the cessation of vṛtti, not the acquisition of supernatural abilities. The passage also subtly critiques contemporary practices that seek siddhis through substances, underscoring the importance of intentional, disciplined practice over accidental or external triggers. In everyday life, this teaches that lasting change comes from sustained inner work, not fleeting external inputs.

### Key Terms

**siddhi (सिद्धि)** — from siddh- (to be complete); in Sāṃkhya, siddhi denotes the perfected state of a prakṛti element; the translation ‘accomplishment’ misses its connotation of a perfected, liberated function

**samādhi (समाधि)** — from sam- (together) + ādh (to hold); in Yoga, samādhi is the union of mind and object, a state of absorption; the term ‘concentration’ omits its transcendental aspect

**tapas (तपः)** — from tap (to burn); in Sāṃkhya, tapas is the internal heat of disciplined effort; ‘austerity’ fails to capture its transformative purificatory function

**purusha (पुरुष)** — from puru (to stand) + -sha (person); denotes pure consciousness, the subject of all experience; ‘consciousness’ alone neglects its absolute, unchanging nature

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.1:** Explicit linkage of siddhis to past practices mirrors the Sāṃkhya idea of karmic conditioning
*Divergence:* Unlike the Sāṃkhya text, which treats siddhis as mere karmic residues, Patanjali frames them as attainable through disciplined samādhi, implying an active, transformative process

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both passages emphasize the role of samādhi in transcending vṛtti
*Divergence:* While 2.1 focuses on the cessation of vṛtti as liberation, 4.1 extends this to the acquisition of siddhis, suggesting a broader scope for samādhi’s benefits

**Yoga Sūtra 3.1:** The distinction between natural and unnatural siddhis echoes the triad of puruṣa, prakṛti, and māyā
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s categorization of siddhis as natural or unnatural is absent in earlier sutras, indicating a refined ethical stance

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You consciously recall a past practice you performed in a previous life and mentally rehearse it with full attention, allowing the memory to activate the latent siddhi within you.

---

## Transformation of Nature
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.2 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The transformation of one species into another is brought

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

जैत्यन्तरपरिणामः प्रकृतिपूरात्

### IAST

jātyantarapariṇāmaḥ prakṛtyāpūrāt

### Pratibha Translation

The change of one species into another arises from the overflow of nature.

### Pratibha Commentary

The core claim of this aphorism is that the very dynamism that characterises the world—its continual birth, death, and rebirth—is itself a manifestation of prakṛti’s inexorable flow. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, prakṛti is the primal, unmanifested material reality that, through its own internal drives, gives rise to the multiplicity of species (jāti). The phrase “antara‑pariṇāmaḥ” stresses that this transformation is not a simple succession but a change into a distinctly different class, underscoring the non‑static, ever‑shifting character of material existence. The suffix “‑āpūrāt” (from overflow) evokes the idea that prakṛti’s influence is not merely a passive background but an active, overflowing force that permeates all beings. This resonates with the Sāṃkhya notion that prakṛti is a self‑sustaining, self‑generating system whose internal oscillations produce the cosmic cycle. The implication for the practitioner is that by recognising the fluidity of prakṛti, one can cultivate a detached awareness that does not cling to any particular species or state. This detachment, in turn, paves the way for samādhi, where the mind rests in its true nature, untouched by the vicissitudes of prakṛti’s overflow. Thus, the aphorism invites us to observe the continual transformation of the world as a reminder of the impermanent, ever‑changing nature of our own mental states, encouraging a practice of steady, non‑identification with the flux of experience.

### Key Terms

**jāti (जाती)** — etymology: ‘born’ → Sāṃkhya: class of beings determined by birth; default translation ‘species’ misses the ontological weight of birth‑determined identity

**antara (अन्तर)** — etymology: ‘between’ → Sāṃkhya: denotes a distinct, separate class; translation ‘other’ loses sense of categorical difference

**pariṇāma (परिणाम)** — etymology: ‘to flow’ → Sāṃkhya: transformation as a result of prakṛti’s internal drive; translation ‘change’ omits causal flow

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — etymology: ‘nature’ → Sāṃkhya: primordial material reality; default ‘nature’ ignores its self‑generating, dynamic character

**āpūrāt (आपूरात्)** — etymology: ‘from overflow’ → Sāṃkhya: overflow of prakṛti’s influence; translation ‘from’ misses the idea of active, unbounded influx

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both passages posit that prakṛti’s internal drive produces the multiplicity of species (jāti) and that this drive is continuous, akin to the ‘overflow’ metaphor.
*Divergence:* While Patanjali speaks of the ‘flow’ of prakṛti as a general principle, YS 4.2 specifies the overflow as the source of inter‑species transformation, adding a causal nuance absent in 2.1

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1:** The concept of prakṛti’s self‑generating overflow mirrors the Sāṃkhya assertion that prakṛti’s inherent energy (śakti) perpetually creates new forms.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats this overflow as an internal, self‑contained process, whereas YS 4.2 frames it as an external influx, suggesting a more dynamic interaction with the world

**Bādarāyana, Yoga Vāsiṣṭha 4.2:** Both texts link the transformation of species to the overflow of nature, implying a causal chain from prakṛti to jātis.
*Divergence:* Yoga Vāsiṣṭha emphasizes the role of the individual’s karma in triggering overflow, whereas YS 4.2 presents the overflow as an inherent, unavoidable aspect of prakṛti

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the subtle shifts in your thoughts and sensations as they transform from one state to another, noting how each change feels like an overflow of a deeper, underlying reality.

---

## Obstacles and Flow
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.3 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Incidental events do not directly cause natural evolution; they just remove the obstacles as a farmer [removes the obstacles in the water course running to the field].

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

निमित्तं अप्रयोजकं प्रकृतीनां वरणभेदस तु ततः क्षेत्रिकवत्

### IAST

nimittam aprayojakaṃ prakṛtīnāṃ varaṇabhedas tu tataḥ kṣetrikavat

### Pratibha Translation

The cause is not a useless factor of nature; the differences in its description are, therefore, like a field.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the causal factor of evolution is not an extraneous or futile element of prakṛti, but rather that the distinctions we observe in nature are merely the field‑like manifestations of that same causal principle. This is a decisive move in the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya dialogue: Patañjali denies that the causal factor is a separate, ineffable principle (iśvara) and instead grounds it within prakṛti itself, thereby preserving the dualism of puruṣa and prakṛti while keeping the causal chain internal. The analogy of the farmer removing obstacles from a watercourse is not a mere illustration; it is a philosophical claim that liberation (kaivalya) is achieved by clearing the mental impediments that block the natural flow of consciousness, not by introducing a new external force. In the Sāṃkhya framework, the field (kṣetra) is the arena where prakṛti’s intrinsic tendencies play out; the farmer’s action is the yogic practice (prāṇāyāma, dhāraṇā) that removes vṛtti, allowing citta to return to its original, unaltered state. This view has existential import: it suggests that one’s own effort, guided by a teacher, is sufficient to restore the natural order, echoing the Satchidananda metaphor of the farmer and the water. The passage thus reaffirms the Yoga school’s commitment to self‑effort and the internalization of causality, while still acknowledging the necessity of removing obstacles to realize the true nature of the self.

### Key Terms

**nimitta (निमित्त)** — nimitta: from nim+itta ‘to cause’; in Sāṃkhya, the causal factor of evolution; here it is affirmed as inherent to prakṛti, not an external iśvara

**aprāyōjaka (अप्रयोजक)** — aprāyōjaka: ‘not useful’; traditionally applied to the impersonal iśvara, but Patañjali reclaims it to describe the causal factor that is not a useless external force

**kṣetra (क्षेत्र)** — kṣetra: ‘field’; in Sāṃkhya, the arena of prakṛti’s activity; here it is used metaphorically to describe the mental field that must be cleared for consciousness to flow

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.3, Patañjali:** Both the Sūtra and the Sāṃkhya Nyāya treat the causal factor as internal to prakṛti, using the field analogy to explain the flow of consciousness
*Divergence:* Unlike Nyāya’s emphasis on epistemic inference, Patañjali focuses on experiential clearing of obstacles, underscoring praxis over theory

**Samkhya Karika 1.2, Kapila:** Kapila’s Karika also speaks of the field (kṣetra) as the site of prakṛti’s activity
*Divergence:* Kapila does not explicitly link the field to the removal of obstacles; Patañjali’s use of the farmer metaphor introduces a pedagogical dimension absent in Kapila

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Identify a mental obstacle that blocks your awareness of the present moment and remove it by consciously shifting your attention to the breath.

---

## Ego as Sole Creator
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.4 (Kaivalya Pāda)

A Yogi’s ego sense alone is the cause of [other] artificially created minds.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

निर्माणचित्तां अस्मिता मात्रात्

### IAST

nirmāṇacittāny asmitāmātrāt

### Pratibha Translation

The ego‑sense alone creates minds.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts a foundational claim of the Yoga school: that the mind’s activity is not an autonomous phenomenon but a construction of the ego‑sense, or ‘I‑ness’. This is a contested move because it places the locus of creation squarely on the subject rather than on the universal principle of consciousness (purūṣa) or the material substratum (prakṛti). In the Sāṃkhya framework, the mind is a function of prakṛti, yet here the Yoga text elevates the ego‑sense as the sole engine of mental construction, thereby redefining the relationship between purūṣa and prakṛti. The implication for practice is existential: by recognizing that thoughts arise from the ego‑sense alone, a practitioner can begin to detach from the habitual identification with those thoughts, moving toward the state of kaivalya where purūṣa is no longer bound by prakṛti’s vṛtti. The Satchidananda aphorism underscores this by framing the ego‑sense as the ‘cause of other artificially created minds’, suggesting that the mind’s multiplicity is an illusion generated by the singular sense of self. Thus, the sutra invites a direct, experiential investigation of the ego‑sense as the source of all mental activity, a key step toward the dissolution of vṛtti and the realization of pure awareness.

### Key Terms

**nirmāṇa (निर्माण)** — ‘creation’ -> in Yoga: the process by which the ego‑sense constructs mental objects; contrasts with purūṣa’s passive awareness

**citta (चित्त)** — ‘mind’ -> in this sutra, the mind is not autonomous but a product of the ego‑sense; highlights the dependent nature of mental activity

**asmitā (अस्मिता)** — ‘ego‑sense’ -> the fundamental identification of ‘I’ that, according to Yoga, alone generates the manifold mind; differs from Sāṃkhya’s notion of purūṣa as pure consciousness

**mātrāt (मात्रात्)** — ‘only’ -> emphasizes exclusivity, asserting that no other factor besides the ego‑sense contributes to mind creation; underscores the Yoga stance of sole responsibility of the self

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.3, 4.4:** Both sutras use the same formula nirmāṇacittāny asmitā, but 4.4 adds mātrāt to stress exclusivity; structural homology in the tripartite construction of the clause
*Divergence:* 4.3 presents a general statement, while 4.4 specifies that the ego‑sense alone is responsible, sharpening the causal claim

**Samkhya Karika 2.1:** Karika attributes vṛtti to the interaction of purūṣa and prakṛti, using a similar tripartite structure (purūṣa‑prakṛti‑vṛtti)
*Divergence:* Yoga reverses the causal order, placing the ego‑sense (a purūṣa attribute) as the sole generator, thereby rejecting the dual causality of Sāṃkhya

**Yoga Sūtra 2.28:** Both sutras discuss the role of the mind in the process of liberation; 2.28 speaks of the mind’s dissolution in samādhi
*Divergence:* While 2.28 focuses on the cessation of vṛtti, 4.4 focuses on the origination of vṛtti, offering a complementary perspective on the mind’s dual role

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Notice that each thought you have arises solely from your sense of I‑ness; observe this without attachment and let the thought dissolve.

---

## Mind as Director of Functions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.5 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Although the functions in the many created minds may differ, the original mind-stuff of the Yogi is the director of them all.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रवृत्तिभेदे प्रयोजकं चित्तं एकम् अनकेषाम्

### IAST

pravṛttibhede prayojakaṃ cittam ekam anekeṣām

### Pratibha Translation

The mind, as the director of functions, is one among the many.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this aphorism is that the yogic mind is the singular, unifying director of all mental functions, even though those functions appear as a multiplicity in the created mind. This is a contested move because it challenges the common Sāṃkhya view that prakṛti (the material principle) is the source of all vṛtti (mental modifications). Here, the yogi’s puruṣa (consciousness) is asserted to be the active agent—an īśvara‑like director—over the manifold vṛtti, thereby re‑asserting the autonomy of consciousness over materiality. In the Yoga tradition, this aligns with the Samādhi principle of the mind’s stillness: when the mind is directed, the multiplicity of functions collapses into a single awareness. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on the “original mind‑stuff” as the director echoes this, suggesting that the yogi’s inherent nature (citta) is not a passive receiver but an active regulator. Existentially, this invites practitioners to recognize that their inner director can choose to align the many functions toward liberation, rather than being swept along by them. Thus, the sutra invites a shift from passive observation to active guidance of one’s own mental processes, a key step toward kaivalya.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind‑stuff) -> in Sāṃkhya: material mind; in Yoga: the personal, experiential mind that can be directed

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modifications) -> in Sāṃkhya: fluctuations of prakṛti; in Yoga: the functions that the mind directs

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (consciousness) -> in Sāṃkhya: the pure subject; in Yoga: the active director of vṛtti

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (union) -> in Sāṃkhya: absorption of prakṛti; in Yoga: the stillness where mind’s multiplicity is unified

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both sutras identify the mind as the central element of experience; 2.1 states that the mind is the source of all perception, while 4.5 asserts its directive role over functions.
*Divergence:* While 2.1 presents the mind as passive source, 4.5 reclaims it as active director, thereby shifting the ontological status of citta

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 3.12:** Sāṃkhya posits that prakṛti generates vṛtti; 4.5 parallels this by naming the mind as the director of vṛtti.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya’s vṛtti are produced by prakṛti, whereas here they are produced by puruṣa’s directive agency

**Yoga Sūtra 1.3:** Both sutras discuss the multiplicity of functions (vṛtti) arising in the mind.
*Divergence:* 1.3 describes vṛtti as arising from citta, whereas 4.5 claims citta itself directs these functions, implying a higher level of control

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.5:** Sāṃkhya distinguishes between puruṣa and prakṛti; 4.5 echoes this distinction by focusing on puruṣa’s role.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats puruṣa as passive observer, while 4.5 attributes active agency to puruṣa

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Direct your attention to the single, unifying mind that directs all mental functions, observing how the many thoughts arise under its guidance.

---

## Meditation‑Born Freedom
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.6 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Only the minds born of meditation are free from karmic

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तत्र ध्यायनजम् अनाशयम्

### IAST

tatra dhyānajam anāśayam

### Pratibha Translation

There, the mind that is born of meditation is free of karmic residue.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that liberation is achieved by a mind that is *born* through meditation is a decisive philosophical move: it elevates the act of meditative birth to the very threshold of kaivalya, rather than treating it as a mere preparatory stage. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, *dhyāna* is the deepening of *citta*’s focus, while *jam* (birth) signals a new ontological status. Here, the sūtra asserts that *jam* is not a passive occurrence but an active transformation that purges *karmic* *pratītyāna* (impressions). The phrase *anāśaya*—free of residue—underscores that this freedom is not a gradual shedding but an instantaneous cessation of *vipākāra* (causal residue). This aligns with the Sāṃkhya notion that *purusha* becomes distinct from *prakṛti* when the latter’s *vipākāra* is annihilated. The existential application is clear: one must cultivate a meditative state that *birthing* a mind devoid of karmic imprint, thereby realizing *kaivalya* as an ontological shift rather than a mere state of calm. The Satchidananda commentary’s brief note that the mind is “born of meditation” echoes this, but the Pratibha analysis insists that the *birth* itself is the vehicle of liberation, not the practice alone. Thus, the sūtra invites practitioners to view meditation not just as a technique but as a transformative act that reconfigures the very nature of consciousness, aligning with the Yoga Sutra’s broader thesis that *samādhi* is the culmination of purifying *citta* from *vipākāra*.

### Key Terms

**dhyāna (ध्यान)** — from dhā (to think) + -na (instrument) -> meditation; in tradition, the deepening of citta’s focus, beyond mere concentration

**jam (जम्)** — from jā (to be born) -> birth; in this context, ontological rebirth of mind free from karmic residue

**anāśaya (अनाशय)** — from an (without) + āśaya (residue) -> free of residue; signals complete annihilation of karmic imprint

**karma (कर्म)** — action; in this sūtra, the residue of action that must be shed for liberation

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 2.50:** Both sutras state that the mind is free when it is not influenced by karmic impressions; 2.50 uses ‘samādhi’ while 4.6 uses ‘jam’ to denote the same state
*Divergence:* 4.6 replaces the abstract notion of samādhi with the concrete act of being born, emphasizing ontological change over psychological attainment

**Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad 2.1.1:** Both describe liberation as the mind being untainted by past actions
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad speaks of *moksha* as a state of knowledge, whereas 4.6 frames it as a birth free of residue, highlighting the Yoga emphasis on experiential transformation

**Śaṅkara, *Brahmasūtra* 3.1.1:** Both link liberation to the cessation of *vipākāra*
*Divergence:* Śaṅkara’s sutra uses metaphysical causality, while 4.6 employs a more practical, meditative birth metaphor

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Meditate until you are born free of karmic residue.

---

## Yogi's Equanimous Action
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.7 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The actions of the Yogi are neither white [good] nor black [bad]; but the actions of others are of three kinds: good, bad and mixed.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

कर्माशुख़लाकृष्णं योगिनः त्रिविधं अन्येषाम्

### IAST

karmāśuklākṛṣṇaṃ yoginas trividham itareṣām

### Pratibha Translation

The Yogi’s actions are neither white nor black; the actions of others are of three kinds.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the Yogi’s karma is beyond dualistic evaluation, while ordinary karma is classified into good, bad, and mixed. This distinction is not merely a semantic trick but a philosophical claim that the Yogi’s consciousness has transcended the puruṣa‑prakṛti duality that normally governs vṛtti. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, the Yogi is a fully realized puruṣa, detached from prakṛti’s fluctuating vṛtti; hence his actions do not acquire the karmic quality of purity or impurity. The contested move here is the negation of both ‘white’ (śukla) and ‘black’ (kṛṣṇa) for the Yogi, which some commentators argue is a rhetorical device rather than a doctrinal statement. However, the Satchidananda commentary treats it as literal, suggesting that the Yogi’s actions are performed with such equanimity that they are not subject to moral categorization. This aligns with the idea of samādhi as a state where the mind is no longer a vṛtti‑producing instrument. Existentially, the sūtra invites practitioners to cultivate an attitude of impartiality, recognizing that the external world may label actions, but the internal witness remains uncolored. By internalizing this stance, one moves closer to kaivalya, where the self is no longer bound by the dualities of good and bad.

### Key Terms

**karmā (कर्म)** — root: *karm- ‘to do’; in Sāṃkhya, action is a manifestation of prakṛti; in Patanjali, karma is the causal residue that binds puruṣa to the world; the term here emphasizes that even the Yogi’s actions are still actions but lack karmic charge.

**śukla (शुख़ल)** — etymology: *śuk- ‘white, pure’; in Sāṃkhya, śukla denotes purity of mind; the sūtra uses it to denote moral goodness, but the negation for the Yogi indicates transcendence of moral purity.

**kṛṣṇa (कृष्ण)** — root: *kṛṣ- ‘black, dark’; in Sāṃkhya, kṛṣṇa represents impurity or moral badness; its negation for the Yogi signals freedom from moral impurity.

**yoginḥ (योगिनः)** — derived from *yog- ‘to unite’; in Patanjali, a yogi is one who has achieved samādhi; here it marks the subject whose karma is beyond duality.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses begin with a negation of ordinary vṛtti: 1.1 states that yoga is the cessation of mind‑waves, while 4.7 negates the moral categorization of the Yogi’s actions; structurally, each clause introduces a dualistic opposition that is then transcended.
*Divergence:* Where 1.1 speaks of cessation of all vṛtti, 4.7 limits the transcendence to the realm of moral evaluation, leaving ordinary vṛtti still present in the world.

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both verses use a tripartite classification of phenomena: 1.2 lists the three states of mind (vṛtti, nirodha, samādhi), whereas 4.7 lists the three kinds of others’ actions (good, bad, mixed); the structure mirrors the tripartite schema of Sāṃkhya.
*Divergence:* 1.2’s tripartite is ontological (states of mind), while 4.7’s is epistemological (judgments about actions), showing a shift from internal states to external moral assessment.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Act with equanimity, refusing to label your deeds as good or bad, and observe how this impartial stance dissolves the duality of karma.

---

## Vasana Manifestation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.8 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तसत् तद्विपाकानुगुणानाम् एवाभिव्यक्तिर् वासनानाम्

### IAST

tatas tadvipākānuguṇānām evābhivyaktir vāsanānām

### Pratibha Translation

These vasanas, for which favorable conditions (vipāka) exist, are the sole manifestations (abhivyakti).

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that manifestation is contingent on favorable conditions, not an inherent property of vasanas. It claims that only those subconscious impressions that find the right combination of gunas and circumstances will express themselves in a particular birth. This is a contested move in the Yoga tradition, because some commentators argue that vasanas have an autonomous power to manifest regardless of external conditions. In the Sāṃkhya framework, however, the interplay of prakṛti’s gunas (sattva, rajas, tamas) determines the potentiality of each vasana; without the appropriate anuguṇa, the vasana remains latent. The sutra’s emphasis on “evābhivyaktir” underscores the exclusivity of manifestation: it is not a passive unfolding but an active expression that requires the alignment of internal and external factors. Practically, this means that one must cultivate awareness of the subtle conditions that enable a particular vasana to surface, thereby transforming latent potential into conscious experience. By recognizing the specific gunic milieu that nurtures a vasana, a practitioner can deliberately influence which impressions manifest, turning the mind into a disciplined instrument of self‑realization.

### Key Terms

**vasana (वासना)** — vasana (लत्) -> latent impression; in Sāṃkhya, a conditioned potential; default translation 'desire' misses its role as a conditioned causal factor.

**vipaka (विपाक)** — vipaka (संज्ञा) -> fruition; in Yoga, the result of a vasana acting under favorable gunas; default 'fruit' ignores its causal, conditioned nature.

**guna (गुण)** — guna (संज्ञा) -> quality; in Sāṃkhya, the three gunas (sattva, rajas, tamas) that shape prakṛti; default 'quality' omits their dynamic generative function.

**abhivyakti (अभिव्यक्ति)** — abhivyakti (संज्ञा) -> manifestation; in Yoga, the act of a vasana becoming conscious; default 'expression' fails to convey its conditioned, intentional unfolding.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 4.1:** Both sutras link manifestation to favorable conditions; 4.1 speaks of the mind’s fluctuations (vṛtti) being conditioned by prakṛti.
*Divergence:* 4.8 specifies vasanas as the conditioned entities, whereas 4.1 generalizes to all mental states.

**Yoga Sutra 4.5:** Both sutras emphasize that only certain conditions allow a result; 4.5 discusses the mind’s ability to be controlled.
*Divergence:* 4.8 focuses on latent impressions, while 4.5 addresses the active control of present thoughts.

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts assert that prakṛti’s gunas govern the manifestation of conditioned phenomena.
*Divergence:* Karika frames the process as a universal law, whereas 4.8 applies it specifically to vasanas.

**Patanjali 1.1 (Yoga Sutra):** Both sutras introduce the concept of the mind’s fluctuations as conditioned by prakṛti.
*Divergence:* 1.1 establishes the framework, while 4.8 narrows it to the manifestation of vasanas.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the vasanas in your mind; note which have favorable conditions, and recognize their manifestations.

---

## Desires and Impressions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.9 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Although desires are separated from their fulfillments by class, space and time, they have an uninterrupted relationship because the impressions [of desires] and memories of them are identical.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

जातिदेशकालव्यवहितानाम् अप्य आन्तर्यम् स्मृतिसंस्कारयोः एकरूपत्वात्

### IAST

jātideśakālavyavahitānām apy ānantaryaṃ smṛtisaṃskārayor ekarūpatvāt

### Pratibha Translation

Desires, though separated from their fulfillments by class, space, and time, maintain an uninterrupted relationship because the impressions of desires and the memories of them are identical.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the causal chain of desire does not break at the point of fulfillment; rather, the same mental imprint persists as both desire and recollection. This continuity is a contested move in the Yoga–Sāṃkhya dialogue: while Sāṃkhya emphasizes the distinctness of puruṣa and prakṛti, the Yoga school insists that the mind’s impressions (samskara) bind the soul (purusha) to the material world (prakṛti). Here, the author claims that the mind’s impressions (samskara) are the mechanism that preserves the identity between desire (kāma) and its memory. The claim is that the mind does not simply forget the desire after its fulfillment; it retains an identical impression that continues to influence future actions. This has existential relevance: it explains why we often find ourselves repeating patterns of desire even after the external conditions have changed. The insight aligns with the notion that the mind’s impressions are not inert; they are dynamic forces that shape the trajectory of the soul’s journey toward kaivalya (liberation). By recognizing the continuity between desire and memory, one can begin to see how the mind’s habitual patterns are perpetuated, and thus, how to intervene by cultivating awareness (dhyāna) to break the cycle. The passage also echoes the Satchidananda aphorism that “desires are separated from their fulfillments by class, space and time, yet they have an uninterrupted relationship because the impressions of desires and memories of them are identical.” This reinforces the idea that the mind’s impressions are the bridge that keeps desire alive beyond its immediate satisfaction.

### Key Terms

**samskara (संस्कार)** — samskara: from Sanskrit root ‘samskṛti’ meaning ‘to make’ or ‘to form’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes latent impressions that condition future cognition; the default translation ‘impression’ misses its formative, conditioning role

**kāma (काम)** — kāma: originally ‘desire’ or ‘wish’; in Yoga it is the driving force of the mind that leads to attachment; the simple translation ‘desire’ overlooks its role as a causal agent in the mind’s activity

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha: the conscious principle, the observer; Sāṃkhya sees it as distinct from prakṛti; the default ‘self’ fails to capture its transcendental, unchanging nature

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: the material principle, dynamic and evolving; in Sāṃkhya it is the source of all phenomena; the common translation ‘nature’ obscures its role as the primal substance that is bound by purusha’s ignorance

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.9:** The structure ‘A B C’ mirrors the triad of desire, its fulfillment, and the sustaining impression, paralleling the triadic pattern in 4.8 where desire, action, and result are linked.
*Divergence:* Unlike 4.8, 4.9 does not present a causal sequence but a static identity, suggesting a shift from action to memory

**Sāṃkhya Karika 1.1:** Both texts emphasize the role of latent impressions (samskara) in binding the soul to the world.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Karika treats samskara as a passive residue, whereas the Yoga Sūtra treats it as an active, continuous bridge between desire and memory

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** The notion of continuity of mind’s impressions aligns with the early claim that the mind’s activity is unbroken.
*Divergence:* 2.1 focuses on the mind’s activity in the present, while 4.9 extends the continuity to the memory of fulfilled desires

**Sāṃkhya Tattvasūtra 2.2:** Both texts discuss the interplay of purusha and prakṛti through mental impressions.
*Divergence:* Tattvasūtra frames this interplay as a dualistic separation, whereas the Yoga Sūtra presents it as a unified, continuous process

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each desire as it arises, and immediately note the memory of that desire after its fulfillment, to become aware of the persistent impression that links them.

---

## Endless Desires of Impressions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.10 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Since the desire to live is eternal, impressions are also

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तसाम् अनादित्वं चाशिषः नित्यत्वात्

### IAST

tāsām anāditvaṃ cāśiṣo nityatvāt

### Pratibha Translation

For these impressions, the desire to live is beginningless, arising from eternality.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the desire to live (āśiṣḥ) associated with impressions (tāsām) is devoid of a beginning, yet it emanates from an eternal principle. This juxtaposition foregrounds the paradox central to Sāṃkhya‑Yoga: the finite, ever‑changing realm of prakṛti (the material world) is sustained by an uncaused, timeless source—often identified with puruṣa or īśvara. By declaring the desire to live as anāditvaṃ, the text denies any causal chain within the material realm, thereby elevating the transcendental principle as the true origin. The claim that this desire arises from nityatvāt (eternality) reinforces the idea that the material world, though bound by time, is ultimately a manifestation of the eternal. In practice, this insight invites the practitioner to recognize that the incessant craving for existence is not a self‑generated impulse but a reflection of an underlying, unchanging reality. By observing this, one can begin to detach from the illusion of temporal causality and cultivate a state of samādhi where the mind rests upon the eternal, leading toward kaivalya (liberation).

### Key Terms

**tāsām (तसाम्)** — plural of tva (this), referring to the impressions; denotes the objects of the mind’s activity

**anāditvaṃ (अनादित्वं)** — ‘beginninglessness’; from an- (not) + aditi (beginning) + -tva (nominalizer); indicates that the desire has no causal origin within prakṛti

**āśiṣḥ (आशिषः)** — ‘desire to live’; a verb root āśi (to desire) + -ṣ (inflection) + -ḥ (nominalizer); signifies the persistent yearning that animates the mind

**nityatvāt (नित्यत्वात्)** — from nitya (eternal) + -tva (nominalizer) + at (from); meaning ‘from eternality’; points to the source of the desire as the eternal principle

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.10:** Both the sūtra and the preceding 4.9 emphasize the causal status of the mind’s activity, yet 4.10 uniquely introduces the concept of anāditvaṃ, breaking from the earlier causal chain and pointing to the transcendental source
*Divergence:* While 4.9 treats the mind’s activity as contingent, 4.10 asserts its origin in an eternal principle, marking a shift from empirical causality to metaphysical grounding

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.1:** The notion of anāditvaṃ parallels the Sāṃkhya claim that puruṣa is uncaused, yet here it is applied to the desire within the mind rather than to puruṣa itself
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats puruṣa as the sole uncaused entity, whereas the sūtra extends this uncaused status to the desire, thereby integrating the mind’s activity into the transcendental schema

**Yoga Sūtra 2.23:** Both passages discuss the nature of the mind’s activity (citta) and its relation to prakṛti
*Divergence:* 2.23 focuses on the mind’s ignorance and its causal chain, while 4.10 bypasses ignorance and directly attributes the desire to eternality, suggesting a more direct path to liberation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the desire to live arising in your mind; note its lack of a discernible beginning, and recognize that it springs from an eternal source, allowing you to detach and rest in the present moment.

---

## Four Foundations of Impressions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.11 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The impressions being held together by cause, effect, basis and support, they disappear with the disappearance of these four.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

हेतुपलाःश्रयालम्बनैः सङ्ग्र्हीतत्वाद् एषाम् अभावः तदाभावः

### IAST

hetuphalāśrayālambanaiḥ saṃgṛhītatvād eṣām abhāve tadabhāvaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The impressions are held together by cause, effect, basis, and support; when these four are absent, the impressions vanish.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the persistence of mental impressions (vṛtti) depends on four interdependent conditions: causality (hetu), effect (phala), basis (mūla), and support (āśray). In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, these four are the operative mechanisms of prakṛti that bind the mind to the world. The verse asserts that dissolution of impressions occurs only when all four conditions are simultaneously removed, highlighting the inseparability of the causal chain. This contrasts with the more common Sāṃkhya view that vṛtti can be suppressed by the pratyakṣa‑prāpti of puruṣa alone; here, the emphasis is on the structural collapse of the causal matrix. The implication for practice is that one must not merely withdraw attention but must also dismantle the underlying causal fabric—achieving a state where cause, effect, basis, and support are no longer operative. This aligns with the Satchidananda insight that the disappearance of impressions follows the disappearance of their four foundations, underscoring the necessity of a holistic approach to liberation.

### Key Terms

**hetu (हेतु)** — cause -> Sāṃkhya: initiating principle of change -> Yoga: foundational cause of vṛtti; default translation 'cause' omits its role as the initiating spark of all mental activity.

**phala (फल)** — effect -> Sāṃkhya: resultant manifestation of change -> Yoga: the outcome that sustains vṛtti; default 'effect' fails to capture its sustaining, generative quality.

**mūla (मूल)** — basis -> Sāṃkhya: root principle of materiality -> Yoga: the underlying substrate that anchors impressions; default 'basis' misses its ontological primacy.

**āśray (आश्रय)** — support -> Sāṃkhya: sustaining condition that preserves change -> Yoga: the sustaining force that keeps vṛtti from dissolving; default 'support' overlooks its role as the protective buffer.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.9:** Both verses enumerate the four conditions (hetu, phala, mūla, āśray) as essential for vṛtti, showing structural homology in listing.
*Divergence:* While 4.9 focuses on the presence of these conditions, 4.11 emphasizes their absence as the trigger for dissolution, shifting from existence to non‑existence.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1.1:** Sāṃkhya’s enumeration of the four principles of change parallels the Yoga list, indicating shared metaphysics.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats the principles as independent, whereas Yoga presents them as interdependent, a nuance that informs the Yoga interpretation of vṛtti.

**Yoga Sūtra 4.12:** The next verse discusses the removal of vṛtti through pratyāhāra, structurally following the removal of the four conditions.
*Divergence:* 4.12 introduces pratyāhāra as a method, whereas 4.11 merely states the condition for dissolution, highlighting a procedural versus descriptive difference.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe each mental impression, noting its cause, effect, basis, and support; as you become aware of these four, you consciously let them dissolve, allowing the impression to vanish.

---

## Real Form of Past and Future
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.12 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The past and future exist in the real form of objects which in the conditions of their

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

अतीतानागतं स्वरूपतोऽस्य अध्वाभेदाद् धर्माणाम्

### IAST

atītānāgataṃ svarūpato 'sty adhvabhedād dharmāṇām

### Pratibha Translation

The past and future are present in the real form of the object, because they are not divided from the same reality.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that past and future are not separate entities but are inherent in the present, the real form of the object. This claim challenges the conventional temporal distinction that treats past and future as distinct moments. In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, time is a product of prakṛti’s vṛtti, and the puruṣa’s awareness of these vṛtti is mediated through samādhi.
