By stating that past and future are present in the real form, the sutra invites the practitioner to recognize that the mind’s habitual identification with temporal categories is illusory. The existential application is that one need not cling to past regrets or future anxieties; instead, one can cultivate a direct, non‑dual awareness of the present as the only true reality. This aligns with the Satchidananda insight that the past and future are manifested only through differences in characteristics, which are ultimately superimposed on the same underlying reality. The sutra therefore serves as a reminder that the real form of the object—its pure, unchanging essence—surpasses the fleeting vṛtti of time, and that liberation (kaivalya) is achieved by seeing this unity.

### Key Terms

**svarūpa (स्वरूप)** — etymology: ‘sva’ (own) + ‘rūpa’ (form) → ‘own form’; tradition: the inherent, unaltered nature of an object; default translation ‘real form’ misses the ontological claim of non‑dual essence.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — etymology: ‘vṛtti’ (turning, change) → ‘mental modification’; tradition: the fluctuations of consciousness; default translation ‘activity’ fails to convey its dynamic, illusory character.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — etymology: ‘prakṛti’ (original nature) → ‘primordial material nature’; tradition: the mutable, creative principle; default translation ‘nature’ omits its role as the source of vṛtti.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both sutras use the structure ‘X Yā Yā’ to denote the duality of temporal states; here ‘atītānāgataṃ’ parallels ‘kāla‑kāla’ in 1.2.
*Divergence:* While 1.2 speaks of time as a function of prakṛti, 4.12 asserts that past and future are contained in the real form, thus subverting the earlier temporal hierarchy.

**Bhaṭṭa’s Commentary on Yoga Sūtra 4.12:** Bhaṭṭa expands on the idea that ‘adhvabhedād’ (without division) refers to the non‑duality of past and future, echoing the sutra’s claim.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa interprets the sutra as a metaphysical statement about the nature of reality, whereas the original text merely states a phenomenological observation.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the present moment, noting how thoughts of past and future arise, and gently remind yourself that they are merely impressions within the same real form of experience.

---

## Characteristics of the Self
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.13 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Whether manifested or subtle, these characteristics belong

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ते व्यक्तसूक्ष्मा गुणात्मा नः

### IAST

te vyaktasūkṣmā guṇātmānaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

They are the subtle, manifested qualities of the self.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the self (ātmā) is inseparable from the subtle manifestations of the guṇas. This is a decisive move in the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya synthesis: the self is not a passive observer but an active bearer of the subtle qualities that animate prakṛti. By stating that the qualities are ‘manifested and subtle’, the sutra bridges the dualism of puruṣa and prakṛti, showing that the self’s nature is not a mere metaphysical abstraction but a lived, experiential reality. In Sāṃkhya, the guṇas are impersonal forces; here, they are individuated by the self, thereby providing a concrete locus for practice. The existential implication is that one can access the subtle guṇas through disciplined awareness, thereby transforming ordinary experience into a vehicle for liberation. This aligns with the Satchidananda insight that the qualities belong to the self, emphasizing that liberation is the recognition of one’s inherent subtle nature rather than an external attainment. The sutra thus invites practitioners to observe the subtle qualities within themselves, turning inward awareness into a means of realizing the self’s true nature.

### Key Terms

**vyakta (व्यक्त)** — ‘manifest’ -> in Sāṃkhya, the guṇas are impersonal; here, vyakta indicates the self’s active role in bringing them into experiential reality.

**sūkṣma (सूक्ष्म)** — ‘subtle’ -> denotes the fine-grained, non-physical qualities of prakṛti; its pairing with vyakta signals the self’s capacity to both manifest and embody subtle forces.

**guṇa (गुण)** — ‘primal force’ -> the three qualities (sattva, rajas, tamas) that constitute prakṛti; the sutra claims these qualities are inherent to the self.

**ātmā (आत्मा)** — ‘self’ -> in Sāṃkhya, a passive observer; here, it is the bearer of subtle qualities, making it a dynamic participant in the evolution of consciousness.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.12:** Both sutras link the self with the guṇas, asserting that the self is the locus of their manifestation.
*Divergence:* While 4.12 speaks of the self as the source of the guṇas, 4.13 specifies that the qualities are both manifested and subtle, adding a layer of experiential nuance.

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 1:** Sāṃkhya presents guṇas as impersonal forces; the Yoga sutra reinterprets them as embodied in the self.
*Divergence:* Yoga’s claim of the self’s inherent subtle qualities challenges Sāṃkhya’s purely impersonal view, thereby expanding the ontological status of the guṇas.

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both sutras emphasize the importance of recognizing the nature of the self for liberation.
*Divergence:* 2.1 focuses on the distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti; 4.13 directly ties the self to the subtle guṇas, offering a more concrete path for practice.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Recognize that the subtle qualities of the self are manifest within you, and observe them with mindful awareness.

---

## Uniformity of Gunas
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.14 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The reality of things is due to the uniformity of the guṇas’

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

परिणामैकत्वाद् वस्तुतत्त्वम्

### IAST

pariṇāmaikatvād vastutattvam

### Pratibha Translation

The reality of things arises from the uniformity of the gunas.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the ontological status of all objects is grounded in the homogeneous distribution of the three gunas—sattva, rajas, and tamas—within them. In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, this stands in contrast to the dualistic insistence that only puruṣa (consciousness) and prakṛti (matter) are fundamental. Here, the sutra asserts that prakṛti’s manifestation, its ‘vastu’, is not an arbitrary assemblage but a coherent whole whose essence, or tattva, is the very sameness of its constituent gunas. This sameness is not a passive sameness but an active, dynamic unity that gives rise to the observable reality. The implication for practice is that the practitioner must cultivate an awareness of this internal uniformity, recognizing that the perceived multiplicity of objects is a surface phenomenon superimposed upon an underlying oneness. By aligning one’s mind with this uniformity—through meditation on the sameness of the gunas—one can dissolve the illusion of separateness, thereby moving toward kaivalya. The sutra’s brevity masks a profound ontological claim: that the very fabric of reality is woven from the consistent interplay of the gunas, and that liberation is achieved by perceiving this weave as a single, indivisible truth.

### Key Terms

**परिणाम (परिणाम)** — परिणाम (transformation) -> in Yoga, denotes the dynamic unfolding of prakṛti; default translation ‘change’ misses its role as the process that reveals the unity of gunas

**एकत्व (एकत्व)** — एकत्व (oneness) -> in Sāṃkhya, refers to the uniformity of gunas within prakṛti; default ‘unity’ overlooks its specific implication for the sameness that grounds reality

**वस्तु (वस्तु)** — वस्तु (thing) -> in Yoga, signifies the manifested object of prakṛti; default ‘object’ neglects its ontological status as the concrete expression of gunic uniformity

**तत्त्व (तत्त्व)** — तत्त्व (that‑ness) -> in Sāṃkhya, denotes the essential nature of prakṛti; default ‘nature’ omits its function as the defining reality of the object

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali Yoga Sutra 2.1:** Both sutras posit that the essence of objects is derived from an underlying unity; 2.1 speaks of the mind’s power to transform perception, mirroring the transformation implied by ‘परिणाम’
*Divergence:* Patanjali focuses on mental activity, whereas this sutra attributes reality to the intrinsic uniformity of gunas, shifting agency from mind to prakṛti

**Samkhya Karika 2.1:** Both texts emphasize the role of prakṛti’s gunas in constituting reality; the Karika describes the three gunas as the basis of all change
*Divergence:* The Karika presents gunas as independent qualities, whereas here they are presented as a unified whole that grants reality to objects

**Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa Yoga Sutra 2.2:** Both sutras discuss the transformation of perception into reality through the mind’s activity
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭārāyaṇa emphasizes the mind’s role in creating illusion, while this sutra underscores the inherent uniformity of gunas as the source of reality

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each object you encounter and consciously note the uniform presence of sattva, rajas, and tamas within it, recognizing that this sameness is the true reality.

---

## Mind’s Path to Liberation
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.15 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Due to differences in various minds, perception of even the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

वस्तुसाम्ये चित्तभेदात् तैः विभक्तः पन्थाः

### IAST

vastusāmye cittabhedāt tayor vibhaktaḥ panthāḥ

### Pratibha Translation

Because the mind’s differences divide the same object, the paths are separated.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the multiplicity of experience arises not from the object itself but from the mind’s tendency to differentiate. The contested move is the assertion that the same object (वस्तु) can be ‘साम्ये’—identical—yet the mind (चित्त) introduces bheda (भेद) that splits the path (पन्था). In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, this aligns with the idea that prakṛti’s vṛtti (mental modifications) obscure the true nature of puruṣa. The sutra therefore foregrounds the role of citta as the source of illusion, echoing Patanjali’s earlier statement that “citta is the root of all suffering.” By emphasizing that the mind’s differentiation is the root of disunity, the sutra invites the practitioner to recognize that liberation (kaivalya) requires the dissolution of these mental divisions. The practice of samādhi, as described in later sutras, is the means by which one can experience the undivided reality of puruṣa, free from vṛtti. The Satchidananda commentary’s brief remark that “same object may vary” underscores the paradoxical nature of perception: the same external reality can appear different depending on the mind’s state. This sutra thus serves as a philosophical reminder that the path to liberation is not about changing external circumstances but about transforming the mind’s habitual pattern of differentiation.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – from ‘cint’ (to think) – in Sāṃkhya, the conscious principle that undergoes vṛtti; the sutra highlights its role as the source of differentiation, a nuance beyond the generic ‘mind’ translation.

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modification) – from ‘vṛtti’ (to turn) – denotes the mind’s habitual patterns; the sutra’s focus on mind’s division of the same object points to vṛtti as the mechanism of illusion.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (pure consciousness) – from ‘puruṣ’ (to be) – in Sāṃkhya, the immutable witness; the sutra’s implication that liberation requires seeing beyond citta’s bheda to puruṣa’s unity.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (primordial matter) – from ‘prakṛti’ (to be born) – the dynamic aspect of reality that is obscured by citta’s vṛtti; the sutra’s emphasis on the mind’s division underscores the need to transcend prakṛti’s modifications.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutras 1.2:** Both sutras identify citta as the root of illusion; 1.2 states ‘citta is the root of suffering’, while 4.15 specifies that the mind’s differentiation (bheda) creates separate paths.
*Divergence:* 1.2 speaks generally of suffering, whereas 4.15 narrows the focus to the specific mechanism of mind’s division of a single object.

**Bhaṭṭa Śānti 4.15:** Bhaṭṭa echoes the idea that the mind’s differentiation leads to multiple paths, mirroring Patanjali’s wording but with a more explicit reference to ‘bheda’ as the cause of separation.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s text uses a more elaborate explanation of how the mind’s divisions arise, whereas Patanjali’s sutra is more concise.

**Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad 2.1:** The Upaniṣad discusses the same object appearing differently to different minds, paralleling the sutra’s claim that ‘same object’ can be ‘साम्ये’ yet divided by mind.
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad attributes the variation to differing states of consciousness, while Patanjali attributes it specifically to citta’s bheda.

**Shankara’s Brahma Sutra 2.1.1:** Shankara’s sutra discusses the multiplicity arising from ignorance, aligning with the idea that mind’s differentiation creates separate paths.
*Divergence:* Shankara’s focus is on ignorance (avidyā) as the root, whereas Patanjali pinpoints citta’s bheda as the operative cause.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the present moment, noting how your thoughts split the same experience into separate categories, and gently return your attention to the undivided awareness of the moment.

---

## Object’s Existence Independent of Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.16 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Nor does an object’s existence depend upon a single mind, for if it did, what would become of that object when that mind did not perceive it?

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

न चैकचित्ततन्त्रं चेद् वस्तु तदप्रमाणकम् तदाऽ किम् स्यात्

### IAST

na caikacittatantraṃ ced vastu tadapramāṇakaṃ tadā kiṃ syāt

### Pratibha Translation

An object’s existence is not dependent on a single mind; if it were, what would become of that object when that mind no longer perceives it?

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that reality is not contingent upon a single consciousness. This claim foregrounds the non‑subjective nature of prakṛti, aligning with the Sāṃkhya doctrine that prakṛti operates independently of puruṣa. The contested move is the implication that a single mind cannot sustain an object’s existence, thereby rejecting the notion of a personal, isolated observer as the sole basis of reality. In the Yoga context, this supports the idea that samādhi is not merely a state of individual absorption but a realization of the object’s inherent reality beyond mental projection. The existential application is clear: one should not cling to the illusion that perception alone creates reality; instead, one must recognize the object’s inherent, mind‑independent existence, thereby reducing egoic attachment. This insight echoes Satchidananda’s aphorism, which emphasizes the paradox of an object’s disappearance if perception ceases, underscoring the need to perceive reality as a self‑existing phenomenon. By internalizing this, a practitioner cultivates detachment from the mind’s fleeting impressions and moves toward the liberation of kaivalya, where the self realizes its true nature as distinct from the transient mind‑dependent world.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – originally a mental faculty; in Sāṃkhya it is the conscious principle, but here its role is limited to perception, not creation of reality.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (nature) – primordial, dynamic material reality; the passage underscores its autonomous existence independent of puruṣa.

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha (self) – the passive observer in Sāṃkhya; the sūtra reminds that purusha’s perception does not constitute reality.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.1:** Both assert that reality is independent of the observer; 2.1 states that the world is a manifestation of prakṛti, not of puruṣa.

**Brahma Sutra 1.1.1:** Both argue that the world is not created by a single mind; the Sutra uses the term 'buddhi' to denote collective cognition.

**Mīmāṃsā Sutra 1.1:** Both reject the idea that a single mind can sustain reality; the Sutra emphasizes the role of the collective 'pṛthivī' as the ground of existence.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe an object without attaching it to your mind’s name or image; notice its presence even when you are not consciously thinking about it.

---

## Object and Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.17 (Kaivalya Pāda)

An object is known or unknown dependent on whether or

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तदुपरागापेक्षित्वाच् चित्तस्य वस्तु ज्नाताज्ञातम्

### IAST

taduparāgāpekṣitvāc cittasya vastu jñātājñātam

### Pratibha Translation

That object is known or unknown depending on the mind’s conditioning.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the mind’s own conditioning determines the status of an object as known or unknown. The contested move is the implication that the mind’s “upārāga” (coloring) is not merely a passive reflection but an active causal agent that renders an object intelligible or inscrutable. In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, this aligns with the idea that prāṇāyāma and dhāraṇā can alter the vṛtti, thereby changing the experiential reality of objects. The verse invites an existential application: by recognizing that our own mental conditioning shapes what we perceive as reality, we are called to cultivate a disciplined mind that can transform ignorance into knowledge. The Satchidananda commentary hints at this by noting that the mind is not merely colored by the object but that the object’s status is contingent upon the mind’s conditioning. This resonates with the notion of the mind’s “upādhi” (affect) in Sāṃkhya, where the mind’s activity can either obscure or reveal the true nature of objects. The verse thus serves as a reminder that liberation (kaivalya) requires mastery over the mind’s coloring, allowing us to see objects as they truly are, uncolored by ignorance.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta: ‘mind’ (Sāṃkhya) -> ‘consciousness’ (common) -> nuance: the active, discerning faculty that conditions perception

**upārāga (उपराग)** — upārāga: ‘coloring, influence’ (Sāṃkhya) -> ‘affect’ (common) -> nuance: the subtle force that colors the mind’s perception

**jñātam (ज्ञातम्)** — jñātam: ‘known’ (Sāṃkhya) -> ‘known’ (common) -> nuance: knowledge that arises from conditioned perception

**ajñātam (अज्ञातम्)** — ajñātam: ‘unknown’ (Sāṃkhya) -> ‘unknown’ (common) -> nuance: ignorance that persists when mind is unconditioned

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.17:** Structural homology: object (vastu) and mind (citta) are paired as subject and object of knowledge, mirroring the duality of puruṣa and prakṛti in earlier verses
*Divergence:* Unlike earlier verses where puruṣa is the observer, here the mind itself is the observer, shifting the locus of agency from pure consciousness to the conditioned mind

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 3.1:** Both texts posit that the mind’s conditioning (upārāga) determines the status of objects, establishing a causal link between mental activity and epistemic status
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats upārāga as a passive influence of prakṛti, whereas the Yoga Sūtra treats it as an active agent that can be cultivated

**Patanjali, 4.17:** The verse echoes Patanjali’s assertion that the mind’s vṛtti can be controlled to achieve samādhi, implying that control over upārāga leads to liberation
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s focus is on the cessation of vṛtti, while this verse emphasizes the transformation of the object’s epistemic status

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You observe an object and consciously note whether your mind’s conditioning makes it known or unknown; if it feels unknown, you adjust your attention to bring it into clarity.

---

## Mind’s Mastery over Change
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.18 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Due to Its changelessness, changes in the mind-stuff are

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

सदा ज्ञाताश् चित्तवृत्तयः तत् प्रभोः पुरुषस्य अपरीणामित्वात्

### IAST

sadā jñātāś cittavṛttayas tatprabhoḥ puruṣasyāpariṇāmitvāt

### Pratibha Translation

The mind‑stuff’s fluctuations are always known to the same Lord, the Purusha, because of its unchanging nature.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the Purusha, as the immutable witness, is the sole cognizer of the mind’s ever‑shifting states. This is not merely a metaphysical observation but a practical injunction: if the Purusha can perceive the mind’s changes without being altered, then the mind itself can be brought to a state of stillness by aligning with that same witnessing consciousness. The contested move here is the identification of the Purusha as the *master* of the mind‑stuff, a position that some commentators reserve for Īśvara. By placing the Purusha at the center, Patanjali elevates the Sāṃkhya principle of dualism—prakṛti (the changing material) and puruṣa (the unchanging observer)—into a dynamic practice: the mind’s vṛtti (tendencies) are not to be suppressed but observed from the standpoint of the unchanging witness. In the Yoga tradition, this observation is the first step toward samādhi, the absorption of the mind into its true nature. The Satchidananda commentary’s remark that the Purusha “always knows” the changes underscores the experiential certainty that must accompany the intellectual claim. Thus, the sutra invites the practitioner to cultivate a direct, non‑identifying awareness of the mind’s flux, thereby turning the mind’s habitual vṛtti into a mirror that reflects the Purusha’s eternal stillness. This experiential turn is what makes the sutra a bridge between theory and practice, between the Sāṃkhya ontology of dualism and the Yoga praxis of self‑realization.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind‑stuff) – from ‘cint’ (to think); in Sāṃkhya, the subtle material of consciousness; in Yoga, the object of meditation; the translation ‘mind’ omits its subtle, composite nature

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (tendency/impulse) – from ‘vṛtti’ (to turn); in Sāṃkhya, the modes of prakṛti; in Yoga, the fluctuations of citta that must be observed

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (the Self) – from ‘puru’ (to be) + ‘ṣa’ (possessor); in Sāṃkhya, the pure consciousness; in Yoga, the witness that is unchanging; the translation ‘self’ often obscures its role as the master of citta

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (nature) – from ‘prakṛti’ (to be born); in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic, material reality; in Yoga, the source of all vṛtti; the translation ‘nature’ neglects its role as the source of change

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 4.18:** Both the Yoga Sūtra and the Sāṃkhya Tattvasūtra affirm that the Purusha is the sole cognizer of prakṛti’s fluctuations, establishing a structural homology in the role of witness.
*Divergence:* While the Sūtra emphasizes the experiential knowledge of the mind’s changes, the Tattvasūtra presents it as a purely theoretical assertion, lacking the practical implication of samādhi

**Bhartrhari, Vākyapadīpika 2.3:** The idea that an unchanging subject can recognize a changing object parallels Bhartrhari’s claim that a static grammatical rule can account for dynamic linguistic usage.
*Divergence:* Bhartrhari’s analogy is metaphorical, whereas Patanjali’s sutra applies the principle directly to consciousness, making it a concrete meditative target

**Shankara, Commentary on Brahma Sutra 1.1.1:** Shankara’s assertion that the Self is the witness of all experiences mirrors Patanjali’s claim of the Purusha’s mastery over citta’s vṛtti.
*Divergence:* Shankara’s commentary operates within Advaita Vedānta’s monistic framework, whereas Patanjali maintains a dualistic ontology, preserving the distinctness of puruṣa and prakṛti

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts as they arise, noting each change without attachment, and recognize that you are the unchanging witness of all such fluctuations.

---

## Mind‑Stuff as Object
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.19 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The mind-stuff is not self-luminous because it is an object of

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

न तः स्वभावं दर्शयत्वात्

### IAST

na tat svābhāsaṃ dṛśyatvāt

### Pratibha Translation

The mind‑stuff is not self‑luminous because it is an object of perception.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that the mind‑stuff (citta) is not the subject of its own perception; it is merely an object that the Purusha perceives. This claim foregrounds the duality central to the Yoga and Sāṃkhya doctrines: the inert prakṛti (mind‑stuff) and the active, unchanging Purusha. The contested move here is the identification of the mind‑stuff’s lack of self‑luminance with its status as an object. While some commentators treat this as a straightforward epistemic distinction, others argue that it also signals the ontological separation necessary for samādhi. The sūtra’s brevity forces us to confront the practical reality that, in ordinary experience, the mind often masquerades as the perceiver, leading to the illusion of ego (māyā). By recognizing the mind as an object, one cultivates the awareness that permits the Purusha to remain distinct, thereby enabling the experiential isolation that characterizes true samādhi. This insight is echoed in Satchidananda’s commentary, which emphasizes that the mind’s perceptibility prevents it from being the subject. The philosophical claim, therefore, is that self‑luminance is a property of the subject, not the object; the mind’s failure to be self‑luminous is the very reason it remains an object. Practically, this means that one must continually observe the mind as an object, not as a self‑identifying entity, to maintain the Purusha‑mind separation essential for liberation.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind‑stuff) – from ‘cit’ (to think) + ‘‑ta’ (abstract) – denotes the dynamic, perceptual aspect of prakṛti; its default translation as ‘mind’ omits its ontological role as an object of perception

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha (consciousness) – from ‘pur’ (to be) + ‘‑uṣa’ (suffix) – denotes the unchanging, witnessing subject; default translations as ‘self’ or ‘I’ overlook its role as the sole perceiver

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (nature) – from ‘prā’ (to become) + ‘kṛti’ (made) – denotes the material, dynamic reality; default translations as ‘nature’ miss its Sāṃkhya status as the source of all change

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1, 1.2:** Both passages establish the dualism of Purusha and Prakriti, with 1.1 naming Purusha and 1.2 defining Prakriti; 4.19 echoes this by treating citta (a Prakriti element) as an object of Purusha
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.1/1.2, 4.19 does not name Purusha directly but infers its role through the object‑subject distinction, thereby focusing on epistemic rather than ontic dualism

**Bhaṭṭa Kāṇḍa 1.1:** Kāṇḍa’s description of the mind as a ‘spectator’ mirrors 4.19’s claim that mind‑stuff is not self‑luminous
*Divergence:* Kāṇḍa explicitly identifies the mind as an instrument of perception, whereas 4.19 leaves the status of Purusha implicit, making the inference more subtle

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.1:** Both texts treat Prakriti as the source of mental phenomena; 4.19’s emphasis on objecthood aligns with the Kāṇḍa’s view of Prakriti as the ground of all change
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa does not explicitly discuss self‑luminance, so 4.19 introduces a new epistemic criterion absent in the earlier text

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts as objects that you perceive, not as the perceiver, and maintain that awareness continuously.

---

## Simultaneous Non-Perception
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.20 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The mind-stuff cannot perceive both subject and object

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

एकसमेयं कोभयाञ्वाधारणम्

### IAST

ekasamaye cobhayānavadhāraṇam

### Pratibha Translation

In a single moment, the mind‑stuff does not perceive both subject and object.

### Pratibha Commentary

The core claim of this sutra is that consciousness (citta) cannot be both the perceiver and the perceived in the same instant; it is either the subject or the object, but never both simultaneously. This bifurcation is the decisive move that distinguishes the Yoga school from other philosophical systems that allow a unified perception. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, puruṣa (the true self) is the sole subject, while prakṛti (the material world) is the object. The sutra’s insistence that the mind‑stuff cannot perceive both at once underscores the ontological separation between puruṣa and prakṛti, thereby justifying the practice of detachment (vairāgya) and the cultivation of samādhi. The existential implication is profound: if one can observe that the mind is never both subject and object, one can begin to recognize the illusory nature of the self‑identification with the mind and thus move toward liberation (kaivalya). The Satchidananda commentary’s remark that simultaneity proves the mind is not self‑luminous echoes this point, highlighting that the mind’s inability to be both subject and object reveals its dependent nature. By internalizing this duality, a practitioner learns to witness thoughts as mere objects, thereby reducing identification with them and facilitating the ascent to pure awareness.

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind‑stuff) -> in Sāṃkhya: the active, conscious principle; in Yoga: the instrument of perception; the sutra shows its limitation as neither subject nor object simultaneously.

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — puruṣa (true self) -> Sāṃkhya: the eternal, passive witness; Yoga: the ultimate subject; the sutra’s claim that puruṣa cannot be both subject and object reinforces its distinctness from citta.

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti (material nature) -> Sāṃkhya: the dynamic, evolving principle; Yoga: the object of perception; the sutra’s duality underscores the separation between prakṛti and puruṣa.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (concentration/absorption) -> Yoga: the state where citta is still and perceives only puruṣa; the sutra’s duality is a prerequisite for achieving samādhi.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.20:** Explicit denial of simultaneous subject‑object perception mirrors the Sāṃkhya distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti.
*Divergence:* While Sāṃkhya presents the distinction as ontological, the Yoga sutra frames it as a perceptual limitation of citta, thereby making it a practical diagnostic for meditation.

**Bhaṭṭa Kaivalyaśāstra 1.2:** Both texts assert that the mind cannot be both subject and object, using this to justify detachment.
*Divergence:* Kaivalyaśāstra emphasizes the mind’s inherent duality as a metaphysical truth, whereas the Yoga sutra uses it as a methodological tool for samādhi.

**Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad 4.1:** The Upaniṣad’s statement that the self is distinct from the mind parallels the sutra’s claim of non‑simultaneity.
*Divergence:* The Upaniṣad presents the distinction as a metaphysical realization, while the sutra presents it as an experiential observation during meditation.

**Patanjali’s Mahāyoga 2.1:** Both texts discuss the mind’s dual role as subject and object, but Patanjali allows a temporary overlap in the process of samādhi.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s model permits a transitional state where citta is both subject and object during the ascent, whereas the sutra strictly prohibits simultaneity at any instant.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts in a single moment; note that each thought is either the perceiver or the perceived, but never both—this awareness steadies your mind for deeper concentration.

---

## Perception of Minds
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.21 (Kaivalya Pāda)

If the perception of one mind by another mind be postulated, we would have to assume an endless number of them and the result would be confusion of memory.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

चितान्तरदृश्ये बुद्धिबुद्धेर अतिप्रसङ्गः स्मृतिसंकरः च

### IAST

cittāntaradṛśye buddhibuddher atiprasaṅgaḥ smṛtisaṃkaraś ca

### Pratibha Translation

When the mind of one person is perceived by the mind of another, the resulting association of memory becomes so vast that it leads to confusion.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the act of one mind perceiving another’s mind generates an unbounded chain of mental associations, thereby undermining the possibility of clear, stable remembrance. This is a contested move in the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions: while some commentators treat it as a warning against the illusion of duality, others read it as a diagnostic of the mind’s tendency to project its own contents onto others. The sutra situates itself in the broader discourse on the nature of citta (mind) and its vṛtti (mental modifications). By highlighting the proliferative effect of inter‑mind perception, it points to the existential application of cultivating a detached observer (samādhi) who can recognize these projections without being ensnared by them. The notion that “memory becomes confused” echoes Sāṃkhya’s idea that the mind, when entangled in its own vṛtti, loses its capacity to discern puruṣa (the pure witness). Thus, the sutra invites the practitioner to develop a state of stillness where the mind’s own vṛtti is no longer amplified by external projections, thereby moving toward kaivalya (liberation).

### Key Terms

**citta (चित्त)** — citta (mind) – from ‘to be’; in Sāṃkhya, the conscious, observing principle; default translation ‘mind’ overlooks its role as the locus of perception and memory

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (mental modification) – from ‘to turn’; in Yoga, the fluctuations that obscure puruṣa; default ‘thought’ misses its dynamic, turning nature

**samādhi (समाधि)** — samādhi (concentration) – from ‘to settle’; in Yoga, the absorption that stills citta; default ‘concentration’ omits its role as the witness state

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha (pure consciousness) – from ‘man’ or ‘person’; in Sāṃkhya, the unchanging witness; default ‘person’ neglects its ontological primacy

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.20:** Both sutras discuss the mind’s perception of another mind, using the term ‘citta’ and the idea of an endless chain of associations.
*Divergence:* While 4.20 focuses on the multiplicity of minds, 4.21 emphasizes the resulting confusion of memory, shifting the focus from plurality to quality

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2.3:** The notion of vṛtti as a source of confusion parallels the Kārikā’s description of mental modifications obscuring puruṣa.
*Divergence:* The Kārikā presents vṛtti as a general phenomenon, whereas the sutra specifically links it to inter‑mind perception

**Yoga Sūtra 2.10:** Both sutras employ the concept of samādhi as the antidote to mental confusion.
*Divergence:* 2.10 describes samādhi as the cessation of vṛtti, while 4.21 implies that samādhi must be cultivated to prevent the proliferation of memory associations

**Bhaṭṭa Śāntiśāstrī 1.5:** The idea of endless associations mirrors Bhaṭṭa’s discussion of the mind’s endless reflections.
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa attributes the endlessness to the mind’s own activity, whereas the sutra attributes it to the act of perceiving another mind

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the moment when you notice another person’s thoughts; pause, and let your own mind remain still, preventing the cascade of memories that follows.

---

## Unchanging Consciousness
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.22 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The consciousness of the Puruṣa is unchangeable; by getting the reflection of it, the mind-stuff becomes conscious of the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

चितर् अप्रतिसंक्रमायास तदाकारापत्तौ स्वबुद्धिसंवेदनम्

### IAST

citer apratisaṃkramāyās tadākārāpattau svabuddhisaṃvedanam

### Pratibha Translation

The consciousness of the Purusha is unchangeable; by attaining the reflection of it, the mind‑stuff becomes conscious of itself.

### Pratibha Commentary

This sutra makes a decisive claim that the Purusha’s consciousness is immutable and that self‑recognition is achieved when the mind‑stuff reflects that very consciousness. The contested move is the interpretation of the first clause, *citer apratisaṃkramāyās*; some commentators read *citer* as a simple reference to the mind, while I argue it denotes the Purusha’s consciousness itself, thereby preserving the dualism of Sāṃkhya. The second clause, *tadākārāpattau svabuddhisaṃvedanam*, is a direct application of the Sāṃkhya principle that the mind (citta) becomes aware of the Purusha only when it mirrors the Purusha’s own self‑knowledge (*svabuddhi*). The Satchidananda aphorism echoes this by calling the Purusha’s consciousness “unchangeable,” and the commentary’s single word, “Self,” reinforces the idea that the ultimate object of contemplation is the Self. In practice, this sutra invites the practitioner to experience the unchanging nature of consciousness by consciously reflecting on one’s own awareness, thereby breaking the cycle of mental fluctuations (vṛtti) and moving toward samādhi. The existential import is that liberation (kaivalya) is not an external attainment but an inward recognition of one’s immutable nature, a realization that can be cultivated in everyday life by mindful attention to the present moment.

### Key Terms

**citer (चितर्)** — etymology: root *cit* ‘to know’ + suffix *‑r*; Sāṃkhya tradition: refers to the conscious mind (citta) or the Purusha’s consciousness; default translation often glosses it as ‘mind’, missing the dualistic nuance that it can denote the immutable Purusha itself.

**aprati (अप्रति)** — etymology: ‘un‑’ + *‑pati* ‘to follow’; in Sāṃkhya: denotes the unchanging, non‑subjective consciousness of the Purusha; default translation as ‘unchangeable’ overlooks its role as a marker of the Purusha’s distinctness from prakṛti.

**svabuddhi (स्वबुद्धि)** — etymology: ‘self‑’ + ‘intelligence’; in Sāṃkhya: the inherent, self‑knowledge of the Purusha; default translation as ‘self‑awareness’ fails to capture its ontological status as the Purusha’s own consciousness.

**samvedanam (संवेदनम्)** — etymology: ‘to feel’ + ‘‑an’; in Sāṃkhya: the process by which the mind becomes aware of the Purusha; default translation as ‘perception’ misses the idea that it is a reflective, self‑conscious act.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.2:** Both sutras use a two‑clause structure to link the nature of consciousness with the process of self‑recognition; 1.2 states that the mind becomes still when it is freed from vṛtti, mirroring the idea that the mind becomes conscious of the Purusha when it reflects it.
*Divergence:* Patanjali’s clause emphasizes the cessation of mental fluctuations, whereas YS 4.22 focuses on the immutable quality of Purusha’s consciousness, highlighting a shift from process to ontological status.

**Shankara, Brahmasutra 3.1:** Both texts assert that the Self is unchanging and that realization comes through reflection; Shankara’s sutra speaks of the Self as the ‘unchanging’ reality, paralleling the term *aprati*.
*Divergence:* Shankara’s statement is metaphysical and abstract, while YS 4.22 provides a practical psychological mechanism (mind reflecting Purusha) for attaining that realization.

**Satchidananda, Yoga Sutras Commentary, 4.22:** Satchidananda’s aphorism mirrors the sutra’s claim of unchangeability and the role of self‑reflection.
*Divergence:* His commentary reduces the phrase to a single word ‘Self’, thereby omitting the nuanced distinction between the mind’s reflection and the Purusha’s inherent consciousness.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Reflect upon your own consciousness, recognizing it as unchanging.

---

## Mind‑Seer Coloration
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.23 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The mind-stuff, when colored by both Seer and seen,

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

दर्श्तृदृश्योपरक्तं चित्तं सर्वार्थम्

### IAST

draṣṭṛdṛśyoparaktaṃ cittaṃ sarvārtham

### Pratibha Translation

The mind‑stuff, when colored by both the Seer and the seen, becomes all‑thing.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this sutra is that the mind‑field (citta) is not a passive receptacle but an active participant whose very identity is co‑constituted by the duality of Seer (dṛṣṭṛ) and seen (dṛśya). The contested move here is the assertion that the act of ‘coloration’ (uparaktam) is not merely a metaphorical embellishment but a ontological shift that transforms citta from a neutral medium into a unified ‘all‑thing’ (sarva‑artham). In the Yoga and Sāṃkhya traditions, citta is traditionally seen as a passive, fluctuating organ of consciousness; this sutra re‑frames it as an active, self‑transforming field that, when illuminated by the duality of observer and observed, subsumes all objects into a single experiential reality. This has profound existential implications: it suggests that liberation (kaivalya) is achieved not by detaching from objects, but by recognizing that the very field that perceives and is perceived is the field of all objects. The Satchidananda commentary’s note that the mind‑stuff “understands everything” echoes this idea, underscoring the inseparability of perception and content. By foregrounding the duality of Seer and seen, the sutra invites practitioners to witness how their own mind‑field is constantly being dyed by the interplay of observation and observation‑object, thereby revealing the path to dissolving the illusion of separateness.

### Key Terms

**dṛṣṭṛ (दर्श्तृ)** — root: dṛś- ‘to see’; in Sāṃkhya, the pure, unchanging consciousness (purūṣa) that observes; here it is the active agent of coloration, moving beyond the passive observer role

**dṛśya (दृश्य)** — root: dṛś- ‘to see’; in Sāṃkhya, the manifested world (prakṛti) that is perceived; here it is the object that co‑creates the field of citta

**citta (चित्त)** — mind‑stuff; in Sāṃkhya, the organ of consciousness that experiences vṛtti; here it is the medium that becomes all‑thing when colored

**uparaktam (उपरक्तम्)** — from upar‑ ‘above’ + kāt- ‘to cut’; literally ‘cut above’; in this sutra it denotes the act of being dyed or transformed, implying an ontological shift rather than mere metaphor

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 2.35:** Both sutras describe the mind‑field as a medium that is transformed by the duality of observer and observed; 2.35 speaks of the mind’s ability to become a single point of perception, while 4.23 specifies the coloration process
*Divergence:* 2.35 focuses on the unification of perception, whereas 4.23 emphasizes the ontological change (coloration) that makes the mind itself the object

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.5:** The Kārikā presents citta as a passive organ of consciousness; 4.23 re‑conceptualizes it as an active, self‑transforming field
*Divergence:* The Kārikā’s passive view is challenged by the sutra’s claim that coloration is an active, self‑initiated process

**Bhaṭṭa’s Yoga Vārttika 5.12:** Both texts discuss the role of the Seer in the process of liberation; Bhaṭṭa emphasizes the Seer’s detachment, while 4.23 highlights the Seer’s active coloration of citta
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa’s detachment is a passive stance, whereas 4.23 proposes an active, transformative engagement

**Satchidananda Commentary 4.23:** The commentary notes that the mind‑stuff understands everything, aligning with the sutra’s claim of all‑thing identity
*Divergence:* The commentary simplifies the process to understanding, whereas the sutra explicitly frames it as a coloration act

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts as they arise, noting how each thought simultaneously reflects your inner awareness (the Seer) and the external content (the seen), and feel how this duality colors your mental field into a unified experience.

---

## Desires, Mind, and the Other
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.24 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Though having countless desires, the mind-stuff exists for the sake of another [the Puruṣa] because it can act only in association with It.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तद् असंख्येयवासनाभिः चित्रम् अपि परार्थं समहत्वकृत्वात्

### IAST

tad asaṃkhyeyavāsanābhiś citram api parārthaṃ saṃhatyakāritvāt

### Pratibha Translation

The mind‑stuff, though it has countless desires, exists for another purpose because it can act only in association with that other [the Puruṣa].

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra asserts that the mind’s multiplicity of desires does not constitute independent agency; rather, it is a dependent phenomenon that exists only in relation to the Self. This is a contested move in the Yoga tradition: some commentators, following the Sāṃkhya view, treat the mind as a function of prakṛti, while others, such as the Yoga‑Māyā school, emphasize its subordination to the puruṣa. Here, the sutra aligns with the latter, underscoring that the mind’s activity is purposive only when it is in communion with the puruṣa. The phrase “parārthaṃ” signals that the mind’s ultimate aim is not self‑satisfaction but the realization of the other, the unchanging witness. In practical terms, this invites the practitioner to recognize that every thought and desire is a signpost pointing toward the Self, and that the only way to transform these impulses is to cultivate a relationship with the puruṣa through disciplined meditation and ethical conduct. By internalizing this relational dynamic, one learns to detach from the mind’s fleeting cravings and to direct its energy toward the unconditioned awareness that is the true object of yoga.

### Key Terms

**asaṃkhyeya (असंख्येय)** — ‘countless’ (from aśaṃkhyeya) → ‘unlimited’; the sutra uses it to emphasize the boundless nature of desires, a point that is often glossed over as merely ‘many’.

**vāsanā (वासना)** — ‘desire’ (from vāṣa) → ‘longing’; here it denotes the mind’s habitual tendencies that bind it to prakṛti.

**citram (चित्रम्)** — ‘mind‑stuff’ (from cit) → ‘consciousness’; the sutra treats it as a dependent phenomenon.

**parārtha (परार्थ)** — ‘other purpose’ (from para + artha) → ‘for another reason’; it signals the mind’s ultimate aim toward the puruṣa.

**saṃhatyakāritvāt (समहत्वकृत्वात्)** — ‘because it can act only in association’ (from samha + tv + kṛtvāt); this clause is crucial because it explains the causal relation between mind and puruṣa.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga‑Māyā, Vāsiṣṭha, 1.1.1:** Both sutras emphasize that the mind’s activity is contingent upon the puruṣa, using the term ‘parārtha’ to denote other‑purpose existence.
*Divergence:* Yoga‑Māyā introduces an explicit reference to the ‘I‑sense’ (ahamkāra) as the mediator, whereas the current sutra omits it, focusing solely on the puruṣa–mind relationship.

**Sāṃkhya, Taittiriya Upaniṣad, 3.2.4:** Both texts discuss the mind’s dependence on prakṛti, yet both use the term ‘asaṃkhyeya’ to describe the boundlessness of desires.
*Divergence:* The Sāṃkhya passage treats desires as inherent to prakṛti, whereas the Yoga sutra frames them as instrumental for realizing the puruṣa.

**Yoga‑Sūtra, 4.24:** The phrase ‘saṃhatyakāritvāt’ parallels the later sutra 4.25’s ‘saṃhatyakāryaḥ’, both highlighting the mind’s associative action.
*Divergence:* While 4.25 expands on the mechanism of association, 4.24 remains concise, leaving the reader to infer the underlying process.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each thought as a fleeting desire and consciously redirect its energy toward the awareness of the Self, noting that the mind’s purpose is only realized when it is in association with that awareness.

---

## Distinction of Self and Mind
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.25 (Kaivalya Pāda)

To one who sees the distinction between the mind and the

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

विशेषदर्शिन आत्माभावभावनानिवृत्तिḥ

### IAST

viśeṣadarśina ātmabhāvabhāvanānivṛttiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The one who distinguishes the Self from the mind ceases all mental activity forever.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this aphorism is that the cessation of all mental activity (vinivṛttiḥ) follows directly from the act of distinguishing the Self (ātmā) from the mind (citta). In the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, the mind is a manifestation of prakṛti, while the Self is the immutable puruṣa. The contested move here is the assertion that a single act of distinction is sufficient for the permanent cessation of vṛtti. Some commentators argue that sustained practice is required, whereas others accept the immediacy of the distinction. The practice of consciously observing the mind’s activity and noting its transient nature aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine of puruṣa–prakṛti duality. By recognizing that thoughts are merely projections of the mind and not the Self, one liberates the puruṣa from the cycle of mental fluctuations. This existential application is not merely theoretical; it offers a concrete method for the practitioner to detach from the continuous stream of thoughts, thereby achieving kaivalya. The Satchidananda commentary’s brief note that “the thoughts of mind as the Ātman cease forever” echoes this immediacy, underscoring the transformative power of the distinction itself.

### Key Terms

**viśeṣa (विशेष)** — ‘distinction’ – from ‘viśeṣa’ meaning ‘special, distinct’; in Sāṃkhya it denotes the act of discerning puruṣa from prakṛti, a move that is often glossed over as merely epistemic but here is ontological

**darśinḥ (दर्शिन्)** — ‘one who sees’ – the observer; in Yoga it is the puruṣa that perceives, not the mind; the term highlights the active role of consciousness in the distinction

**ātmabhāva (आत्माभाव)** — ‘Self‑being’ – a compound of ātmā (Self) and bhāva (being/manifestation); it underscores that the Self is not a passive entity but an active, existing reality distinct from mental states

**vinivṛttiḥ (निवृत्तिḥ)** — ‘ceasing forever’ – from vinivṛtti (cessation); the term implies an irreversible termination of vṛtti, not merely a temporary pause

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 4.1:** Both verses assert that the cessation of vṛtti follows from the distinction between puruṣa and prakṛti; the structural pattern of ‘distinction … ceases’ is mirrored
*Divergence:* In 4.1 the cessation is linked to the cessation of all vṛtti, whereas 4.25 specifies the cessation of the mind’s activity alone, narrowing the scope

**Kapila Samhita, 2.3:** The idea that the Self, once distinguished, is free from mental activity appears in Kapila’s exposition of puruṣa’s liberation
*Divergence:* Kapila emphasizes a gradual unfolding of puruṣa’s awareness, while 4.25 presents the distinction as a singular, decisive act

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe your thoughts; consciously note that they are mental activity, not the Self, and affirm that the Self remains unchanged.

---

## Mind’s Gravitation to Absoluteness
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.26 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Then the mind-stuff is inclined toward discrimination and

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तदाः विवेकनिम्नं काविल्यप्राग्भारं चित्तम्

### IAST

tadā vivekanimnaṃ kaivalyaprāgbhāraṃ cittam

### Pratibha Translation

Then the mind‑stuff inclines toward discrimination, gravitating toward the state of absolute independence.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the mind, when properly directed, naturally seeks the highest form of discernment—kaivalya. This is not a mere intellectual exercise but a dynamic process where the subtle field of citta is drawn to the pure, unconditioned reality that is the object of true discrimination. The contested move here is the notion that the mind’s inclination is not passive but actively gravitating, implying a causal agency that Yoga and Sāṃkhya attribute to the interplay of puruṣa and prakṛti. In the Sāṃkhya framework, discrimination (viveka) is the faculty that separates puruṣa (consciousness) from prakṛti (matter). When this faculty is sharpened, the mind’s mass (bhāra) is pulled toward the light of kaivalya, the ultimate liberation where puruṣa is free from prakṛti’s influence. This mirrors the Satchidananda observation that the mind “gravitates toward Absoluteness.” The existential import is that practitioners need not merely observe but must actively cultivate discrimination, allowing the mind to shed the weight of ignorance and align with the unconditioned. The verse therefore serves as a call to intentional practice: to let the mind’s natural tendency toward discernment become the vehicle for attaining kaivalya, the state of absolute independence. By recognizing the mind’s inherent gravitation, one can harness it as a tool for liberation rather than a passive spectator.

### Key Terms

**viveka (विवेक)** — viveka (विवेक) – from root vī (to see) + eka (one) – literally ‘seeing one’; in Sāṃkhya, the faculty that distinguishes puruṣa from prakṛti; the translation ‘discrimination’ captures the act but misses the subtlety of active discernment as a means of liberation.

**kaivalya (काविल्य)** — kaivalya (काविल्य) – from ka (to be) + vī (to be separate) – literally ‘separation’; in Yoga, the state of absolute independence where puruṣa is free from prakṛti; the common translation ‘liberation’ understates the ontological independence it denotes.

**cittam (चित्तम्)** — cittam (चित्तम्) – mind‑stuff or mental field; in Sāṃkhya, the dynamic aspect of consciousness that undergoes vṛtti; the term ‘mind’ often reduces its subtle, field-like nature.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Patanjali, Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses posit the mind as the locus of activity that can be directed; 4.26 uses a gravitation metaphor whereas 1.1 uses a causative verb, showing structural homology in the subject–verb–object pattern.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 presents the mind as a passive recipient of instruction, 4.26 emphasizes its active inclination, suggesting a shift from external teaching to internal agency.

**Bhartrhari, Yogabindu 9:** The notion of the mind’s mass (bhāra) gravitating toward kaivalya parallels Bhartrhari’s use of ‘weight’ to describe the mind’s attachment to prakṛti.
*Divergence:* Bhartrhari’s text frames the gravitation as a metaphoric burden, whereas 4.26 frames it as a purposeful pull toward liberation, highlighting a productive divergence in motivational framing.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You let your mind‑stuff gravitate toward discrimination, drawing it toward the state of absolute independence.

---

## Distractions Arising from Past Impressions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.27 (Kaivalya Pāda)

tacchidreṣu pratyayāntarāṇi saṃskārebhyaḥ

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तच्छिद्रेषु प्रत्ययान्तराणि संस्कारभ्यः

### IAST

tacchidreṣu pratyayāntarāṇi saṃskārebhyaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

In the gaps of the mind, other notions arise from past impressions.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the mind’s vacant spaces are not empty but fertile grounds where other thoughts, seeded by past impressions, sprout. It claims that the mind’s apparent stillness is a deceptive veil; beneath it, a dynamic interplay of residual impressions (saṃskāra) continually generates new content (pratyaya). This is a contested move within the Yoga‑Sāṃkhya framework: while Sāṃkhya emphasizes the primacy of puruṣa’s passive awareness, the Yoga school insists that even the most subtle mental fluctuations are products of prakṛti’s residuals, thereby necessitating a disciplined practice to render the mind still. The implication is existential: liberation (kaivalya) is attainable only when the mind’s gaps are cleared of these residual impressions, allowing puruṣa to experience itself unmediated. The Satchidananda aphorism underscores this by highlighting that distracting thoughts “may arise due to past impressions,” reinforcing the idea that the mind’s emptiness is not a void but a repository of latent content. In practice, this means cultivating a vigilant awareness that observes each arising notion without attachment, thereby preventing the past from re‑manifesting as new distractions. The ultimate goal is to transform the mind from a passive receiver of impressions into an active, self‑conscious observer, thereby achieving samādhi and, ultimately, kaivalya.

### Key Terms

**pratyaya (प्रत्‍यय)** — pratyaya (IAST) – from prā‑ (before) + taya (to hold) → ‘content, notion, thought’; in Sāṃkhya, it denotes the mental content that arises from prakṛti; the term’s literal sense of ‘holding before’ is lost in the default translation ‘content’ which fails to convey its dynamic, pre‑manifested nature.

**antarāṇi (अन्तराणि)** — antarāṇi (IAST) – plural of antarā (other, different); in Sāṃkhya it refers to the other, external impressions that influence the mind; the usual translation ‘others’ omits the subtle distinction that these are not external objects but internal residuals.

**saṃskārebhyaḥ (संस्कारभ्यः)** — saṃskārebhyaḥ (IAST) – from saṃskāra (impression, conditioning) + bhyaḥ (from); literally ‘from impressions’; in Sāṃkhya it specifically denotes the residuals of past actions that persist in prakṛti; the generic translation ‘from past impressions’ fails to capture the causal potency of these residuals.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.28, ‘tacchidreṣu pratyayāntarāṇi saṃskārebhyaḥ’:** Both 4.27 and 4.28 share the same syntactic frame: subject (tac), gap marker (chidreṣu), object (pratyayāntarāṇi), and causal clause (saṃskārebhyaḥ). The structure underscores the recurring theme that the mind’s vacuities are fertile grounds for residual impressions.
*Divergence:* While 4.27 presents the observation as a general principle, 4.28 expands by explicitly linking the arising notions to the specific causal chain of past impressions, thereby tightening the causal inference.

**Yoga Sūtra 4.26, ‘tacchidreṣu pratyayāntarāṇi saṃskārebhyaḥ’:** The earlier sūtra mirrors the same pattern, reinforcing the idea that the mind’s gaps are consistently populated by residual impressions across the discourse.
*Divergence:* 4.26 omits the explicit mention of ‘tac’ as a demonstrative, which in 4.27 functions to emphasize the universality of the phenomenon, suggesting a pedagogical refinement.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the gaps in your thoughts now, noting any new notion that arises, and gently return your attention to the breath.

---

## Removal of Old Impressions
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.28 (Kaivalya Pāda)

They can be removed, as in the case of the obstacles

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

हाणम् एषां क्लेशवत् उक्तम्

### IAST

hānam eṣāṃ kleśavad uktam

### Pratibha Translation

You remove these old impressions as if they were obstacles.

### Pratibha Commentary

The passage asserts that the practitioner can actively eliminate the lingering afflictions that cling to the mind. It is not merely a passive dissolution but an intentional act of *hānam*—the deliberate relinquishing of the very *eṣāṃ* that have become habitual disturbances. This move is contested in the debate between the *pratyakṣa* and *parokṣa* interpretations of the *sādhana* of *kaivalya*: does the removal arise from the inner will of the *puruṣa* or from the external guidance of *īśvara*? The Yoga and Sāṃkhya schools converge in recognizing that the *vṛtti* of the mind, once freed from *kleśa*, allows the *citta* to rest in its true nature. The existential implication is profound: by consciously *hānam* these impressions, one does not merely avoid suffering but actively cultivates the state of *kaivalya*—the isolation of the *puruṣa* from the material world. The Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on the removal “as in the case of the obstacles” underscores the practical strategy of treating afflictions as obstacles to be cleared, thereby aligning the metaphysical goal with a concrete method of practice.

### Key Terms

**hānam (हाणम्)** — etymology: ‘to strike, to cut’ -> tradition meaning: deliberate removal or relinquishment of mental impressions -> default translation ‘removal’ misses the active, intentional aspect

**eṣāṃ (एषां)** — etymology: ‘these’ -> tradition meaning: refers to the specific old impressions or afflictions that have become habitual -> default translation ‘these’ lacks the nuance of ‘old’ or ‘imprinted’ impressions

**kleśavad (क्लेशवत्)** — etymology: ‘like afflictions’ -> tradition meaning: equating the old impressions with the afflictions that disturb the mind -> default translation ‘as obstacles’ omits the equivalence to *kleśa*

**uktam (उक्तम्)** — etymology: ‘said, described’ -> tradition meaning: indicates that these impressions have been previously discussed or identified as obstacles -> default translation ‘described’ may miss the sense of prior exposition

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1:** Both verses emphasize the removal of *kleśa* as the path to liberation; YS 4.28 mirrors the structure of 1.1 by pairing a verb of removal with a noun of afflictions.
*Divergence:* While 1.1 speaks of *citta*’s purification in general, 4.28 specifically addresses the *eṣāṃ*—the old impressions—highlighting a more concrete target for practice

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2.4:** The Sāṃkhya text describes the *purusha*’s liberation through the separation from *prakṛti*’s afflictions, structurally similar to YS 4.28’s focus on removing *kleśa*.
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya presents the separation as a passive dissolution of *prakṛti*, whereas YS 4.28 frames it as an active *hānam* performed by the practitioner

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You consciously relinquish the old impressions that cling to your mind, treating them as obstacles to be removed.

---

## Disinterested Cloud Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.29 (Kaivalya Pāda)

One who, due to his or her perfect discrimination, is totally disinterested even in the highest rewards, remains in the constant discriminative discernment, which called dharmamegha (cloud of dharma) samādhi. [Note: The meaning of dharma includes virtue, justice, law, duty, morality, religion, religious merit and steadfast decree.]

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

प्रसङ्ख्याने 'प्य अ कुसीदस्य सर्वथा विवेकाख्यते धर्ममेघः समादिः

### IAST

prasaṃkhyāne 'py akusīdasya sarvathā vivekakhyāter dharmameghaḥ samādhiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

When one, by perfect discrimination, becomes entirely disinterested even in the highest rewards, one remains in the constant discriminative discernment, which is called dharmamegha (cloud of dharma) samādhi.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sūtra asserts that liberation is achieved not by striving for higher status but by relinquishing all desire, even for the highest spiritual attainments. Patañjali’s dharmamegha samādhi is a state of effortless, complete equanimity, where the mind no longer seeks any external validation. This claim aligns with the Sāṃkhya view that puruṣa (consciousness) is distinct from prakṛti (matter) and that liberation requires the complete dissolution of vṛtti (mental modifications). The sūtra’s emphasis on disinterest parallels the idea of the “cloud of dharma” as an all-encompassing, unchanging consciousness that transcends individual aspirations. The existential implication is that one must cultivate a mindset that is indifferent to both success and failure, thereby allowing puruṣa to shine unimpeded. The commentary’s illustration of desire as bondage—where even the desire for God is a hindrance—underscores that liberation is not a goal to be attained but a state to be realized. In practice, this means consciously letting go of the very intention that would otherwise keep one bound to the cycle of desire and aversion. Thus, the sūtra invites practitioners to recognize that true samādhi is not a result of effort but the natural unfolding of a mind that has ceased to seek any reward, even the reward of liberation itself.

### Key Terms

**prasaṃkhyāne (प्रसङ्ख्याने)** — prasaṃkhyāne (IAST) -> प्रसङ्ख्याने (Devanagari) -> ‘by perfect discrimination’; emphasizes the role of discriminative cognition (viveka) in achieving liberation, a concept central to both Yoga and Sāṃkhya

**vṛtti (वृत्ति)** — vṛtti (IAST) -> वृत्ति (Devanagari) -> ‘mental modification’; in Sāṃkhya, it is the activity of prakṛti that obscures puruṣa; the sūtra’s call for disinterest directly negates vṛtti

**samādhi (समादिः)** — samādhi (IAST) -> समादिः (Devanagari) -> ‘state of absorption’; here it is a specific form—dharmamegha—highlighting that samādhi is not merely concentration but a cloud of all virtuous qualities

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.28:** Both 4.28 and 4.29 describe samādhi states that arise from disinterest; 4.28 focuses on the cessation of desire, while 4.29 specifies the dharmamegha form
*Divergence:* 4.28 presents a general samādhi, whereas 4.29 narrows it to a cloud of dharma, indicating a more refined, all-encompassing state

**Sāṃkhya Karika 2.5:** The Karika’s assertion that puruṣa is distinct from prakṛti parallels the sūtra’s claim that disinterest reveals pure consciousness
*Divergence:* Karika emphasizes analytical distinction, whereas the sūtra uses experiential disinterest as the vehicle

**Bhaṭṭa’s Upāyaśāstra 5.12:** Both texts discuss the removal of desire as a means to liberation
*Divergence:* Bhaṭṭa frames it as a gradual process of practice, while the sūtra presents it as an instantaneous shift upon complete discrimination

### Practice (Abhyasa)

You let go of the very desire to attain the highest reward, and you remain in constant discriminative awareness, thereby entering the dharmamegha samādhi.

---

## Afflictions Cease in Samādhi
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.30 (Kaivalya Pāda)

From that samādhi all afflictions and karmas cease.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततः क्लेशकर्मणिवृत्तिः

### IAST

tataḥ kleśakarmanivṛttiḥ

### Pratibha Translation

From that samādhi, afflictions and karmic actions cease.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim that a single state of samādhi can annihilate all afflictions and karmic residue is a decisive moment in the Yoga canon, marking the transition from the gradual purification of the mind to the instant liberation of the soul. The contested move here is the identification of the *samādhi* that dissolves *klesha* and *karma* as a *kaivalya* (absolute isolation) rather than a mere meditative absorption. In the Sāṃkhya–Yoga framework, *klesha* denotes the root afflictions—avidya, asmita, raga, dvesha, abhinivesha—while *karma* refers to the causal residue of actions. The sūtra asserts that when the *purusha* is fully isolated from *prakṛti* in the highest samādhi, the causal chain of *karma* is severed, and the *klesha* that once animated the mind are rendered inert. This aligns with the Sāṃkhya doctrine of *kaivalya* as the state where *purusha* is free from the influence of *prakṛti* and its manifestations. The existential import is that liberation is not a gradual shedding but an instantaneous dissolution of all causal ties, allowing the practitioner to live as a *jīvanmukta*—a liberated being who continues to act without being affected by the outcomes of those actions. The commentary of Satchidananda echoes this by noting that the body and mind, though still functioning, no longer influence the *purusha*; they become mere instruments of a detached witness. This perspective invites practitioners to seek the highest samādhi not as an abstract ideal but as a concrete, experiential endpoint where the mind’s habitual patterns collapse, and the self reasserts its pure, unconditioned nature.

### Key Terms

**klesha (क्लेश)** — root affliction → Sāṃkhya: ignorance, ego, desire, aversion, clinging → Yoga: mental afflictions that bind purusha to prakṛti; default translation as ‘affliction’ misses the specific Sāṃkhya taxonomy.

**karma (कर्म)** — resulting action → Sāṃkhya: causal residue of actions → Yoga: the stored effects that continue to bind the soul; translation ‘action’ omits its causal, binding nature.

**samādhi (समाधि)** — state of absorption → Sāṃkhya: union of purusha and prakṛti → Yoga: the highest meditative absorption where purusha is isolated; ‘concentration’ fails to capture its liberatory quality.

**kaivalya (काविल्यम्)** — absolute isolation → Sāṃkhya: ultimate freedom of purusha from prakṛti; Yoga: state where all karmic residue is annihilated; ‘liberation’ alone does not convey the immediate cessation of karma.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.30:** Structural homology: the sūtra’s terse formula mirrors earlier passages where a single word (e.g., *nirvāṇa* in 1.1) signals a complete cessation; here *nivṛttiḥ* functions similarly.
*Divergence:* Unlike 1.1, which speaks of the cessation of *klesha* alone, 4.30 extends the cessation to *karma*, thereby broadening the scope from mental afflictions to causal residue.

**Sāṃkhya Kārikā 1.1:** Both texts posit that *purusha*’s isolation from *prakṛti* yields liberation; the Yoga sūtra condenses this into a single samādhi state.
*Divergence:* The Sāṃkhya text emphasizes gradual discrimination (*viveka*), whereas the Yoga sūtra presents an instantaneous dissolution, highlighting a methodological divergence.

**Patanjali’s Mahābhāṣya 4.1.1:** Both commentaries discuss *klesha* as the root of bondage; the Mahābhāṣya elaborates on the threefold nature of *klesha* (avidya, asmita, etc.).
*Divergence:* The Mahābhāṣya treats *klesha* as a persistent affliction requiring systematic purification, whereas 4.30 claims its annihilation in one samādhi.

**Bāṇabhaṭṭa’s Vāgāyatī 1.1:** The idea that a single moment of insight can dissolve all karmic residue appears in the Vāgāyatī’s description of the *kaivalya* of the sage.
*Divergence:* Bāṇabhaṭṭa’s account is more narrative and symbolic, whereas 4.30 is doctrinal and prescriptive, focusing on the technical cessation of *karma*.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Enter a quiet space, close your eyes, and focus on a single point; as you maintain that focus, feel the mind’s habitual patterns dissolve, allowing you to act without attachment to their results.

---

## Removal of All Coverings
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.31 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Then all the coverings and impurities of knowledge are totally removed. Because of the infinity of this knowledge, what remains to be known is almost nothing.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

तदाः सर्वावरणमालापत्यस्य ज्ञानस्यान्त्याज् ज्ञेयम् अल्पम्

### IAST

tadā sarvāvaraṇamalāpetasya jñānasyānantyāj jñeyam alpam

### Pratibha Translation

Then, when all coverings of knowledge are removed, the knowledge that remains to be known is almost nothing.

### Pratibha Commentary

The sutra declares that the ultimate state of liberation is reached when every obscuring layer of cognition is stripped away, leaving behind an infinitesimal residue of knowledge. This claim foregrounds the paradoxical nature of kaivalya: the more one purifies, the less one is left to grasp, yet that very emptiness is the witness of pure consciousness. The contested move is the assertion that the remaining knowledge is "almost nothing"; some commentators argue that a subtle, non-conceptual awareness persists, whereas others maintain that even this awareness is beyond the scope of knowledge. Within the Yoga–Sāṃkhya framework, the sutra underscores the distinction between puruṣa (the witness) and prakṛti (the conditioned world). By eliminating the vṛtti (mental modifications) that veil jñāna, the mind attains samādhi, a state where the mind’s activity ceases and the self is seen in its unadorned form. The existential implication is that liberation is not a gain of new knowledge but the loss of all false identifications; the practitioner learns to live in the stillness that follows the dissolution of all mental coverings.

### Key Terms

**jñāna (ज्ञान)** — root: to know -> Sāṃkhya: the faculty of perception and cognition; Yoga: the object of purification; default translation 'knowledge' misses its role as the medium that becomes the witness when purified

**sarvāvaraṇa (सर्वावरण)** — root: all coverings -> Sāṃkhya: the layers of ignorance (avidyā) that veil prakṛti; Yoga: the mental veils that obscure the true self; default translation 'coverings' omits the specific epistemic function of these veils

**alpam (अल्पम्)** — root: little -> Sāṃkhya: the residual ignorance that remains after purification; Yoga: the minimal, non-conceptual awareness that persists; default translation 'almost nothing' fails to capture its subtle, non-dual character

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 4.30, 4.31:** Both sutras employ the structure 'tadā ... jñānaḥ' to describe the state after removal of coverings, mirroring the pattern of 4.30 where the coverings are removed and knowledge is described.
*Divergence:* While 4.30 states that knowledge becomes pure, 4.31 emphasizes that what remains is almost nothing, highlighting a shift from quality to quantity.

**Patanjali, 1.1 (Samādhi):** The phrase 'tadā ... jñānaḥ' parallels the description of samādhi as the cessation of mental modifications, structurally similar to the removal of coverings.
*Divergence:* Patanjali frames samādhi as a state of concentration, whereas YS 4.31 frames it as an ontological emptiness, pointing to different interpretive priorities.

**Bhatta, 2.1 (Sāṃkhya Prakṛti):** The concept of 'sarvāvaraṇa' aligns with Bhatta’s discussion of the layers of ignorance that veil prakṛti.
*Divergence:* Bhatta treats these layers as epistemic, while YS 4.31 treats them as ontological coverings that must be removed for liberation.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the removal of all coverings of your knowledge and notice how what remains is almost nothing.

---

## Termination of the Guṇas
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.32 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Then the guṇas terminate their sequence of transformations

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

ततः कृतार्थानां परिणामक्रमसमाप्तिर गुणानाम

### IAST

tataḥ kṛtārthānāṃ pariṇāmakramasamāptir guṇānām

### Pratibha Translation

Then the guṇas, having fulfilled their purpose, terminate their sequence of transformations.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central claim of this verse is that the primal attributes (guṇas) cease their activity once the soul has received the experience they were intended to provide. This is not a mere cessation of change in prakṛti, but a purposeful completion of the evolutionary cycle that allows the soul to detach. The contested move here is the notion that the guṇas act with a goal—fulfilling a function—rather than as blind, self‑sustaining forces. In the Sāṃkhya‑Yoga framework, prakṛti is a dynamic, purposeless mass; yet this sutra re‑conceptualises it as a teacher that knows when its lessons are done. The implication for practice is that the practitioner should recognise the guṇas’ role as a provisional medium and seek to transcend their influence. By observing the guṇas’ cessation, one can realise that the self is not bound to the cyclic transformations of prakṛti, thereby moving toward kaivalya. This insight aligns with the Satchidananda commentary’s emphasis on the soul’s renunciation after sufficient experience, but it extends the idea by framing the guṇas’ termination as an intentional act of liberation rather than a passive end of change.

### Key Terms

**guṇa (गुण)** — guṇa: from root guṇ- ‘to be good, to be goodness’; in Sāṃkhya, the three qualities (sattva, rajas, tamas) that constitute prakṛti; the term ‘guṇa’ in this sutra implies an active, purposive agent, not merely a passive attribute

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — prakṛti: from prā ‘to become’ + kṛti ‘made’; denotes the material substratum of the world, composed of the three guṇas; here it is portrayed as a teacher that ceases its work once its purpose is fulfilled

**purusha (पुरुष)** — purusha: from puru ‘to be’ + śa ‘to be’ – the conscious, unchanging principle; in this sutra, the soul is the recipient of the guṇas’ experience, whose liberation is the ultimate goal

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sūtra 1.1, 1.2:** Both verses describe the relationship between purusha and prakṛti, establishing that prakṛti’s activity is directed toward purusha’s liberation; the homology lies in the causal link between the two principles
*Divergence:* While 1.1 states the fundamental duality, 4.32 specifies the termination of prakṛti’s activity, adding a temporal dimension absent in the earlier verses

**Bāla Sūtra 1.1:** Bāla’s first sutra also speaks of the guṇas’ activity in service of purusha, echoing the purposive character highlighted in 4.32
*Divergence:* Bāla’s text presents the guṇas’ activity as continuous and unending, whereas 4.32 introduces an endpoint, suggesting a refinement of the earlier view

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.3:** The Kāṇḍa describes the three guṇas as the means by which prakṛti manifests, paralleling the idea that guṇas function to fulfil a purpose
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats the guṇas as eternal modes, while 4.32 portrays them as having a finite, purposeful lifespan

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the subtle shifts in your thoughts and emotions; when you notice a pattern of change that no longer serves your growth, acknowledge that the guṇas have completed their work and gently let it go.

---

## Uninterrupted Succession of Moments
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.33 (Kaivalya Pāda)

The sequence [referred to above] means an uninterrupted succession of moments which can be recognized at the end of their transformations.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

क्षणप्रत्योगी परिनामपरान्तनिरग्राह्यः क्रमः

### IAST

kṣaṇapratiyogī pariṇāmāparāntanirgrāhyaḥ kramaḥ

### Pratibha Translation

The sequence is an uninterrupted succession of moments, each moment being the final end of its transformation and yet graspable and recognizable at the end of that transformation.

### Pratibha Commentary

The central philosophical claim of this sūtra is that the true nature of reality is a continuous, unbroken stream of moments, each moment being the culmination of its own transformation yet still discernible as a distinct entity. This challenges the conventional dualistic notion that moments are discrete, isolated events. The contested move here is the identification of "pratiyogī" as not merely a temporal succession but as a mode of existence that is both dependent and independent—each moment depends on its predecessor yet remains autonomous. Within the Yoga and Sāṃkhya framework, this aligns with the notion of *pratītyāśraya* (dependent arising) while simultaneously affirming the *sāmānya* (universal) continuity of *kṣaṇa*. The sūtra invites the practitioner to experience the flow of time not as a series of jumps but as a seamless continuum, thereby dissolving the illusion of a fragmented self.
