By recognizing each moment as both the end of its own change and still graspable, one cultivates a state of *samādhi* where the mind rests in the present, free from the tyranny of past and future. This experiential insight is crucial for attaining *kaivalya*, the liberation that the Yoga Sutras ultimately aim for.

### Key Terms

**kṣaṇa (क्षण)** — kṣaṇa (moment) – from root *kṣ* meaning ‘to cut’ implying a precise, indivisible instant; in traditional usage it denotes the smallest unit of time, a point rather than a duration, underscoring the discontinuous yet continuous nature of experience.

**pratiyogī (प्रत्योगी)** — pratiyogī (uninterrupted succession) – etymologically ‘partnering together’; traditionally it connotes a series of dependent events that are nonetheless continuous, challenging the notion of isolated moments.

**pariṇāma (परिणाम)** — pariṇāma (transformation) – from *pari* ‘around’ + *nāma* ‘to take’; it signifies the process of change that culminates in a new state, highlighting the dynamic aspect of each kṣaṇa.

**nirgrāhyaḥ (निरग्राह्यः)** — nirgrāhyaḥ (graspable) – literally ‘without grasping’ yet here meaning ‘recognizable without attachment’; it points to the non-attachment to the moment while still being able to perceive it.

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutras 1.1:** Both sutras emphasize the continuity of consciousness; 1.1 speaks of the mind’s wandering, while 4.33 frames the moments as an uninterrupted sequence.
*Divergence:* 1.1 suggests a fragmented mind, whereas 4.33 asserts a unified, continuous flow, thereby reconciling apparent fragmentation with underlying unity.

**Samkhya Karika 2.3:** The Karika discusses *pratītyāśraya* (dependent arising) as a chain; 4.33 mirrors this with *pratiyogī* but adds the quality of being graspable.
*Divergence:* Karika treats dependence as purely causal, whereas 4.33 introduces a perceptual dimension, allowing moments to be recognized despite their dependence.

**Yoga Vasistha 1.2:** Both texts describe the mind’s temporal experience as a series of moments; Vasistha uses the metaphor of a river.
*Divergence:* Vasistha focuses on illusionary flow, whereas 4.33 asserts the objective reality of the sequence, emphasizing its recognizability.

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe each passing moment as an uninterrupted, recognizable instant, noting how it transforms yet remains distinct, and rest your awareness in that continuous flow.

---

## Supreme State of Independence
**Source:** Patañjali, Yoga Sūtras 4.34 (Kaivalya Pāda)

Thus, the supreme state of Independence manifests while t he guṇas reabsorb themselves into Prakṛti, having no more purpose to serve the Puruṣa. Or [to look from another angle] the power of pure consciousness settles in its own pure nature.

---

### Devanagari
*(editorial reconstruction from IAST; not source-verified)*

पुरुषार्थशून्यानां गुणानां प्रतिप्रसवः काविल्यम् स्वरूपप्रतिष्ठा वा चितीशक्तिरिति

### IAST

puruṣārthaśūnyānāṃ guṇānāṃ pratiprasavaḥ kaivalyaṃ svarūpapratiṣṭhā vā citiśaktir iti

### Pratibha Translation

The supreme state of independence arises when the qualities (guṇas) dissolve back into Prakṛti, leaving no purpose to serve the Purusha; alternatively, the power of pure consciousness settles in its own pure nature.

### Pratibha Commentary

The claim here is that kaivalya is not a new ontological category but the natural conclusion of the Purusha–Prakṛti interaction: when the guṇas cease to act, the Purusha rests in its own nature. This is the contested move that Patañjali makes—he does not posit a separate realm of liberation but a state achieved by the withdrawal of the guṇas. In the Sāṃkhya framework, Purusha is the passive witness, Prakṛti is the active material ground, and the guṇas are the dynamic modes that animate Prakṛti. Liberation is reached when the guṇas, having completed their function of animating Prakṛti, retract themselves; Purusha then is no longer engaged in the cycle of action and becomes a pure observer. The existential implication is that the practitioner need not seek external conditions for freedom; rather, by mastering the mind‑body system so that the guṇas finish their work, one attains a state where consciousness is unbound. This echoes Satchidananda’s observation that “the power of pure consciousness settles in its own pure nature,” but the Pratibha view stresses that this settlement is a natural consequence of the guṇas’ withdrawal, not a metaphysical insertion. The commentary therefore invites the reader to see kaivalya as the inevitable outcome of disciplined practice, not a mystical leap. It also highlights that the Purusha’s rest is not passive idleness but an active, self‑sufficient state of being, aligning with the Yoga Sutra’s broader theme of *citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ* leading to *svarūpa‑vasthānam*.

### Key Terms

**puruṣa (पुरुष)** — Purusha (Sāṃkhya) – the passive, unchanging witness; default translation ‘person’ misses its ontological role as the substratum of consciousness

**guṇa (गुण)** — Guṇa – the three qualities (sattva, rajas, tamas) that animate Prakṛti; default translation ‘quality’ obscures their dynamic, constitutive function

**prakṛti (प्रकृति)** — Prakṛti – the material ground, the unmanifested potential; default translation ‘nature’ fails to convey its role as the substrate of all change

**kaivalya (काविल्यम्)** — Kaivalya – absolute liberation, the state of independence; default translation ‘separation’ neglects its meaning as the natural state of Purusha after guṇa withdrawal

### Cross-Tradition Resonances

**Yoga Sutra 1.1, 1.2, 1.3:** The triad of citta‑vṛtti‑nirodhaḥ leading to svarūpa‑vasthānam mirrors the structure of 4.34 where the withdrawal of guṇas leads to kaivalya
*Divergence:* Unlike the early sutras that focus on mind‑control, 4.34 emphasizes the ontological shift of guṇas, highlighting a different mechanism of liberation

**Sāṃkhya Kāṇḍa 2.3:** Both texts describe Purusha as the passive witness and Prakṛti as the active ground; the movement of guṇas is a shared theme
*Divergence:* Sāṃkhya treats the withdrawal of guṇas as a theoretical possibility, whereas Patañjali presents it as the experiential endpoint of practice

**Yoga Sutra 4.31:** The progression from the dissolution of guṇas to the establishment of kaivalya follows the same causal chain as the earlier sutras’ description of the dissolution of *kleshas*
*Divergence:* 4.31 discusses klesha dissolution, while 4.34 focuses on guṇa dissolution, showing a parallel but distinct path to liberation

### Practice (Abhyasa)

Observe the subtle changes in your thoughts and sensations; when you notice a thought arising, let it pass without attachment, allowing the underlying consciousness to settle into its own pure nature.

---
